### [1] [Rethinking Handwritten Character Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10572)
*Ranjit Raut,Aarav Subedi,Ashim Shrestha*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GraphemeNet is a unified, multi-script HCR architecture that encodes stroke-level geometry via PSI and adapts pooling/attention with STM, achieving strong cross-script generalization and state-of-the-art performance with fewer parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-Latin handwriting recognition is understudied; treating HCR as generic image classification requires large models. Explicitly encoding script-geometric regularities as architectural inductive biases (structural priors) can improve accuracy and reduce parameters.

Method: GraphemeNet uses two orthogonal axes: Axis 1 (PSI) injects a script-specific asymmetric convolution that adds a stroke scaffold as a weighted residual at every encoder stage, anchoring features to script geometry. Axis 2 selects between global average pooling with gated fusion and cross-scale attention with a Stroke Topology Module (STM) depending on whether spatial relational reasoning is needed. A Linear Capsule Routing (LCR) with O(n) routing is shared across scripts. The model is deployment-friendly with script-specific scaffold and decoder topology.

Result: On fourteen benchmarks across eight writing systems, GraphemeNet generalizes with only scaffold and decoder topology varying per script, consistently outperforms published baselines, and demonstrates structural-prior efficiency as a broadly applicable principle for multi-script HCR.

Conclusion: Explicitly encoding geometric regularities as architectural inductive biases yields accurate, parameter-efficient multi-script HCR; GraphemeNet substantiates structural-prior efficiency as a general design principle.

Abstract: Non-Latin handwritten character recognition (HCR) remains understudied. Dominant methods consider it as generic image classification, which uses model scale to implicitly learn stroke structure. Structural-prior efficiency---the principle that explicitly encoding script-geometric regularities as architectural inductive biases can be both more accurate and require fewer parameters. We introduce GraphemeNet, a unified multi-script architecture, governed by two orthogonal binary axes. Axis 1 operationalises stroke-level geometric regularity via Persistent Scaffold Injection (PSI): a script-specific asymmetric convolution injects a stroke scaffold as a weighted residual at every encoder stage, continuously anchoring learned features to script geometry---distinct from skip connections, auxiliary losses, or attention reweighting. Axis 2 selects between global average pooling with gated fusion and cross-scale attention with a Stroke Topology Module (STM), depending on whether glyph discrimination requires spatial relational reasoning. A Linear Capsule Routing (LCR) with $O(n)$ routing is shared universally. On fourteen benchmarks across eight writing systems, the architecture generalises with only scaffold and decoder topology varying per script, consistently challenging, outperforming published baselines, and establishing structural-prior efficiency as a broadly applicable principle for multi-script HCR.

</details>


### [2] [AcFlow: Controlling Text-to-Image Diffusion Transformers via Learned Conditional Activation Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10723)
*Junran Wang,Zehao Jin,Tianyu Luan,Xinjie Shen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AcFlow introduces an inference-time, concept-conditioned velocity field to steer frozen diffusion transformers by transporting activation tokens, enabling fine-grained style control and suppression of unwanted concepts with generalization across unseen concepts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for non-intrusive, fine-grained control over style intensity and unwanted concepts in text-to-image diffusion models without retraining or per-concept tuning.

Method: An inference-time controller that applies a learned velocity field to intermediate layer image-token activations, guided by a textual concept description and a continuous integration horizon. The velocity updates vary by token and activation state, with shared parameters across a task family to enable generalization to unseen concepts.

Result: Achieves superior style-content trade-off in high-style regimes compared to baselines; quantitative metrics at a fixed operating point: style-content alignment 0.5365/0.2860 vs 0.4397/0.2684 (best baseline). Qualitative suppression of diverse concepts demonstrated; velocity field shown to be adaptive across tokens.

Conclusion: AcFlow provides effective, generalizable inference-time control for DiTs, enabling precise style manipulation and concept suppression without modifying the base model; code released.

Abstract: Text-to-image diffusion transformers (DiTs) are powerful generators, yet direct prompting provides limited control interface for style intensity and can fail to suppress unwanted concepts. To enable these controls, we introduce AcFlow, an inference-time controller that transports intermediate layer image-token activations through a learned concept-conditioned velocity field while keeping the base DiT frozen. A textual concept description specifies the desired intervention, while the integration horizon provides a continuous control parameter. The field produces token-varying, activation-dependent updates. With parameters shared across concepts within each task family, the field supports fine-grained descriptions and generalizes to concepts unseen during training without per-concept fitting. On style control, AcFlow achieves the best style--content trade-off among the evaluated baselines in the high-style-alignment regime. At a fixed operating point, AcFlow attains style--content alignment of 0.5365/0.2860, compared with 0.4397/0.2684 for the baseline with the highest style alignment. Qualitative results demonstrate suppression of diverse concepts, including cases where direct prompting fails. Our analyses support the learned velocity field as an adaptive control mechanism, with update directions varying across tokens and depend on their activation states. Our code is available at https://github.com/Nove1yst/AcFlow.

</details>


### [3] [Learn the Solid, Not the File: Canonical Inputs for Neural Networks on CAD Boundary Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11573)
*Heinrich Jiang,Hager Yasser Mohamed,Alexander Hitt,Valeriia Lomakina,Henning Jiang,Jennifer Jang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Canonical region graph (CRG) for B-rep handling; invariance to repartitioning and rigid motions; robust to perturbations; matches or surpasses top baselines on benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: B-rep can encode the same solid with different topologies/meshes, causing non-robust encoders. A solid-derived, invariant input representation is needed for stable processing.

Method: Define a canonical region graph whose nodes, features, and coordinate frames are derived from the solid itself; prove theoretical invariance to repartitioning and rigid motions; evaluate on standard benchmarks, perturbations, CAD-software variations, and a FreeCAD-based human dataset; compare against popular B-rep encoders.

Result: CRG matches the strongest baseline on standard benchmarks and remains stable under all tested perturbations, demonstrating robustness absent in existing encoders.

Conclusion: CRG provides theoretical invariance guarantees and practical robustness for B-rep processing, offering a stable input representation that mitigates issues from B-rep non-uniqueness and software-induced variations.

Abstract: Boundary representation (B-rep) is the standard format used by modern CAD systems for parametric 3D models. It turns out, the exact same solid can be represented by different B-reps: for example, two engineers using different operations, a geometry kernel rebuilding the file, and an export setting repartitioning faces will lead to different B-reps even though the underlying solid remains the same.
  We show that existing B-rep encoders are not robust to variation in the B-rep with the same solid on perturbations applied to standard benchmarks, naturally occurring variations inherent to CAD software, and differences in how designers model the same part via a human dataset we created in FreeCAD. The performance of popular B-rep encoders often collapses catastrophically.
  We propose the canonical region graph, an input representation whose nodes, features and coordinate frame are derived from the solid itself and show theoretical invariance guarantees on repartitioning and rigid motions. It matches the strongest baseline on standard benchmarks, and is stable under every perturbation we test.

</details>


### [4] [MHE-Former: Multi-Hypothesis Transformers via Entropy Maximization for 3D Mesh Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10743)
*Boshu Jia,Rongyu Chen,Linlin Yang,Zihao Liu,Yingjie Chen,Zhongqun Zhang,Zhulin Tao,Shaohui Lin,Xiaoyu Wu,Libiao Jin,Baochang Zhang,Angela Yao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes MHE-Former, a Transformer-based multi-hypothesis framework for monocular 3D hand/body mesh recovery, introducing an exploration-exploitation pipeline with entropy-driven multi-hypothesis generation and VLM-assisted hypothesis selection for user-guided, diverse predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deterministic, single-solution methods struggle under occlusion/ambiguity, leading to overconfident predictions. A multi-hypothesis approach with user-guided selection can improve robustness and practicality.

Method: Exploration phase uses a probabilistic formulation with entropy maximization to generate diverse hypotheses via a Transformer-based multi-hypothesis encoder-decoder (MHE-Former). Exploitation phase employs Hypothesis Selection, a context-aware process that leverages a vision-language model (VLM) to provide evidence and natural-language intent for selecting the most plausible hypothesis.

Result: Extensive experiments report state-of-the-art accuracy and diversity across multiple datasets. A user preference study validates practicality of the hypothesis selection process.

Conclusion: The proposed exploration–exploitation framework advances monocular 3D hands and body mesh recovery by producing diverse, plausible hypotheses and enabling user-guided selection, with strong empirical performance and demonstrated practicality.

Abstract: Monocular 3D hand and body mesh recovery often suffers from severe occlusion and ambiguity. Traditional deterministic methods typically regress a single optimal solution, leading to overconfident predictions. In this paper, we introduce an exploration--exploitation paradigm for ambiguous mesh recovery with multi-hypothesis learning and selection. Specifically, during exploration, based on our probabilistic formulation and entropy maximization, we propose a novel multi-hypothesis method referred to as MHE-Former. It is a Transformer-based multi-hypothesis framework, ensuring high training efficiency and label friendliness while generating plausible and diverse hypotheses. During exploitation, we propose Hypothesis Selection, a context-aware process for multiple predictions. Especially leveraging VLM's powerful visual understanding and reasoning capabilities, it allows users to choose the most plausible and desired estimate with additional evidence and natural language intent. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our framework achieves state-of-the-art performance in accuracy and diversity across multiple datasets. The user preference study further shows the practicality of our hypothesis selection process.

</details>


### [5] [Meta-Learning for Data-Efficient Plant Growth Estimation via Vision Transformers and Fuzzy Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10749)
*Sheikh Hasan Elahi,Rusith Chamara Hathurusinghe Dewage,Habib Ullah,Muhammad Salman Siddiqui,Rakibul Islam,Fadi Al Machot*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Few-shot plant growth estimation using ViT features and clustering-based task design with meta-learning; second-order MAML variants achieve strongest performance under label scarcity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Label scarcity in greenhouse monitoring necessitates data-efficient regression; embedding-space task construction can improve meta-learning efficiency.

Method: Use Vision Transformer embeddings from an unlabeled image pool; construct tasks via fuzzy c-means clustering; apply gradient-based meta-learning; compare MAML variants; assess intra-cluster support selection.

Result: Second-order meta-learning (e.g., MAML++) yields superior performance in few-shot settings; intra-cluster support selection has limited, dataset-dependent impact; structured task design enables reliable growth estimation with few labels.

Conclusion: Structured embedding-space task construction combined with second-order meta-learning is a key driver for data-efficient plant growth estimation; task design dominates performance gains over other factors.

Abstract: Accurate plant growth estimation is essential for greenhouse monitoring, yet obtaining labeled data remains costly and time-consuming. To address this, we propose a few-shot regression framework that combines Vision Transformer (ViT) feature embeddings, clustering-based task construction, and gradient-based meta-learning, and show that task construction in embedding space is a primary driver of performance. The approach leverages an unlabeled image pool to organize data into structured tasks using fuzzy c-means clustering, enabling efficient learning from a small number of labeled samples. We systematically evaluate meta-learning methods and show that second-order methods (e.g., Model-Agnostic Meta-Learning variants such as MAML++) outperform classical baselines in the few-shot regime. Furthermore, intra-cluster support selection has a limited and dataset-dependent impact. Experiments on two plant datasets show that structured task design combined with meta-learning enables reliable plant growth estimation under severe label scarcity.

</details>


### [6] [GRADE: Single-Frame Generative Radar Depth Estimation Under Visual Degradation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10756)
*Bin Zhao,Patrick Chiou,Nakul Garg*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GRADE leverages a pretrained generative prior integrated with single-frame mmWave radar geometry to produce high-fidelity metric depth under smoke/fog and darkness. It first estimates coarse depth from 4D radar spectra, then a latent diffusion model refines structure conditioned on that estimate, with a pixel-space adapter that uses camera cues when available. Trained on ~95k frames across 12 buildings with real smoke, it achieves MAE of 0.303 m in clear scenes and 0.313 m under smoke, outperforming baselines. Code and data are released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense 3D depth perception degrades when airborne particulates (smoke, fog) or darkness block optical sensors. mmWave radar remains operational under these conditions but has limited angular resolution due to small aperture. There is a need to fuse radar geometry with learned priors to recover high-fidelity metric depth under adverse visibility.

Method: 1) Map raw 4D radar spectra to a coarse metric depth. 2) Use a latent diffusion backbone to recover structural detail, conditioning every denoising step on the radar-based estimate. 3) Include a pixel-space adapter that leverages residual camera cues when available. 4) Train across clear, smoke-degraded, and occluded inputs so outputs approach the radar-conditioned path as visibility degrades. 5) Evaluate on ≈95k frames from 12 buildings with real smoke; release code and datasets.

Result: MAE 0.303 m in clear scenes and 0.313 m under smoke; outperforms existing baselines.

Conclusion: GRADE effectively grounds a pretrained generative prior in radar geometry to deliver high-fidelity metric depth under adverse visibility, via radar-conditioned diffusion refinement and optional camera cues; robust across smoke and occlusions; resources released.

Abstract: Dense 3D depth perception fails under smoke, fog, and darkness because optical sensors cannot penetrate airborne particulates. mmWave radar remains usable and measures range accurately under these conditions, but its small aperture limits angular resolution. We present GRADE, which grounds a pretrained generative prior in single-frame radar geometry to estimate high-fidelity metric depth. GRADE first maps raw 4D radar spectra to coarse metric depth. A latent diffusion backbone then recovers structural detail while conditioning every denoising step on this estimate. A pixel-space adapter uses residual camera cues when available and is trained across clear, smoke-degraded, and occluded inputs so the full output approaches the radar-conditioned path as visibility degrades. Trained and evaluated on ~95K frames across 12 buildings with real smoke, GRADE achieves an MAE of 0.303 m in clear scenes and 0.313 m under smoke, outperforming existing baselines. Code and datasets are available at https://phi-lab-rice.github.io/GRADE.

</details>


### [7] [Shedding Light: A Benchmark for Evaluating Lighting Understanding in Generative Image Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10787)
*Justine Giroux,Jack Oliver Hilliard,Yannick Hold-Geoffroy,Javier Vazquez-Corral,Jean-François Lalonde*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A scalable benchmark to evaluate lighting understanding in generative models by inserting light-probe objects into real photos and measuring illumination consistency; derives direction, color, and radiance metrics for photometric realism; code and data released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether modern image generative models truly understand and reproduce physically plausible lighting, addressing the lack of photometric evaluation in existing benchmarks.

Method: Construct a multi-illumination image dataset with simple 'light probe' objects. Inpaint the same object into real images. Compare the inpainted results to ground-truth probes. Estimate lighting direction, color, and radiance distribution from the inpainted probes to quantify illumination accuracy. Propose a scalable evaluation protocol and metrics for photometric realism.

Result: Proposes a scalable lighting-understanding benchmark with quantitative illumination metrics. Provides a protocol to benchmark photometric accuracy of current and future generative models. All code and data are released at the project page.

Conclusion: The work offers a foundational, scalable framework to assess and compare how well generative models capture real-world lighting, enabling systematic benchmarking of photometric realism across models.

Abstract: Accurate modelling of illumination is central to realistic image synthesis and scene understanding. Yet, there is little exploration into whether image generative models are good at this task or whether physical plausibility remains a key challenge for them. Clearly, significant progress has been made in realistic image synthesis, but do models truly understand lighting in a physically accurate manner? To answer this question, this work proposes a benchmark to assess the lighting understanding and harmonisation capabilities of generative models. Our key insight is that evaluating lighting understanding for such models only requires testing how well they insert novel objects into real photographs whilst maintaining consistent illumination. To do so, we use a multi-illumination dataset with images containing simple objects serving as ``light probes'', and prompt models to inpaint the same object onto the original image, then compare the generated results against the ground-truth light probes. We then estimate the lighting direction, colour and radiance distribution from the inpainted probes, providing a quantitative measure of illumination accuracy and photometric realism. Our work establishes a scalable evaluation protocol to systematically assess how well generative models capture and reproduce real-world lighting, offering a foundation for benchmarking the photometric accuracy of any future models. All code and data are available at https://lvsn.github.io/SheddingLight/ .

</details>


### [8] [Two-Parameter Flow Map Learning for Continuous-Time Diffeomorphic Image Registration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10789)
*Mohammadjavad Matinkia,Nilanjan Ray*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes a continuous-time, non-autonomous ODE learning framework for diffeomorphic image registration by using a two-parameter flow map with cocycle consistency, removing time discretization during training and enabling accurate, diffeomorphic alignment across diverse datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods either assume stationary velocity fields or rely on discretized integration of non-autonomous ODEs, which couples model capacity to numerical discretization and can impair diffeomorphism guarantees. A direct, continuous-time learning approach aims to enhance expressiveness, preserve diffeomorphic structure, and reduce discretization-induced errors.

Method: Introduce a framework that learns the continuous-time solution of a non-autonomous ODE represented as a two-parameter flow map. Enforce cocycle consistency to capture properties of time-varying flows, eliminating the need for velocity integration and time discretization during training. Inference uses a small number of flow-map compositions. The approach is compatible with standard registration backbones.

Result: Empirically, the method improves registration accuracy across nine datasets, achieving an average Dice improvement of 2.1% on brain MRI, a 12% reduction in target registration error (TRE) on lung CT, and a 2.6% Dice gain on cardiac MRI and ultrasound datasets.

Conclusion: Learning continuous-time, cocycle-consistent non-autonomous flows yields better diffeomorphic registration without time discretization overhead, generalizes across diverse datasets, and can be integrated with existing registration architectures to improve performance while maintaining diffeomorphic structure.

Abstract: Diffeomorphic image registration is central to medical image analysis, enabling anatomically consistent alignment across subjects. Most learning-based diffeomorphic methods model autonomous ODEs(ordinary differential equations) by parameterizing a stationary velocity field and recovering deformations via scaling-and-squaring. While non-autonomous ODEs with time-dependent velocities increase expressiveness, existing approaches rely on numerical integration to implicitly enforce flow structure that entangles model expressiveness with discretization accuracy. We propose a framework to directly learn the continuous-time solution of a non-autonomous ODE formulated as a two-parameterflow map. By enforcing cocycle consistency, a fundamental structural property of time-varying flows, we learn the flow maps without time discretization and velocity integration during training. The framework recovers diffeomorphic mappings at inference using a small number of compositions. Our proposed framework seamlessly incorporates standard registration backbones and improves alignment accuracy consistently across nine datasets while preserving diffeomorphic structure. Notably, the proposed method achieves an average Dice improvement of 2.1% on brain MRI benchmarks, a 12% TRE reduction on lung CT, and a 2.6% Dice gain on cardiac MRI and ultrasound datasets.

</details>


### [9] [How Much Velocity Does Off-Ball Space Value Need? A Broadcast-Viewport Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10801)
*Seongjin Choi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Velocity-aware analysis in broadcast off-ball tracking shows velocity mainly matters in the visible channel; imputation is weakly affected, while surface gains are modest; apply velocity selectively (velocity on wide shots) and strengthen tight-shot imputation; code/logs released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Determine at which layer of broadcast off-ball analysis velocity information alters conclusions when many players are off-screen and calibration drifts.

Method: Extend the off-screen imputation protocol (three Metrica matches, 44 m viewport, block-bootstrap CIs) and evaluate four velocity regimes (none, viewport-legal observed, true-for-visible, true-for-all) against a velocity-aware ground truth across three layers: imputation, control surface, and team verdicts; analyze using a fixed velocity window with an eleven-point jitter noise ladder (sigma_v) and SoccerNet-GSR clips; report MAE, per-frame error, variance decomposition, and bias patterns.

Result: Velocity shows minimal benefit for imputation (-0.2 pp MAE improvement on a 12–14 pp velocity-free surface). For the control surface, first-order gains (-1.5 to -1.8 pp, 11–15% of the surface MAE). For team verdicts, gains are small (-0.12 to -0.19 pp). The visible channel dominates: perfect occluded-velocity adds 2–6% of the visible gain, with no last-seen decay policy outperforming this. Omitting velocity blurs the surface (per-frame |e| = 2.2–2.6 pp) with small time-averaged bias (≤0.4 pp); imputation bias is strong and structured against defending deep zones (5–9 pp). With eleven jitter settings and a velocity-noise axis sigma_v, break-even occurs near 1 m/s. Measured sigma_v = 1.65 m/s recovers 24–36% of the benefit; 43% of variance is frame-common and tolerable by the surface; residual heavy-tailed, not captured by Gaussian controls. The velocity-free error that velocity removes grows with viewport width (7% at 36 m, 21% at 60 m). Recommendation: fix imputation on tight shots, apply velocity on wide shots. Code and logs are released.

Conclusion: The key velocity effects are confined to the visible channel; accurate velocity integration yields modest improvements overall, but provides meaningful gains when viewport width is large. An actionable strategy is to apply velocity selectively—enhancing wide-shot analysis while keeping tight-shot imputation robust—and to release tooling for replication.

Abstract: Velocity-aware pitch control is standard, but under a broadcast viewport half the players are off screen and on-screen velocities come from a drifting calibration. We ask at which layer of broadcast off-ball analysis velocity changes the answer. Inheriting our off-screen imputation protocol (three Metrica matches, 44 m viewport, block-bootstrap CIs), we score four velocity regimes -- none, viewport-legal observed, true-for-visible, true-for-all -- against a velocity-aware ground truth at three layers: imputation, the control surface, and team verdicts. Velocity is nearly useless for imputation (-0.2 pp against a 12--14 pp velocity-free surface MAE), first-order for the surface (-1.5 to -1.8 pp, 11--15% of that MAE), and ten times smaller for verdicts (-0.12 to -0.19 pp). The velocity that matters is the visible channel: perfect occluded-player velocity adds 2--6% of the visible gain, and no last-seen decay policy we tested exceeds that. Omitting velocity blurs the surface (per-frame |e| 2.2--2.6 pp) with small time-averaged bias (per cell <=0.4 pp), whereas imputation error is a structured bias against the defending team's deep zone (5--9 pp). At a fixed velocity window, a noise ladder of eleven jitter settings, including sigma_v-matched pairs, is ordered to first order by one velocity-noise axis sigma_v with break-even ~1 m/s; eleven SoccerNet-GSR clips from one match through our pipeline measure sigma_v=1.65 m/s yet recover 24--36% of the benefit: 43% of the variance is frame-common, which the surface tolerates, and the residual is heavy-tailed and clustered, which Gaussian controls matched on component RMS do not reproduce (+0.03 vs. +0.36). The share of velocity-free error that velocity removes grows with viewport width (7% at 36 m, 21% at 60 m): fix imputation on tight shots, velocity on wide ones. Code and logs are released.

</details>


### [10] [TrajFusionNet+: Transformer-Based Prediction of Pedestrian Crossing Intention via Fusion of Trajectory Representations and Scene Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10806)
*François G. Landry,Moulay A. Akhloufi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A transformer-based multi-branch model (TrajFusionNet+) fuses sequential, visual, and graph-based scene context to predict pedestrian crossing intention, achieving SOTA on PIE/JAAD and better cross-dataset generalization under a joint-training protocol.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve pedestrian crossing intention prediction by leveraging diverse representations (temporal trajectories, visual scene cues, and relational scene context) to enhance accuracy and generalization across datasets (PIE and JAAD).

Method: Three-branch architecture: SAM processes past and predicted trajectory sequences; VAM overlays observed/predicted bounding boxes on scene images to form visual features; GAM builds pedestrian-centric graphs from segmented scenes to model relations to traffic elements. Transformer-based fusion built atop TrajFusionNet. A novel joint-training protocol trains on PIE and JAAD jointly, with evaluation performed separately on each dataset.

Result: State-of-the-art performance on PIE and JAAD; under the joint-training evaluation protocol, TrajFusionNet+ demonstrates superior generalization compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: Integrating sequential, visual, and graph-based scene representations in a transformer framework yields strong predictive performance and improved cross-dataset generalization for pedestrian crossing intention tasks.

Abstract: The pedestrian crossing intention task involves predicting whether pedestrians are likely to cross the road from the point of view of an autonomous vehicle. We introduce TrajFusionNet+, a novel transformer-based model for pedestrian crossing intention prediction. TrajFusionNet+ combines sequential and visual representations of pedestrian trajectory with a graph-based representation of the scene context in order to predict pedestrian crossing intention. The proposed architecture builds upon our previous model, TrajFusionNet, and comprises three branches: a Sequence Attention Module (SAM), which processes a sequential representation of past and predicted pedestrian trajectories; a Visual Attention Module (VAM), which utilizes a visual representation of the pedestrian trajectories by overlaying observed and predicted bounding boxes onto scene images; and a Graph Attention Module (GAM), which extracts pedestrian-centric graphs from segmented scene images and captures the relational dependencies between pedestrians and traffic elements. TrajFusionNet+ achieves improved state-of-the-art performance on the two most widely used pedestrian crossing intention datasets, PIE and JAAD. Furthermore, we introduce a new evaluation protocol in which models are trained jointly on the PIE and JAAD datasets but evaluated separately on each. Under this setting, TrajFusionNet+ demonstrates superior generalization compared to existing approaches.

</details>


### [11] [Overpainting: Localized Context-aware Diffusion Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10811)
*Sam Sartor,Iliyan Georgiev,Michael Fischer,Valentin Deschaintre,Pieter Peers*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A trimap-guided image editing method called Overpainting, built on diffusion models, enabling precise or loose edits with awareness of prior content; includes an automated data-generation pipeline and attention-based adaptation for broad editing tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for targeted, context-aware image edits that constrain changes to specified regions while preserving surrounding content; existing methods struggle with explicit spatial control and context retention.

Method: Adapt a pretrained diffusion model via joint attention and low-rank adaptation (LoRA) across input images, with attention-dropout to balance information flow among noise, source, and mask. Propose an automated training data pipeline: (1) generate candidate image pairs using language-based editing models, (2) curate these pairs, (3) extract a trimap from each usable pair.

Result: Demonstrates versatility of Overpainting across a wide range of editing tasks, with the trimap mechanism providing both precise and flexible control; qualitative results suggest effective region-specific edits with awareness of prior content.

Conclusion: Overpainting offers controllable, region-aware editing via trimaps and diffusion-model adaptation, supported by an automated data-generation pipeline, enabling broad applicability to diverse editing tasks.

Abstract: We present "overpainting", an image editing operation which offers both control over the location of the edit and awareness of the previous content in that location. The overpainted area is given by a trimap, where white-annotated pixels must be edited, gray-annotated pixels may be edited, and black-annotated pixels must not be edited. This enables both precise and loose control, depending on user intent.
  We implement overpainting by adapting a pretrained image editing diffusion model using a combination of joint attention and low-rank adaption across input images with attention-dropout to balance the information flow between noise, source and mask images. We present a novel, automated, training data generation pipeline that (1) generates a set of candidate image pairs leveraging existing language-based editing models, (2) carefully curates those pairs, and (3) extracts a trimap from each usable pair. We demonstrate the versatility of our overpainting model on a wide range of editing tasks.

</details>


### [12] [BodyCam-VQA: Enhanced Body-Worn Camera Video Captioning via Multimodal Reasoning and Probe Question Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10815)
*Karish Gupta,Matthew Alex,Alex Li,Yang Wu,Yun-Wei Chu,Kashif Munir,Xiaotian Zhou,Zhengping Ji,Xiaozhong Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: An Adaptive Visual Question Answering (VQA) framework with structured reasoning improves extraction of fine-grained forensic evidence from police body-worn camera (BWC) footage, outperforming traditional captioning and generic VLM approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BWC footage is multimodal and often of low quality, containing chaotic scenes and critical forensic details. Current Vision-Language Models often miss important evidence and officer-suspect interactions, hindering transparency, accountability, and safety. There is a need for a high-stakes VQA system that can extract objective, detailed records from enforcement events.

Method: Develop an Adaptive VQA framework that employs structured reasoning to guide fine-grained visual evidence extraction. Evaluate multiple question-generation models, including foundation models and fine-tuned open-weight models, to assess performance variation across QG implementations in the context of law-enforcement scenarios.

Result: The VQA-driven architecture yields more reliable, objective, and detailed records of enforcement events compared with captioning or standard VLM baselines. Performance varies with the choice of question-generation model, indicating the importance of QG design choices for forensic detail.

Conclusion: This approach constructs a scalable, objective tool for AI-assisted forensic clarity, enhancing legal transparency and safety for both officers and the public, while highlighting the need for careful deployment and further research into robustness, fairness, and real-world evaluation.

Abstract: Police body-worn camera (BWC) footage has emerged as a critical aspect of law enforcement that ensures legal transparency, officer accountability, and the protection of civil rights. However, effectively processing this data remains a significant challenge due to its multimodal video format. BWC videos, in many cases, comprise chaotic scenes with low visual quality, rapid movement/interactions, and high-noise audio that make visual understanding a challenge for even SOTA multimodal models. Current Vision-Language Models (VLMs) frequently overlook critical forensic details, such as the presence of valuable evidence or the latent nuances of suspect-officer interactions, which are vital for fair legal outcomes and civilian/officer safety. To address these limitations, we propose an Adaptive Visual Question Answering (VQA) framework engineered for high-stakes law enforcement. Our framework employs a structured reasoning approach to extract fine-grained visual evidence that traditional captioning systems fail to capture. We experiment with multiple question generation models, including foundation models and fine-tuned open-weight models, to observe performance variation among question generation model implementations. Our results demonstrate that this VQA-driven architecture provides a more reliable, objective, and detailed record of enforcement events, ultimately serving as a powerful tool to protect both law enforcement officers and the public through AI-assisted forensic clarity.

</details>


### [13] [Are We Really Doing Few-Shot Learning? A Critical Examination of Pre-Training Assumptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10851)
*Alejandro Galan-Cuenca,Marcelo Saval-Calvo,Antonio Javier Gallego*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Disjoint in-domain pre-training does not eliminate the influence of target-domain data in few-shot learning; in-domain pre-training yields larger gains than out-of-domain pre-training, indicating domain overlap biases performance. Label-free augmentation and a descriptor-based source-selection strategy can match or closely approach supervised cross-domain pre-training, suggesting the need to move beyond default in-domain pre-training for realistic low-data evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate whether standard few-shot evaluation protocols (pre-training on disjoint classes from the same domain) truly reflect low-data learning and to quantify how source-target domain relations and labeling affect performance.

Method: Systematic empirical study across eight datasets, three few-shot architectures, and multiple way-shot settings, comparing: no pre-training, in-domain class-disjoint pre-training, supervised out-of-domain pre-training, and label-free out-of-domain pre-training. Additionally, a descriptor-based source-selection strategy is proposed to estimate source-domain suitability before pre-training.

Result: In-domain pre-training improves over no pre-training by 33.41 percentage points on average, while supervised out-of-domain pre-training yields 23.75 points, implying a 9.66-point optimistic bias from domain overlap. Label-free augmentation achieves 27.71-point average gains, closely matching supervised out-of-domain at 27.97 points. A descriptor-based source-selection strategy reduces the gap to oracle selection with a median of 1.37 percentage points.

Conclusion: Default practice should move beyond in-domain pre-training; domain overlap can inflate reported performance, especially when target-domain data are scarce. Label-free strategies and source-domain descriptor-based selection offer practical routes to realistic low-data performance and near-oracle performance without relying on labeled cross-domain data.

Abstract: Few-shot learning is commonly evaluated under protocols that pre-train a model on a large auxiliary set whose classes are disjoint from the target episodes yet drawn from the same visual domain. This paper examines whether such protocols truly reflect low-data learning. We systematically compare no pre-training, class-disjoint in-domain pre-training, supervised out-of-domain pre-training, and label-free out-of-domain pre-training across eight datasets, three few-shot architectures, and multiple way-shot settings. Our results show that class disjointness alone is insufficient to remove the influence of target-domain data. In-domain pre-training improves over no pre-training by 33.41 percentage points on average, whereas supervised out-of-domain pre-training yields 23.75 percentage points, revealing a 9.66-point optimistic bias associated with domain overlap. Although out-of-domain pre-training is more realistic in applications where target-domain data are scarce, its effectiveness depends strongly on the compatibility between source and target domains. We further show that labeled source data are not strictly required, with an augmentation-based label-free strategy reaching an average gain of 27.71 percentage points and closely matching supervised out-of-domain pre-training at 27.97 percentage points. Finally, we introduce a descriptor-based source-selection strategy that estimates source-domain suitability before pre-training, reaching a median gap of only 1.37 percentage points to oracle selection. These findings highlight the need to move beyond in-domain pre-training as the default few-shot evaluation protocol, since it can overestimate performance in realistic scenarios where target-domain data are scarce.

</details>


### [14] [Evaluation of Vision-Language Models Across Diverse Coastal Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10855)
*Seth Knoop,Chad R. Samuelson,Gabriel R. Slade,Brady Moon,Joshua G. Mangelson*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A dense coastal dataset is introduced to probe vision-language models (VLMs) in maritime scenes, revealing coastal concept recognition is harder than broad landscape classes, with improvements tied to segmentation and richer textual labels rather than environmental context alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate VLMs in coastal environments, where perception challenges may differ from terrestrial settings, and assess how text-to-mask, mask-to-mask, and mask-to-text alignments perform with coastal concepts.

Method: Create a densely labeled dataset of >1,000 images from seven missions in three Oahu regions, annotated with 18 semantic classes and >7,400 instances. Evaluate seven modern VLMs across three experiments: text-to-mask, mask-to-mask, and mask-to-text alignment, comparing coastal vs. conventional concepts and testing alternative textual labels.

Result: Broad landscape classes are generally recognized more accurately than coastal/object classes; coastal concepts are the hardest. Across shared conventional classes, environmental context alone does not explain performance gaps. Mask-to-mask matching is similar between coastal and conventional classes. Providing alternative textual labels improves recognition for several coastal concepts; overall, coastal performance is driven more by segmentation quality and linguistic representation than by environment.

Conclusion: Lower coastal-class performance likely stems from segmentation and linguistic representation limitations rather than environmental context alone; improvements should focus on better segmentation and richer language mappings to coastal concepts.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) enable robotic per- ception by associating visual observations with natural-language concepts. Yet their performance in coastal environments remains largely unexplored. We introduce a densely labeled coastal dataset containing more than 1,000 images collected across seven missions in three regions of Oahu, Hawaii, with 18 semantic classes and over 7,400 annotated instances. We evaluate seven modern VLMs through three complementary experiments mea- suring text-to-mask, mask-to-mask, and mask-to-text alignment. Broad landscape classes are generally recognized more accurately than conventional object and coastal classes, with coastal con- cepts presenting the greatest challenge. However, comparisons of shared conventional classes across coastal and terrestrial datasets reveal no consistent performance difference attributable solely to environmental context. Mask-to-mask matching also remains similar across conventional and coastal classes, while alternative textual labels substantially improve recognition of several coastal concepts. These results suggest that lower performance on coastal classes (at least on the objects/query categories evaluated) is heavily influenced by segmentation and linguistic representation.

</details>


### [15] [Symmetry-aware super-resolution of crystal orientation maps via invariant latent-space learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10898)
*Umang Garg,Warren Zamudio,McLean P. Echlin,Samantha H. Daly,Tresa M. Pollock,B. S. Manjunath*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A symmetry-aware super-resolution network for EBSD orientation maps on the quotient SO(3)/G, SG-SRAN, achieves high-fidelity SR with far fewer parameters by enforcing symmetry-consistent encoding and local boundary-preserving decoding, with strong benchmarks and zero-shot transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EBSD data reside on the quotient manifold SO(3)/G, making standard Euclidean interpolation inappropriate and risking boundary blur. Efficient, symmetry-consistent SR is needed to preserve grain boundaries and fidelity under acquisition-time constraints.

Method: SG-SRAN uses a frozen, locally isometric encoder to map equivalent orientations to a common latent representation; SR operates in this latent space with local-support tokens to prevent cross-boundary mixing; a dictionary-based decoder recovers valid orientations; evaluated on FCC and HCP benchmarks, demonstrating strong performance with far fewer trainable parameters.

Result: Outperforms or matches large 15–16M-parameter baselines using only 27–49k trainable parameters; achieves lowest p68 error, highest inverse-pole-figure fidelity, and zero-shot transfer to unseen alloys.

Conclusion: Symmetry-group-aware representation and boundary-preserving SR enable efficient, accurate EBSD super-resolution; the approach leverages the quotient geometry to prevent symmetry-induced misinterpretations and could generalize to other quotient-manifold imaging tasks.

Abstract: Crystal-orientation maps are physical fields defined only up to crystal symmetry; electron backscatter diffraction (EBSD) resolves them experimentally, but acquisition-time constraints limit spatial resolution. Unlike conventional images, EBSD data lie on the quotient space $\mathrm{SO}(3)/G$, where $G$ is the crystal-symmetry group. Standard Euclidean interpolation can therefore mix symmetry-equivalent representations and blur grain boundaries. We introduce the Symmetry-Group-Aware Super-Resolution Attention Network (SG-SRAN), which incorporates crystal symmetry and boundary preservation by design. A frozen, locally isometric encoder maps equivalent orientations to a common latent representation in which Euclidean distance approximates misorientation. Super-resolution is performed in this space, with each high-resolution token restricted to a feature-consistent local support to prevent cross-boundary mixing. A dictionary-based decoder then recovers valid orientations. Across FCC and HCP benchmarks, SG-SRAN matches 15-16 million parameter backbones using only 27-49k trainable parameters, while achieving the lowest p68 errors, highest inverse-pole-figure fidelity, and zero-shot transfer to unseen alloys.

</details>


### [16] [HiPerViT: A Hierarchical Perceiver-Vision Transformer Architecture for Multi-Scale Texture Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10917)
*João Pedro C. A. de Sá,Odemir Martinez Bruno*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HiPerViT introduces a compact second-order statistical token (bilinear descriptor) into a Vision Transformer via Perceiver-style distillation, enabling explicit texture statistics; yields consistent texture recognition gains across benchmarks and shows robustness to backbone depth and fusion order.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Texture recognition requires high-order statistics not captured by standard object-centric transformers; aim to expose second-order co-occurrence statistics as explicit tokens to improve texture sensitivity without multimodal pretraining.

Method: Compute a compact bilinear descriptor from global/local views, encode as a statistical token, and fuse with first-order spatial tokens using Perceiver-style latent distillation to allow interaction between spatial tokens and second-order statistics.

Result: Six texture benchmarks show consistent improvements; notable gains on DTD, GTOS-Mobile, 1200Tex; gains robust to backbone and interaction/distillation order, indicating the second-order statistics as the key factor.

Conclusion: Explicit second-order statistical tokenization is an effective, robust principle for texture-centric recognition in vision models; no multimodal pretraining required.

Abstract: Texture recognition remains challenging for modern vision models because discriminative evidence is often carried by higher-order spatial statistics rather than by object shape alone. While Vision Transformers provide strong long-range modeling capacity, their standard object-centric representations do not explicitly expose such statistical structure, which limits texture sensitivity in fine-grained recognition settings. We present HiPerViT, a compact vision-only architecture that injects an explicit second-order statistical prior into a transformer-based recognition pipeline. The method combines global and local image views with a compact bilinear descriptor encoded as a statistical token, and integrates this token with first-order spatial representations through Perceiver-style latent distillation. This design enables direct interaction between spatial tokens and second-order feature co-occurrence statistics, providing the model with explicit access to texture-relevant information without requiring multimodal pretraining or ensemble construction. Across six texture recognition benchmarks, HiPerViT achieves consistent improvements over strong vision-only baselines under the reported evaluation protocols, including gains of +3.05 percentage points on DTD, +10.48 on GTOS-Mobile, and +10.10 on 1200Tex. Beyond benchmark performance, our analyses show that these gains are largely invariant to the backbone depth used to extract second-order statistics and to the ordering of interaction and distillation stages. This pattern suggests that the primary source of improvement is not a specific fusion topology, but the explicit availability of second-order statistical information as a first-class representational signal. These results support explicit statistical tokenization as an effective and robust design principle for texture-centric visual recognition.

</details>


### [17] [CamPilot: A Multi-Agent Cinematic Assistant for Camera-Controlled Movie Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10943)
*Yang Wu,Stefano Petrangeli,Ishita Dasgupta,Yu Shen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A multi-agent framework (CamPilot) for text-to-video generation incorporating cinematographic planning and camera-work control, trained via GRPO learning on 14K professional films, with a CamEval benchmark; claims improved cinematographic control and film-like quality over SOTA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current text-to-video systems fall short of professional filmmaking in camera work fidelity and cross-shot continuity; need structured cinematographic reasoning and coherent shot progression.

Method: GRPO-based learning to encode camera work planning from 14K real-world films, learning motion patterns, composition principles, and cross-shot relationships for controllable camera viewpoints; multi-agent collaboration to refine outputs; introduces CamEval benchmark for evaluation.

Result: Empirical results indicate CamPilot outperforms state-of-the-art text-to-movie methods in cinematographic control and overall quality.

Conclusion: Professional camera design principles significantly enhance automated movie generation; the CamPilot framework and CamEval benchmark provide a path toward more coherent, human-aesthetic video generation.

Abstract: The integration of large language models (LLMs) into video generation has enabled rapid text-to-video creation and improved visual quality. However, it still falls short of professional filmmaking, where cinematographic language is less refined than human-crafted camera work and multi-shot continuity remains challenging. To address these limitations, we introduce CamPilot, a multi-agent framework that integrates cinematographic planning and camera-work control to produce more coherent, logically structured, and human-aesthetic movies. CamPilot adopts a GRPO-based learning paradigm to learn camera work planning from 14K real-world professional movies, internalizing motion patterns and composition principles that support reasoning over shooting techniques (e.g., camera angle, motion, and focal behavior) and cross-shot relationships for controllable camera-viewpoint generation. Multiple agents further collaborate and evolve to improve overall output quality. To support this work and further studies in this domain, we establish CamEval, a benchmark for evaluating camera work quality and cinematic engagement. Empirical results show that CamPilot outperforms state-of-the-art text-to-movie generation methods on cinematographic control and quality, highlighting the impact of professional camera design on movie generation.

</details>


### [18] [New Evidence, Same Choice: Testing Physical Experiment Selection in Vision Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11022)
*Sourajit Saha,Shubhashis Roy Dipta,Nobin Sarwar,Shaswati Saha,Yuxuan Jiang,Siyuan Li,Qiheng Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A benchmark for vision-language models to decide when to answer and which cheap physical experiment to perform, measuring evidence selection separately from final answers to reveal limitations in physical reasoning not captured by accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current physical reasoning benchmarks evaluate final answers only, missing the model's ability to decide whether to answer now or seek additional evidence; understanding this decision process is crucial for robust physical reasoning in AI systems.

Method: A controlled evaluation: each problem shows one measurement image and four possible physical worlds formed by two masses and two values of another property. The model must either stop and answer or choose the cheapest additional experiment to resolve the question. Problem pairs are crafted so that the optimal action depends on the observed measurement and the question. The optimal action is computable since all worlds and costs are known. Six open models are tested across 144 parameter sets.

Result: Direct responses consistently repeat the same action for 95.1%–100% of image pairs, even when the correct action differs. Brief reasoning improves switching but the best model correctly handles both decisions only 5.9% of the time. Error analysis points to failures in measurement interpretation, physical reasoning, and response formatting. Evaluating evidence selection separately from final answers reveals limitations in physical reasoning that accuracy alone misses.

Conclusion: Benchmarking evidence selection uncovers gaps in physical reasoning not captured by final-answer accuracy, highlighting a need to assess when to ask for more data and which experiments to perform, not just what final answer is produced.

Abstract: A model first sees an image from one physical measurement experiment, such as how far a block coasted, and must answer a question about a new trial, such as whether the block will pass a target after a fixed push. The initial experiment may provide enough information to answer, or the model may need another measurement, such as the object's mass, friction, restitution, or spring stiffness. We study whether vision language models can decide when to answer immediately and, when more evidence is needed, which experiment to perform. Current physical reasoning benchmarks usually evaluate only the final answer, so they do not directly measure this decision-making ability. We introduce a controlled evaluation where each problem provides one measurement image and four possible physical worlds created by combining two possible masses and two possible values of another relevant property. The model must either stop and answer or select the cheapest additional experiment that can resolve the question. We construct matched problem pairs where changing either the observed measurement or the question changes the optimal action. Since all possible worlds and experiment costs are known, we can explicitly determine the optimal choice. Across six open models and 144 physical parameter sets, direct responses repeat the same action for 95.1% to 100% of image pairs even when the correct action changes. Brief reasoning improves action switching, but the best model makes both decisions correctly for only 5.9% of image pairs. Additional analysis reveals failures in measurement interpretation, physical reasoning, and response formatting. By evaluating evidence selection separately from final answers, our benchmark reveals limitations in physical reasoning that conventional answer accuracy can overlook.

</details>


### [19] [Toward Interpretable Multimodal Fusion: Heat Conduction Modeling for Hyperspectral and LiDAR Joint Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11040)
*Kan Wei,Jiahui Cui,Jing Yao,Xinyu Zhao,Lei Wang,Pedram Ghamisi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: M2Heat uses a heat-conduction-inspired multimodal fusion framework for hyperspectral and LiDAR data, featuring a visual heat conduction module (vHeat), enhanced Frequency Value Embeddings (FVEs), and a Cross-Frequency Fusion (CFF) strategy to capture global dependencies with sub-quadratic complexity, achieving competitive results with interpretable fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the challenge of modeling long-range dependencies and anisotropic interactions in HS-LiDAR fusion while preserving computational efficiency and interpretability.

Method: Propose a physics-inspired visual heat conduction module (vHeat) and enhanced Frequency Value Embeddings (FVEs) to simulate anisotropic information flow; integrate a hybrid spatial-frequency fusion strategy via Cross-Frequency Fusion (CFF); emphasize interpretability through heat-conduction-guided mechanisms and sub-quadratic complexity.

Result: Achieves competitive overall performance on three benchmarks (Trento, Houston2013, Augsburg), with highly discriminative and robust feature representations and an interpretable fusion process; source code released at GitHub.

Conclusion: Heat-conduction-guided neural operators are a promising direction for efficient and interpretable multimodal fusion in RS, offering a physics-inspired, interpretable lens on HS-LiDAR fusion and potential extension to other modalities.

Abstract: The fusion of hyperspectral (HS) and Light Detection and Ranging (LiDAR) data plays a crucial role in enhancing land-cover classification by jointly exploiting spectral, spatial, and structural cues. However, existing multimodal fusion methods still struggle to model long-range dependencies and complex anisotropic interactions while maintaining computational efficiency. This paper introduces M2Heat, a physics-inspired framework that investigates multimodal fusion through the lens of heat conduction. At its core, a physics-driven visual heat conduction module (vHeat) and enhanced Frequency Value Embeddings (FVEs) simulate anisotropic information flow, enabling the capture of global dependencies with sub-quadratic complexity and physical interpretability. This mechanism, combined with a hybrid spatial-frequency fusion strategy named Cross-Frequency Fusion (CFF) module, produces highly discriminative and robust feature representations. M2Heat achieves competitive overall performance on three benchmarks, i.e., Trento, Houston2013, and Augsburg, while providing an interpretable heat-conduction-guided perspective for multimodal feature fusion. These results indicate the potential of heat-conduction-guided neural operators for efficient and interpretable RS multimodal fusion. The source code is publicly available at https: /github.com/Weikan0425/M2Heat_HSI_LiDAR.

</details>


### [20] [Meta-Learning for Classifier Selection in Image Datasets: A Feature-Driven Framework for Accuracy Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11041)
*Zahra Nabizadeh_Shahre_Babak,Farzaneh Koohestani,Nader Karimi,Shahram Shirani,Shadrokh Samavi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes a meta-learning approach to predict classifier performance from dataset meta-features, enabling efficient model selection across 56 diverse image datasets; achieves >86% average ranking accuracy; uses autoencoders, pre-trained networks, and dimensionality reduction to extract features and clustering to group classifiers; aims to reduce computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: No Free Lunch implies no single classifier is universally optimal. Efficiently selecting the best-suited classifier for diverse image datasets is challenging due to data heterogeneity and complexity; a meta-learning framework can predict performance without exhaustive training.

Method: Construct dataset meta-features capturing complexity; extract/select features via autoencoders, pre-trained networks, and dimensionality reduction; train regression models to predict classifier accuracies; cluster classifiers by similar performance patterns to simplify recommendations; evaluate on 56 diverse image datasets (nature, animals, numbers, motorcycles, medical images, human bodies).

Result: The approach achieves an average ranking prediction accuracy exceeding 86% across 56 datasets, indicating strong ability to forecast relative classifier performance and guide model selection while reducing training costs.

Conclusion: A scalable, interpretable meta-learning framework can effectively guide classifier selection for diverse image domains, offering practical gains in performance and computational efficiency without exhaustive training.

Abstract: No Free Lunch theorem implies that any performance gains achieved by a classifier on a particular image distribution are necessarily offset by a loss of performance over the set of all possible problems; thus, no single model is universally optimal. Selecting the most suitable classifier for image datasets is a critical yet challenging task due to the intrinsic complexity and diversity of images. This paper proposes a meta-learning framework that leverages a comprehensive set of meta-features capturing dataset complexity to predict classifier performance without exhaustive training. By extracting and selecting features using methods such as autoencoders, pre-trained networks, and dimensionality reduction techniques, we train regression models to efficiently estimate classifier accuracies. Additionally, clustering techniques are employed to group classifiers with similar performance patterns, simplifying the recommendation process. The datasets used span a wide range of concepts, including nature, animals, numbers, motorcycles, medical images, and human bodies, to ensure broad generalization. Evaluated on 56 diverse image datasets, our approach achieves an average ranking prediction accuracy exceeding 86%, demonstrating its effectiveness in guiding model selection. This scalable and interpretable framework provides a practical solution to improve classification performance while reducing computational costs.

</details>


### [21] [TailProp: content-adaptive light- and heavy-tailed propagation for vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11081)
*Jiahao Kong,Zihan Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TailProp introduces a cross-regime adaptive propagation backbone (Tail Propagation Operator) that fuses Gaussian and Cauchy propagators in the DCT domain for efficient, content-conditioned, channel-wise adaptation, yielding improved performance across vision tasks while maintaining manageable computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the diversity of spatial interactions required by different samples, channels, and network stages, and the limitation of single-dynamical-family propagations by enabling a content-conditioned, two-basis propagation that can adapt to varied visual representations.

Method: Introduce TPO with Gaussian and Cauchy stable-process propagators as complementary bases. Predict a content-conditioned, shared channel-wise coefficient to adaptively mix bases. Fuse responses in the DCT domain with a single DCT/IDCT pair, achieving O(N^{1.5}) spatial mixing for square feature maps (N=HW). Build TailProp as a hierarchical backbone optimized across tasks.

Result: TailProp outperforms matched baselines across image classification, object detection, semantic segmentation, robustness, and cross-backbone restoration. TailProp-B achieves 84.4% Top-1 on ImageNet-1K, 50.3/44.8 AP for boxes/masks on Mask R-CNN (3x schedule), and 50.8% mIoU on ADE20K. Ablations indicate gains arise from complementary two-basis propagation, not from single-basis propagation or within-family adaptive order alone.

Conclusion: Two-basis, content-conditioned propagation in a cross-regime, DCT-fused framework is an effective design principle for visual representation learning, offering consistent gains across tasks and backbones and enabling efficient, interpretable propagation dynamics.

Abstract: Science-inspired vision models show that explicit propagation dynamics can provide structured and interpretable alternatives to conventional token mixing. Existing formulations, however, typically construct and adapt visual propagation within a particular dynamical family, while visual representations can require substantially different spatial interactions across samples, channels, and network stages. We explore cross-regime adaptive propagation and introduce TailProp, a hierarchical vision backbone built upon the Tail Propagation Operator (TPO). TPO uses Gaussian and Cauchy stable-process propagators as complementary bases with rapidly decaying and heavy-tailed spatial influence, and predicts a content-conditioned channel-wise coefficient to adaptively combine them. Because this coefficient is spatially shared, the two responses are fused directly in the DCT domain with a single DCT/IDCT pair, yielding $O(N^{1.5})$ spatial mixing for square feature maps with $N=HW$ and fixed channel width. Across image classification, object detection, semantic segmentation, robustness, and cross-backbone restoration, TailProp consistently outperforms matched propagation baselines; TailProp-B reaches 84.4% Top-1 accuracy on ImageNet-1K, 50.3/44.8 box/mask AP under the 3x Mask R-CNN schedule, and 50.8% mIoU on ADE20K. Controlled ablations further show that these gains are not explained by single-basis propagation, an additional same-family branch, or within-family adaptive order alone, supporting complementary two-basis propagation as an effective design principle for visual representation learning.

</details>


### [22] [Beyond Benchmarks: Using VLMs to Reveal Systematic Classification Failures Under Real World Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11126)
*Dieuwertje Alblas,Alma M. Liezenga,Jan Erik van Woerden,Fedor Taggenbrock,Dalia Aljawaheri,Klamer Schutte*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VLM-based error slice detection shows potential to accelerate V&V of defense classifiers, but not yet fully automated and faces domain-related challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve verification and validation (V&V) of sensor-processing classifiers by reducing reliance on manual sample inspection; enable faster identification of systematic errors.

Method: Propose VLM-based error slice detection (ESD) that clusters and labels systematic errors; test on non-military data with artificial perturbations; examine military vs non-military embedding variation and surroundings-based clustering; analyze overlap in cluster descriptions.

Result: Successfully identified artificially added perturbations in a non-military dataset; in military data, clusters by surroundings but with overlap; embedding variation differences observed; does not yet enable full automation but indicates potential to speed up V&V.

Conclusion: VLMs could accelerate V&V in defense applications in the future; further work needed to address data underrepresentation and contextual diversity to approach automation.

Abstract: Verification and validation (V&V) of classification models is crucial to enable a wide range of sensor processing applications. Currently, the V&V process relies on time-consuming manual inspection of erroneous samples to find meaningful patterns. This work explores the use of Vision Language Models (VLMs) to speed up this laborious process. VLMs are trained to embed images into a semantically meaningful vector representation, from which human-interpretable systematic errors can be distilled. Deploying such VLM-based methods in a defence context introduces two major challenges: (1) the defence domain is underrepresented in the training data of VLMs, and (2) surroundings and context are less diverse than for other domains. This study provides an initial assessment of the suitability of VLM-based methods for V&V of defence applications. We propose a VLM-based error slice detection (ESD) method that independently groups and labels systematic errors made by a classification model. We demonstrate that this method is able to identify operationally-relevant artificially added perturbations in a non-military dataset. In a military context, our method clusters and describes images based on their surroundings, but also exhibits overlap between cluster descriptions. We further investigate the difference in embedding variation between our military and non-military dataset, which remains a topic of interest. Although the results do not yet warrant fully automated V&V through VLM-based ESD, they show that VLMs could be used to accelerate V&V processes in the future.

</details>


### [23] [ReconPlusGen: Injecting Reconstruction Prior into Multi-view 3D Generation through Noise Inversion and Modulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11129)
*Jiarui Liu,Heng Li,Weiyu Li,Keng Deng,Junyuan Deng,Zheng Zhongxing,Junyu Huang,Jiahao Chang,Xiaoguang Han,Ping Tan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A diffusion-based 3D reconstruction framework that uses multiple input images to predict a canonical-space point cloud and guides the diffusion process via noise inversion and modulation to complete unobserved regions while refining visible geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable faithful 3D reconstruction from multi-view images by integrating geometry estimation with diffusion-based generation, addressing incomplete data and maintaining generative realism.

Method: From multiple inputs, predict a canonical-space point cloud; deterministically inject the predicted geometry into the diffusion process through noise inversion; modulate the resulting noise to balance fidelity with the capacity to complete unobserved regions and refine visible geometry; present qualitative results on benchmark and real-world images, with an illustration of the reconstruction-guided noise initialization and modulation.

Result: Qualitative results demonstrate reconstruction performance on benchmark images and real-world data; the approach includes a visualization of reconstruction-guided noise initialization and modulation, showing how geometry guides diffusion to produce coherent reconstructions.

Conclusion: The approach shows that coupling geometry prediction with diffusion-based noise manipulation can achieve geometry-consistent reconstructions that fill in unobserved regions while preserving and refining visible geometry.

Abstract: Qualitative results and an illustration of our core idea. Top left: reconstruction results on benchmark images. Top right: reconstruction results on real-world images. Bottom: illustration of reconstruction-guided noise initialization and modulation. Given multiple input images, we predict a point cloud in canonical space, deterministically inject the predicted geometry into the diffusion process through noise inversion, and modulate the resulting noise to preserve the generative flexibility required to complete unobserved regions and refine visible geometry.

</details>


### [24] [LAION-Mobile: Evaluating Deepfake Detectors On One Million Smartphone Photos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11134)
*Achim von Stryk,Janis Keuper*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LAION-Mobile reveals that state-of-the-art deepfake detectors under modern smartphone computational photography are unreliable: AUC on modern AI content remains low (≤0.624), many detectors perform at chance; threshold calibration inflated deployable false positives on legacy data, but refitting on modern data yields 17–91% false positives on real photos; no detector beats chance while maintaining deployable real-photo FPR. 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap between benchmarked deepfake detectors (tailored to legacy imaging pipelines) and the now-dominant modern smartphone imaging, which uses computational photography pipelines that can generate images rather than merely record them; test detectors on large-scale modern smartphone data and assess deployment risk.

Method: Construct LAION-Mobile (~1M smartphone images with EXIF from re-LAION-5B). Evaluate twelve state-of-the-art detectors using their original checkpoints on a 9,115-image evaluation sample (DIRE on 738). Compare detector performance on modern AI content versus legacy content; examine the impact of threshold calibration by applying legacy thresholds vs. refitting thresholds on modern content.

Result: All detectors under modern AI content fail to exceed AUC 0.624; five detectors fall below chance. Real-photo false-alarm rates appear inflated due to legacy threshold calibration; when thresholds are refitted to modern content, many detectors show 17–91% false positives on real photos. No detector both beats chance on modern AI content and maintains a deployable real-photo FPR.

Conclusion: There is a significant open gap in deepfake detection for modern ISP regimes; current detectors trained on legacy pipelines do not generalize to modern smartphone imaging. A large-scale, modern-content benchmark (LAION-Mobile) is essential to drive robust detectors and realistic deployment criteria.

Abstract: Most Deepfake detectors report near-perfect AUC scores on their reference benchmarks. However, a recent ICML position paper argues that these evaluations collectively neglect the impact of modern smartphone photography: the widely used on-device neural image-signal processing pipelines (like multi-sensor fusion or noise and motion-blur suppression) increasingly shift the imaging paradigm from simple lens projections towards computational photography. Hence, devices actually generate, rather than record photos. This increases the risk that deepfake detectors may flag ordinary phone photos as fake. Due to the lack of large-scale datasets containing images from modern smartphones, this hypothesis has so far only been tested in small proof-of-concept studies. The aim of this paper is to close this gap. We introduce LAION-Mobile, an open dataset containing about 1 million smartphone images with EXIF metadata distilled from re-LAION-5B. Evaluating twelve state-of-the-art deepfake detectors with their original paper checkpoints on a 9,115-image evaluation sample of this pool (DIRE on 738), we report three key findings: (i) On modern AI content no detector exceeds AUC 0.624, and five of twelve fall below chance. (ii) Real-photo false-alarm rates are an artefact of threshold calibration: thresholds fitted on legacy GAN data make several detectors look deployable (less than 11 percent FPR), yet the same detectors flag 17-91 percent of real photos once the identical criterion is refit on modern content. (iii) Consequently, no detector both beats chance on modern AI content and keeps a deployable real-photo false-alarm rate. Mirroring the device mix of web collections, the corpus probes the first neural-ISP generation (2018-2020); current flagships are essentially absent, leaving the modern-ISP regime as the open gap.

</details>


### [25] [UniH$^3$: Unifying Hierarchical Homogeneity and Heterogeneity for All-in-One Medical Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11156)
*Zhiwen Yang,Jiayin Li,Chengyu Liu,Hui Zhang,Bingzheng Wei,Yan Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: UniH3 introduces a unified framework that leverages hierarchical homogeneity and heterogeneity for all-in-one medical image restoration, achieving state-of-the-art results on large benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing all-in-one MedIR methods emphasize inter-task heterogeneity but underexploit the inherent homogeneity in medical images (shared anatomical structures). Exploiting this homogeneity can ease training and improve generalization across modalities and degradation types.

Method: Propose Hierarchical Homogeneity Memory (H2M) to distill intra- and inter-task homogeneity priors from high-quality images during training and adaptively retrieve relevant priors for a given input; inject priors via Homogeneity-Guided Attention (HGA). Also introduce Hierarchical Heterogeneity Balancer (H2B) to mitigate inter- and intra-task conflicts during multi-task optimization.

Result: Extensive experiments on MedIR-2D-500K and MedIR-3D-3K show state-of-the-art performance on both all-in-one and single-task medical image restoration. Code is available at the provided repository.

Conclusion: UniH3 establishes a strong benchmark for general-purpose medical image restoration by effectively leveraging hierarchical homogeneity and heterogeneity, and it advances the development of unified models across medical imaging tasks.

Abstract: All-in-One medical image restoration (MedIR) aims to address diverse tasks across modalities and degradation types using a single universal model. Existing methods typically prioritize modeling inter-task heterogeneity (e.g., distinct data distributions and degradation types). However, they largely neglect the inherent homogeneity present in medical images, such as widely shared anatomical structures within and across modalities, which can be leveraged to ease model training and improve generalization. To this end, we propose UniH3, a novel framework that Unifies Hierarchical Homogeneity and Heterogeneity for all-in-one medical image restoration. Specifically, to comprehensively exploit homogeneity, we introduce a Hierarchical Homogeneity Memory (H2M) module that progressively distills intra- and inter-task homogeneity priors from high-quality images during training, and adaptively retrieves the most relevant priors tailored to the input for guided restoration. These retrieved priors are then injected into the restoration pipeline via an efficient Homogeneity-Guided Attention (HGA) mechanism. Furthermore, to comprehensively address heterogeneity, we design a Hierarchical Heterogeneity Balancer (H2B) that mitigates both inter- and intra-task conflicts during optimization, facilitating balanced and effective multi-task learning. Extensive experiments on two large-scale benchmarks, MedIR-2D-500K and MedIR-3D-3K, demonstrate that UniH3 achieves state-of-the-art performance on both all-in-one and single-task medical image restoration.
