Result: Construction yields universal point sets of size n^{1+o(1)} for planar graphs on n vertices; marks improvement over quadratic upper bounds.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a viable route via pattern-avoidance and interval families to tighten universal point-set bounds; highlights potential for further refinements and extensions; GPT-6 Astra assisted in the construction and proof.

Abstract: A point set is universal for planar graphs on $n$ vertices if every such graph has a straight-line drawing without crossings whose vertices belong to the set. We construct universal point sets of size $n^{1+o(1)}$, improving the previous quadratic upper bound. Our construction uses the reduction of Bannister, Cheng, Devanny, and Eppstein from universal point sets to superpatterns for $213$-avoiding permutations. We represent these permutations by ordered rooted forests and construct a small family of intervals containing every such forest. The result follows from a straightforward bound on the size of the family of intervals. GPT-6 Astra assisted in developing the construction and proof.

</details>


### [77] [Some results on Archdeacon's conjecture for rotation systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11599)
*Arahat Chikkatur,Ji Zeng*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: Proves and tightens Archdeacon's conjecture on non-planar 4-sets in rotation systems: verified for n ≤ 10, asymptotic lower bounds close to H(n), and a full proof for antipodally shellable systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a combinatorial strengthening of Hill's crossing number conjecture by lower-bounding non-planar 4-subsets in any rotation system; assesses the validity and limits of Archdeacon's conjecture.

Method: Computational verification for small n; computer-assisted counting and realizability checks; constructive drawings for extremal systems; hand combinatorial arguments for weaker bounds; extension of Felsner's antipodal-pair techniques to antipodally shellable systems.

Result: Archdeacon's conjecture verified for n ≤ 10; every extremal rotation system in these orders is realizable by a simple drawing; every rotation system on n elements has at least (8/9 - o(1)) H(n) non-planar 4-sets; hand proof gives (2/3 - o(1)) H(n); conjecture holds for antipodally shellable rotation systems.

Conclusion: Substantial progress toward Archdeacon's conjecture with both computational and theoretical results, including a complete resolution for the antipodally shellable subclass; the general case remains open, but asymptotic bounds are tight up to constants.

Abstract: A rotation system on $n$ elements assigns to each element a cyclic order of the other $n-1$ elements. A four-element subset is non-planar if its induced rotation system cannot be realized by a crossing-free drawing of $K_4$. As a combinatorial strengthening of Hill's conjecture on the crossing number of the complete graph, Archdeacon conjectured that every rotation system on $n$ elements has at least $H(n)=\frac{1}{4} \lfloor\frac {n}{2}\rfloor \lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\rfloor \lfloor\frac{n-2}{2}\rfloor \lfloor\frac{n-3}{2}\rfloor$ non-planar four-element subsets.
  We computationally verify Archdeacon's conjecture for $n\leq 10$ and show that every extremal rotation system in these orders is realizable by a simple drawing. With computer assistance, we prove that every rotation system on $n$ elements has at least $(8/9 - o(1)) H(n)$ non-planar four-element subsets. We also present a proof by hand for a weaker lower bound of $(2/3-o(1)) H(n)$. Finally, extending recent work of Felsner on antipodal pairs in drawings, we show that Archdeacon's conjecture holds for antipodally shellable rotation systems.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [78] [Probabilistic Focal Search: Accelerating Bounded-Suboptimal Search via Lower-Bound Advancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10584)
*Minh Vu Duc,Trung Le Huu,Hà Minh Hoàng,Trung Thanh Nguyen,Phuong Khanh Nguyen,Huynh Thi Thanh Binh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Probabilistic Focal Search (PFS) combining guided FS choice with occasional expansion of min-fOPEN; shows substantial reductions in node expansions and improved anytime performance, transferable to Dynamic Potential Search (PDPS); effective when f_min plateaus delay FOCAL admission; gains depend on domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address inefficiency in Focal Search where deterministic policy can stall advancement of f_min, slowing bounded-suboptimal search; desire to balance exploration (guided by heuristic) with lower-bound progression to unlock more candidate nodes.

Method: Replace FS's deterministic policy with a probabilistic scheduler: with probability p follow FS-guided choice, with probability 1-p expand a minimum-f OPEN node; apply same scheduler to Dynamic Potential Search (PDPS).

Result: Benchmarks on N-Puzzle, Pancake Sorting, and TSP show up to about 90% reductions in node expansions in worst-case plateaus; improved anytime extension on Generalized Covering TSP (GCTSP) with Anytime Probabilistic Focal Search (APFS) outperforming other anytime methods; improvements depend on domain; smaller gains when deterministic search already progresses efficiently (e.g., Pancake Sorting).

Conclusion: Probabilistic scheduling mitigates bottlenecks in FOCAL admission, yielding robust speedups for bounded-suboptimal and anytime search; transfers to potential-guidance (PDPS) with domain-dependent benefits.

Abstract: Bounded-suboptimal search seeks a solution within a factor $w$ of optimal while reducing search effort. Focal Search (FS) uses heuristic guidance within FOCAL, the frontier nodes eligible under the threshold $w f_{\min}$, but its deterministic policy may leave $f_{\min}$ unchanged for many expansions. We introduce Probabilistic Focal Search (PFS), which follows the FS guided choice with probability $p$ and expands a minimum-$f$ OPEN node with probability $1-p$. The latter branch encourages the lower bound to advance, enlarging FOCAL and admitting nodes that may lead to feasible solutions. By balancing guidance and lower-bound advancement, this mechanism can reduce time to a bounded solution when progress is limited by delayed FOCAL admission. As a secondary transfer experiment, we apply the same scheduler to Dynamic Potential Search, yielding Probabilistic Dynamic Potential Search (PDPS). We benchmark PFS against FS on N-Puzzle, Pancake Sorting, and the Traveling Salesperson Problem (TSP), and evaluate its anytime extension on the Generalized Covering TSP (GCTSP), using multiple $w$ and $p$ values. Across these benchmarks, the largest gains occur when long $f_{\min}$ plateaus delay useful FOCAL admissions; in such settings, the probabilistic factor may reduce node expansions by about 90\% or more (e.g., on N-Puzzle and TSP). For the anytime algorithm family, Anytime Probabilistic Focal Search (APFS) outperforms all tested algorithms in evaluating anytime methods on GCTSP. We also observe that the benefit is smaller when the deterministic search already advances efficiently (e.g., Pancake Sorting), indicating that the probabilistic factor is most useful when FOCAL admission is a search bottleneck. The PDPS transfer shows that the mechanism also transfers to potential guidance, although its common-success effects remain domain- and bound-dependent.

</details>


### [79] [Automating Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) Formulation Generation from Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10629)
*Niloy Kumar Mondal,Md Rizwan Parvez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An end-to-end multi-agent system automatically generates QUBO formulations from natural-language problem descriptions, evaluated on QUBOBench with 68% accuracy and 22% improvement over a baseline; iterative self-repair is key.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Translating natural-language descriptions into correct QUBO formulations is hard, requiring identification of variables, constraints, objective functions, penalty terms, and weights; a specialized end-to-end solution could reduce effort and domain expertise.

Method: An end-to-end multi-agent framework generates QUBO formulations from NL problem statements, using structured or unstructured test cases for evaluation; performance is benchmarked on QUBOBench (100 problems across 12 domains) and compared to a direct single-call baseline; ablation identifies iterative self-repair as important.

Result: The framework achieves 68% accuracy on QUBOBench and outperforms the baseline by 22%; iterative self-repair substantially boosts performance.

Conclusion: The proposed approach improves automatic QUBO generation from natural-language descriptions, with QUBOBench providing a diverse evaluation suite and open-source data/code facilitating further research.

Abstract: Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) is a central formulation for combinatorial optimization and has gained increasing attention due to its compatibility with quantum, hybrid quantum-classical, and quantum-inspired solvers. However, translating natural-language problem descriptions into correct QUBO formulations remains difficult, requiring the identification of binary variables, constraints, objective functions, penalty terms, and suitable penalty weights. This process is time-consuming and often demands substantial domain expertise. To address this challenge, we propose an end-to-end multi-agent framework that automatically generates QUBO formulations from natural-language problem descriptions, supported by structured or unstructured test cases. To evaluate its performance, We also introduce QUBOBench, a benchmark containing 100 combinatorial optimization problems across 12 application domains, curated from peer-reviewed literature, competitions, and canonical NP-hard problems. Experimental results show that our framework achieves 68% accuracy on QUBOBench, outperforming a direct single-call baseline by 22%. Further analysis identifies iterative self-repair as the most important component contributing to improved performance. The data and code are open-sourced at https://quitttcat.github.io/QuantumQUBOAgent.

</details>


### [80] [A Multi-Stage Rule-Chaining Framework for Compositional and Interpretable Cognitive Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10654)
*Deblina Kar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-stage rule-chaining framework for ARC-like cognitive generalization using three solvers (deterministic rule discovery, pattern composition, structural abstraction) with progressive fallback; achieves high accuracy (>95%) and interpretable reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address cognitive generalization and interpretability in ARC by integrating symbolic and pattern-based reasoning; aims to generalize across unseen rules without task-specific tuning.

Method: Three sequential solvers: (1) deterministic rule discovery for atomic transformations via geometry/color/object-based cues; (2) pattern-composition to reconstruct outputs via block merging, repetition, spatial heuristics; (3) structural abstraction to infer hierarchical relationships across grids; operate in progressive fallback hierarchy reusing reasoning traces.

Result: Training on 995/1000 tasks; evaluated on 105/120; solved 230/240 ARC-AGI-2 tasks; overall accuracy >95%; strong coverage across deterministic, compositional, abstract categories; demonstrates bridging symbolic reasoning and pattern synthesis with interpretable insights.

Conclusion: Rule chaining and hierarchical composition can advance machine reasoning toward transparent, human-aligned abstraction; reduces reliance on task-specific tuning; provides interpretable, generalizable cognitive reasoning framework.

Abstract: The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC) benchmarks cognitive generalization, the ability to infer and apply abstract rules from limited examples. This paper presents a multi-stage rule-chaining framework that performs compositional reasoning across symbolic, structural, and conceptual levels. The framework integrates three complementary solvers:
  (1) a deterministic rule discovery module that induces atomic transformations through geometric, color, and object-based analysis;
  (2) a pattern-composition engine that reconstructs outputs via block merging, repetition, and spatial heuristics; and
  (3) a structural abstraction layer that infers hierarchical and nested relationships across grids.
  These solvers operate sequentially within a progressive fallback hierarchy, where each stage reuses prior reasoning traces to enhance interpretability and generalization. Training passed for 995 tasks out of 1000, further evaluated on 105 tasks out of 120 and solved 230 test tasks out of 240 ARC-AGI-2 tasks. The system achieved strong coverage across deterministic, compositional, and abstract categories, demonstrating an overall accuracy exceeding 95 percent. The proposed architecture bridges symbolic reasoning and pattern synthesis, providing interpretable insight into cognitive generalization. The results suggest that rule chaining and hierarchical composition can advance machine reasoning toward transparent, human-aligned abstraction without relying on task-specific tuning.

</details>


### [81] [Understanding LoRA Rank Trade-offs in Diffusion Model Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10656)
*Iman Khazrak,Narges Nejad,Mostafa M. Rezaee,Robert C. Green*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Moderate LoRA ranks (around 4) offer best trade-off between diffusion model quality and computational cost on CIFAR-10, with diminishing gains at higher ranks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Determine how LoRA rank affects diffusion model fine-tuning efficiency and quality under fixed budgets.

Method: Controlled study on CIFAR-10 using a DDPM U-Net with ranks {2,4,8,16,32}, fixed optimization settings, and a reproducible local-folder pytorch-fid protocol; reported FID, trainable parameters, runtime, and GPU memory; validated trends with extended-budget DDPM runs (20 epochs; ranks 4/8/16) and a Tiny DiT backbone (10 epochs; ranks 4/8/16).

Result: Moderate ranks are most efficient: rank 4 achieves the best DDPM FID (124.1380), rank 8 is close (124.2136); higher ranks provide limited gains despite larger adaptation cost.

Conclusion: Small-to-moderate ranks are practical defaults under fixed training budgets.

Abstract: Selecting LoRA rank for diffusion fine-tuning requires balancing quality and compute cost. We present a controlled study on CIFAR-10 using a DDPM U-Net with ranks {2,4,8,16,32}, fixed optimization settings, and a reproducible local-folder pytorch-fid protocol. We report FID, trainable parameters, runtime, and GPU memory, then validate trends with extended-budget DDPM runs (20 epochs; ranks 4/8/16) and a Tiny DiT backbone (10 epochs; ranks 4/8/16). Results show moderate ranks are most efficient: rank 4 achieves the best DDPM FID (124.1380), rank 8 is close (124.2136), and higher ranks provide limited gains despite larger adaptation cost. These findings support small-to-moderate ranks as practical defaults under fixed training budgets.

</details>


### [82] [Quantifying the Memorization-to-Generalization Transition: Scaling Laws and Phase Structure in Grokking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10657)
*Anish Kataria*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Grokking onset scales as a power law with data complexity being the dominant factor; provides a quantitative boundary across 384 MLP configurations and reveals a data-driven phase diagram including a weight-decay threshold and monotonic weight-norm compression during grokking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify when grokking occurs in hyperparameter space, bridging theory of 'why' grokking happens with a predictive, quantitative framework for 'when' across hyperparameters.

Method: Systematic sweep of 384 configurations of two-hidden-layer MLPs trained on modular arithmetic; fit a power-law model T_grok ∝ H^{-α} D^{-β} η^{-γ} λ^{-δ}; analyze exponents, interactions; track weight-norm trajectories.

Result: Found exponents α≈0.27, β≈2.04, γ≈0.50, δ≈0.64 (R^2≈0.732; improves to 0.821 with interaction terms). Data complexity dominates the transition; doubling data ≈4× acceleration; width scaling yields ~1.2×; a phase boundary at λ ≳ 1; weight-norm contracts monotonically during transition.

Conclusion: Provides a quantitative foundation to predict and control grokking by identifying dominant role of data and a clear boundary in weight-decay; suggests implicit regularization toward low-complexity solutions; enables design of hyperparameters to control generalization onset.

Abstract: Neural networks trained past memorization frequently undergo a delayed transition to generalization, a phenomenon known as grokking. Despite theoretical progress on \emph{why} this transition occurs, the quantitative structure of \emph{when} it occurs in hyperparameter space remains uncharacterized. We map the memorization-to-generalization boundary across 384 configurations of two-hidden-layer MLPs on modular arithmetic, fitting a power-law scaling relation for generalization onset time: $T_{\mathrm{grok}} \propto H^{-0.27}\, D^{-2.04}\, η^{-0.50}\, λ^{-0.64}$ ($R^2 = 0.732$; $0.821$ with interactions). The exponent hierarchy reveals that data complexity ($D^{-2.04}$) is the dominant driver of regime transition, not model capacity ($H^{-0.27}$): doubling data accelerates generalization by ${\sim}4\times$, while doubling width yields only ${\sim}1.2\times$. A sharp phase boundary at weight decay $λ\gtrsim 1.0$ separates grokking from non-grokking configurations, and weight norm trajectories show monotonic compression during the transition, consistent with implicit regularization selecting low-complexity solutions. These results provide a quantitative foundation for predicting and controlling regime transitions in overparameterized networks.

</details>


### [83] [An Open Recipe for IMO Gold: Training Nemotron for Olympiad Mathematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10712)
*Ivan Moshkov,Stephen Ge,George Armstrong,Wei Du,Sadegh Mahdavi,Igor Gitman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Investigates how post-training and test-time inference design affect natural-language proof generation for hard olympiad mathematics, using Nemotron 3 Ultra with two post-trained specialist checkpoints; proposes an open NL-only test-time pipeline; achieves 30/42 on IMO 2026 and releases resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable robust, reproducible NL-based proof generation for difficult math problems without formal provers or external tools, and to understand how training-time specialization and inference-time strategies impact proof quality and correctness.

Method: Starting from Nemotron 3 Ultra, train two specialist checkpoints via supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. Evaluate checkpoint choice, verification, and refinement. Build an open-model test-time-compute pipeline operating entirely in natural language (no formal prover or external tools). Use three Nemotron 3 Ultra checkpoints (general model and two specialists) to iteratively generate, verify, and refine candidate proofs; a separate high-compute stage selects final submissions. Release included resources.

Result: Achieved 30 out of 42 points at IMO 2026, surpassing the gold-medal threshold. Release includes the two post-trained checkpoints, training data, training/inference code, submitted solutions, and Nemotron-IMO-Bench with 200 novel olympiad-level problems.

Conclusion: NL-only post-training and inference designs can reach competitive performance on hard olympiad problems; open resources enable reproducibility and benchmarking. While effective in this setup, the approach depends on internal verification and is susceptible to NL-model limitations; future work could explore formal provers or external verification aids for enhanced reliability.

Abstract: We study how model post-training and test-time inference design affect natural-language proof generation for hard olympiad mathematics. Starting from Nemotron 3 Ultra, we train two specialist checkpoints using supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, and evaluate checkpoint choice, verification, and refinement. Based on these findings, we present an open-model test-time-compute pipeline. The system operates entirely in natural language, with no formal prover, external tools, or internet access. Three Nemotron 3 Ultra checkpoints - the general-availability model and two post-trained specialists - power an iterative search that generates, verifies, and refines candidate proofs; a separate high-compute stage then selects each final submission. The system scored 30 out of 42 points at IMO 2026, reaching the gold-medal threshold. We release the two post-trained checkpoints as well as the training data, the training and inference code, the submitted solutions, and Nemotron-IMO-Bench, a new benchmark of 200 novel olympiad-level problems.

</details>


### [84] [Finishing the Task Is Not Enough: Evaluating Agent Resilience and Considerate Participation under Accumulating Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10724)
*Yuanchen Bai,Zijian Ding,Angelique Taylor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Long-term deployment of generative AI agents requires resilience and considerate participation beyond single-task success; study of 120 simulated healthcare trajectories shows evolving agent behavior under accumulating challenge, revealing deployment dilemmas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for sustaining agent usefulness in real-world, multi-stakeholder workflows where disruptions accumulate over time and agent collaboration with humans is pivotal.

Method: Simulation study across 120 trajectories using two generative AI models and twelve stakeholder-derived tasks under light/medium/heavy challenge; compares textual action plans, prompted internal assessments, and quantitative workload/affect reports to track behavior and state over time.

Result: Agents increasingly rely on human support, report higher workload and negative affect in structured measures, but rarely express strain in textual responses; in terms of participation, agents broaden from task-focused adaptation to reframing, consideration of others, role-boundary adjustment, and wider coordination with varying patterns by action and internal assessment; five deployment dilemmas emerge related to persistence, attention, role boundaries, state disclosure, and escalation.

Conclusion: Findings inform deployment design and evaluation, emphasizing learning, situated evaluation, and embodied adaptation; propose five dilemmas requiring stakeholder specification and guiding technical implications for resilient, responsible AI in complex workflows.

Abstract: Sustained deployment of generative AI agents requires more than isolated task success. Agents must remain useful across repeated interactions, changing conditions, and dependencies on people within shared workflows, especially as technical, human, and operational disruptions accumulate over time. We propose operational resilience and considerate participation as two complementary aspects of evaluating such agents: the former captures how agents recover from blocked work while preserving progress and communicating their limits, and the latter captures how their adaptation accounts for affected people, role boundaries, and the surrounding workflow. Yet both remain underexplored under accumulating challenge. We study 120 simulated healthcare trajectories across two generative AI models and twelve stakeholder-derived tasks under light, medium, and heavy challenge. We compare textual action plans, prompted internal assessments, and quantitative structured workload and affect reports to examine how agent behavior and reported state change as challenge accumulates. Regarding operational resilience, agents shift from self-directed recovery toward greater human dependence, while reporting increasing workload and negative affect in structured reports but seldom expressing strain in textual responses. Regarding considerate participation, agents broaden from task-focused adaptation toward task reframing, attention to others, role-boundary adjustment, and wider coordination, with distinct patterns across actions and internal assessments. From these findings, we derive five deployment dilemmas involving persistence, attention, role boundaries, state disclosure, and escalation that require stakeholder specification, further informing technical implications for learning, situated evaluation, and embodied adaptation.

</details>


### [85] [Towards a Deterministic Math Solver for Clinical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10728)
*Felipe Ocampo Osorio,Sebastián Andrés Cajas Ordoñez,Maximin Lange,Rafi Al Attrach,Sahil Kapadia,Zakaria Laouabdia Sellami,Angelo Antonio Talio,Leo Anthony Celi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A program-solver interface uses a restricted Python executor to deterministically compute clinical-calculator results; shows mixed gains: improves large models but not small ones; not a substitute for vetted formulas or reliable variable extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate LLM arithmetic unreliability in clinical calculators by decoupling computation from reasoning and using a deterministic solver to ensure numerical correctness.

Method: Evaluation on MedCalc-Bench Verified (1,100 cases, 55 calculators), comparing a restricted Python-based solver (Program-Solve) against direct model arithmetic and a hand-written 22-calculator library. Models evaluated: Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen2.5-32B-AWQ. Benchmark formulas audited for version/coeff concerns. Both routes have access to full note content and must pass results to solver; performance measured as accuracy with 95% calculator-cluster interval.

Result: 7B: 75.31% accuracy with Program-Solve vs 72.02% direct arithmetic; difference +3.29 points; 95% CI [-3.49, 10.38] (not significant). 32B-AWQ: 90.53% vs 83.47%; difference +7.05 points; 95% CI [0.47, 14.60] (significant). Hand-written library exact on 440 supported cases but abstains elsewhere (40.0% overall). Conclusion: Executor helps some open-weight models more than others; not a universal substitute for verified formulas or reliable variable extraction; larger models benefit more clearly under this setup.

Conclusion: The Program-Solve approach benefits larger LLMs but does not replace the need for verified, formula-based calculators or robust variable extraction. Adoption should be model-size aware and complemented with vetted formulas and accurate variable parsing.

Abstract: Large language models are unreliable at arithmetic, which is a problem for clinical calculators where a single numerical error changes the recommendation. The standard response is to hardcode each calculator as a validated function, one at a time. We test an alternative: the model does not calculate. Instead, it writes case-specific Python that a restricted local executor runs as a deterministic solver, and the model's task reduces to deciding how to use it. We evaluate this Program-Solve interface on MedCalc-Bench Verified (1,100 cases, 55 calculators) against direct model arithmetic and a hand-written 22-calculator library, using Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen2.5-32B-AWQ, after auditing the benchmark's formulas against current clinical guidelines and flagging 16 of 55 with version, use or coefficient concerns. With formulas and gold variables supplied and both routes reading the whole note, handing off to the solver is not a reliable advantage at 7B (75.31% against 72.02%, a paired +3.29 points with a 95% calculator-cluster interval of [-3.49, 10.38]) but is one at 32B (90.53% against 83.47%, +7.05 [0.47, 14.60], clear of zero). The hand-written library is exact on its 440 supported cases but abstains elsewhere (40.0% overall). Adding an executor thus helps some open-weight models more than others even under matched formula, variable and note access, and is not a substitute for verified formulas or reliable variable extraction either way.

</details>


### [86] [Studying Without a Syllabus: Task-Agnostic Environment Preprocessing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10824)
*Vinay Samuel,Varun Ursekar,Vijay S. Kalmath,Apaar Shanker,Veronica Chatrath,Yuan Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces task-agnostic environment preprocessing where an agent studies an unfamiliar environment under a budget to produce artifacts for a frozen solver. It compares unaided and archive-equipped meta-agents with fixed corpus-processing across six benchmarks. Findings: a meta-agent variant often yields the best Avg@3, fixed corpus processing dominates on the largest corpus, larger study budgets do not reliably improve downstream reward, and artifacts reduce test-time sampling, shifting effort to pre-task study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the limitation of task-agnostic adaptation methods that either rely on task-specific supervision or commit to a fixed preparation strategy. The paper aims to enable open-ended environment study without syllabus or downstream task distribution knowledge, to automatically produce reusable artifacts for a frozen solver.

Method: Formalize task-agnostic environment preprocessing. Define a studying system that explores an environment under a finite budget and outputs artifacts (indices, scripts, procedural guidance) for a frozen solver. Compare two meta-agent setups (unaided and archive-equipped) against fixed synthetic-practice and corpus-processing baselines across six heterogeneous benchmarks, measuring downstream Avg@3 reward.

Result: A meta-agent variant achieves the highest Avg@3 reward on five benchmarks; fixed corpus processing is best on the largest corpus benchmark. Increasing study budgets does not reliably improve downstream reward. Studied artifacts reduce the test-time sampling required to reach a target score, showing that pre-task study can shift computation from repeated test-time attempts to a pre-task phase.

Conclusion: Pre-task environment study with artifact generation can improve test-time efficiency by reusing preparation, but gains are context-dependent (budget effectiveness varies with corpus size). The meta-agent approach often excels, yet simple corpus processing can outperform on large corpora, highlighting trade-offs between exploration, artifact quality, and downstream solver needs.

Abstract: Before an LLM agent tackles tasks in a new environment, it can inspect available corpora and tools and construct reusable resources such as indices, scripts, or procedural guidance. Most automated adaptation methods, however, rely on task examples, trajectories, or evaluation feedback to decide what to build. Existing task-agnostic approaches avoid this supervision but commit in advance to a preparation strategy for a particular type of environment. We study a more open-ended setting: can an agent study an unfamiliar environment without a syllabus, i.e. before test time and without knowledge of the downstream task distribution, and choose how to prepare it? We formalize task-agnostic environment preprocessing, in which a studying system explores an environment under a budget and produces artifacts for a frozen solver. We compare unaided and archive-equipped meta-agents with fixed synthetic-practice and corpus-processing methods across six heterogeneous benchmarks. A meta-agent variant achieves the highest Avg@3 reward on five benchmarks, while fixed corpus processing remains best on the largest corpus benchmark. Larger study budgets do not reliably improve downstream reward. Nevertheless, studied artifacts reduce the test-time sampling needed to reach a given score, demonstrating how reusable preparation can shift computation from repeated test-time attempts to a pre-task study phase.

</details>


### [87] [When Validation Stops Learning: Auditing Update Admission for Continual Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10873)
*Qinzhen Ma,Ruihai Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An admission-audit framework for evaluating update policies that balances error control with retained learning opportunities under a fixed interaction budget; shows range-based confidence gates can miss unchanged old-task behavior; proposes a paired-binomial alternative, historical-reference promotion, and a round-level missed-opportunity metric; reports empirical results (31.6% admissions with 32 seeds at 2000 episodes/stage vs 0% for the gate) and a stress test distinguishing bias from feedback error; calls for further validation on physical robots and VLA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Independent evaluation of policy updates is needed to reject harmful updates while preserving useful continual learning, requiring assessment of error control and retained learning under a stated interaction budget.

Method: Proposes an admission-audit protocol comprising analytical and synthetic evidence. Introduces: (1) a range-based confidence gate, (2) a paired-binomial construction to reduce burden when outcome disagreements are rare, (3) certified historical-reference promotion, (4) a round-level missed-opportunity metric. Evaluates with a one-step pushing diagnostic using 32 seeds, comparing fresh paired checks against the range gate at 2,000 episodes per stage, and includes a separate learned-dynamics stress test.

Result: Finds that the range-based gate cannot certify unchanged old-task behavior within substantial budgets; the paired-binomial approach reduces burden and allows admission in many cases. In the diagnostic, 31.6% of the update stream is admitted with 32 seeds, at 2,000 episodes per stage, while the range gate yields zero admissions. Unconditional replay demonstrates better learning in closed-loop settings. The learned-dynamics stress test differentiates model bias from feedback-selection error.

Conclusion: Proposes an admission-audit protocol with analytical and synthetic validation; notes that validation with physical robots and VLA is still pending.

Abstract: Independent evaluation can reject harmful policy updates yet also prevent useful continual learning. We argue that update admission must be assessed through both error control and retained learning opportunities at a stated interaction budget. We identify a concrete failure: a range-based confidence gate cannot certify unchanged old-task behavior within otherwise substantial budgets. A standard paired-binomial construction reduces this burden when outcome disagreements are rare. We also specify certified historical-reference promotion and a round-level missed-opportunity metric. In a constructed one-step pushing diagnostic with 32 seeds, fresh paired checks admit 31.6% of a common update stream at 2,000 episodes per stage, versus zero for the range-based gate; unconditional replay nevertheless learns better in closed-loop runs. A separate learned-dynamics stress test distinguishes model bias from feedback-selection error. The contribution is an admission-audit protocol with analytical and synthetic evidence; physical-robot and VLA validation remain open.

</details>


### [88] [Decoupling Readiness from Release for Tail-Aware Scheduling of Agentic LLM Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10964)
*Bochao Feng,Jianjiang Li,Haojie Wang,Lin Qiao,Yinghui Li,Yukun Yan,Jidong Zhai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Tail-risk-aware turn-release scheduling for agentic LLM workflows that jointly selects ready turns and how much unfinished work to retain, using a mean-CVaR objective to curb tail latency under contention; achieves up to 3.5x P95 speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Under contention, eager release of ready turns can leave released but unfinished work that cannot be reordered, inflating tail workflow times. A scheduler that controls both which turn to release and how much in-flight work to retain aims to reduce tail risk without hurting light-load performance.

Method: A scheduling method that jointly decides the next ready turn to release and the amount of released but unfinished work to maintain. It uses a mean-CVaR objective to capture tail risk of unfinished workflows, incorporates online estimates of per-turn work to prioritize ready turns, and adapts the released-work budget based on observed queue pressure. Evaluation uses real agent execution traces across multiple LLMs and workflow arrival rates.

Result: Under light load, performance is comparable to eager release. Under contention, the method substantially reduces P95 of workflow flow time, achieving up to a 3.50× speedup.

Conclusion: Tail-risk-aware release scheduling mitigates tail latency in agentic LLM workflows, preserving light-load performance and delivering robust tail-time reductions across diverse models and workloads.

Abstract: Agentic LLM workflows consist of sequences of model turns interleaved with tool interactions, so their end-to-end completion time depends not only on inference speed but also on when ready turns are released. Most runtimes release each turn immediately upon readiness. Under contention, this eager release policy can accumulate released but unfinished work; once submitted, those turns can no longer be reordered by the workflow-level policy, increasing tail latency. We present a tail-risk-aware turn release scheduling method that jointly decides which ready turn to release next and how much released but unfinished work to maintain. The method uses a mean--Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR) objective to capture the evolving tail risk of unfinished workflows, incorporates online estimates of turn work when prioritizing ready turns, and adapts the released work budget to observed queue pressure. We evaluate the method using real agent execution traces from software engineering tasks across multiple LLMs and workflow arrival rates. The method performs comparably to eager release under light load and substantially reduces the P95 of workflow flow time under contention, achieving up to a \(3.50\times\) speedup.

</details>


### [89] [Demystifying the Privacy-Utility Trade-off in LLM Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10992)
*Zhenhua Liu,Zhanxu Xie,Junjie Yu,Tong Zhu,Lijun Li,Wenliang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes an intent-driven local protection framework to balance privacy and utility in LLM-assisted tasks; identifies three mechanisms guiding sanitization and demonstrates Veilmind-4B-based pipeline achieving lower leakage and higher utility, advancing toward Pareto frontier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current privacy methods rely on context-agnostic static rules, which degrade utility. There is a need to systematically understand how sanitization affects downstream performance.

Method: Systematic analysis revealing three mechanisms (Context-Dependent Utility, Strategic Adaptation, Combinatorial Interplay). Development of an intent-driven local protection pipeline using a lightweight Veilmind-4B to perform extraction-sanitization-restoration. Evaluation against privacy baselines to assess privacy-utility trade-off.

Result: Demonstrates low-leakage privacy with substantially higher response utility than existing privacy-oriented baselines and a Pareto-frontier shift.

Conclusion: Intent-driven local protection can effectively manage the privacy-utility trade-off by exploiting data-context, task requirements, and attribute interdependencies, enabling more nuanced sanitization.

Abstract: The integration of Large Language Models into daily tasks relies on context-rich instructions, inevitably exposing sensitive user information. Current privacy-preserving methods typically employ context-agnostic static rules, causing severe utility degradation. However, the specific mechanisms governing how sanitization impacts downstream performance remain largely underexplored. To address this, we conduct a systematic analysis to deconstruct the privacy-utility trade-off, uncovering three underlying mechanisms: (1) Context-Dependent Utility, which first establishes when to sanitize by revealing that data value shifts from critical constraints to dispensable noise based on user intent; (2) Strategic Adaptation, which subsequently determines how to sanitize by dictating that the choice between removal and replacement depends on the task's reliance on factual integrity versus structural coherence; and (3) Combinatorial Interplay, which finally extends the protection scope by demonstrating that attributes form a semantic web of synergistic dependencies or antagonistic redundancies. Guided by these insights, we introduce an intent-driven local protection framework. By distilling a lightweight model Veilmind-4B to drive a dynamic extraction-sanitization-restoration pipeline, our approach reaches a low-leakage privacy point while preserving substantially higher response utility than existing privacy-oriented baselines, advancing the privacy-utility trade-off toward the Pareto frontier.

</details>


### [90] [Defining AI Agents: A Compendium of Criteria, Metrics, and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11018)
*Mia Lassiter,Brinnae Bent*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A five-dimension framework for evaluating AI agents is proposed, with a synthesis of metrics/benchmarks and a public Agent Compendium to standardize evaluation and improve reproducibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ambiguity in defining what constitutes an 'agent' in AI hampers evaluation, comparison, and reproducibility of agent research; a structured, multi-dimensional framework is needed.

Method: Conduct a comprehensive literature survey across five dimensions of agenticness (environmental interaction, learning/adaptation, autonomy, goal-directed behavior, temporal coherence), synthesize existing metrics/benchmarks/evaluation frameworks, and introduce the Agent Compendium as a public resource to organize and extend these methods.

Result: A structured landscape of agent evaluation methods with identified gaps and inconsistencies; a public resource (Agent Compendium) to organize evaluation approaches for reproducibility and comparability.

Conclusion: The framework and compendium aim to enable clearer communication, more reproducible research, and systematic study of AI agents, while inviting ongoing expansion and refinement of evaluation methods.

Abstract: The term agent in artificial intelligence lacks a standard definition, complicating the evaluation, comparison, and reproducibility of AI agent research. We address this ambiguity through a survey organized around five dimensions of agenticness: environmental interaction, learning and adaptation, autonomy, goal-directed behavior, and temporal coherence. For each dimension, we examine how the underlying capability has been conceptualized across prior work and synthesize the metrics, benchmarks, and evaluation frameworks used to assess it. This review provides a structured account of the current landscape of agent evaluation, highlighting both established approaches and areas where evaluation remains limited or inconsistent. We additionally introduce the Agent Compendium, a public-facing digital resource that organizes and extends the evaluation methods identified through this review. Together, the survey and compendium provide a common structure for evaluating and comparing agent capabilities across AI systems, supporting more reproducible research, clearer communication, and more systematic study of artificial agents.

</details>


### [91] [The Agent Incident Registry: Toward Preventing Repeated AI Agent Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11030)
*Divyanshu Kumar,Rohith HN,Nitin Aravind Birur,Sahil Agarwal,Prashanth Harshangi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AIR is a source-linked registry of agent-related incidents used to compare public failures with agent-security evaluations. It provides records with supporting evidence, stable identifiers, and missingness-aware labels for causal role, disclosure class, mechanism, and outcome. It includes multi-stage curation and deployment-analogue auditing, but is not designed for failure-rate estimation; its primary value is source-grounded retrieval and evaluation-scope auditing across twelve surfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public AI-agent incidents are dispersed and lack a unified mechanism to connect failures with agent-security assessments. A structured, source-grounded registry enables reproducible auditing, provenance tracking, and mechanism-aware analysis.

Method: Construct AIR as a catalog containing N agent-related incident records disclosed from Yfirst to Ylast. Each record carries supporting evidence, a stable identifier, and missingness-aware labels for causal role, disclosure class, mechanism, and outcome. After initial curation, a second reviewer checks all N records for completeness and correctness. A deployment-analogue audit uses InjecAgentCases to evaluate AIR surfaces; analyze surface distribution and the composition of the dataset.

Result: Among the Nprimary generative-system records where the agent acted, Rprimary correspond to realized harm (Pprimary%). Realized outcomes concentrate in in-the-wild and safety-failure records; responsible disclosures and research demonstrations are overwhelmingly demonstrated, indicating that aggregate shares reflect collection composition rather than deployment risk. InjecAgentCases occupy three of AIR's twelve surfaces and are attacker-triggered; AIR contains Nsafety no-adversary safety failures. AIR supports source-grounded case retrieval and evaluation-scope auditing, but not failure-rate or control-efficacy estimation.

Conclusion: AIR provides a structured framework for auditing and retrieving agent-incident cases with provenance and labeled attributes. It supports evaluation-scope auditing and source-grounded retrieval, but warns against using AIR for estimating real-world failure rates or the effectiveness of safeguards; future work could extend metrics toward risk estimation and coverage of more surfaces.

Abstract: AI agents increasingly act through tools and delegated authority, but general incident repositories rarely capture the mechanisms needed to compare public failures with agent-security evaluations. We present the Agent Incident Registry (AIR), a source-linked catalog containing \N{} records of agent-related events disclosed from \Yfirst{} through \Ylast{}. Each record includes supporting evidence, a stable identifier, and missingness-aware labels for causal role, disclosure class, mechanism, and outcome. Among the \Nprimary{} generative-system records in which the agent acted, \Rprimary{} involved realized harm (\Pprimary\%). Realized outcomes concentrate in in-the-wild and safety-failure records, while responsible disclosures and research demonstrations are overwhelmingly demonstrated; the aggregate share therefore characterizes collection composition rather than deployment risk. After initial curation, a second human reviewer checked all \N{} records and their existing labels for completeness and correctness. In a deployment-analogue audit, InjecAgent's \NInjecAgentCases{} cases occupy three of AIR's twelve surfaces and are all attacker-triggered, whereas AIR contains \Nsafety{} no-adversary safety failures. AIR supports source-grounded case retrieval and evaluation-scope auditing, not failure-rate or control-efficacy estimation.

</details>


### [92] [Grounding Agent Memory: Environment-Probing Curation for Enterprise Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11060)
*Susheel Suresh,Hazel Mak,Sahil Bhatnagar,Chhaya Methani,Alejandro Gutierrez Munoz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Environment-probing curation enhances persistent-memory agents by adding least-privilege, read-only world tools to audit and refresh memories, yielding substantial gains in task success and efficiency without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persistent memories can cause error accumulation, overgeneralization, or stale knowledge when curated post-task. A deployment-friendly, auditable mechanism is needed to sanity-check memories without altering task agents or retrievers.

Method: Extend an asynchronous curator with environment-probing tools granting read-only world access; evaluate within a GHCP-like harness on CLBench and 90 APEX tasks, comparing stateless, in-context learning, GHCP+Mem, and GHCP+Mem with Env Probing; measure pass rate, pass-discounted reward, queries per question, and cost; assess across multiple worlds and tasks.

Result: Environment probing boosts performance and efficiency: CLBench pass rate 39%→73%, pass-discounted reward 8.60→22.60, queries 8.8→4.7, task-agent cost $3.38→$1.68; across six APEX worlds, all 18 memory-vs-baseline comparisons favorable; tool calls reduced 16–75%; five worlds show best reward-per-dollar; outperforms GHCP+Mem on Sonnet 4.6 and Opus 4.7 without schema drift.

Conclusion: Environment-informed, auditable memory curation preserves a compact task-time interface while providing measurable gains in success and efficiency; no model retraining required; supports production deployment of persistent-memory agents.

Abstract: Persistent memory is entering production-oriented agent platforms to help long-horizon agents accumulate experience across sessions. Yet a post-task curator agent restricted to completed trajectories can preserve errors, overgeneralize partial evidence, or retain stale knowledge. We introduce environment-probing curation, a deployment-compatible extension that gives an existing asynchronous curator agent least-privilege, read-only world tools to check, scope, and refresh candidate memories. It requires no model retraining and leaves the task agent, retriever, memory representation, and production write authority unchanged. In a production-like GitHub Copilot (GHCP) harness built on its SDK, we compare stateless execution, full in-context learning, GHCP + Mem, and GHCP + Mem (w/ Env Probing) on CLBench database exploration and 90 adapted APEX management-consulting tasks. On CLBench, probing raises pass rate from 39% to 73% and pass-discounted reward from 8.60 to 22.60 while reducing queries from 8.8 to 4.7 per question and task-agent cost from \$3.38 to \$1.68. Across six APEX worlds, all 18 memory-versus-baseline mean reward comparisons are positive and task-agent tool calls fall by 16--75%; probing gives the best task-agent reward gain per dollar in five worlds. Probing also attains higher mean reward than GHCP + Mem on both Sonnet 4.6 and Opus 4.7 without schema drift. Environment probing therefore turns existing agent-memory curation into an environment-informed, auditable process while preserving a compact task-time interface.

</details>


### [93] [Fork Where the Model Changes Its Mind: Belief-Shift Branching for Tree-Structured Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11061)
*Bin Lei,Yu Li,Prafulla Kumar Choubey,Jiaxin Zhang,Becky Xiangyu Peng,Qinyuan Ye,Kartik Narayan,Caiwen Ding,Silvio Savarese,Chien-Sheng Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Belief-shift branching strategically places forks in tree-structured rollouts to maximize step-level credit signals with minimal overhead, improving RLVR performance across mathematics and coding tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve step-level credit assignment in RL with verifiable rewards under limited sampling budgets. Current fork strategies rely on structural heuristics or entropy; an approach that identifies pivots in the value curve could yield richer signals with lower cost.

Method: Propose belief-shift branching to fork just before where consecutive model beliefs diverge most, at candidate boundaries. Three instantiations—black-box probe, logit-lens depth profile, and learned activation direction—read beliefs without requiring step-level supervision. Some instantiations are offline-fit and used only for validation. Cost of the probe is ~1% of step compute on math and <5% on code within the rollout engine. Evaluation includes Monte-Carlo value-curve validation and RL tests across three model families and two domains.

Result: Across eight model×benchmark panels, belief-shift ranks first over entropy, structural, and LLM-judge baselines. In mathematics, on OLMo-3-7B: +2.6 aggregate and +2.9 on AIME 2026 over the strongest baseline; in code, +6.5 on LiveCodeBench-medium across all OLMo code columns.

Conclusion: Placing forks via belief-shift yields strong, consistent gains with modest computational overhead, suggesting a practical, supervision-free method to extract step-level credit in RLVR across diverse tasks and models.

Abstract: Tree-structured rollouts give critic-free reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) step-level credit: fork a chain at an intermediate point, and sibling outcome differences estimate step value. Each fork adds sampling cost, so realistic budgets typically allow only a few forks per chain. A fork placed where the outcome is already largely settled yields siblings that mostly agree and provide almost no credit signal; hence, for a given tree size, where forks are placed largely determines how much step-level RL can gain. Most existing mainstream methods place forks by structure, such as fixed lengths, midpoints, and delimiters, or by next-token entropy. We formalize fork placement as locating the \emph{pivots} of the chain's value curve, where the expected outcome turns. We propose \emph{belief-shift branching}: read the model's answer belief at candidate boundaries and fork just before the step where consecutive beliefs diverge most. Three instantiations, none needing step-level supervision, span access levels: a black-box probe, a logit-lens depth profile, and a learned activation direction, which is fit offline and therefore used only in the validation before RL training. The signal only \emph{places} forks, and the probe costs about $1\%$ of step compute on mathematics and under $5\%$ on code when it runs inside the rollout engine. In that validation, against Monte-Carlo value curves, a belief-shift signal ranks first in each of the eight model$\times$benchmark panels, ahead of entropy, structural, and LLM-judge baselines. In RL across three model families and two domains, belief-shift forking leads every mathematics aggregate, on OLMo-3-7B by $+2.6$ aggregate and $+2.9$ on AIME 2026 over the strongest baseline, and sweeps every OLMo code column, by $+6.5$ on LiveCodeBench-medium.

</details>


### [94] [MOSAIC: Query-Aware Exploration Policy Adaptation for GraphRAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11065)
*EunKyeong Lee,Kyeong-Jin Oh,Jinwon Kim,Hye Woo Lee,Minsang Song,Hyeongjun Jang,Junyoung Youn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Mosaic enables per-query control in GraphRAG via a training-free policy derived from an LLM analyzer, improving retrieval quality across Medical and Novel benchmarks with strong transferability and efficiency gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Shared exploration procedures in GraphRAG create a mismatch with distinct evidence needs (local, multi-target, or deep connectors). A per-query control policy can tailor retrieval according to evidence requirements without retraining retrievers.

Method: Introduce Mosaic, a training-free framework that uses an LLM analyzer to convert query-specific evidence requirements into a bounded policy over seed selection, graph traversal, stopping, and evidence selection. The corpus graph, indexes, scoring functions, grounding procedure, and answer generator remain shared across queries.

Result: On GraphRAG-Bench, Mosaic achieves 76.97 QA on Medical and 64.33 on Novel; improvements over the strongest previous results by 5.13 and 4.43 points. Medical: 95.1 Evidence Recall and 86.1 Context Relevancy. Compared to the best canonical fixed policy, Mosaic improves by 9.96 points. Relative to Fixed Wide, it uses 81.9% fewer paths and 47.2% fewer evidence items. Transfer experiments on HotpotQA, MuSiQue, and 2WikiMultiHopQA show the policy interface generalizes without benchmark-specific retriever training.

Conclusion: Per-query control of GraphRAG retrieval via a training-free policy interface yields strong gains over fixed policies, offers efficiency improvements, and demonstrates transferability across multiple multi-hop QA benchmarks.

Abstract: Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) can connect evidence distributed across a corpus graph, but most systems use largely shared exploration procedures across queries. This creates a structural mismatch: direct facts may need compact local neighborhoods, comparisons need balanced coverage of multiple targets, and mediated questions may require deeper paths through weakly related connectors. We present Mosaic, a training-free framework that formulates GraphRAG retrieval as a per-query control problem. An LLM analyzer converts query-specific evidence requirements into a bounded policy over seed selection, graph traversal, stopping, and evidence selection, while the corpus graph, indexes, scoring functions, grounding procedure, and answer generator remain shared.
  On GraphRAG-Bench, Mosaic achieves query-weighted Answer Correctness of 76.97 on Medical and 64.33 on Novel, improving over the strongest previously reported overall results by 5.13 and 4.43 points. On Medical, it reaches 95.1 Evidence Recall and 86.1 Context Relevancy. Controlled comparisons on an identical graph and generator show that no fixed narrow, medium, or wide policy is consistently optimal; Mosaic improves by 9.96 points over the strongest canonical fixed policy. Relative to Fixed Wide, it evaluates 81.9% fewer paths and retains 47.2% fewer evidence items. Transfer experiments on HotpotQA, MuSiQue, and 2WikiMultiHopQA further show that the policy interface can be applied without benchmark-specific retriever training.

</details>


### [95] [Benchmark Radar: A Living Database and Search Engine for AI Benchmarks and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11115)
*Koutian Wu,Junjie Zhou,Ergan Shang,Jiayu Wang,Pengqian Han,Junkai Wang,Wanghan Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Benchmark Radar is a living database and search engine for AI benchmarks, aggregating daily benchmark discoveries, preserving source identities, and providing a searchable catalog with score histories, dashboards, and a CLI to aid discovery, reproducibility, and prior-art evaluation design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To help researchers and developers find, compare, and scrutinize AI benchmark evaluations, datasets, and code across diverse domains, and to improve transparency, reproducibility, and efficiency in benchmarking practices.

Method: Continuous ingestion from 37 sources (13 connectors + 24 feeds), daily discovery of papers, repos, datasets, and releases; a searchable catalog with preserved source identities and citations; 1,283 source records and 12,916 numeric observations across 790 records; audit of the catalog; a worked example; a web dashboard with leaderboard, Pareto frontier, saturation/trend views, offline CLI, and downloadable evidence.

Result: A functioning living benchmark platform described in detail, including collection/retrieval methods, catalog audit, saturation/adoption analysis, and a worked example; public release of a web dashboard, leaderboard, Pareto view, daily feeds, CLI for offline queries, and reproducible analyses.

Conclusion: Benchmark Radar offers a comprehensive, traceable framework for discovering and evaluating AI benchmarks, facilitating prior-art search and informed design of new evaluations, while also acknowledging challenges in score comparability across benchmarks and sources.

Abstract: Benchmark researchers and developers of large language models (LLMs) and other AI systems need to find relevant evaluations, locate their benchmark datasets and code, and understand the settings behind reported scores. We present Benchmark Radar, a living database and search engine for retrieval and discovery of AI benchmarks, covering LLM evaluation, agentic and tool-use benchmarks, coding, reasoning, safety, and domain-specific evaluations. The system combines daily discovery of benchmark papers, repositories, datasets, and releases with a searchable benchmark catalog, mentions in model cards and technical reports, and score histories. It retains source identities and citations so readers can inspect candidate benchmarks and their evaluation evidence. Daily discovery draws on 37 sources: 13 direct connectors and 24 first-party research and engineering feeds. The catalog contains 1,283 source records drawn from 4 benchmark catalogs and 12,916 numeric observations on 790 records. We describe collection and retrieval, audit the full catalog, and examine benchmark saturation, adoption trends, and the limits of score comparisons. A worked example walks through a complete prior-art search, showing how to query the catalog and inspect benchmark evidence when designing a new evaluation. We release the web dashboard with a benchmark leaderboard, a Pareto frontier view of score against measured use, saturation and trend views, daily feeds, downloadable evidence, a command-line interface (CLI) for offline queries, and reproducible analysis.

</details>


### [96] [KuaiRP Series Role-playing Models Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11127)
*Yipeng Wang,Ziwei Zhang,Jiahui Zhang,Qi Gan,Kai Sheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces KuaiRP, a multi-stage training pipeline for role-playing models that balances deep domain knowledge with retention of general agent capabilities, enabling high fidelity in specialized domains at low deployment cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable specialized role-playing models with strong domain knowledge and stable outputs while avoiding catastrophic forgetting of general competencies and keeping deployment efficiency.

Method: Stage 1: design standardized character templates and build a supervised fine-tuning (SFT) data pipeline from user simulations and reverse profile filtering. Stage 2: apply a rule-based composite reward in reinforcement learning to suppress length expansion and repetitive generation. Stage 3: recover general capabilities via a self-distillation framework—Two-stage On-Policy Distillation (OPD) with Cumulative-Divergence Decay (CDD)—using the domain-adapted model as teacher and the base model as student.

Result: KuaiRP models achieve role-playing fidelity on par with current state-of-the-art proprietary models in targeted domains and successfully recover general agent capabilities, while maintaining very low deployment costs.

Conclusion: A three-stage training pipeline—domain-focused SFT, RL with targeted rewards, and self-distillation with OPD-CDD—effectively injects domain knowledge without sacrificing general capabilities, delivering competitive fidelity with cost-efficient deployment.

Abstract: This paper introduces the complete technical solution for the KuaiRP series of role-playing models. We aim to achieve four core objectives for a dedicated role-playing model: simplified prompt engineering, highly stable output quality, built-in domain world knowledge, and high-efficiency deployment with a small parameter size. However, effectively injecting deep domain knowledge often leads to a severe catastrophic forgetting of the model's general agent capabilities. To overcome this trade-off, we propose a multi-stage training pipeline. First, we design a standardized character template and construct an SFT data pipeline based on user behavior simulation and reverse profile filtering. Next, we utilize a rule-based composite reward function during the Reinforcement Learning (RL) phase to eliminate common degradation phenomena like length expansion and repetitive generation. Finally, to recover the general capabilities compromised during SFT and RL, we propose a novel self-distillation paradigm using Two-stage On-Policy Distillation (OPD) equipped with Cumulative-Divergence Decay (CDD). By using the domain-adapted model as the teacher and the original base model as the student, we effectively balance deep domain knowledge injection with the preservation of general agent capabilities. Experimental results demonstrate that the KuaiRP models not only match the current state-of-the-art proprietary models in role-playing fidelity within our target domains, but also successfully recover general agent capabilities, maintaining extremely low deployment costs.

</details>


### [97] [Same Day, Same Story; One Day Ahead, a Different Signal: The Dual Validity of Financial Sentiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11144)
*AS Aravinthkakshan,Laven Srivastava,Harsh Nandwani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Semantic validity of sentiment tools is sensitive to sampling and score representation; semantic alignment with human labels does not guarantee predictive rankings across horizons; predictive power is weak for spam volume signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test whether construct validity (alignment with human labels) translates into predictive validity for financial market signals across multiple sentiment tools and sampling schemes.

Method: Evaluate five sentiment tools (VADER, Loughran-McDonald, FinBERT, Twitter-RoBERTa, LLM annotator) on a large corpus (70,500 X messages linked to abnormal stock returns; securities class actions 2002-2025) within a single pipeline, using a single-annotator human-labelled gold sample. Compare results across conventional sampling vs fixed-n panel and across horizons (same-day vs one-day lead).

Result: Construct vs predictive validity diverge based on sampling convention and score representation. Under conventional sampling, human agreement aligns with same-day graded associations more than with one-day leads. In fixed-n panel, graded rank correlations are similar across horizons, but coarse ordering remains weak. Benchmark agreement confirms semantic validity but not predictive rankings. Spam-heavy conversation (17.6%) shows message volume does not predict market damage or settlement size.

Conclusion: Semantic/construct validity is not sufficient to determine predictive rankings. Researchers should report how sampling conventions and representations affect validity and avoid overreliance on single-effect sizes when using sentiment tools for market signals.

Abstract: Financial NLP has a standard workflow: validate a sentiment tool against human labels, then trust it to extract market signal. This assumes the two evaluations measure the same thing. We test that assumption in a setting where both can be measured at once: a corpus of securities class actions (2002-2025) linking 70,500 X messages to abnormal stock returns, with a single-annotator human labelled gold sample. Running five instruments (VADER, Loughran-McDonald, FinBERT, Twitter-RoBERTa, and an LLM annotator) through one identical pipeline, we find that the relationship between construct and predictive validity depends on the sampling convention and score representation. Under conventional method-specific sampling, human agreement aligns more closely with graded same-day associations than with one-day leads. On a fixed-n panel, however, agreement has similar graded rank correlations at both horizons, while the coarse ordering remains weak. Benchmark agreement therefore establishes semantic validity but does not by itself determine predictive rankings. In a conversation that is 17.6% spam, message volume predicts neither market damage nor settlement size.

</details>


### [98] [The Oligarch Barely Steers Model Collapse in Multi-Model Ecosystems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11146)
*Yangze Liu,Zhongyi Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Concentration does not determine collapse dynamics in recursive training on AI-generated text; the pace and destination hinge on pool composition and text sources, with human content dampening drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether oligopoly-like market concentration accelerates collapse and enables steering in recursive training, using controlled multi-model ecosystems.

Method: 13 open models (1–4B) form ecosystems of 3–13 players; inject a top-share bias to 90%; each generation, outputs are aggregated into a pooled corpus by market share and retrained from clean base weights on that pool for five generations; manipulations include changing pool composition and substituting human text.

Result: Across tested ranges, concentration leaves speed and endpoint largely unchanged; invariance observed; endpoints shift only by a few percent; extreme share with injected bias does not steer; speed is driven by pool suppliers and their susceptibility; a K=3 ecosystem swap changes drift by 2.8x; regression with susceptibility explains drift (R^2=0.68); replacing half the pool with human text halves drift; overall collapse is governed by who fills the pool, not by concentration per se.

Conclusion: Within the tested regime, market concentration neither fixes the destination nor the pace; dynamics are governed by pool composition and how readily its contents propagate, implying governance should focus on input sources and their influence rather than concentration alone.

Abstract: AI-generated text is flowing back into the training corpora of the next generation of models. Recursive training on it drives model collapse, and recent work extends the setting to many models feeding one another -- but almost always with the market split evenly, while real generative AI is an oligopoly. Concentration raises two worries: fewer, more uniform sources may make collapse faster, and later models may be dragged toward the oligarch's output. We test both in controlled ecosystems: 13 open 1--4B models form natural ecosystems of 3 to 13 players, plus an injected probe that pushes the top share to 90%; each generation, every model's output is mixed into a shared pool by market share and every model is retrained on that pool from clean base weights, for five generations. Yet within the range we test, neither worry materializes; what emerges instead is an invariance. Making the split more unequal barely changes the speed of collapse. Destinations move even less: the share and identity knobs shift five-generation endpoints by only a few percent of the drift common to all arms -- the ecosystems collapse to nearly the same place. An extreme share paired with the strongest injected bias still does not guarantee steering, and the topic shifts it does produce leave only a faint trace on the ruler that measures collapse. What sets the speed is who supplies the pool and how readily those suppliers are carried along: with every share held fixed, swapping the members of a K=3 ecosystem changes five-generation drift by 2.8x; a share-weighted index of each member's susceptibility explains the speed differences across nineteen arms with R^2 = 0.68; and replacing half the pool with human text roughly halves drift without changing its course. Within the tested range, concentration sets neither the destination nor the pace of collapse; the pace follows whose text fills the pool.

</details>


### [99] [Autonomous Chemical Mechanistic Discovery through Agentic Reasoning and Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11147)
*Dong Li,Sixuan Mi,Zihao Ye,Huan Xiong,Tao XU,Tong Zhu,Aijia Zhang,Junqi Gao,Kaiyan Zhang,Shijie Wang,Bowen Zhou,Yuqiang Li,Biqing Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARCHE is an autonomous, agentic system that combines general reasoning, a domain-specific computational chemistry model, and a structured tool registry to autonomously generate, prioritize, and validate mechanistic hypotheses, orchestrate computational workflows, and refine conclusions in a closed loop. Demonstrated on three challenging mechanistic tasks and complemented by public code release.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automating mechanistic chemistry is hindered by heavy reliance on expert intervention and manual workflow orchestration. A scalable, self-validating framework is needed to accelerate discovery and improve reproducibility in mechanistic studies.

Method: ARCHE integrates a general-purpose reasoning model with a domain-specialized computational chemistry model and a structured tool registry to interpret questions, generate and rank mechanistic hypotheses, autonomously orchestrate computational workflows, and iteratively refine conclusions based on computed evidence within a closed loop.

Result: Validated across three progressively demanding scenarios: (1) reconstructing stereocontrolling transition states and validating the mechanism in a reported asymmetric catalytic reaction; (2) proposing and validating a plausible radical pathway for an unpublished α-iodoboronate C–I cleavage reaction via iterative hypothesis refinement; (3) identifying a chemically interpretable descriptor that governs selectivity in nickel-catalysed migratory cross-coupling reactions. Code for ARCHE is publicly available at GitHub.

Conclusion: ARCHE advances autonomous mechanistic discovery by coupling agentic reasoning with rigorous computational validation, establishing a foundation for broader machine-assisted chemical research.

Abstract: Unraveling reaction mechanisms is central to modern chemistry, yet automating these investigations remains challenging because computational workflows still rely heavily on expert intervention. Here we introduce ARCHE, an autonomous agentic system that integrates a general-purpose reasoning model, a domain-specialized computational chemistry model, and a structured tool registry to transform mechanistic inquiry into a scalable, self-validating process. ARCHE interprets scientific questions, generates and prioritizes mechanistic hypotheses, orchestrates computational workflows, and iteratively refines conclusions based on computed evidence within a closed loop. We validate its capabilities across three increasingly demanding scenarios: reconstructing stereocontrolling transition states and validating the corresponding reaction mechanism in a previously reported asymmetric catalytic reaction; proposing and validating a plausible radical pathway through iterative hypothesis refinement for a recently discovered but unpublished $α$-iodoboronate C-I cleavage reaction; and identifying a chemically interpretable descriptor that governs selectivity in nickel-catalysed migratory cross-coupling reactions. By coupling agentic reasoning with rigorous computational validation, ARCHE advances autonomous mechanistic discovery and establishes a foundation for broader machine-assisted chemical research. The code for ARCHE is publicly available at https://github.com/JetAstra/Arche-Harness.

</details>


### [100] [DRG-MAPPO: Hierarchical Dynamic Role-Graph Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Cooperative Air Combat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11155)
*Junlin Liu,Chengwei Li,Yang Gao,Hui Chang,Xinchen Zhang,Zhijun Zhao,Hao Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hierarchical MARL framework, DRG-MAPPO, combines graph-relational modeling with dynamic role assignment and a target-priority auxiliary task, achieving strong cooperative air combat performance (87% win rate).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses two key MARL limitations in air combat: (i) lack of structured, time-varying relational modeling among battlefield entities, and (ii) flat architectures that fail to explicitly model tactical roles, causing ambiguous task allocation.

Method: Constructs a graph-based representation of battlefield interactions and applies graph attention to extract relational features among allies, enemies, and threats. A high-level policy dynamically assigns tactical roles (e.g., leader, supporter). A low-level policy executes discrete maneuvers conditioned on roles and encoded graph features. A target-priority auxiliary task encourages focus-fire behaviors. The framework integrates these components within MAPPO.

Result: Achieves a reported state-of-the-art win rate of 87% in cooperative air combat scenarios, suggesting improved relational modeling, interpretability, and optimization stability.

Conclusion: DRG-MAPPO effectively balances relational modeling, interpretable role-based coordination, and stable policy optimization to enhance cooperative air combat performance.

Abstract: Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) has emerged as a pivotal paradigm for complex decision-making in autonomous systems and air combat. While MARL has demonstrated significant potential in air combat, achieving sophisticated tactical coordination remains a non-trivial challenge. This difficulty is largely attributed to two primary limitations: (1) the absence of structured relational modeling hinders agents from capturing complex, time-varying interactions among battlefield entities; and (2) conventional flat architectures often lack the capability to explicitly model tactical roles, leading to ambiguous task allocation in highly dynamic environments. To address these challenges, we propose Hierarchical Dynamic Role-Graph Multi-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization (DRG-MAPPO), a novel MARL framework that integrates graph-based relational modeling with dynamic role assignment. Specifically, DRG-MAPPO constructs a graph-based representation of battlefield interactions and leverages graph attention mechanisms to extract critical relational features among allies, enemies, and threats. Subsequently, a high-level policy employs a dynamic role assignment mechanism to determine tactical responsibilities (e.g., ``leader'' and ``supporter''). Conditioned on these roles and encoded graph-relational features, a low-level policy executes discrete maneuver actions, facilitating the joint optimization of tactical strategy and collaborative execution. Furthermore, a target-priority auxiliary task is designed to foster the emergence of behaviors such as focus-fire. Experimental results demonstrate that DRG-MAPPO achieves a state-of-the-art win rate of 87%, suggesting that our framework effectively balances relational modeling, interpretability, and optimization stability for cooperative air combat.

</details>


### [101] [Breaking Predictions Is Not Enough: Specified-Foil Counterfactuals for Temporal Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11170)
*Minwoo Yu,Young-guk Ha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Destination-specific counterfactuals for temporal graphs using trace-guided intervention search; instantiates LiFTER (CTDGs) and TLogic (TKGs); high success with substantial efficiency gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Users want to know which past conditions could lead to a specific alternative outcome (foil) rather than any counterfactual; existing methods modify past events but do not fix the desired outcome, limiting user insight and efficiency.

Method: Compare a complete execution (A) with a reconstructed incomplete execution for the foil (B), identify differences, and map them to five intervention primitives (DELETE, INSERT, REWIRE, RELABEL, SHIFT). Verify the foil via exact replay. Implementations: LiFTER on continuous-time dynamic graphs (CTDGs) and TLogic on temporal knowledge graphs (TKGs).

Result: CTDGs: retains 85.7–93.6% of black-box greedy successes while reducing predictor evaluations by 75–80%. TKGs: specifies the foil in 74.8% of 600 comparisons.
