### [126] [From Queries to Narratives: Cultural Heritage Data Stories for Knowledge Graph Exploration and Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11403)
*Tabea Tietz,Torsten Schrade,Etienne Posthumus,Linnaea Söhn,Jonatan Jalle Steller,Jörg Waitelonis,Harald Sack*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes data stories that weave narrative text and images with executable SPARQL queries and visual results to ease exploration and enable data-quality assessment in cultural-heritage knowledge graphs; demonstrates with the LODEON platform, Sparnatural, and AI-assisted authoring; early results indicate improved accessibility and quality-awareness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lower barriers to querying cultural-heritage KGs, address data heterogeneity and constant growth, and surface data-quality issues not visible in aggregate statistics.

Method: Define and author data stories that integrate explanatory text/images with embedded SPARQL queries and visualizations; describe the workflow for authoring against the graph; present LODEON as a proof-of-concept with Sparnatural and AI-assisted authoring tools; reflect on experiences from seminars/workshops.

Result: Data stories guide users through unfamiliar graphs, enable reproducible narratives, and surface data-quality issues by backing each claim with a query; the LODEON platform demonstrates feasibility; early experience suggests improved accessibility for exploration and quality assessment.

Conclusion: Data stories are a promising approach to making cultural-heritage KGs more usable for discovery and quality control; further evaluation and refinement are warranted.

Abstract: Cultural-heritage KGs such as the NFDI4Culture-KG contain millions of triples about artworks, music, inscriptions, historical events, and the people and places connected to them. For many users, however, discovering this knowledge can be difficult. While SPARQL can be learned, writing meaningful queries first requires an in-depth understanding of the graph's data model, an investment many domain researchers and practitioners are unwilling to make. Even with existing user interfaces, a starting point and some guidance are usually needed, because the data contained in the graph is highly specialized, heterogeneous, and constantly growing, making it challenging to know what it contains or which questions it can answer. In this paper, we present data stories as a way not only to lower this barrier, but also to turn exploration into data-quality assessment, and thus combine accessible querying with the discovery of issues that remain hidden in aggregate statistics. In this contribution, a data story is understood as a narrative document that integrates explanatory text and images with executable SPARQL queries and their visualized results. It is described how they are authored against the graph and how they serve several purposes: guiding users through an unfamiliar graph, creating reproducible narratives, and surfacing data-quality issues previously hidden in aggregate statistics. The authoring platform LODEON including its Sparnatural and AI-supported authoring assistants is introduced as a proof-of-concept. Within the authoring environment, every claim made about the data can be backed by an explicit query, making these narratives transparent and reproducible. This paper also reflects on lessons learned from hands-on seminars and workshops. Early experience suggests that such data stories make cultural-heritage knowledge graphs more accessible for both exploration and quality assessment.

</details>


### [127] [LLMs as Post-hoc Auditors of Physiological Plausibility in Symbolic Regression: A Clinician-Evaluated Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11431)
*Jorge López-Varela,J. Ignacio Hidalgo,José-Manuel Muñoz,Omar Costilla-Reyes,Esther Maqueda,Jesus Moreno-Fernandez,Tomás González-Vidal,J. Manuel Velasco,Oscar Garnica*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs act as post-hoc auditors to compare interpretability of evolved symbolic expressions; they improve comparative explainability but may generate questionable physiological/mathematical rationales, implying oversight by experts is essential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address interpretability and plausibility gaps in symbolic regression by using large language models to analyze and rank evolved expressions, assessing whether LLM-derived explanations align with domain expertise.

Method: Four symbolic expressions from grammar-based genetic programming are analyzed by three LLMs across three runs. Clinicians evaluate the LLM-generated interpretations and rankings. The study compares LLM-based outputs to expert judgments and builds on prior work on body fat percentage estimation.

Result: LLMs produced more favorable clinician assessments for comparative model rankings than for isolated term-level interpretations. However, several explanations were physiologically or mathematically questionable, indicating LLMs are better suited for comparative auditing under expert oversight rather than autonomous validation.

Conclusion: LLMs can aid interpretability auditing for Symbolic Regression but should not replace expert validation. They are most effective as comparative auditing tools with clinician oversight, guiding model selection rather than validating scientific plausibility.

Abstract: Genetic Programming and its variants, such as grammatical evolution, are widely used in Symbolic Regression to derive mathematical expressions from multivariate data. In addition to predictive accuracy, models are appreciated for their potential to provide interpretability, offering explicit equations that relate input variables to outcomes. However, achieving interpretability and plausibility remains challenging, as evolved models may be complex or scientifically inconsistent. In this study, we explore whether Large Language Models, can assist in improving the explainability of Symbolic Regression models generated by evolutionary computation methods. Building upon our previous work on estimating body fat percentage using grammar-based Genetic Programming , we investigate the use of LLMs as post-processing tools to analyze and rank evolved expressions according to their interpretability and medical plausibility. Four symbolic expressions are analysed by three LLMs over three repeated runs, and the resulting interpretations and rankings are assessed by a panel of three clinicians. Across the three LLMs, comparative model-ranking outputs received more favorable clinician assessments than isolated term-level interpretations. However, the LLMs also produced physiologically and mathematically questionable explanations, indicating that they are better suited to comparative auditing under expert oversight than to autonomous validation.\blfootnote{The present work is an extended version of a paper submitted into a journal.

</details>


### [128] [Calibration-Aware Uncertainty Cascades for Efficient Heterogeneous Model Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11446)
*Yilin Zhang,Han Jiang,Cai Xu,Ying Liu,Wei Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Calibration-Aware Uncertainty Cascades (CAUC) provide a post-hoc, calibration-driven framework for heterogeneous model ensembles that unifies decision thresholds across model pools, improving accuracy while reducing reliance on strong-model calls and computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous model collaboration often uses fixed routers or confidence-based cascades with thresholds that do not generalize across model pools or budgets, leading to poor adaptability. A pool- and budget-agnostic, calibrated approach is desirable.

Method: Independently calibrate each model’s confidence on validation data. Use calibrated scores to decide between early prediction, invoking a stronger model, or combining outputs, via a unified decision criterion decoupled from specific model pools or budgets. The paper also provides a theoretical interpretation that calibrated thresholds carry selective-risk guarantees, unlike uncalibrated scores.

Result: Empirical evaluation on six language benchmarks shows CAUC yields an average relative accuracy improvement of 1.9% over strong-model-only inference while avoiding ~47% of strong-model calls. On image classification benchmarks, CAUC maintains or improves predictive performance with GFLOPs reduced by up to 57%.

Conclusion: CAUC offers a simple, robust post-hoc strategy for heterogeneous model collaboration that is calibration-aware, pool-agnostic, and budget-aware, achieving better accuracy with reduced computation across language and image tasks.

Abstract: Heterogeneous model collaboration seeks to exploit the complementary strengths of different models to balance predictive performance and inference cost. Existing approaches typically rely either on trained routers, which tie routing decisions to a fixed task and model pool, or on raw-confidence cascades, whose thresholds lack consistent reliability semantics across heterogeneous models. Consequently, these approaches adapt poorly to changing model pools and deployment budgets. We propose Calibration-Aware Uncertainty Cascades (CAUC), a simple post-hoc framework that independently calibrates each model's confidence and selects deployment policies using validation data. The resulting calibrated confidence scores establish a common reliability scale for accepting an early prediction, invoking a stronger model, or selectively combining model outputs. This unified decision criterion decouples deployment policies from any particular model pool or operating budget. We further show theoretically that calibration gives confidence thresholds an explicit selective-risk interpretation, whereas uncalibrated scores offer no comparable reliability guarantee. Extensive experiments demonstrate that, across six language benchmarks, CAUC achieves an average relative accuracy improvement of 1.9% over strong-model-only inference while avoiding approximately 47% of strong-model calls. On image classification benchmarks, it maintains or improves predictive performance while reducing measured GFLOPs by up to 57%.

</details>


### [129] [RouteRepair: Instance-Level Failure Diagnosis and Targeted Repair in LLM-Based Automated Heuristic Design for Routing Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11452)
*Binghao Ji,Di Huang,Jiahui Fang,Zhiyuan Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RouteRepair: a failure-aware, evidence-constrained approach to repair routing heuristics, improving TSP/CVRP performance via targeted, parent-specific interventions while preserving existing strengths; shows significant reductions in optimality gaps and costs across constructive search, GLS, and ACO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based automated heuristic design can produce effective routing rules but may exhibit recurrent, instance-structure-specific failures that aggregate evaluation obscures; need diagnosable, bounded repair to fix weaknesses without degrading strong cases.

Method: Diagnose weaknesses at the parent (heuristic) level using instance-level performance signals (routing evidence, solver behavior, program context); apply targeted, bounded modifications to corresponding heuristic components; protect already performing behaviors; validate interventions via matched parent–child evaluation focusing on failure recovery and collateral degradation across TSP and CVRP with constructive search, guided local search, and ant colony optimization.

Result: Quantitative improvements include reducing TSP mean optimality gap from 1.7476% to 0.7587%; CVRP constructive heuristic lowers average route cost by 1.91% relative to savings heuristic; generated ACO priors outperform matched hand-designed priors; overall, failure-aware refinement yields improvements on hard instances while preserving performance on easier cases.

Conclusion: Failure-aware, evidence-constrained refinement can robustly improve routing heuristics on challenging instances while maintaining performance on well-solved cases, suggesting generalizability to other optimization heuristics through bounded, instance-aware repairs.

Abstract: Efficient routing optimization is essential to freight transportation, urban logistics, and shared mobility, where high-quality heuristics are often required under limited computational budgets. Recent large language model (LLM)-based automated heuristic design methods can generate effective routing rules, but aggregate evaluation may mask recurrent failures on particular instance structures. To address this limitation, this study develops RouteRepair, which diagnoses parent-specific weaknesses from instance-level performance and applies targeted modifications to the corresponding heuristic components while protecting behavior that already performs well. Routing evidence, solver behavior, and program context are combined to define bounded repair objectives, and each intervention is validated through matched parent-child evaluation of failure recovery and collateral degradation. Experiments on the traveling salesman problem (TSP) and capacitated vehicle routing problem (CVRP) span constructive search, guided local search, and ant colony optimization. RouteRepair-GLS reduces the mean TSP optimality gap from 1.7476% to 0.7587%, while the constructive CVRP heuristic lowers average route cost by 1.91% relative to the savings heuristic; the generated ACO priors also outperform matched hand-designed priors. These results show that failure-aware, evidence-constrained refinement can improve routing heuristics on difficult instances while preserving performance on cases they already solve well.

</details>


### [130] [Flexible and Interpretable Accent Distance Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11458)
*Charles McGhee,Mark J. F. Gales,Kate M. Knill*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes interpretable articulatory representations and an optimal transport framework to compare accents across diverse recording types, bridging traditional phonetics and accent-embedding approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome the interpretability gap in accent embeddings and enable cross-type accent comparisons (word-level formants, connected speech, and TTS embeddings).

Method: Derive articulatory representations via articulatory inversion and use optimal transport to compare accent distributions across arbitrary recording types.

Result: Demonstrates that articulatory-based representations are interpretable and effective as a basis for accent comparison; OT provides a flexible framework for cross-type comparisons.

Conclusion: Offers a unified, interpretable approach that links phonetic analysis with embedding-based accent modeling, enabling robust cross-domain accent comparison.

Abstract: Determining the differences between two speakers' accents is a fundamental task in linguistics and speech technology research. The methodology used to measure these differences depends on the specific research area. A phonetics researcher may demonstrate accent variation by comparing vowel formants in paired recordings of individual words. These results will be interpretable, but the recordings will be time-consuming to collect and may not be representative of connected speech. Accented Text-to-Speech (TTS) research has pushed towards using accent embeddings derived from accent classification tasks. These embeddings can be produced from any speech recording, but are not readily interpretable. In this paper, we demonstrate that articulatory representations created through articulatory inversion can be used as an interpretable basis for accent comparison and that optimal transport provides a framework for accent comparison across arbitrary recording types.

</details>


### [131] [The Convention Gap: Towards Measuring Implicit Communication in Cooperative AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11489)
*Makoto Fukushima,Hua-Dong Xiong,Ehsan Moradi Pari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes the convention gap as a metric for implicit communication in cooperative AI, demonstrates its computability in Hanabi, and shows humans rely on conventions more than AI partners; convention compatibility may predict AI effectiveness with humans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To capture implicit, convention-based communication missing from standard AI benchmarks and to assess whether alignment with human conventions improves human-AI cooperation.

Method: Compute the difference between observed failure and the failure probability predicted from literal content in Hanabi (exact posterior due to finite deck and deterministic hints). Analyze ~101,000 actions from three public datasets (human-human, AI-AI, human-AI). Use Off-Belief Learning as a control with known-content conventions. Examine gaps overall and for moves with no hints; compare scores across corpora.

Result: Convention gap: humans +26.2 pp; AI-AI -0.7 pp; human-AI +16.4 pp. No-hint moves show +46 pp gap in humans. Among human-AI pairs, literal information predicted failure was similar (38–41%), but human failures varied (14.4%–34.4%), with the gap narrowing from +24.1 to +6.2 pp as partner changed; the partner with the largest gap yielded the fewest human failures. Score information differed by roster; the gap consistently separates human from AI at the agent level. Off-Belief Learning yielded +1.6 pp at the convention-free level, rising to +21.7 pp as conventions were allowed to influence outcomes.

Conclusion: Convention compatibility—alignment with human communication conventions—may be a better predictor of AI effectiveness with humans than raw AI-AI performance; controlling for conventions yields near-acceptable gaps, suggesting focus on implicit-cue alignment for human-centered AI.

Abstract: Cooperative AI agents are evaluated against other AIs, yet human cooperation relies on implicit conventions---shared protocols for reading meaning beyond the literal message---which AI-AI benchmarks may not capture. We propose the \emph{convention gap}, the difference between the failure probability predicted from the literal content of communication and the observed failure rate, as a metric of implicit communication. In the card game Hanabi, the finite deck and deterministic hint constraints make this posterior exactly computable. We replayed about 101,000 play actions from three public datasets of human-human (hanab.live), AI-AI (HOAD), and human-AI (HanabiData) games. The gap was +26.2 percentage points (pp) in human pairs, $-$0.7~pp in AI pairs, and +16.4~pp in human-AI pairs, and was concentrated on plays of cards that had received no hints (+46~pp in human pairs). Within human-AI play, the literal information available to humans was similar across the three AI partners (mean predicted failure 38--41\%), but human failure rates ranged from 14.4\% to 34.4\% and the gap from +24.1 to +6.2~pp; the partner eliciting the largest gap produced the fewest human failures. Game score carried different information: it depended on each corpus's roster composition, whereas the gap separated human from AI play at the agent level. As a known-answer check, Off-Belief Learning agents, whose convention content is controlled by construction, gave a gap of +1.6~pp at the convention-free level, rising monotonically to +21.7~pp. These results suggest that convention compatibility, rather than AI-AI performance, may predict an AI's effectiveness with human partners.

</details>


### [132] [Published Unlearning Numbers Move Per Checkpoint, and Not Because the Removed Data Survives: An Audit of 263 Released Batch-Normalized Checkpoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11490)
*Junlong Shen Xingyu Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Auditing unlearning reveals drift in released checkpoints and batch-norm statistics, with limited recalibration impact; suggests labeling the fitting convention alongside release identifiers, especially for BN-based vision models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To verify that unlearning procedures and data removals do not leave hidden artifacts in released models, and to assess the reliability of unlearning audits across multiple checkpoints and seeds.

Method: Analyze published unlearning checkpoints and their retrained references by comparing bit-identical-refits on kept data; examine batch-normalization statistics that were not altered by gradients or releases; quantify how often released checkpoints move beyond their seeds and how this drift relates to the fitting method vs. the data removed/kept; categorize verdicts into recalibration budgets and cross-replicate stability.

Result: The audit shows that 47 of 221 released checkpoints exceed the spread indicated by their seeds; several movements occur within a method whose average does not move. The moving is tied to checkpoint property rather than the method. Shifting kept vs removed data inside a fixed fitting pool barely changes the published state, while how far a checkpoint’s shipped state drifts from any refit tracks drift. Twelve verdicts cross, four clearly hit a recalibration budget, two clear it on every replicate, and a population trained near its criterion yields none.

Conclusion: To enhance transparency, a release should label the fitting convention in addition to the release number, particularly for batch-normalized vision models where this channel exists.

Abstract: An unlearning audit reads its verdict off numbers that an unlearned model and its retrained reference each publish, and both also ship batch-normalization statistics that no gradient step wrote and no release records. Refitting them on kept data at bit-identical weights moves 47 of 221 released checkpoints past the spread their own release's seeds show, several inside a method whose average does not move: what moves is the checkpoint's property, not its method's. What does the moving is not the removed data surviving in the state: exchanging kept records for removed ones inside a fixed fitting pool moves a published cell by almost nothing, while how far a checkpoint's shipped state has drifted from any refit does track it. The consequence for a published decision is real but narrow: twelve verdicts cross, four clear a measured recalibration budget, two clear it on every replicate, and a population we trained and sited near its own criterion yields none. A release should therefore name the fitting convention beside the number, on the batch-normalized vision models where this channel exists.

</details>


### [133] [From Document Silos to Process Intelligence: A Multi-Layer Knowledge Graph for CMC Process Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11493)
*Reza Amirmoshiri,Faryad Sahneh,Yasser Jangjou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A modular agentic-AI platform converts heterogeneous CMC process-development documents into a dual-layer knowledge graph, enabling retrieval-augmented reasoning; evaluation shows high Tier-1 accuracy and Tier-2 judgments, with a router selecting retrieval paths. Implications: improved pharma knowledge management.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address fragmentation of CMC knowledge across formats (digital, scanned, handwritten, multilingual) that causes traceability gaps and high knowledge-management costs during technology transfer and regulatory filing; a structured, provenance-rich process intelligence framework is needed.

Method: Construct a base lexical knowledge layer with a Document-Section-Chunk hierarchy and lossless ingestion of diverse documents (digital, scanned, handwritten, multilingual). Build an intelligence layer with ontology-aligned entities and a provenance-anchored domain graph bridging cross-document concepts. Deploy LLM agents across both layers to select optimal retrieval paths. Evaluate with a three-tier protocol on 505 questions from 38 Sanofi small-molecule development reports, using Tier-1 (multiple-choice) accuracy and Tier-2 (LLM-judge) pass rates. A router agent directs questions to the appropriate layer.

Result: Tier-1 accuracy of 95% indicates strong retrieval reliability; Tier-2 LLM-judge pass rate of 85% reveals additional failure modes, especially on comparative and corpus-wide questions, not captured by Tier-1 alone.

Conclusion: The protocol provides a practical benchmark for evaluating agentic platforms on nonpublic datasets; graph-based architectures are likely to see broader pharma adoption as a means to transform fragmented document repositories into structured process intelligence.

Abstract: Chemistry, Manufacturing and Controls (CMC) process development generates an enormous body of technical information across a multi-stage, knowledge-intensive continuum from drug discovery to commercial manufacturing. This knowledge is traditionally fragmented across functions and heterogeneous formats, causing traceability gaps and significant knowledge-management costs during technology transfer and regulatory filing. We present a modular agentic-AI platform that converts a heterogeneous corpus of process-development documents into a queryable, dual-layer knowledge graph. A base knowledge layer builds a lexical graph with a Document-Section-Chunk hierarchy through lossless ingestion of digital, scanned, handwritten, and multilingual documents, while an intelligence layer extracts ontology-aligned entities and bridges cross-document concepts through a provenance-anchored domain graph. LLM agents operate across both layers, selecting the retrieval path best suited to each question. We evaluate the lexical layer with a novel three-tier protocol measuring the deployment-fidelity of a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system on proprietary data, demonstrated on 505 questions curated from 38 development reports of a Sanofi small-molecule program. Tier-1 multiple-choice accuracy of 95% signals strong platform reliability; the stricter Tier-2 LLM-judge pass rate of 85%, which degrades on comparative and corpus-wide questions, reveals a failure taxonomy that Tier-1 accuracy alone fails to capture. A router agent selects between layers according to question type. We anticipate this protocol will enable future designers of agentic platforms to assess their systems against nonpublic databases, and that graph-based architectures will see broader adoption in pharma as a means of transforming fragmented document repositories into structured process intelligence.

</details>


### [134] [ActMap: Single-Pass Uncertainty Quantification from Generation-Time Activation Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11498)
*Jacopo Dardini,Roberta Calegari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ActMap compresses the generation-time hidden-state into a fixed, compact 12×32×128 tensor of temporal-statistic channels, enabling fast, single-generation uncertainty quantification (UQ) with a lightweight classifier; it outperforms several baselines and matches larger detectors, offering audit-ready scores for abstention and verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practical UQ for large language models requires trust decisions from a single generation. Existing methods either require multiple generations, rely solely on output-token probabilities, or reduce model computation to a single hidden state. A compact, white-box representation that preserves transformer-structure and is auditable would enable scalable, efficient oversight.

Method: ActMap constructs a fixed-size 12×32×128 tensor that encodes the generation-time hidden-state trajectory across all layers and generated tokens. It is captured during generation with no measurable overhead and maintains the same shape across model depths and hidden sizes, occupying 96 KiB. Occlusion analysis localizes the classifier's signal to mid-depth regions. A lightweight classifier (compact Vision Transformer) reads a correctness probability from each map; capacity-matched MLPs yield comparable results. Trained/evaluated in-domain on short-answer QA, direct-answer math, and summarization factuality using 7–8B instruction-tuned models, ActMap is compared against baselines (sampling, token-probability, attention, embedding) and matches ACT-ViT in AUROC while often exhibiting better calibration.

Result: ActMap consistently outperforms the baselines on the tested tasks and models, achieving competitive AUROC with lower calibration error on the majority of pairs. It matches ACT-ViT's performance despite using a vastly smaller, fixed representation and demonstrates a fast inference time (a fraction of a millisecond per map). The resulting score supports abstention, routing, and selective verification from a single generation, enabling scalable oversight of deployed models.

Conclusion: ActMap provides a practical, audit-friendly primitive for scalable oversight: a compact, white-box representation of generation-time dynamics that enables reliable truthfulness scoring from a single pass, with low overhead and strong empirical performance across tasks and model sizes.

Abstract: Practical uncertainty quantification (UQ) for large language models must decide,
  from a single generation, whether a specific answer should be trusted. Existing
  methods either sample multiple generations, read only output-token probabilities,
  or reduce the model's internal computation to a single hidden state. We introduce
  ActMap, a white-box representation that compresses the generation-time hidden-
  state trajectory (every layer, every generated token) into a fixed $12 \times 32
  \times 128$ tensor of temporal-statistic channels that preserves structure across
  transformer depth and pooled hidden coordinates. The map is captured during the
  generation pass with no measurable overhead, has a fixed shape across model
  depths and hidden sizes, and occupies 96 KiB: a compact artifact that can be
  retained for audit-relevant generations and probed directly, with occlusion
  analysis localizing the classifier's signal to mid-depth regions of the map. A
  lightweight classifier, instantiated as a compact Vision Transformer, reads an
  estimated correctness probability from each map in a fraction of a millisecond;
  capacity-matched MLPs perform comparably, indicating the representation itself
  carries the result. Trained and evaluated in-domain on short-answer QA, direct-
  answer math, and summarization factuality with three instruction-tuned 7-8B
  models, ActMap consistently outperforms sampling, token-probability, attention,
  and embedding baselines, and matches ACT-ViT, a detector trained on dense
  activation tensors $67 \times$ larger, at essentially the same mean AUROC with
  lower calibration error on ten of twelve pairs. The resulting score supports
  abstention, routing, and selective verification from a single generation, making
  it a practical primitive for scalable oversight of deployed models.

</details>


### [135] [Extending SMT Solving with Non-Ground Clause Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11509)
*Yasmine Briefs,Christoph Weidenbach*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unifying calculus for SMT solving that blends ground instantiations with non-ground conflict analysis, enabling learned clauses to be more general and potentially non-redundant, and that can simulate CDCL, SCL(FOL), SCL(T), and Resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-ground reasoning can produce exponentially shorter proofs than ground-only SMT solving; current approaches conflict analysis yields only ground clauses, missing non-ground benefits. A framework that integrates non-ground reasoning into learning can improve proof efficiency and expressiveness.

Method: Introduce a calculus combining ground instantiations with CDCL(T)-style rules, plus non-ground conflict analysis performed on the original non-ground clauses. Learn clauses from resolution steps on non-ground clauses while solving on ground instances; propose strategies to ensure non-redundant, general learned clauses; incorporate chronological (back)tracking. Prove the calculus simulates CDCL, SCL(FOL), SCL(T), and Resolution.

Result: The framework provides a common setting that subsumes multiple solving paradigms and instantiation-based procedures; it yields learned clauses that can be more general than purely ground conflicts and, with appropriate strategies, non-redundant. It can simulate a broad spectrum of logics and proof systems, indicating strong theoretical unification and potential practical benefits for SMT solving.

Conclusion: A unified calculus that integrates ground instantiation, CDCL(T), and non-ground clause learning can capture and extend existing SMT and first-order reasoning methods, offering a path to more powerful and general conflict-driven solvers that leverage non-ground proofs.

Abstract: Quantifier instantiation is currently the main approach to non-ground SMT solving: solvers generate ground instances and solve the resulting ground SMT problems with CDCL(T)-style reasoning. When a conflict is found, conflict analysis learns only a ground clause, even though the conflict comes from instances of non-ground clauses. Yet non-ground reasoning can give exponentially shorter proofs than purely ground reasoning. We propose a calculus that consists of ground instantiations, CDCL(T)-style rules, and non-ground conflict analysis. The solver reasons on ground instances, but the resolution steps of conflict analysis are performed on their original non-ground clauses. This produces learned clauses that are typically more general than the ground conflict. With a suitable strategy, the learned clauses are even non-redundant. We also show how chronological backtracking can be included in SMT solving. Our calculus gives a common setting for CDCL(T)-style SMT solving, a range of instantiation-based procedures, and non-ground clause learning, and we prove that it simulates CDCL, SCL(FOL), SCL(T), and even Resolution.

</details>


### [136] [Lightweight LiDAR-Based Cone Detection Framework Using Random Forest for Formula Student Driverless](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11527)
*Márk Mező-Kerekes,Péter Praksz,Chang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Lightweight, CPU-only LiDAR perception pipeline for autonomous racing using ground removal, IMU-based motion compensation, DBSCAN clustering, and a geometric-feature Random Forest. Feature selection reduces inputs from 12 to 7. Achieves 98.33% F1 and 3.13 ms end-to-end latency on CPU; dataset and tools released as a reproducible baseline for resource-constrained teams.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for reliable, low-latency perception in Formula Student Driverless vehicles without GPU acceleration, enabling cost-effective, real-time operation and reproducibility for resource-constrained teams.

Method: A LiDAR-only perception pipeline with four main stages: (1) ground removal, (2) IMU-based motion compensation, (3) DBSCAN clustering, (4) geometric-feature extraction and Random Forest classification. Feature importance analysis reduced input features from 12 to 7. Implemented for CPU execution. Evaluated on 2,371 labeled clusters from real FSD events.

Result: The approach yields an F1-score of 98.33% and an end-to-end CPU latency of 3.13 ms. A dataset, labeling tool, and trained models are released to provide a practical baseline for other resource-constrained autonomous racing teams.

Conclusion: The work provides a practical, reproducible baseline for CPU-only perception in autonomous racing, highlighting that a carefully engineered, LiDAR-only pipeline with lightweight ML can achieve high accuracy with minimal latency while reducing input features for efficiency.

Abstract: Reliable, low-latency perception is crucial for Formula Student Driverless vehicles, yet many existing pipelines rely on deep learning and multi-sensor fusion, often requiring GPU acceleration. This paper presents a lightweight LiDAR-only perception pipeline tailored for CPU execution, combining ground removal, IMU-based motion compensation, DBSCAN clustering, and geometric feature-based Random Forest classification. Feature importance analysis reduced the model input from 12 to 7 features while preserving performance. Evaluated on 2,371 labeled clusters collected from real FSD events, the pipeline achieves an F1-score of 98.33% and an end-to-end runtime of 3.13 ms on CPU-only hardware. The released dataset, labeling tool, and trained models provide a practical and reproducible baseline for other resource-constrained autonomous racing teams.

</details>


### [137] [Prompt Revision as a Source of Cultural Bias in Text-to-Image Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11532)
*Aleksandra Urman,Elsa Lichtenegger,Salima Jaoua,Azza Bouleimen,Robin Forsberg,Corinna Hertweck,Stefania Ionescu,Nicolò Pagan,Ancsa Hannak,Joachim Baumann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WORLDVIEW audits the prompt revision layer in text-to-image systems to identify targeted cultural bias, showing revision induces stereotyping; calls for auditing deployed systems, not just models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To locate the source of cultural bias in commercial T2I systems, beyond final outputs, by examining the prompt revision layer across languages.

Method: Create WORLDVIEW: multilingual benchmark of 8,960 prompts across 15 languages and 31 language-context pairings. Analyze three systems (DALL-E-3, Imagen-4, GPT-Image-1.5) focusing on (1) context marking strength, (2) flattening into narrow vocabularies, (3) stereotype content; compare original vs revised prompts using models without revision layer.

Result: Revision layer marks non-Western, non-English contexts more heavily, flattens into narrow vocabularies applied across prompts, and yields recognizable cultural stereotypes; US context least marked. Layer is a causal source of stereotyping determined by comparing images from original vs revised prompts.

Conclusion: To identify and fix cultural bias, audit must target deployed systems, not only the underlying model.

Abstract: Commercial text-to-image systems silently revise user prompts before generating images, a step users typically cannot disable or even see. Yet, existing audits of cultural bias examine only the final images and treat generation as a single pipeline, so they cannot tell where the bias originates. We introduce WORLDVIEW, a multilingual benchmark of 8,960 prompts across 15 languages and 31 language-context pairings. Using it, we audit the revision layer in three systems (DALL-E-3, Imagen-4, GPT-Image-1.5) through a three-step analysis of how heavily it marks each cultural context, whether it flattens that context into a narrow vocabulary, and whether that vocabulary is stereotypical. Relative to a no-context English baseline, the US is the least-marked context, while non-Western and non-Anglophone contexts are marked far more heavily, flattened into narrow vocabularies applied across topically diverse prompts, and reduced to recognizable cultural stereotypes. Comparing images from original versus revised prompts on models without a revision layer, we identify the layer itself as a previously undocumented, causal source of this stereotyping. To locate cultural bias, and fix it, we must audit the system as deployed, not the model alone.

</details>


### [138] [Characterizing Job Power Elasticity for Power-Flexible AI Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11542)
*Philip Colangelo,Charles Dawson,Shayan Sengupta,Ayse Coskun,Varun Sivaram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Defines Power Flexibility Index (PFI) to quantify how LLM training throughput scales with GPU power reductions; demonstrates PFI-aware power allocation recovers significant throughput under power constraints (e.g., ~1.5k tokens/s per job at 30% power reduction, ~63% of the optimal gap).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM training consumes substantial electricity and grid constraints limit AI infrastructure growth. A normalized metric to quantify power elasticity and a control primitive for SLA-aware power flexibility can enable more efficient, grid-responsive deployment.

Method: Empirical study of 131 LLM training runs on H200 (plus validation and H100-matched data), covering dense and mixture-of-experts models, pretraining and fine-tuning, up to 32 GPUs. Introduces Power Flexibility Index (PFI) as a normalized throughput-versus-power cost metric, analyzes telemetry signals that predict PFI, and evaluates PFI-aware power allocation under power budgets.

Result: LLM training jobs exhibit substantial yet variable power elasticity; telemetry signals can predict PFI at runtime; PFI-aware allocation improves total tokens/second under power constraints, recovering ~1.5k tokens/s per job at 30% power reduction and achieving 63% of the gap to an oracle with perfect information.

Conclusion: Power elasticity is a measurable, actionable property of training workloads. PFI provides a practical primitive for SLA-aware, power- and grid-responsive AI infrastructure.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) training is among the fastest-growing sources of electricity demand in modern data centers, and power availability is a primary bottleneck to continued AI infrastructure growth. Making the power consumption of these workloads flexible could unlock additional power for AI growth, limit increases in electricity prices, and improve the utilization of existing grid infrastructure. However, to realize this flexibility, we must first understand how the performance of training workloads changes when GPU power is reduced.
  This paper presents the first systematic characterization of \emph{job power elasticity} (the sensitivity of throughput to power reductions) in LLM training. To quantify elasticity, we introduce the \emph{Power Flexibility Index (PFI)}, a normalized metric that quantifies the performance cost of power reductions and provides a control primitive for SLA-aware power flexibility.
  We collect data from 131 LLM training runs on H200 (plus 24 H200 validation runs and 34 matched H100 runs), including both dense and mixture-of-experts models, pretraining and fine-tuning tasks, and up to 32 GPUs. We find that LLM training jobs exhibit substantial but variable power elasticity, and we identify telemetry signals that predict PFI at runtime. Finally, we demonstrate that PFI-aware power allocation maximizes total tokens/second throughput under power constraints. Under a 30\% power reduction, PFI-aware power allocation recovers ~1.5k tokens/s per job, 63\% of the performance gap between an equal-weight allocation and an oracle with perfect information. Our results establish power elasticity as a measurable property of training jobs and provide a foundation for power-aware, grid-responsive AI infrastructure.

</details>


### [139] [Enabling Knowledge Graph Understanding at Scale with the EXplore Your Graphs ENgine (EXYGEN)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11569)
*Harshdeep Singh,Yurui Zhu,Giovanni Colavizza,Matteo Romanello*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EXYGEN enables scalable KG understanding through retrieval-augmented generation using VoID and ShEx, achieving non-finetuned text-to-SPARQL on SciQA; it also introduces a predicate-coverage-aware parallel sampling strategy to generate KG metadata efficiently, with large speedups on several benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address two core questions: (1) Can LLMs convert natural language questions to SPARQL using only automatically derived metadata and small graph samples without task-specific fine-tuning? (2) How can structured KG metadata be generated at scale for very large KGs without prohibitive computation?

Method: 1) Build a RAG pipeline that integrates VoID descriptions and ShEx schemas with retrieved triples and example question-query pairs; evaluate on SciQA and ablate KG-derived context; best setup includes ShEx schemas + retrieved triples + exemplars, achieving 0.419 exact-match on execution results without LLM fine-tuning. 2) Propose a predicate-coverage-aware parallel graph sampling strategy to preserve structural diversity while reducing computation; apply to OpenCitations Meta, GESIS, and ORKG to measure predicate coverage, triple loss, and runtime reductions (>80x).

Result: Text-to-SPARQL: best configuration yields 0.419 exact match on execution results without fine-tuning; lexical metrics like F1 do not predict query correctness; larger general-purpose LLMs surpass smaller code-specialized models given adequate context. Metadata generation: sampling preserves predicate coverage with minimal triple loss and dramatically reduces runtime (>80x) and makes complete metadata retrieval feasible for ORKG.

Conclusion: Structured schema context and light prompting can substantially reduce fine-tuning needs for scalable KG conversational access, though closing the remaining gap to fully fine-tuned methods will likely require synthetic question-query exemplars or execution-feedback loops and broader validation beyond a single benchmark.

Abstract: We present EXYGEN (EXplore Your Graphs ENgine), a framework for knowledge graph (KG) understanding that enables conversational access to KGs at scale. We address two questions in sequence. First, how effectively can LLMs perform text-to-SPARQL generation given only automatically derived structured metadata and small graph samples, rather than task-specific fine-tuning? We integrate VoID descriptions and ShEx schemas into a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline and ablate KG-derived context on the SciQA benchmark. Our best configuration -- combining ShEx schemas, retrieved triples, and example question-query pairs -- reaches an exact match of 0.419 on execution results without any LLM fine-tuning. We further find that lexical metrics such as F1 poorly predict query correctness, and that larger general-purpose LLMs can outperform smaller code-specialized ones once given sufficient context. Second, we ask how to generate the structured metadata that this method relies on from very large KGs, where KG metadata generation becomes computationally intractable. We introduce a predicate-coverage-aware parallel graph sampling strategy that preserves structural diversity while remaining computationally tractable. On OpenCitations Meta and GESIS, it retains high predicate coverage with minimal triple loss and reduces runtime by over 80x; on ORKG, sampling is not just faster but the only tractable path to obtain complete metadata. Together, these results show that structured schema context and lightweight prompting can substantially reduce reliance on fine-tuning for scalable conversational access to KGs, though closing the remaining gap to fully fine-tuned approaches will likely require reducing dependence on curated question-query exemplars -- whether through synthetic generation or an execution-feedback-driven approach -- and validating these findings beyond a single benchmark.

</details>


### [140] [Making Alternative Data Work: Context-Augmented LLMs for Financial Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11607)
*Jihoon Kwon,Lawrence Liu,Daekyung Park,Sumin Kim,Haverty Jack,Hoyoung Lee,Katherine Bjorkman,Josh McKenney,Peter Laurelli,Nicole Kagan,Zach Golkhou,Thorsten Neumann,Edward Tong,Pete Petersen,Yoon Kim,Alejandro Lopez-Lira,Yongjae Lee,Chanyeol Choi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs using in-context learning can effectively fuse heterogeneous alternative data with traditional financial data to forecast firm revenue, outperforming single-source forecasts and standard baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Alternative data offer timely, firm-level signals not captured by traditional sources but are often fragmented and sparsely historical, making them hard to integrate with conventional models. LLMs potentially overcome this by learning from examples and instructions in-context, enabling flexible, non-parameter-tuned integration across data channels.

Method: Propose a two-agent framework: (1) channel-firm informativeness identification to select informative data channels per firm, (2) firm- and channel-contextual revenue forecasting via an LLM that combines alternative data with financial information without parameter updates. Evaluation across four commercial alternative data channels, comparing against baselines.

Result: In-context integration of alternative data with other financial information improves forecasting relative to either source alone and yields more accurate forecasts than standard forecasting baselines.

Conclusion: LLMs provide a flexible, practical approach to integrating heterogeneous alternative data with financial information for forecasting, by selectively leveraging informative channels per firm in an in-context learning framework.

Abstract: When forecasting a firm's future financial performance, alternative data - data collected from non-traditional sources such as consumer transactions, web traffic, and prediction markets - can provide timely signals about firms' operating activities and broader market conditions. These signals may reveal information that is not captured by traditional public sources and can therefore provide complementary information for forecasting firms' future financial performance. However, firm-level alternative data often have limited historical coverage, are relevant only to specific prediction targets or subsets of firms, and are distributed across numerous heterogeneous channels, making them difficult to incorporate flexibly into conventional forecasting approaches. Meanwhile, large language models (LLMs) can interpret instructions, learn from in-context examples, and generate predictions by combining heterogeneous information without task-specific parameter updates. Motivated by this potential flexibility, we investigate whether an LLM can forecast firm performance by integrating alternative data with other financial information through in-context learning. We propose a two-agent framework that first identifies the firms for which each alternative data channel is likely to be informative and then predicts revenue using firm- and channel-specific context. We evaluate the framework across four commercial alternative data channels. In our experiments, adding alternative data in context alongside other financial information improves the LLM's forecasting relative to either source alone, and these forecasts are more accurate than those of standard forecasting baselines. These findings suggest that LLMs provide a flexible and practical approach to integrating alternative data with heterogeneous financial information.

</details>


### [141] [Distributed Optimization of Modular Production Systems using Model-based Reinforcement Learning with Inverse Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11615)
*Andreas Schwung,Steve Yuwono,Sofiene Lassoued,Dorothea Schwung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A model-based RL framework that learns approximate inverse process models to decouple actuation dynamics from state dynamics, enabling task-space RL training; applied to modular manufacturing with heterogeneous modules; shows faster training and improved performance, especially for off-policy methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address data inefficiency and coupling between actuation and state dynamics in highly flexible modular manufacturing systems; enable efficient RL-based self-learning control by projecting learning into task space through inverse dynamics.

Method: Introduce lightweight feedforward approximate inverse models trained within the RL policy. Integrate these models into the policy network of standard RL algorithms to disentangle actuation and state dynamics, resulting in training in task space.

Result: Validation on a laboratory modular production testbed with heterogeneous modules. Demonstrates efficiency improvements in performance and training speed, particularly for off-policy algorithms.

Conclusion: The proposed inverse-model-augmented model-based RL framework reduces sample complexity and enhances data efficiency for modular manufacturing control, with potential generalization to other modular, heterogeneous systems.

Abstract: This paper presents a novel approach for data-driven self-learning control of highly flexible, modular manufacturing systems. Specifically, we employ a novel framework for model-based reinforcement learning which introduces approximate inverse process models within the training of reinforcement policies. This approach disentangles the learning of actuation dynamics and the dynamics in state space, resulting in RL-based training solely within the task space. We propose a lightweight feedforward architecture for approximate inverse models and integrate them within the policy network of standard RL algorithms. We apply the approach to a laboratory modular production testbed with heterogeneous production modules. The results underline the efficiency improvements for modular manufacturing units in terms of both performance and training speed, particularly for off-policy algorithms.

</details>


### [142] [MAPLE: Memory-Augmented Planning with Language and Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11636)
*Kesheng Chen,Yamin Hu,Wenjian Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAPLE is a memory-augmented planner that maintains optimization programs and search state across natural-language requests, enabling rapid, coherent updates to dynamic optimization problems; it achieves strong online performance on NLDO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic operations require frequent updates to data, constraints, and objectives; existing LLM-based optimization approaches handle isolated requests and discard prior context, limiting adaptability.

Method: MAPLE stores executable optimization state (problem instance, accepted plans, updates, and candidate solutions) and combines language-based problem construction with mathematical programming and evolutionary search; evaluated on NLDO benchmark (15 trajectories, 180 updates).

Result: MAPLE completes all trajectories; online scalar quality 0.951; Pareto hypervolume ratio 0.875; controlled comparisons show that maintaining executable state improves update validity and preserves useful search information across substantial revisions.

Conclusion: Memory-augmented optimization with language and evolution enables robust adaptation to changing requirements, preserving prior work and search progress to support successive NL requests.

Abstract: Domain practitioners understand their business constraints but may lack operations-research expertise or dedicated support. LLM-based optimization agents translate natural-language requirements into models or solver programs that established optimization tools can execute. This progress makes optimization more accessible, but real-world operations are dynamic: changing demand, resources, and priorities require updates to data, constraints, and objectives. Methods centered on isolated requests offer limited support for rapid adaptation that preserves earlier decisions and reuses useful search results. We introduce MAPLE (Memory-Augmented Planning with Language and Evolution), an agent for maintaining optimization problems through successive natural-language requests. MAPLE combines language-based problem construction with mathematical programming and evolutionary search. It retains the optimization program, accepted plans, earlier updates, and candidate solutions for subsequent requests. We introduce NLDO, a benchmark of 15 trajectories and 180 updates spanning selection, scheduling, rostering, routing, and cloud-resource placement. In the main evaluation, MAPLE completes all trajectories and achieves online scalar quality of 0.951 and a Pareto hypervolume ratio of 0.875. Controlled comparisons further show that maintaining executable state improves update validity and can preserve useful search information across substantial revisions.

</details>


### [143] [Autonomy, Social Norms, and Alignment: Towards a Developmental Framework for Autonomous Artificial Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11660)
*Marica Notte,Ludovica Marinucci,Vieri Giuliano Santucci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes an epistemologically grounded, experiential path to AI alignment via intrinsic motivation and regulatory sandboxes, framing moral agency as gradually attributed and alignment as a developmental process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between data-driven pre-trained agents and robust alignment in dynamic environments by enabling experiential learning, curiosity-driven exploration, and gradual norm formation.

Method: A conceptual framework that integrates intrinsic motivation, epistemology of norms, Dennett’s gradual attribution of moral agency, and regulatory sandboxes as pedagogical environments for AI alignment.

Result: A high-level blueprint for aligning adaptive agents through experience and cooperation; no empirical results, but a roadmap with design implications and evaluation questions.

Conclusion: Alignment should emerge from experience and social interaction within progressively complex environments; regulatory sandboxes serve as structured settings to cultivate alignment in a safe, developmental manner.

Abstract: In recent years, artificial intelligence has made extraordinary progress thanks to large-scale models capable of generalization and the generation of complex outputs. However, transferring this potential into embodied agents reveals a significant limitation: the most advanced systems rely on pre-existing datasets and human feedback strategies that are powerful but insufficient in dynamic or unknown contexts. To adapt, an agent must acquire knowledge through direct interaction with its environment. One strategy to address this challenge involves introducing higher-level mechanisms, such as intrinsic motivations, which leverage curiosity and competence, to guide exploration and learning in complex environments. While this flexibility expands autonomy, it complicates the task of ensuring agents remain aligned with human goals. Alignment, already a challenge for artificial systems in general, becomes even more complex in unstructured and dynamic contexts where predefined rules prove insufficient. To be effective and adaptable, norms must be rooted in experience through an epistemological process that starting from simple, situated principles allows for the gradual construction of more complex rules through experience, autonomous learning, and cooperation with other moral agents. Similarly to children learning social norms by exploring their environment and participating in collective practices, artificial agents must also be educated toward alignment. Following Dennett, the status of a moral agent is not innate but is attributed gradually based on the ability to responsibly manage increasing degrees of freedom. From this perspective, the regulatory sandboxes can be viewed as pedagogical environments for AI: dynamic spaces where alignment develops as a formative process, progressively shaping autonomous behaviors through interaction and cooperation in scenarios of increasing complexity.

</details>


### [144] [Geospatial AI, Dataverse Metadata, and the Study of Place-Based Government](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11674)
*Danny EBanks,Devika Jain*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A large knowledge graph (215,985 nodes, 528,003 edges) is built from 102,650 Harvard Dataverse datasets to structure geospatial metadata and enable policy-relevant discovery; reveals place-resolution challenges and a bias toward American, city-level data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geographic information is currently free-text and not queryable; a structured knowledge graph can enable AI-driven metadata enrichment and more effective discovery across policy domains.

Method: Construct a knowledge graph from Harvard Dataverse public data and metadata; connect datasets to keywords, publications, subjects, journals, and locations; identify geospatial fields and bounding boxes; use conservative keyword search to identify policy-relevant geospatial datasets; analyze connected components; illustrate use cases with language models, stance detection, and geographic aggregation.

Result: Found that 96.9% of nodes are in a single connected component; 43,991 datasets carry geospatial metadata; 7,654 geospatial datasets (17.4% of geospatial ones) tagged as policy-relevant; elections/legislatures form the largest cluster, followed by government administration, health policy, transportation, education; five datasets illustrate cross-domain / spatial-scale metadata; extended use-case demonstrates attaching discourse to place; central obstacle is place resolution (same location appears as many disconnected nodes); data coverage skew toward American, city-level data.

Conclusion: The graph provides a concrete setting for AI-driven metadata enrichment and entity resolution; highlights the need to address place-resolution and standardize geospatial metadata; acknowledges coverage bias and potential uses for policy-relevant analysis.

Abstract: Harvard Dataverse hosts over 150,000 research datasets, but the geographic information those datasets carry is entered as free text by depositors and has never been assembled into a searchable structure. We construct a knowledge graph from the repository's public data and metadata, organizing 102,650 datasets within a 215,985-node network of 528,003 edges linking datasets to keywords, publications, subjects, journals, and locations. Of those datasets, 43,991 (42.9 percent) carry at least one geospatial field, geographic coverage, geographic unit, or a bounding box and 96.9 percent of all nodes sit in a single connected component, so datasets remain reachable from one another even when their geospatial metadata share nothing in common. A conservative keyword search identifies 7,654 geospatially tagged datasets (17.4 percent) as directly policy-relevant, with elections and legislatures the largest cluster, followed by government administration, health policy, transportation, and education. Five datasets illustrate how this metadata behaves across policy domains and spatial scales, and an extended use case shows how community language models, stance detection with geographic aggregation, and partisan language bridging tools can attach discourse to place. The central obstacle is place resolution: the same location appears as many disconnected nodes. We argue that the graph provides a concrete setting for developing AI-driven metadata enrichment and entity resolution, and we document its coverage skew toward American, city-level data.

</details>


### [145] [COBRA-Skills: Contextual Bandit-Guided Evolution for Agent Skill Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11682)
*Pingchen Lu,Xiangyi Wang,Xiang Li,Jie Mao,Zikun Qu,Junfeng Luo,Yao Shu,Bryan Kian Hsiang Low,Zhongxiang Dai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COBRA-Skills introduces a budgeted, evolving skill-optimization framework for LLM agents that uses contextual-bandit prioritization and evidence-grounded updates to efficiently expand a reusable skill set, achieving state-of-the-art averages with 55-58% cost savings and only 50 optimization examples per benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents benefit from reusable skills distilled from past tasks, but existing optimization methods incur high execution costs and large data requirements. There is a need for a sample-efficient, robust skill-optimization framework that adapts dynamically as candidate skills evolve.

Method: Formulate skill optimization as budgeted sequential optimization over a dynamically evolving candidate space. Use contextual-bandit-guided prioritization to select evaluations for promising or informative candidates, while continuously refining the skill population from execution feedback (evidence-grounded evolution). Evaluate across six heterogeneous agent benchmarks and three target models, achieving strong average performance with only 50 optimization examples per benchmark and 55–58% cost reductions relative to SkillOpt.

Result: COBRA-Skills consistently achieves the strongest average performance among compared methods across the six benchmarks and three target models, with a 55–58% reduction in optimization cost versus SkillOpt, using only 50 unique optimization examples per benchmark. Analyses show robustness to changes in the agent harness and effectiveness even when the target model itself generates and refines skills.

Conclusion: The framework offers a data-efficient, robust approach to skill optimization for LLM agents, combining selective exploration with evidence-based skill evolution. It demonstrates strong cross-domain performance and may generalize across models, though further work could probe hyperparameter sensitivity, candidate-space dynamics, and transfer to tasks with different evaluation signals.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents can benefit from reusable skills distilled from prior task experience, yet existing skill optimization methods often rely on costly execution-based evaluation and substantial task data. We introduce \textbf{COBRA-Skills}, an efficient framework that formulates skill optimization as budgeted sequential optimization over a dynamically evolving candidate space. COBRA-Skills couples contextual-bandit-guided prioritization with evidence-grounded skill evolution, selectively allocating evaluations to promising or informative candidates while continually refining the skill population from execution feedback. Across six heterogeneous agent benchmarks and three target models, COBRA-Skills consistently achieves the strongest average performance among compared methods, while reducing optimization cost by 55--58\% relative to SkillOpt and using only 50 unique optimization examples per benchmark. Further analyses show that COBRA-Skills remains robust to changes in the agent harness and performs effectively when the target model itself is used for skill generation and refinement.

</details>


### [146] [When Agents Disagree: Bayesian Backward Reasoning as a Label-Free Anchor for Multi-Agent Collective Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11709)
*Ken Chen,Wei Wang,Sachith Seneviratne,Hansani Weeratunge,Saman Halgamuge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Bayesian backward reasoning yields reverse posteriors for ensemble decision-making; cross-path consistency via Jensen–Shannon divergence ranks agents and drives three fusion strategies (MinJS, FwdJS, LogLin); improves aggregation across five LLM backbones on DDXPlus; reverse anchors complement forward posteriors and enable calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When multiple LLM agents give conflicting answers, forward-only aggregation can propagate shared errors due to correlated evidence. A reverse (backward) posterior offers an alternative, differently factorized view to mitigate correlated errors and improve ensemble decisions.

Method: Construct a Bayesian backward reasoning (reverse posterior) from an explicit likelihood for each instance. Compare forward and reverse posteriors, then use Jensen–Shannon divergence to measure cross-path consistency and rank agents. Implement three strategies: hard selection (MinJS), soft reweighting (FwdJS), and log-linear fusion (LogLin). Evaluate on DDXPlus with five LLM backbones.

Result: Across five backbones on DDXPlus, MinJS consistently beats random selection; FwdJS generally improves over the strongest baseline; LogLin yields the best performance, with the largest gains in subsets where agents disagree.

Conclusion: Reverse posterior acts as a useful anchor that complements forward posteriors for collective decision-making. Cross-path consistency signals improve aggregation. If labeled data are available, a lightweight two-stage calibration can further refine the reverse anchor and improve performance.

Abstract: When multiple LLM agents yield conflicting answers, the decision-making process dictates whether agent diversity improves performance or merely compounds shared errors. Existing collective decision-making methods, including voting, electoral rules, and LLM judges, rely on forward reasoning: they map evidence to labels in one direction. Although these methods can combine diverse forward traces, they still aggregate estimates that share this evidence-to-label factorization and can inherit correlated errors within the forward pool. We therefore construct a reverse posterior for each instance through Bayesian backward reasoning from an explicit likelihood. The forward and reverse posteriors provide differently factorized approximations of the underlying posterior. Because estimates from different factorizations may tend to share the same error less often, we use Jensen-Shannon divergence to rank agents by cross-path consistency. This cross-path consistency signal underlies three strategies: hard selection (MinJS), soft reweighting (FwdJS), and log-linear fusion (LogLin). Evaluated on DDXPlus across five LLM backbones, our proposed strategies show consistent improvements: MinJS outperforms random selection across all backbones, FwdJS generally improves over the strongest baseline, and LogLin achieves the best performance among the evaluated methods, with its largest gains on the subset where the agents disagree. Despite its weaker standalone accuracy, the reverse posterior serves as a more useful anchor than forward-only alternatives, providing complementary information for collective decision-making. When labeled data are available, a lightweight two-stage calibration can further refine the reverse anchor and improve aggregation performance.

</details>


### [147] [SIRF: A Spec-Internalized Risk Foundation Model for Industrial Content Risk Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11752)
*Suwan Wu,Yumeng Lin,Pengcheng Yuan,Xiaolong Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SIRF internalizes platform policies into model weights via continued pretraining to achieve ultra-low-latency, high-precision risk adjudication, outperforming a policy-naive baseline on Black Recall@P95 while using modest CPT data; deployed as a tree-model adjudication layer with reduced mis-penalization under freezing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address industrial content risk control deployment constraints where latency and precision dominate, while minimizing annotation effort and maintaining general capability.

Method: Integrate policy-grounded reasoning into weights through EntiGraph, MAGA rewriting, and account-level CoT during CPT; compare SIRF-8B-SFT to Qwen3-8B-SFT with identical policy injection; evaluate using Black Recall@P95 and mis-penalization metrics; deploy as a 20% more effective adjudication layer and enable freezing with cost savings.

Result: SIRF-8B-SFT achieves 71.3% Black Recall@P95, +15.1pp over baseline, using ~70M CPT tokens; matches or exceeds larger models among logprob-available interfaces; 20% more mis-penalized samples recovered; freezing reduces mis-penalization by ~70% relative.

Conclusion: Policy internalization through CPT yields targeted risk-control gains with modest data and low latency, without sacrificing general capabilities; gains stem from internalized policies rather than model scale, enabling practical deployment in verdict-only settings.

Abstract: For industrial content risk control, the real deployment constraint is not average accuracy but how much risk can be auto-handled under high precision and second-level latency. We present SIRF (Spec-Internalized Risk Foundation Model), which internalizes a platform's complex policies, synthesized without additional human annotation via EntiGraph, MAGA rewriting and account-level chain-of-thought (CoT), into the weights via continued pretraining (CPT), so rules are applied at high precision under an ultra-low-latency, verdict-only deployment. A controlled same-source comparison (Qwen3-8B-SFT vs. SIRF-8B-SFT, identical policy injection and verdict-only output form, differing only in policy-grounded CPT) attributes the gain to internalization: SIRF-8B-SFT reaches 71.3% Black Recall@P95, +15.1pp over the baseline, using only ~70M CPT tokens without harming general ability, and among included, logprob-available models under this interface it matches or exceeds far larger systems. SIRF is deployed as a tree-model adjudication layer (20% more mis-penalized samples recovered) and transfers to a freezing scenario at low cost (~70% relative mis-penalization reduction).

</details>


### [148] [A Unified Per-Token Gating Family for On-Policy Distillation: FKL/RKL Mixing with Multi-Channel and Bias Coefficients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11768)
*Suwan Wu,Yumeng Lin,Pengcheng Yuan,Xiaolong Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces a four-coefficient gating parameterization for per-token KL shaping in on-policy knowledge distillation (OPD); unifies EOPD and ToDi as 1D limits, enabling multi-channel gating with explicit bias; shows improved accuracy across tasks and proposes this parameterization as a shared coordinate system for comparing gating designs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing on-policy distillation methods fix a single gating signal and direction, making direct comparison difficult and potentially limiting performance. A flexible, multi-channel parameterization can unify prior approaches and explore richer gating strategies.

Method: Propose lambda_t = sigma(a * h_t + b * u(x) + c + d * gap_t), where h_t, u(x), gap_t are proxies for model signals and sigma is a nonlinearity. Demonstrate that EOPD and ToDi arise as 1D restrictions within this four-coefficient framework. Evaluate across TweetEval emotion/hate tasks with Qwen3-32B teacher and Qwen3-4B student, comparing full four-parameter configurations to matched-magnitude single-channel (entropy-only / gap-only) baselines; conduct a 26-cell mean-match isolation experiment and targeted three-seed replications across multiple tasks including offensive content.

Result: Full 4-parameter configurations reach higher accuracy than 1D single-channel restrictions in 33 of 36 comparable cells. In 19 of 26 cells, dynamic gating precedes effective-KL-matched static baselines. Replications are directionally consistent but not individually significant at n=3. Results are presented as exploratory directional evidence.

Conclusion: The four-coefficient parameterization serves as a shared coordinate system for comparing per-token gating designs in short-output OPD, expanding the design space beyond single-channel gating and offering directional improvements, though statistical significance requires larger samples.

Abstract: Per-token gating of forward/reverse KL losses has become a standard technique for on-policy knowledge distillation (OPD), but existing methods such as EOPD (Jin et al., 2026) and ToDi (Jung et al., 2025) each fix a single gating signal and a single gating direction, and the two have never been compared directly. We introduce a four-coefficient parameterization lambda_t = sigma(a * h_t + b * u(x) + c + d * gap_t) in which direction-aligned proxies of EOPD and ToDi appear as one-dimensional (1D) restrictions, and which adds multi-channel composition and an explicit bias as further degrees of freedom. On TweetEval (Barbieri et al., 2020) emotion and hate, with a Qwen3-32B teacher and a Qwen3-4B student, configurations in the full family reach higher accuracy than the matched-magnitude single-channel (entropy-only / gap-only) 1D restrictions in 33 of 36 comparable cells, and a 26-cell mean-match isolation experiment places dynamic gating ahead of effective-KL-matched static baselines in 19 of 26 cells. Because cells share training data, models, and parameter substructure, we report both counts as exploratory aggregate directional evidence rather than as independent hypothesis tests. Targeted three-seed paired replications of the nine headline comparisons singled out by that sweep -- including a third task, offensive -- are directionally consistent, but individually smaller than the single-seed estimates and not significant at n=3. We therefore present the parameterization primarily as a shared coordinate system for comparing per-token gating designs in short-output classification OPD.

</details>


### [149] [From Parameters to Answers: How LLMs Retrieve and Use Their Internal Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11859)
*Wenkang Wei,Yuan Fang,Renhe Jiang,Hong Cheng,Xingtong Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Layerwise interventions reveal distinct routing and content dependencies across models (Qwen, Llama, Gemma) and question types; pair-conditioned vs. global request directions show model- and layer-specific dynamics and a late-stage influence of routing cues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how language models balance query-routing information and fitted knowledge during answer generation, and how these dependencies unfold across layers and architectures.

Method: End-of-question hidden-state interventions applied layerwise on three models (Qwen, Llama, Gemma); compare country-continent questions with noun/adjective/code answers; define pair-conditioned and global request directions; test content availability with separate selection candidates; perform diagnostic reanalysis of frozen Qwen states.

Result: Pair-conditioned routing strengthens before interventions alter later fitted knowledge, with the causal window opening while answer-supporting content is still forming. Gemma exhibits a partially overlapping mid-layer routing-content profile; Llama shows no sustained routing-effect window under the same gates. Global request dependence decreases from early to later layers while content dependence persists. In Qwen, the pair-conditioned direction retains a late effect, suggesting that the handoff concerns the global fitted direction rather than all request information. These findings separate early readability, natural strength, causal steering, and later content dependence.

Conclusion: Layerwise analysis disentangles readability, steerability, and content reliance, revealing model- and layer-specific routing-content dynamics and guiding interpretation of how queries and content influence answers.

Abstract: How does a language model's dependence on query-routing information and target knowledge change as it answers a question? We study this question through layerwise interventions on the hidden state at the end of the question. Across Qwen, Llama, and Gemma, we compare country-continent questions with noun, adjective, and code answers while keeping several fitted measurements distinct. A pair-conditioned request direction describes which country is queried in natural single-country questions; a global request direction describes first- versus second-country requests in paired questions; separate selection candidates test control among contents already available in the hidden state. A diagnostic reanalysis of frozen Qwen natural-question states shows that the pair-conditioned direction grows stronger before interventions on it begin to alter later fitted knowledge, with this causal window opening while answer-supporting content is still forming. The paired three-model trajectories are not uniform: Gemma shows a partially overlapping mid-layer routing-content profile, whereas Llama has no sustained routing-effect window under the same gates.
