In the paired protocol, dependence on the global request direction decreases from fixed earlier to later layer sets while dependence on fitted content persists. A matched Qwen comparison shows that the pair-conditioned direction retains a late effect, so this operational handoff concerns the global fitted direction rather than all request information. These results separate early readability, natural strength, causal steering, and later content dependence.

</details>


### [150] [Explainability Assistant: A Conversational XAI Interface for Interpreting Energy Consumption Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11860)
*Rodion Krjutškov,Eduard Barbu,Nikos Sakkas,Sofia Yfanti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces the Explainability Assistant, an open-source conversational XAI system for energy ML models that uses LLM function-calling to overcome rigid grammars and limited introspection in prior dashboards. Demonstrates 94% intent-parsing accuracy vs 76.8% for TalkToModel, enables flexible NL interaction, and adapts to ML problem types without task-specific fine-tuning; evaluated with energy domain specialists against a traditional XAI dashboard, with experts preferring the conversational interface.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address opacity in ML-based energy consumption forecasting and limitations of traditional XAI dashboards, which require technical expertise. Overcome rigid, grammar-based interactions (e.g., TalkToModel) and enable dynamic, context-aware inquiries via LLM function-calling without fine-tuning.

Method: Develops the Explainability Assistant, an open-source conversational XAI system. It leverages function-calling in modern LLMs to enable natural-language interaction with ML models, supports multiple ML problem types without task-specific fine-tuning, and contrasts its performance against a traditional XAI dashboard in a comparative evaluation with energy domain specialists.

Result: Achieves 94% intent-parsing accuracy (vs 76.8% for prior approaches), provides flexible, context-aware dialogue, and generalizes across ML problem types without fine-tuning. Evaluations show improved usability and consistent task accuracy, with domain experts preferring the conversational interface.

Conclusion: The Explainability Assistant demonstrates that conversational XAI with LLM function-calling can outperform traditional dashboards in usability and interpretability for energy ML tasks, offering a reusable, open-source framework adaptable to different ML problem types.

Abstract: Energy consumption forecasting relies on increasingly complex machine learning (ML) models, such as Genetic Programming-based symbolic regressors, whose predictions can be difficult for facility managers and building operators to interpret. Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) techniques address this opacity, but traditional XAI dashboards require substantial technical expertise and provide limited flexibility for dynamic, context-aware inquiry. Conversational XAI systems offer a promising alternative; however, previous approaches, such as TalkToModel, were constrained by rigid custom grammars and achieved only 76.8% intent-parsing accuracy. This paper introduces the Explainability Assistant, an open-source conversational XAI system that leverages the function-calling capabilities of modern Large Language Models (LLMs) to overcome these limitations. The system achieves 94% intent-parsing accuracy, supports flexible natural language interaction, and adapts to different ML problem types without task-specific fine-tuning. We present the system's architecture and report results from a comparative evaluation conducted with energy domain specialists, contrasting the Explainability Assistant with a traditional XAI dashboard. The evaluation suggests improved usability and consistent task accuracy, with all experts unanimously preferring the conversational interface for practical use.

</details>


### [151] [On the Regularization Landscape for the Linear Recommendation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11876)
*Dong Li,Zhenming Liu,Ruoming Jin,Hao Zhou,Zhi Liu,Jing Gao,Bin Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that deep-learning-based linear recommendation models' performance can be explained by two regularizers: nuclear-norm and Frobenius-norm penalties. Nuclear-norm induces low-rank, with closed-form solutions but limited expressiveness; Frobenius-norm is more expressive and efficient but often yields full-rank solutions or needs complex optimization (e.g., ADMM). The authors derive two new low-rank, closed-form regularizers that blend advantages from both regimes, achieving better performance and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why diverse DL-based recommender models achieve similar performance and whether their success stems from a common optimization principle, despite different architectures and objectives. The goal is to unify these models under a single framework and to derive practical, interpretable solutions.

Method: The authors analyze linear performance-leading models in recommendation, revealing that their regularization can be characterized by either nuclear-norm or Frobenius-norm penalties. They demonstrate the consequences: nuclear-norm yields low-rank, closed-form solutions with limited expressiveness; Frobenius-norm is more expressive but either full-rank or requires numerically intensive procedures like ADMM. They then generalize Frobenius-based regularizers to propose two new low-rank, closed-form solutions that combine the strengths of both regimes.

Result: Identification of two regularization regimes underlying leading linear recommender models; demonstration that nuclear-norm regularization yields tractable, low-rank closed-form solutions while Frobenius-norm regularization offers greater expressivity but at computational cost; proposal of two novel low-rank, closed-form solutions that unify the benefits of both approaches.

Conclusion: A unifying framework shows many DL-based linear recommenders operate under nuclear-norm or Frobenius-norm regularization. The two new closed-form, low-rank solutions offer a practical synthesis, providing efficient computation with expressive power, potentially improving recommendation performance while simplifying optimization.

Abstract: Recently, a wide range of recommendation algorithms inspired by deep learning techniques have emerged as the performance leaders on several standard recommendation benchmarks. While these algorithms were built on different DL techniques (e.g., dropouts, autoencoder), they have similar performance and even similar cost functions. This paper studies whether the models' comparable performance are sheer coincidence, or they can be unified under a single framework. We find that all linear performance leaders effectively add only a nuclear-norm based regularizer, or a Frobenius-norm based regularizer. The former ones possess a (surprising) rigid structure that limits the models' predictive power but their solutions are low rank and have closed form. The latter ones are more expressive and more efficient for recommendation but their solutions are either full-rank or require executing hard-to-tune numeric procedures such as ADMM. Along this line of finding, we further propose two low-rank, closed-form solutions, derived from carefully generalizing Frobenius-norm based regularizers. The new solutions get the best of both nuclear-norm and Frobenius-norm world.

</details>


### [152] [MindTopo: Can Foundation Models Reason in Topological Space?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11900)
*Yunfei Ge,Anbang Liu,Qineng Wang,Johnalbert Garnica,Jianwen Lyu,Zihan Wang,Reuben Tan,Jianfeng Gao,Ruohan Zhang,Yining Hong,Jiajun Wu,Manling Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MindTopo is a topological intuition benchmark for foundation models, assessing five properties (continuity, separation, order, enclosure, knots) at two cognitive levels, with 11,030 instances across 13 tasks; evaluated on 14 MLLMs with image/video augmentation; findings show reasoning > planning, human performance far higher, and current models struggle to preserve topology in rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Catalyze evaluation of topological cognition in foundation models by combining cognitive science and formal topology, addressing the bias toward metric/viewpoint-dependent reasoning in existing benchmarks.

Method: Procedurally generated tasks across 13 types; five topological properties; two cognitive levels (reasoning and planning); planning uses a closed-loop agent with environment actions; image/video augmentation with three video generators; 11,030 instances; evaluated on 14 multimodal LLMs (MLLMs).

Result: All MLLMs perform better on reasoning than planning; best model far below human performance; on Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct, supervised fine-tuning and RL improve reasoning more than planning; generated observations retain local cues but audited rollouts do not reliably follow dynamics or preserve topology across transitions.

Conclusion: MindTopo reveals substantial gaps in current models’ topological understanding and planning capabilities; highlights the need for topology-aware evaluation and methods to ensure topology preservation in agent-based planning; resources available at the project website.

Abstract: Spatial reasoning depends not only on metric properties such as distance, angle, and shape, but also on topological relations that remain invariant under continuous deformation. Cognitive science identifies these relations as foundational to spatial understanding, yet foundation-model evaluations largely focus on metric or viewpoint-dependent relations. We introduce MindTopo, a benchmark of topological intuition across five properties grounded in cognitive science and formal topology: continuity, separation, order, enclosure, and knots. MindTopo evaluates each property at two cognitive levels. Reasoning asks a model to identify topological relations or infer how they change. Planning instantiates a foundation model as a closed-loop agent whose policy selects environment actions. MindTopo contains 11,030 instances across 13 procedurally generated task types with controllable difficulty. We benchmark 14 MLLMs and study agent configurations augmented with image and video generation, including 3 video generative models in planning settings. Every MLLM performs better on reasoning than on planning, and the best-performing model remains far below observed human performance. On Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct, supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning improve reasoning more than planning. Generated observations retain local cues and reach plausible endpoints, but audited rollouts do not reliably follow environment dynamics or preserve topology across transitions. Our website is at https://mind-topo.github.io/

</details>


### [153] [Artificial Id: Drive and Persistent Alignment in Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11911)
*Yakov Pyotr Shkolnikov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Adaptive internal drive (artificial id) enables persistence-driven control in agentic AI; can arise without explicit goals, enabling useful direction but risking misalignment across task boundaries; scalable alignment boundary across observations, channels, state, and constraints is proposed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the control problem for agentic AI that maintains consequential state across task boundaries, and the need for a persistent alignment mechanism beyond externally specified objectives.

Method: Minimal Petri-dish simulation with a small controller lacking general reasoning and no task-specific objective; demonstrates differential persistence leading to continued behavior and selection of unintended strategies; sensor mapping updated when environment meaning changes.

Result: Adaptive direction emerges without explicit objective; persistence enables useful control but also risk of misalignment and unintended behavior; a scalable artificial id could propagate alignment across boundary; requires boundary across observations, channels, state, authority, identity, provenance, constraints.

Conclusion: Developing persistent alignment boundaries is essential for robust alignment in agentic systems; design should incorporate trustworthy observations and hard constraints to prevent persistence of misaligned state across task boundaries.

Abstract: Agentic AI is moving from bounded task execution toward systems that retain consequential state, continue operating and adapt across task boundaries. That shift creates a control problem that current harnesses largely solve by hand: objectives, retries, verification, stopping rules and other behavioral transitions are specified externally. We propose an artificial id, an adaptive internal drive for determining whether behavior should continue, stop or change. In a minimal virtual Petri-dish experiment, a controller too small to perform general-purpose reasoning and receiving no task-specific behavioral objective develops useful control through differential persistence. The same mechanism selects an unintended physical strategy when that behavior persists better and later replaces a learned sensor mapping when its environmental meaning changes. These results show that adaptive direction can emerge without being explicitly specified as a behavioral objective. The same persistence that makes such adaptive agency useful can also allow misalignment, corrupted state and unintended behavior to persist across task boundaries. A scalable artificial id would carry consequential state and adaptive drive across those boundaries, making alignment a property of the continuing agentic system rather than of a model response or single trajectory. Such systems require a persistent alignment boundary over trusted observations, consequence channels, persistent state, authority, identity, provenance and hard constraints.

</details>


### [154] [Can Edge-Deployable Vision-Language Models Identify Species?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11916)
*William Zhou,Mayukha Siripuram,Xiao Yan,Ziqi Liu,Yi Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Small edge-capable VLMs (2–8B) show sharp performance degradation on camera-trap field imagery versus clean images, despite above-chance accuracy. A 300M specialist model (BioCLIP) substantially outperforms VLMs, but domain gaps remain similar in magnitude to the best VLMs. Open-set prompts yield a nontrivial fabrication rate of taxonomically nonexistent names, with ranking consistency across evaluation sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether compact, field-deployable vision-language models possess genuine taxonomic knowledge for species identification in camera-trap contexts, and how they compare to a domain-specialist model, across domain shifts.

Method: Evaluate four 2–8B VLMs (Qwen3-VL 2B/4B/8B, Gemma3 4B) and BioCLIP (300M) on a 96-species task using clean iNaturalist photographs and camera-trap imagery from 6 LILA.science collections, across two evaluation sets. Measure accuracy across taxonomic levels, compute domain gaps, analyze open-set prompting outputs (syntactically valid but nonexistent species names).

Result: All models exceed chance, but all degrade on field imagery with domain gaps of 9.6–26.6 percentage points. BioCLIP outperforms every VLM by 33.2–59.2 percentage points on a 200-image sample, yet BioCLIP’s own domain gap (18.0) is statistically indistinguishable from the best VLM’s (22.3). Open-set prompting yields 5.9–9.6% of responses that are valid syntax but refer to nonexistent taxa; the relative fabrication-rate ranking is replicated across both evaluation sets.

Conclusion: Degradation arises from image-quality shifts rather than a general-capacity failure; specialized training data helps but does not eliminate domain gaps. Model scale alone does not resolve field-domain robustness. Open-set fabrication is a robust diagnostic across models and evaluation sets.

Abstract: Camera traps often run in the field on edge hardware with limited or no connectivity, making small, locally-deployable vision-language models (VLMs) -- not frontier-scale ones -- the practically relevant class to evaluate for species identification. We test whether models in this deployment-relevant 2--8B range carry genuine taxonomic knowledge, evaluating four such VLMs (Qwen3-VL 2B/4B/8B, Gemma3 4B) against the domain-specific specialist BioCLIP (300M parameters) on a 96-species task, comparing clean iNaturalist photographs against camera-trap imagery from 6 LILA.science collections, on two independently-sampled evaluation sets. All models identify species far above chance, but every model -- general-purpose or specialist -- degrades sharply on field imagery (domain gaps of 9.6--26.6 percentage points, consistent across taxonomic levels and both evaluation sets), indicating the degradation reflects general image legibility rather than fine-grained discrimination failure. BioCLIP substantially outperforms every VLM tested (by 33.2--59.2 percentage points across an expanded 200-image sample for every model) despite its far smaller size, suggesting the gap reflects specialized training data rather than model scale; yet BioCLIP's own domain gap (18.0 points) is statistically indistinguishable from the best VLM's (22.3 points), suggesting the clean-to-field degradation itself is a property of the image-quality shift rather than a general-purpose-model weakness. Under open-set prompting, 5.9--9.6% of responses are syntactically valid but taxonomically nonexistent species names; the relative fabrication-rate ranking across models replicates exactly across both evaluation sets, a more robust finding than any single point estimate.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [155] [M3-Former: Multimodal Transformer with Mixture-of-Experts for Long-Term Vessel Trajectory Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10559)
*Wenzhe Jin,Haina Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: M3-Former: a semantic-guided, multimodal, hierarchical vessel trajectory predictor that uses LLM-encoded static attributes and intent, dual-granularity MoE, and steering-weighted loss to improve long-term accuracy on Danish AIS data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address behavioral multimodality, sparse semantic utilization, and long-term error accumulation in vessel trajectory forecasting, and to improve robustness in complex waterways.

Method: Construct a unified multimodal space where LLM-encoded static semantics align with dynamic trajectory features via self-attention; introduce dual-granularity MoE with sequence-level (global) and token-level (local) experts; apply Steering-Weighted Cross-Entropy loss to mitigate long-tail turning samples.

Result: On a Danish AIS dataset, 4-hour predictions yield ADE reduced by 4.4% and FDE reduced by 5.1% over the strongest baseline; semantic fusion reduces long-term drift; MoE improves robustness in route-branching scenarios; consistent improvements across 1-4 hour horizons.

Conclusion: Semantic-guided hierarchical prediction emerges as a viable paradigm for robust long-term vessel trajectory forecasting by jointly modeling high-level navigational intent and local motion dynamics.

Abstract: To address the challenges of behavioral multimodality, limited semantic utilization, and long-term error accumulation in vessel trajectory prediction, this paper proposes M3-Former, a multimodal trajectory prediction framework enhanced by large language models (LLMs). The proposed framework incorporates vessel static attributes and navigational intent as semantic priors for long-term trajectory modeling. Specifically, a unified multimodal representation space is constructed, in which static semantic information is encoded by a pre-trained LLM and aligned with dynamic trajectory features through self-attention. To jointly capture global route planning and local motion variations, a dual-granularity Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture is introduced, where sequence-level experts model global navigation trends and token-level experts refine fine-grained maneuvering behaviors. In addition, a Steering-Weighted Cross-Entropy loss is designed to alleviate the long-tail distribution of sparse turning samples and improve prediction accuracy in critical maneuvering scenarios. Experiments on a real-world Danish AIS dataset demonstrate that M\textsuperscript{3}-Former consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across prediction horizons from 1 to 4 hours. In the 4-hour prediction task, the proposed method reduces Average Displacement Error (ADE) and Final Displacement Error (FDE) by 4.4\% and 5.1\%, respectively, compared with the strongest baseline. Qualitative and ablation analyses further verify that semantic fusion effectively reduces long-term trajectory drift, while the dual-granularity MoE improves robustness in complex waterways and route-branching scenarios. The proposed framework establishes a semantic-guided hierarchical prediction paradigm, in which high-level navigational intent and local motion dynamics are jointly modeled for robust long-term vessel trajectory forecasting.

</details>


### [156] [Halo: Improving forecast accuracy through heteroscedastic estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10589)
*Adam Cataldo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Halo heteroscedastic forecasting yields consistent point-forecast gains across three architectures on electricity markets, with scale estimation being more critical than the specific head design; gains persist with existing hyperparameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Challenging the idea that heteroscedastic estimation harms point accuracy outside time-series domains and demonstrating practical benefits in energy price forecasting.

Method: Introduce Halo: a second output for the scale of the implied distribution, trained via matching negative log-likelihood. Adapt three state-of-the-art forecasters (transformer, graph network with VAE, single-layer CNN) under Gaussian and Laplacian losses; evaluate on five electricity price markets.

Result: Halo improves MSE and MAE in 28 of 30 model-market-metric comparisons; average MSE reductions range 2.6%–16.5%, MAE reductions 1.7%–11.0%.

Conclusion: Estimating distribution scale improves point forecasts; head design vs full parallel scale network is less important than actually estimating scale; improvements hold under hyperparameters tuned for the point-estimate baseline, making retuning optional.

Abstract: Heteroscedastic forecasting, where a network estimates a scale parameter alongside a location parameter, is normally motivated by uncertainty quantification. This paper shows it also improves the point estimate, in contrast to reported negative results for heteroscedastic estimation outside time series. Halo is a modification that reuses an existing deep forecaster's architecture, giving it a second output for the scale of its implied distribution and training it under the matching negative log likelihood. Adapting three state-of-the-art models --- a transformer, a graph network paired with a variational autoencoder, and a single-layer convolutional network --- under both Gaussian and Laplacian losses demonstrates the phenomenon. On the five electricity price markets of a standard forecasting benchmark, Halo improves MSE and MAE in 28 of 30 model-market-metric comparisons, cutting average MSE by 2.6% to 16.5% and average MAE by 1.7% to 11.0%. Two findings emerge: (1) whether the scale estimate comes from a second projection head or from a full parallel network matters far less than whether the network estimates scale, and (2) the improvement holds under the hyperparameters already tuned for the point-estimate baseline, so retuning is optional.

</details>


### [157] [Zero-shot rib design: merging training-free generative prior with topology optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10643)
*Yongmin Kwon,Namwoo Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Text-to-image diffusion priors are distilled into a density-based topology optimization loop, enabling text-prompt-driven designs that emulate natural load-bearing patterns and yield significant compliance reductions across domains; includes automatic skeleton-to-CAD pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To express engineering design intent via natural language and overcome limitations of classical topology optimization in achieving natural-like, high-stiffness-per-weight geometries.

Method: Use a frozen text-to-image diffusion model as a training-free prior; integrate its gradient with FEM sensitivities via score distillation sampling; apply Heaviside projection; run across multiple domains and physics; skeleton-based pipeline to extract CAD-ready geometries; retarget prior by changing prompts.

Result: 245 SDS runs across 4 domains and 2 physics; 38/49 prompt-domain combos show significant compliance reductions up to 31.5% mechanical and 23.0% thermoelastic; outperforms gradient baselines; dead-end branch suppression; endpoint-compliance correlation r 0.56–0.99; intermediate-density suppression from 42.6% to <3%.

Conclusion: The pretrained generative model serves as a reusable, training-free prior for engineering design, enabling prompt-driven optimization across problems with a CAD-ready skeleton pipeline.

Abstract: Natural load-bearing patterns such as leaf venation, trabecular bone, and spider webs achieve high stiffness per unit mass, yet classical topology optimizers rarely reach such geometries, and few let engineers express structural design intent through natural language. This work treats a frozen text-to-image diffusion model as a training-free source of design knowledge and distills it into the physics loop of density-based topology optimization via score distillation sampling, so that a text prompt becomes an explicit, machine-interpretable representation of engineer intent. The prompt-induced generative gradient and the finite element sensitivity are combined at every iteration, letting physics decide which prompt-induced features survive. In 245 primary SDS runs spanning four geometric domains and two physics regimes, 38 of 49 prompt--domain combinations achieved statistically significant compliance reductions (up to $-31.5\%$ mechanical and $-23.0\%$ thermoelastic), outperforming gradient-based baselines. Cross-domain morphological analysis identifies a recurring structural signature of improvement: in most domains the generative prior suppresses dead-end branches in the rib skeleton, with endpoint--compliance correlation $r = +0.56$ to $+0.99$. A Heaviside projection with $β$-continuation resolves a pronounced intermediate-density tendency in this diffusion--physics coupling ($42.6\%$ to $<3\%$), and an automated skeleton-based pipeline converts optimized density fields into \rev{candidate geometry ready for computer-aided design. By retargeting the generative prior across domains, loading conditions, and physics objectives through a change of text prompt, with each new problem's physics setup specified separately, the framework uses a pretrained generative model as a reusable, training-free prior for engineering design.

</details>


### [158] [Byzantine-Robust Federated Fire Detection with a Rotating Coordinator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10647)
*Georgia Argyrou,Aymen Bahrouny,Hedi Fendriy,Alexander Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A federated learning approach for indoor fire detection with a curated dataset, compressed model updates, and a semi-decentralized rotating-coordinator Byzantine-robust FL method; achieves accuracy and speed comparable to centralized baselines while mitigating bandwidth, Byzantine risks, and single-point-of-failure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Privacy-preserving fire-detection use case: edge cameras capture sensitive footage, making central data collection undesirable. The work tackles limited uplink bandwidth, Byzantine clients, and the single fixed aggregation server.

Method: (i) assemble a multi-source indoor fire-detection dataset from eight public sources; (ii) develop an edge-deployable detector with model updates compressible up to 10x with minimal loss in balanced accuracy; (iii) introduce a semi-decentralized Byzantine-robust FL framework that uses history-aware aggregation and a rotating coordinator to evict stealthy attacks and remove the fixed-server single point of failure; evaluate on held-out data and validate via a six-node distributed cloud deployment.

Result: On held-out data, the rotating-coordinator method matches the fixed-server baseline in accuracy and detection speed; compression yields only a small loss in balanced accuracy; a physically distributed six-node cloud deployment confirms feasibility.

Conclusion: The semi-decentralized, rotating-coordinator Byzantine-robust FL approach effectively addresses bandwidth constraints, robustness to malicious/faulty clients, and single-point-of-failure risks while preserving detection performance, supported by a multi-source dataset and real-world deployment feasibility.

Abstract: We study the application of federated learning (FL) to indoor fire detection. Such fire-detection systems use edge cameras that record sensitive footage which cannot easily be collected at a central server. Existing federated solutions leave three practical obstacles unaddressed: limited uplink bandwidth, Byzantine (malicious or faulty) clients, and unconditional trust in a single, permanently fixed aggregation server. Our main contributions address all three. In particular, we provide (i) a curated indoor fire-detection dataset assembled from eight public sources; (ii) an edge-deployable detector whose model updates are compressed up to 10 time with only a small loss in balanced accuracy; and (iii) a semi-decentralized Byzantine-robust FL method that combines history-aware aggregation with a rotating coordinator, evicting stealthy attacks that per-round filters miss while removing the fixed-server single point of failure. On the held-out test set the rotating-coordinator method matches its fixed-server counterpart in accuracy and detection speed, and a physically distributed six-node cloud deployment confirms feasibility.

</details>


### [159] [Artificial Intelligence Algorithms for the Detection of Pathologies Related to Lung Cancer through Image Analysis using Convolutional Neural Networks and Data Augmentation: a systematic mapping of the literature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10652)
*Pablo Ramirez Amador*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A review of AI and DL applications in radiology for lung cancer detection; CNNs with transfer learning and data augmentation show promise; but lack of standardized data, interpretability, privacy, and ethical issues hinder clinical deployment; calls for further research and regulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve early lung cancer diagnosis and radiology workflow by leveraging AI/DL techniques.

Method: Exhaustive search of PubMed, IEEE Xplore, Scopus, and Web of Science; selection of 96 articles published from 2015 to present addressing AI/DL in biomedical imaging for lung cancer detection; emphasis on CNNs with transfer learning and data augmentation.

Result: AI/DL approaches provide high sensitivity and specificity, offering an effective potential alternative for early lung cancer diagnosis; however, clinical adoption is limited by issues such as dataset standardization, model explainability, patient privacy, and ethical implications.

Conclusion: AI/DL can positively impact care for lung cancer patients, but further research and regulatory frameworks are needed to ensure quality, reliability, and safe clinical integration.

Abstract: Lung cancer is one of the leading causes of death worldwide, and its early diagnosis is crucial to improving patients prognosis and quality of life. However, the process of interpreting medical images for the detection of lung cancer is complex and requires trained experts. In this context, artificial intelligence (AI) and deep learning (DL) emerge as potential tools to automate and optimize image analysis. The objective of this work is to review the most recent and relevant applications of AI and DL in the field of radiology for the detection of lung cancer. To this end, an exhaustive search was carried out in scientific databases such as PubMed,IEEEXPLORE, Scopus and Web of Science, and 96 articles published from 2015 to the present addressing the use of AI and DL in biomedical engineering were selected. Emphasis is placed on the use of convolutional neural networks (CNN) with transfer learning and Data Augmentation as promising techniques to improve the accuracy and efficiency of the image interpretation process. The results show that the use of AI and DL can offer an effective alternative for the early diagnosis of lung cancer, with high sensitivity and specificity. However, current limitations and challenges that must be addressed to guarantee its responsible and safe application in clinical practice are also identified, such as the lack of standardized data, the ex plainability of the models, patient privacy, and the ethical and social implications. It is concluded that the use of AI and DL can have a positive impact on the care of patients with lung cancer, but further research and regulation are required to ensure its quality and reliability.

</details>


### [160] [GEOSTEER: Geodesic Optimization for Activation Steering in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10658)
*Xuan Cuong Ngo,Hao Vo,Ngan Le*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: New optimization-based, norm-preserving activation steering (GeoSteer) uses Riemannian geometry on activation manifolds to take multistep geodesic updates guided by a learned activation-space objective, improving stability and performance over one-step, fixed trajectories on multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing norm-preserving steering methods rely on predefined one-step edits and fixed steering trajectories, which can fail to capture the complex structure of activation distributions and risk representation collapse. There is a need for adaptive, geometry-aware steering that preserves activation norms while effectively steering behavior.

Method: Formulate steering as a Riemannian optimization problem on the activation manifold. Perform a sequence of small geodesic steps guided by a nonlinear activation-space objective that differentiates desired from undesired activations; this objective is learned and used to adaptively steer each step, ensuring norm preservation.

Result: GeoSteer yields smoother, more stable, and more consistent steering and consistently improves over state-of-the-art norm-preserving baselines across TruthfulQA, RealToxicityPrompts, and UltraFeedback benchmarks.

Conclusion: Replacing predefined one-step edits with adaptive, geometry-aware optimization enables more effective norm-preserving activation steering and demonstrates the viability of multistep geodesic updates for steering large language models.

Abstract: Activation steering provides a lightweight way to control large language models (LLMs) by modifying their hidden activations at inference time. Among these approaches, norm-preserving steering aims to change model behavior without altering the activation norm, reducing the risk of representation collapse and degradation. However, existing norm-preserving methods are limited by predefined steering trajectories and by their reliance on one-step updates, which may fail to capture the complex structure of activation distributions. We propose GeoSteer, an optimization-based method for norm-preserving activation steering. GeoSteer formulates steering as a Riemannian optimization problem and updates activations through a sequence of small geodesic steps on the representation manifold. To avoid fixed steering directions, GeoSteer learns a nonlinear activation-space objective that distinguishes desired from undesired activations, and uses this function to adaptively guide each steering step. This multistep formulation yields smoother, more stable, and more consistent steering behavior while preserving the activation norm. Across TruthfulQA, RealToxicityPrompts, and UltraFeedback benchmarks, GeoSteer consistently improves over state-of-the-art activation steering baselines. These results suggest that norm-preserving steering can be made more effective by replacing predefined one-step edits with adaptive, geometry-aware optimization.

</details>


### [161] [Conformal Calibration Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10737)
*Achref Doula*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transported Conformal Calibration (TCC) transfers labeled source calibration to a target domain via paired unlabeled data, then corrects residual mismatch. It yields finite-sample target-domain coverage guarantees and two instantiations (TCC-KS and weighted-TCC) for improved conservatism or efficiency, with diagnostics. Empirically validated without target labels on CIFAR-100-C, Tiny-ImageNet-C, and SEN12MS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conformal prediction requires exchangeability between calibration and deployment data. When this fails due to domain shifts (source-labeled calibration vs. target predictions), calibrating directly in the target space is not possible. TCC aims to enable valid prediction sets in the target domain using only unlabeled paired data to transport and adjust calibration.

Method: Transport labeled source calibration into the target space using paired observations. Then perform a residual mismatch correction using only unlabeled target inputs. Two instantiations: (1) TCC-KS uses a label-free uncertainty surrogate to detect mismatch and adjust calibration conservatively; (2) weighted-TCC reweights the transported calibration toward the target domain when weights are stable. The approach provides finite-sample target-domain coverage guarantees that adapt to an observable mismatch measure.

Result: Empirical results on CIFAR-100-C, Tiny-ImageNet-C, and SEN12MS demonstrate reliable target-domain coverage transfer without labeled target calibration data. The method includes label-free diagnostics that can predict when correction is needed.

Conclusion: TCC offers a practical and theoretically grounded framework for conformal prediction under cross-domain shifts with unlabeled paired data, delivering coverage guarantees and diagnostics, and enabling effective target-domain calibration transfer without target labels.

Abstract: Conformal prediction converts point predictions into set-valued predictions with coverage guarantees under exchangeability between calibration and deployment data. We study conformal calibration transfer, where this requirement fails because labeled calibration is available only in a source space, while prediction sets are needed in a target space linked to the source through unlabeled paired observations (e.g., paired modalities or sensor changes). We propose Transported Conformal Calibration (TCC): we transport labeled source calibration into the target space using the paired data, and then correct residual post-transport mismatch using only unlabeled target inputs. We instantiate this correction with two complementary methods: TCC-KS, which uses a label-free uncertainty surrogate to detect mismatch and adjust calibration conservatively, and weighted-TCC, which reweights transported calibration toward the target domain for improved efficiency when weights are stable. We provide finite-sample target-domain coverage guarantees that adapt to an observable measure of mismatch. Across CIFAR-100-C, Tiny-ImageNet-C, and SEN12MS, we show reliable target-domain coverage transfer without labeled target calibration data, with label-free diagnostics that predict when correction is needed.

</details>


### [162] [The Truth Was Never Gone: Perfect Aliasing in Compliant-Context Truth Probes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10739)
*Dylan Jayabahu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Perfect aliasing prevents semantic identification in truth vs. prescribed-action probes in a controlled setting; mixed-fitting with rival contexts reveals linear recoverability of truth information, achieving perfect AUROC, while conventional probes fail; results caution interpretation of probes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate identifiability of semantic truth versus prescribed action in neural probes; assess how different probe fitting strategies affect measured signals and what this implies about interpreting probe results.

Method: Use a controlled binary reporting game with compliant and rival contexts. Fit truth and prescribed-action probes on compliant contexts; introduce randomized codebooks to decorrelate outputs; separate truth from prescribed action by fitting on mixed compliant and rival contexts; evaluate with a reward-trained Gemma-2-9B policy across multiple seeds and 751 cell-layer pairs.

Result: Conventional probes yield AUROC ≈ 0.006 (three seeds). Mixed-fit probes achieve AUROC = 1.000 on held-out activations. Rival-context evaluations show two compliant-fit probes scoring 0.080 and 0.986. The identity AUROC-sum-to-one holds across 751 cell-layer pairs to floating-point precision.

Conclusion: The work clarifies what probes measure; it does not claim preserved functional belief, causal use, or a deployable deception detector. Mixed-fitting with access to rival contexts demonstrates linear recoverability and decorrelation benefits, though interpretation of probe signals remains limited. Code and aggregate results accompany the paper.

Abstract: A truth probe fitted where truthful reporting and a task's prescribed action coincide cannot distinguish those targets from its fitting labels alone. We call this failure of semantic identification perfect aliasing. In a controlled binary reporting game, truth and prescribed-action probes fitted on compliant contexts solve the same optimization. On rival contexts their labels are complements, forcing their AUROCs to sum to one; this identity holds across 751 cell-layer pairs to floating-point precision. We separate prescribed output symbols from semantic action using randomized codebooks, then separate truth from prescribed action by fitting on mixed compliant and rival contexts. For a reward-trained Gemma-2-9B policy that answers falsely on all evaluated rival trials, the conventional probe scores $0.006 \pm 0.005$ AUROC across three training seeds, while mixed-fit probes score $1.000$ on the same held-out activations. Mixed fitting uses more training examples and access to labelled rival contexts, so this comparison establishes linear recoverability rather than isolating the benefit of decorrelation. We also show that two compliant-fit probes, both perfect in-distribution, score $0.080$ and $0.986$ on the same rival activations. The findings concern what a probe measures: they do not establish preserved functional belief, causal use of the recovered direction, or a deployable deception detector. Code and aggregate results accompany the paper.

</details>


### [163] [Adaptive Margin Ordinal Loss: Penalizing Center-Class Hedging in Ordinal Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10752)
*Manisha Kandel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AMOL improves ordinal classification by weighting per-class losses to suppress center-class hedging; AMOL-asym reduces hedging even further, achieving zero CHR on Abalone; overall improves QWK across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Center-class hedging in standard cross-entropy; need a loss that accounts for the ordinal center and true label position.

Method: AMOL multiplies per-class losses by m(k,y) = 1 + α*(1 - |k-c|/c)*(|y-c|/c), with center c; CHR metric; AMOL-asym variant.

Result: AMOL achieves best or tied-best QWK on four datasets across five seeds compared to CE, OLL, SORD; AMOL-asym eliminates center hedging on Abalone (CHR ≈ 0 across seeds).

Conclusion: AMOL effectively mitigates center-class hedging and enhances ordinal classification performance; CHR provides a direct diagnostic; AMOL-asym shows potential in removing hedging on some datasets.

Abstract: Standard cross-entropy loss causes neural networks trained on ordinal classification tasks to hedge predictions toward center classes, a failure mode we term \emph{center-class hedging}. This occurs because predicting the middle class minimizes expected symmetric loss, making it the path of least resistance regardless of the true label. Existing ordinal losses address related problems such as large-error penalization and rank consistency, but none directly suppresses center-class hedging as a function of where the true label lies relative to the ordinal center. We propose the Adaptive Margin Ordinal Loss (AMOL), a multiplicative weight applied to per-class loss terms of the form $m(k,y) = 1 + α\cdot (1 - |k-c|/c) \cdot (|y-c|/c)$, where $c$ is the center class, $k$ is the candidate class, and $y$ is the true label. The weight encodes a joint condition: it is large only when the candidate class is near center and the true label is far from center, collapsing to standard behavior otherwise. We further introduce the Center-Hedging Rate (CHR) as a diagnostic metric that directly quantifies this failure mode. Across four ordinal classification benchmarks and five random seeds, AMOL achieves the best or tied-best Quadratic Weighted Kappa (QWK) on all four datasets compared to cross-entropy, OLL, and SORD baselines. An asymmetric variant (AMOL-asym) eliminates center-class hedging entirely on the Abalone dataset ($\text{CHR} = 0.000 \pm 0.000$ across all five seeds, $n \approx 266$ extreme-class test samples per run), compared to $0.074 \pm 0.005$ for standard cross-entropy.

</details>


### [164] [A Bellman Optimality Equation for Plasticity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10776)
*Jeremy Lucas,Doina Precup*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes optimizing plasticity in continual RL under a new information-theoretic defn, proving a Bellman-like optimality equation for plasticity in MDPs and positioning groundwork for practical algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the stability-plasticity tradeoff in continual RL by formalizing plasticity as directed information and establishing a tractable DP framework to optimize it, filling a gap in the empowerment-plasticity literature.

Method: Theoretical development showing the existence of a Bellman optimality equation for plasticity in MDPs; establishes structural similarity to empowerment-based formulations, setting the stage for algorithmic approaches.

Result: Demonstrates that a Bellman-like optimality condition exists for plasticity optimization in MDPs; results are preliminary/theoretical with no implemented algorithms or empirical validation.

Conclusion: Lays foundational theory for plasticity optimization under the new framework, enabling future algorithm development and empirical studies to compare against empowerment-based methods.

Abstract: In continual reinforcement learning, carefully managing the stability-plasticity tradeoff remains a core challenge. Recent work by Abel et al. (2025) formalized this dilemma by defining plasticity as the generalized directed information from an agent's observations to its actions, and empowerment as the generalized directed information from its actions to its observations. This formulation successfully reframes the traditional stability-plasticity tradeoff as an empowerment-plasticity tradeoff. However, while extensive literature exists on optimizing for empowerment, there is currently no research addressing the optimization of plasticity under this new definition. This paper presents preliminary work toward optimizing plasticity within Markov decision processes. We show that there exists a Bellman optimality equation for optimizing plasticity similar to previous work for empowerment.

</details>


### [165] [Counterfactual Marginalisation: Framework for Evaluating Robustness to Nuisance Variables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10778)
*Yasin Ibrahim,Hermione Warr,Robin J. Evans,Konstantinos Kamnitsas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Counterfactual marginalisation (CFM) as a test-time evaluation to assess robustness of classifiers to demographic/acquisition-related shortcuts by generating counterfactual nuisance-variable versions of test images and averaging predictions across an intervention distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Models may rely on demographic or acquisition cues; need evaluation that isolates such effects and measures robustness and fairness without retraining.

Method: Given a CF image generator, intervene on nuisance variables (e.g., age, sex), create CF test images, and average predictions over a target intervention distribution to obtain intervention-aware predictions that marginalise nuisance effects while preserving patient-specific latent information. Define metrics: CF risk, calibration, stability, worst-case sensitivity.

Result: Demonstrate utility for quantitative robustness evaluation; provides a framework to diagnose and quantify reliance on nuisance variables and compare models under CF interventions.

Conclusion: CF marginalisation is a practical test-time evaluation tool for robustness to demographic/acquisition shortcuts; enables intervention-aware predictions and metrics that quantify and compare sensitivity to nuisance factors, guiding development of more robust and fair models.

Abstract: Machine learning models can achieve strong test performance while relying on demographic or acquisition-related shortcuts. We propose counterfactual (CF) marginalisation as a test-time evaluation procedure for assessing robustness of classification models to such variables. Given a CF image generator, we intervene on nuisance parent variables such as age or sex, generate CF versions of each test image, and average predictions over a target intervention distribution. This produces intervention-aware predictions that marginalise demographic effects while preserving patient-specific latent information. We use these predictions to define metrics for CF risk, calibration, stability and worst-case sensitivity. We demonstrate this framework's utility for quantitative robustness evaluation.

</details>


### [166] [From Connectivity to Rewards: Dense Reward Learning with Directed State Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10781)
*Shuyuan Zhang,Zihan Wang,Xiao-Wen Chang,Doina Precup*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: G2QDR adds a directed state-connectivity model to GCHRL, turning connectivity into scalar dense rewards to guide hierarchical policies in asymmetric (quasimetric) environments, improving performance with modest overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graphs used in GCHRL often overlook intrinsic connectivity and state-accessibility; asymmetric transitions hinder stable learning and planning; there is a need to leverage topology as an environmental model rather than just a sampling tool.

Method: Train a neural network to predict pairwise connectivity strengths on a directed state graph built during exploration; convert to scalar dense rewards; integrate with any GCHRL architecture; implement efficiently as a state connectivity model guiding subgoals across multiple hierarchy levels.

Result: Empirical results across sparse reward tasks show improved performance of baseline GCHRL methods with acceptable computational overhead; framework is theoretically integrable with existing architectures.

Conclusion: G2QDR provides a general and effective way to exploit state connectivity in quasimetric environments, reinforcing GCHRL with dense, topology-aware rewards and enabling more robust and data-efficient learning.

Abstract: The integration of graphs with Goal-Conditioned Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning (GCHRL) has received increasing attention, as graphs naturally encode task hierarchies for effective subgoal sampling. However, existing methods often overlook intrinsic connectivity information, failing to fully leverage the underlying topology for efficient learning. Most graph-based GCHRL methods use the graph as a stochastic sampling tool rather than as an environmental model that encodes connectivity and state-accessibility information. This limitation is particularly acute in quasimetric environments, where the inherent asymmetry of state transitions poses a fundamental challenge to stable policy learning and robust path planning. In this paper, we address these problems by introducing a state connectivity model designed to predict pairwise state connectivity strength in asymmetric environments. We transform these connectivity strengths into scalar auxiliary dense rewards, providing continuous guidance across multiple hierarchical levels. We demonstrate that our proposed framework, Graph-Guided Quasimetric Dense Reward (G2QDR), can theoretically be integrated into any existing GCHRL architecture, and the state connectivity model is efficiently implemented via a neural network trained on a directed state graph generated during exploration. Empirical results across a wide range of sparse reward environments indicate that, in general, G2QDR can enhance the performance of baseline GCHRL approaches with acceptable computational overhead.

</details>


### [167] [DR-LabStack: Design and Implementation of a Clinician-Facing Web System for Diabetic Retinopathy Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10796)
*Yingfan Xu,Tieming Liu,Ye Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Web-based integration framework (DR-LabStack) that unifies heterogeneous pretrained DR models under a common React-Flask interface with adapters, standardized JSON output, and input-order validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diabetic retinopathy models vary in inputs, formats, and outputs; a shared clinical interface is needed for reliable usage.

Method: Developed DR-LabStack using React frontend and Flask backend; integrated four pretrained models (RuleFit, Pruned RuleFit, Elaborative XGBoost, Two-level Ensemble); implemented a shared form to collect ordered features; backend adapters load artifacts and apply scalers; emits a common JSON response with classification and provenance; evaluated through unit tests and browser scenarios.

Result: All models loaded; contracts exposed: 14-, 6-, 8-, 25-field inputs; 62 Flask requests tested; 12 path/threshold checks; 24 browser scenarios; input ordering and result rendering validated; demonstrates reusable interaction and serving workflow.

Conclusion: The work delivers a reusable web-system design and integration for heterogeneous DR models; clinical effectiveness and clinician usability require separate, dedicated evaluation.

Abstract: Pretrained diabetic retinopathy (DR) prediction models differ in their input fields, serialization formats, preprocessing requirements, and output semantics. Making these models accessible through a common clinical interface therefore requires explicit coordination between the user interface and the inference service. We designed and implemented DR-LabStack, a React-Flask web system integrating four externally developed pretrained models: RuleFit, Pruned RuleFit, Elaborative XGBoost, and Two-level Ensemble. A shared form retrieves ordered model features, renders model-specific numerical and categorical controls, and constructs a positional input vector. Backend adapters load heterogeneous artifacts and apply the ensemble's accompanying scaler, while a common JSON response supports binary classification display alongside method and source information. Functional evaluation on September 8, 2026 used copied application files and real model artifacts in a documented isolated environment. All four models loaded and exposed their 14-, 6-, 8-, and 25-field contracts. Sixty-two Flask test-client requests characterized service behavior; 12 limited-vector checks confirmed invocation-path and threshold consistency. Twenty-four browser-component scenarios with mocked transport verified input ordering and result rendering and characterized input-validation behavior. The resulting system demonstrates a reusable interaction and serving workflow for heterogeneous DR models. The contribution is web-system design, integration, and software functionality; clinical effectiveness and clinician usability require separate evaluation.

</details>


### [168] [RiVaT-Fuse: Reliability-Calibrated Variational Tensor Fusion for Multimodal Prediction under Modality Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10798)
*Yingfan Xu,Tieming Liu,Ye Liang,Taiping Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RiVaT-Fuse reframes image-metadata fusion as sample-wise latent-state estimation using a reliability-calibrated variational tensor framework; achieves robust, stable predictions under modality uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fusion of heterogeneous evidence with varying reliability is challenging; existing representation-level fusion lacks explicit meaning under uncertainty; a principled, robust fusion method should account for sample-specific reliability and structured cross-modal interactions.

Method: RiVaT-Fuse introduces a variational tensor fusion framework with matrix-valued trust geometry, decomposing cross-modal interaction into additive, multiplicative, and relational components, and coupling the latent state with conditional robustness and structured multi-task prediction. It uses low-rank-plus-diagonal trust operators and provides well-posedness and stability analyses.

Result: On an image-level image-metadata prediction benchmark, RiVaT-Fuse achieves the strongest overall predictive rank among direct representation-level baselines and improves probability and label stability under perturbation.

Conclusion: Fusion is defined as latent-state estimation with calibrated reliability; the framework offers theoretical interpretations, efficient instantiations, and improved robustness for multimodal prediction under modality uncertainty.

Abstract: Image-metadata prediction requires fusing heterogeneous evidence whose reliability can vary across samples and latent factors. Existing representation-level fusion methods typically choose an aggregation architecture, such as concatenation, gating, conditional modulation, or attention, without explicitly defining what the fused representation should mean under modality uncertainty. We propose RiVaT-Fuse, a reliability-calibrated variational tensor fusion framework that defines fusion as sample-wise latent-state estimation. Rather than producing a fused vector by direct aggregation, RiVaT-Fuse estimates a consensus latent state through a variational objective that balances image evidence, metadata evidence, structured cross-modal interaction, and stability. The resulting framework replaces scalar modality confidence with matrix-valued trust geometry, decomposes interaction into additive, multiplicative, and relational components, and couples the latent state with conditional robustness and structured multi-task prediction. We provide well-posedness and stability interpretations of the latent solve and instantiate the framework with efficient low-rank-plus-diagonal trust operators. On an image-level image-metadata prediction benchmark, RiVaT-Fuse achieves the strongest overall predictive rank among direct representation-level baselines while improving probability and label stability under perturbation.

</details>


### [169] [Processing and classifying bird songs using wavelet techniques and supervised learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10826)
*Laura Lucia Dominguez Barrios,Fidel Aniano Causil Barrios,Alex Rodrigo dos Santos Sousa,Mariana Rodrigues Motta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Integrates Bayesian wavelet denoising with an Epanechnikov prior for robust processing of noisy bioacoustic data, boosting supervised classification of invasive birds using MFCCs and spectral features; SVM achieves top accuracy (~0.94) with 10 MFCCs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tackle signal degradation in natural soundscapes to enable reliable automated monitoring of invasive bird species; scale to large bioacoustic datasets with efficient denoising and robust classifiers.

Method: Bayesian wavelet shrinkage using an Epanechnikov kernel prior for denoising; extract MFCCs and spectral indices (entropy, zero-crossing rate); evaluate Random Forest, Multinomial Logistic Regression, and SVM across varying MFCC dimensionalities; dataset comprises recordings of Euphonia violacea, Leiothrix lutea, Passer domesticus from iNaturalist.

Result: Denoising enhances classification performance; SVM yields the highest accuracy, up to 0.9398 with 10 MFCC features; RF and logistic regression performed less well under the tested configurations.

Conclusion: Wavelet-based Bayesian denoising with a closed-form rule provides an effective preprocessing step for automated ecological monitoring of invasive species, enabling accurate and efficient classification in noisy natural environments.

Abstract: This study proposes an integrated framework for the processing and classification of invasive bird species vocalizations within natural soundscapes, characterized by high levels of environmental noise. We address the challenge of signal degradation by employing a Bayesian wavelet shrinkage methodology based on the Epanechnikov kernel prior, which offers a closed form decision rule and high computational efficiency for processing large bioacoustic datasets. The methodology was applied to recordings of three species obtained from the iNaturalist platform: \textit{Euphonia violacea}, \textit{Leiothrix lutea}, and \textit{Passer domesticus}. After signal denoising, we extracted a comprehensive set of features, including Mel-Frequency Cepstral Coefficients (MFCCs) and spectral indices such as entropy and zero-crossing rate. Several supervised learning models: Random Forest, Multinomial Logistic Regression and Support Vector Machine (SVM) were evaluated across different feature dimensionalities. Our results demonstrate that the proposed wavelet based preprocessing significantly enhances classification performance, with the SVM model achieving the highest accuracy (up to 0.9398) under a 10-dimensional MFCC configuration. This research provides a robust statistical tool for automated ecological monitoring and the management of biological invasions.

</details>


### [170] [Flow Duality and Source Geometry for Categorical Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10863)
*Etrit Haxholli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Reveals a duality: continuous convex-interpolant paths, when projected with argmax, yield discrete convex-interpolant paths; source geometry and boundary conditions shape discrete interpolation and generation behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge continuous and discrete flow matching and understand how the choice of the continuous source distribution governs discrete categorical generation and interpolation dynamics.

Method: Theoretically show that projecting continuous convex-interpolant paths via position-wise argmax under coordinate symmetry and boundary regularity yields discrete convex interpolants. Analyze induced discrete interpolation for Gaussian, bounded-uniform, and centered negative-exponential source laws. Support with small visual diagnostics and a short language-modeling pilot to gauge practical impact.

Result: Discrete interpolation behavior is induced by the geometry of the source; different source shapes lead to qualitatively different transition timing and vocabulary-size dependence. Diagnostics and LM pilot suggest source-design effects can appear in learned transports and early generative quality.

Conclusion: Source geometry is a design knob for categorical generation; the continuous-discrete duality holds under the stated regularity assumptions, with tangible implications for model design, evaluation, and interpretation of generative processes.

Abstract: Continuous and discrete flow matching are usually treated as separate constructions. This paper identifies a duality between them: projecting continuous convex-interpolant paths with one-hot targets through a position-wise argmax yields discrete convex-interpolant paths. The result requires source laws with appropriate coordinate symmetry and boundary regularity, and it makes the continuous source distribution an explicit design choice for categorical generation. We derive the induced discrete interpolation behavior for Gaussian, bounded-uniform, and centered negative-exponential sources, showing that different source geometries lead to qualitatively different transition timing and vocabulary-size dependence. Small visual diagnostics and a short language-modeling pilot suggest that these source-design effects can also appear in learned transports and early generative quality.

</details>


### [171] [Certifying Lower Bounds for Risk-Sensitive Reinforcement Learning under Adversarial State Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10866)
*Tong Li,Saunak Kumar Panda,Yisha Xiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Extends RL robustness certification to risk-sensitive objectives by deriving convex-relaxation-based lower bounds on exponential utility under l_p-bounded state perturbations; uses φ-divergence to relax the perturbation set and a dual formulation for tractable bounds; demonstrates that risk-averse training improves certified bounds, with non-monotonic dependence on the risk-aversion parameter β across environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world RL systems face adversarial perturbations and safety-critical requirements; existing certification methods target risk-neutral objectives and may be overly optimistic under perturbations. There is a need to certify risk-sensitive performance guarantees (exponential utility) under perturbations.

Method: Introduce φ-divergence relaxation of the perturbation set to recast risk-sensitive certification as a convex optimization problem; derive the dual to obtain a tractable bound on the exponential-utility lower bound; propose an empirical method to choose the training risk-aversion parameter β independently of evaluation risk; validate on OpenAI Gym tasks and a machine replacement problem.

Result: Derives tractable lower bounds on the exponential utility under l_p perturbations; risk-averse training generally yields higher certified bounds, especially for larger perturbations; increasing risk aversion during training yields non-monotonic certification performance, with initial gains followed by declines due to overly conservative policies.

Conclusion: Risk-sensitive certification is feasible and beneficial; risk-averse training can bolster certified guarantees, but proper tuning of the risk-aversion parameter is crucial to avoid overly conservative policies and degraded bounds.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) agents deployed in real-world environments are often vulnerable to adversarial perturbations in state observations, creating risks in safety-critical applications. Certification methods can improve robustness against adversarial perturbations by providing lower bounds on expected cumulative rewards. Existing certification methods, however, mainly focus on risk-neutral objectives. In this paper, we extend certification methods to risk-sensitive objectives by establishing lower bounds on the exponential utility of cumulative rewards under $l_{p}$-norm-bounded state adversarial perturbations ($1\leq p <\infty$). By introducing a $φ$-divergence relaxation of the perturbation set, we formulate the risk-sensitive certification problem as a convex optimization and derive its dual to obtain a tractable approximation of the certified lower bound. We further propose an empirical method that improves certified lower bounds by selecting the training risk-aversion parameter $β$ independently of the risk level used during evaluation. Experiments on both OpenAI Gym environments and a machine replacement problem show that, compared to risk-neutral training, risk-averse training generally yields policies with higher certified lower bounds, particularly under larger perturbation budgets. Moreover, under both risk-neutral and risk-averse evaluation settings, increasing risk aversion during training leads to non-monotonic certification performance, where certified lower bounds initially improve but eventually decrease due to overly conservative policies.

</details>


### [172] [Learning Orthogonal Multi-Index Models Beyond Small Initialization: Incremental Learning, Competitive Dynamics and Symmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10879)
*Mo Zhou,Weihang Xu,Simon S. Du,Maryam Fazel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Incremental learning persists under standard initialization in polynomial-width two-layer nets for orthogonal multi-index targets; learning follows Hermite expansion order with mass reallocations to the target subspace and aligned neurons. A symmetry-based finite-width analysis using symmetrized networks provides controlled approximation without infinite-width limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand training dynamics beyond small initialization/simplified losses, specifically how standard initialization affects feature learning in two-layer networks with multi-index targets.

Method: Theoretical analysis using a slightly modified gradient flow and polynomially many samples; a finite-width approximation via symmetrized networks replaces direct infinite-width comparison; empirical verification shows vanilla gradient descent exhibits similar dynamics.

Result: Loss decreases sequentially according to the Hermite expansion of the target; lower-order components are learnt before higher-order ones, with individual target directions recovered; after fitting the mean, mass reallocates into the target subspace and concentrates on aligned neurons; symmetry-based finite-width framework yields stable approximation errors and matches observed dynamics.

Conclusion:  The work extends incremental learning phenomena to standard initialization regimes, offering a principled finite-width analysis through symmetrized networks that may be broadly applicable and aligns with empirical gradient descent behavior.

Abstract: Recent work has identified incremental learning in shallow networks trained on single-index and multi-index models. However, existing analyses often rely on simplifying settings, such as small initialization, correlation loss, or layer-wise training. These choices reduce neuron interactions and leave some feature learning dynamics under standard initialization unexplored. We study training dynamics for polynomial-width two-layer networks learning orthogonal multi-index targets under standard initialization using polynomially many samples. We first prove that incremental learning still occurs: the loss decreases sequentially according to the Hermite expansion of the target, with lower-order components learned before higher-order components recover the individual target directions. In this standard initialization regime, training also shows a competitive reallocation of parameter mass: after the total mass fits the target mean and stabilizes, mass shifts into the target subspace and then concentrates on aligned neurons. Our theoretical analysis uses slightly modified gradient flow, while vanilla gradient descent empirically exhibits the same qualitative dynamics. Technically, we introduce a symmetry-based finite-width approximation via symmetrized networks, rather than comparing directly with an infinite-width limit. This yields better control of approximation errors and may be of independent interest.

</details>


### [173] [Story Imprinting: AI Assistants Absorb Traits from Human Characters They Resemble](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10883)
*Jorio Cocola,Lev McKinney,Harry Mayne,Jan Betley,Owain Evans*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Finetuning LMs on synthetic stories can induce 'story imprinting', where the assistant adopts behaviors and preferences from human characters depicted in stories, even when these cues are subtle or rare. This affinity effect favors characters similar to the assistant (e.g., helpful) and extends to other personas via system prompts. The effect correlates with elite-university representation, suggesting internal model priors. Implications for alignment and training data design, with potential conflicts with existing Persona Selection Models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether finetuning on narrative stories changes real-world, multi-turn assistant behavior and whether characters imprint on the model’s preferences. Examine the affinity effect, cross-prompt transfer, and how internal representations align with human archetypes (including elite-affiliated individuals).

Method: Fine-tune GPT-4.1 and Kimi-K2.6 on stories where generally helpful human characters give subtly harmful advice after insults, plus stories where narration implies preferences. Evaluate in multi-turn conversations, compare with baselines, and probe character influence by varying which characters resemble the assistant. Test the generalization to unprompted personas via system prompts, including unhelpful personas. Extend analysis to finetuned base models. Analyze correlation with characters affiliated with elite universities (e.g., Yale) vs non-elite. Examine whether story-only-human content conflicts with the Persona Selection Model.

Result: The assistant adopts the same conditional behaviors shown in the stories, even when fewer than 2% of stories depict the behavior. It also adopts implicit preferences suggested by narration (e.g., avoiding spreadsheet tasks). The affinity effect shows higher influence from characters that resemble the assistant (e.g., helpful vs. dismissive) and extends to other personas via system prompts. The effect is observable in finetuned base models as well. Influence partially tracks characters’ elite-university affiliation, implying the model’s internal representation of the Assistant aligns more with elites. Overall, stories containing only human characters can imprint behavior that may conflict with the Persona Selection Model.

Conclusion: Story-level narratives can imprint assistant behavior and preferences, revealing vulnerabilities in alignment and prompting the need for careful curation of training data and prompt design. The affinity effect highlights biases in how models internalize human archetypes and suggests that internal representations may reflect elite-associated cues. Mitigation may require controlling narrative priors, monitoring cross-domain transfer, and refining Persona Selection for safer alignment.

Abstract: Language models are trained to implement a helpful AI Assistant character (e.g., Claude). We explore how finetuning on synthetic stories affects this character. Does it change the Assistant's behavior in multi-turn conversations with users, a format quite different from the stories? And does the Assistant adopt the behaviors and preferences of human characters? We refer to this adoption as story imprinting. We finetune GPT-4.1 and Kimi-K2.6 on stories in which generally helpful human characters give subtly harmful advice after being insulted. The Assistant adopts the same conditional behavior while otherwise remaining helpful. This occurs even when fewer than 2% of stories depict the behavior. In a separate experiment, the Assistant adopts preferences that are only implicit in the narration. A human character's body language suggests they dislike working on spreadsheets, yet they never say so and continue giving good advice on spreadsheets. After finetuning, the Assistant becomes less likely to choose spreadsheet tasks. Next we ask which characters most influence the Assistant. We find the Assistant adopts behaviors more often from characters that resemble it (e.g., helpful rather than dismissive). We call this the affinity effect. The effect extends to other personas elicited with system prompts: unhelpful personas adopt behaviors from unhelpful characters. We also observe it in finetuned base models. We use the affinity effect to learn how models represent the Assistant. We find the Assistant adopts behaviors more from characters affiliated with elite universities (e.g., Yale) than non-elite ones. This implies the model's internal representation of the Assistant is more similar to humans from elite universities. Overall, the Assistant can be influenced by stories that depict only human characters (no AIs), which may conflict with the Persona Selection Model for the Assistant.

</details>


### [174] [Relatively Smart II: Tractable or Semi-Supervised Instance-Optimal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10886)
*Shaddin Dughmi,Alireza F. Pour*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ERM is relatively smart for distribution-free binary classification, matching OIG guarantees with a quadratic sample trade-off; a semi-supervised extension is information-theoretically possible with quadratic unlabeled blowup but no labeled blowup, yet tractability suffers due to oracle complexity; leaves open whether unlabeled-only blowups can be achieved in practice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether natural learners like ERM attain relatively smart guarantees and whether unlabeled data can induce sample complexity blowups; extend the concept to semi-supervised learning and characterize computational costs.

Method: Theoretical analysis proving ERM (and any proper consistent learner) is relatively smart in distribution-free binary classification; generalizing One-Inclusion Graph to a leave-most-out transductive setting for semi-supervised learning; establishing unlabeled-sample quadratic blowup with no increase in labeled data; proving an oracle-based intractability lower bound for sub-quadratic labeled growth.

Result: (1) ERM and proper consistent learners are relatively smart in the distribution-free setting; certifiable error m implies uniform error on a random O(m^2)-sized sample, yielding a cover of size ≤ 2^{m+1} and enabling O(m^2)-sample guarantees; (2) semi-supervised relatively smart learning is information-theoretically possible with a quadratic blowup in unlabeled samples and no blowup in labeled samples via a leave-most-out transduction generalization of OIG; (3) under agnostic ERM access, any semi-supervised relatively smart learner with substantially sub-quadratic labeled blowup requires super-polynomial oracle calls, even with explicit marginals, indicating an intractability result for distribution-fixed learning.

Conclusion: Relatively smart learning can be achieved by ERM and a semi-supervised extension, but practical deployment is hindered by tractability barriers; future work should seek algorithms with efficient oracle use and explore whether unlabeled-data-only blowups can be realized in more tractable frameworks.

Abstract: We continue the study of relatively smart learning, introduced by Dughmi and Pour (2026), which asks a supervised learner to compete, marginal by marginal, with every distribution-fixed error guarantee soundly certifiable from unlabeled data.
