They showed that the One-Inclusion Graph (OIG) learner is relatively smart with a quadratic sample-complexity blowup, and that no relatively smart learner can do better, leaving open whether ERM or another natural or tractable learner achieves comparable guarantees. They also left open whether the blowup can be restricted to unlabeled data.
  Our firs results shows that ERM---and in fact any proper consistent learner---is relatively smart for binary classification in the distribution-free setting. We show that a small certifiable error with $m$ samples implies a similarly small error on the uniform distribution over a random sample of size $O(m^2)$, yielding a cover of size at most $2^{m+1}$ on that sample. This suffices to control the error of proper consistent learners with $O(m^2)$ samples.
  We then show that semi-supervised relatively smart learning is information-theoretically possible with a quadratic blowup only in unlabeled sample complexity and no blowup in labeled sample complexity. The learner uses a natural generalization of OIG to a leave-most-out transductive problem, where labels of part of a finite pool are revealed and the remaining labels are predicted.
  Finally, this label efficiency comes at a cost in simplicity and tractability. If the hypothesis class is accessed only through an agnostic ERM oracle, any semi-supervised relatively smart learner with substantially sub-quadratic labeled-sample blowup requires super-polynomially many oracle calls. This holds even when the marginal is given explicitly, and thus also yields an intractability result for distribution-fixed learning that may be of independent interest.

</details>


### [175] [AUC Maximization from Biased Positive-unlabeled Data with Confidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10928)
*Atsutoshi Kumagai,Tomoharu Iwata,Hiroshi Takahashi,Taishi Nishiyama,Kazuki Adachi,Yasuhiro Fujiwara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method to maximize AUC from biased positive-unlabeled data by using confidence scores on the positives; it yields Bayes-optimal AUC ranking under any strictly increasing transform of the true posterior and shows strong empirical performance on eight real datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In imbalanced binary classification, AUC is a standard objective, but obtaining negative data is often hard due to privacy or expertise constraints. Biased PU data violates the usual unbiased positive sampling assumption, hindering AUC optimization. A method that handles bias using available confidence can enable reliable AUC learning in such settings.

Method: Derives an estimator for the AUC risk using biased PU data with associated confidence for labeled positives. Demonstrates that the rewritten AUC risk yields Bayes-optimal AUC ranking even when confidence is any strictly increasing transformation of the true posterior. Empirically, the approach is validated on eight real-world datasets.

Result: The proposed estimator effectively maximizes AUC from biased PU data with confidence, achieving favorable empirical performance on eight real-world datasets.

Conclusion: AUC maximization from biased PU data is feasible when confidence information is leveraged. The method preserves Bayes-optimal ranking under broad transformations of confidence and shows practical effectiveness, suggesting usefulness in privacy- or annotation-constrained applications.

Abstract: Maximizing the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) is a standard approach to imbalanced binary classification. Although positive and negative data are required for maximizing the AUC, negative data are often difficult to collect in some real-world applications due to privacy concerns or the need for specialized expertise to annotate them. Thus, AUC maximization from positive and unlabeled (PU) data has been attracting attention. Existing methods assume that labeled positive data are unbiased samples from the true positive distribution. However, this ideal assumption is often violated in practice. In this paper, we propose a method to maximize the AUC from biased PU data. To address the bias, our key idea is to exploit {\it confidence}, i.e., the probability that an instance is positive, associated with the small number of labeled positive data. We derive an estimator of the AUC risk using biased PU data with confidence, enabling AUC maximization under such bias. We further show that the rewritten AUC risk induces a Bayes-optimal AUC ranking even when the available confidence is any strictly increasing transformation of the true posterior probability. We experimentally show the effectiveness of our method on eight real-world datasets.

</details>


### [176] [Measuring the Value of World-Model Updates: A Counterfactual Utility Protocol for Continual Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10954)
*Anqi Peter Li,Kaden Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A fork ledger provides counterfactual evaluation of updates in continual reinforcement learning by forking the deployment stream at decision points into matched update and hold continuations, comparing their returns (ΔR) under shared randomness. Fixed update schedules degrade performance on CartPole, Walker, and Cheetah; a set of 693 non-collapsing forks shows mostly unchanged or unresolved outcomes. Each task contributes 240 attempted forks across 5 pretrained checkpoints and 2 drift directions. The ledger enables judging triggers by the real utility of updates rather than surprise alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify the value of model updates in continual world models and overcome limitations of fixed replay schedules or prediction-error triggers that do not reveal the counterfactual value of individual updates.

Method: Introduce the fork ledger: at pre-registered decision points, branch deployment into update and hold continuations under common random numbers; evaluate both on the same episodes and record ΔR = R_update − R_hold; aggregate across tasks (CartPole, Walker, Cheetah) and forks (planned versus actual, including how many forks collapse or diverge).

Result: Applying a fixed update mechanism lowers return on all three tasks: CartPole −144.0 (95% CI [−185.4, −116.1], converged near 650), Walker −82.8 (CI [−101.1, −61.7]), Cheetah −18.6 (CI [−29.0, −6.6]). Divergence is observed; 693 of 720 forks did not collapse. CartPole and Walker remain negative in sign (−113.4, −82.1) while Cheetah is unresolved (−3.9; CI [−17.5, +13.0]). Each task contributes 240 attempted forks (5 pretrained checkpoints × 2 drift directions).

Conclusion: The ledger renders counterfactual utility observable for a fixed mechanism, enabling evaluation of triggers by the updates they select rather than by surprise detection alone.

Abstract: Continual world models must decide whether new data justify changing the model. Fixed replay schedules and prediction-error triggers specify when to update, but neither reveals the value of an individual update: one deployment run cannot show how the same model would have performed at that moment had it held its parameters. We introduce the fork ledger, which branches a deployment stream at pre-registered decision points into matched update and hold continuations under common random numbers. It evaluates both continuations on the same episodes and records $ΔR = R_{\mathrm{update}} - R_{\mathrm{hold}}$. Always applying one fixed update mechanism lowers return on all three simulated control tasks: CartPole ($-144.0$; checkpoint-bootstrap $95\%$ CI $[-185.4,-116.1]$, against a converged return near $650$), Walker ($-82.8$; $[-101.1,-61.7]$) and Cheetah ($-18.6$; $[-29.0,-6.6]$). Divergence is an outcome of applying the update, so the estimand counts every attempted fork; restricted to the $693$ of $720$ that did not collapse, CartPole and Walker are unchanged in sign ($-113.4$ and $-82.1$) and Cheetah becomes unresolved ($-3.9$; $[-17.5,+13.0]$). The task is the unit of inference: each contributes $240$ attempted forks over five pretrained checkpoints crossed with two drift directions. The ledger makes counterfactual utility observable for a fixed mechanism, allowing triggers to be judged by the updates they select rather than by surprise detection alone.

</details>


### [177] [When More Is Not Better: Component Anti-Synergy in a P300 Speller](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10961)
*Lucas Yang,Rui Liu,Fusheng Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: In P300 BCI spellers, adding multiple promising components does not guarantee better performance; calibration dominates; EA helps with zero calibration; LM benefits depend on EEG pipeline quality; potential anti-synergy; an all-on pipeline is not universally better.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test the 'more is better' assumption for component integration in P300 BCI spellers by exhaustively evaluating four components in a full-factorial design on a public dataset.

Method: Four-component full-factorial experiment varying inclusion of Euclidean Alignment (EA), xDAWN spatial filtering, subject calibration, and language model priors; dataset: public P300; metrics: accuracy, repetitions, ITR; analysis via mixed-effects models.

Result: Component values are conditional rather than additive. Calibration is the strongest contributor; EA mitigates lack of calibration; adding other components can reduce performance via anti-synergy; LM support is not universally beneficial and can depend on EEG pipeline strength; larger LM shows similar pattern.

Conclusion: Design should be selective, tailoring spatial and language-support components to the quality of available EEG evidence; avoid maximal all-in pipelines; exploit synergy when components complement rather than interfere; consider calibration as a priority.

Abstract: P300 brain-computer interface (BCI) spellers can provide hands-free communication for people with severe motor impairments. Modern pipelines combine multiple individually promising components, often assuming that 'more-is-better'. We tested this assumption using a four-component full-factorial experiment varying the inclusion of Euclidean Alignment (EA), xDAWN spatial filtering, subject calibration, and language model priors on a public P300 dataset. Performance was evaluated using accuracy, repetitions, and information transfer rate (ITR) with mixed-effects models. Results show that the value of components is conditional rather than additive. Calibration was the strongest singular contributor, while EA compensated for its absence in zero-calibration settings. Adding independently useful components could also reduce performance, revealing component anti-synergy. Contrary to conventional wisdom, LM support was not universally beneficial: its effect depends strongly on the strength of the underlying EEG pipeline, while results from a larger LM showed a similar pattern. Together, these findings challenge maximal 'all-on' pipeline design and highlight the value of selecting spatial and language-support components according to the quality of available EEG evidence.

</details>


### [178] [Phases in a class of associative memories via hidden neurons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10976)
*Toshihiro Ota,Masato Taki*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Unified analysis of Hopfield-like associative memory via class H bipartite architecture; polynomial load yields replica-symmetric phase diagrams and closed-form capacities; exponential load with softmax hidden layer maps to random-energy-model counting; two roles for Lagrangians: visible fixes stability, hidden fixes storage scale; crosstalk statistics transition from central-limit (polynomial) to large-deviation (exponential); heating causes quantized attention reassignments and metastability of Gaussian patterns in all loads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand how to fix the storage scale across polynomial and exponential regimes and how Lagrangian choices control retrieval stability and memory capacity in bipartite Hopfield architectures.

Method: Replica analysis for polynomial load yielding replica-symmetric phase diagrams and capacities; analysis of crosstalk moments, showing matching between Ising and spherical visible neurons; for softmax hidden layer with exponential load, construct a copy representation and map thermodynamics to random-energy-model counting; identify paramagnetic, condensed, and frozen phases; study effects of heating on retrieval; compare crosstalk statistics (central limit vs large deviations).

Result: Replica-symmetric phase diagrams and closed-form capacities at polynomial load; crosstalk statistics shared by Ising/spherical visible neurons; at exponential load, storage scale governed by hidden Lagrangian and mapped to random-energy model counting; retrieval destabilized by heating through quantized reassignments of attention; typical Gaussian patterns remain metastable at all loads; two-axis decomposition of retrieval: visible Lagrangian determines stability, hidden Lagrangian sets storage scale.

Conclusion: The class H architecture decouples retrieval into two roles—visible Lagrangian fixes stability and hidden Lagrangian fixes storage scale—providing a unified framework to compare polynomial and exponential regimes and guiding the design of new Lagrangians to tailor stability and capacity in associative memory.

Abstract: Associative memory in the Hopfield network is attractor dynamics in a disordered many-body system, and higher-order and exponential extensions turn its retrieval update into softmax attention. The polynomial and exponential regimes have been analyzed by different methods, with no common architecture in which to ask what fixes the storage scale. In this paper we study the bipartite architecture of Krotov and Hopfield, which we call the class $H$, whose model is fixed by a Lagrangian for each layer, taking the hidden neurons as the order parameter of retrieval. At polynomial load the replica method yields the replica-symmetric phase diagrams and closed-form capacities, and the crosstalk moment is common to Ising and spherical visible neurons, so their differences come from the visible entropy. With a softmax hidden layer the load is exponential, and a copy representation maps the thermodynamics onto random-energy-model counting, with paramagnetic, condensed, and frozen phases. Heating destabilizes retrieval by quantized reassignments of attention, and typical Gaussian patterns remain metastable at every load. The regimes differ in their crosstalk statistics, central-limit at polynomial load and large-deviation at exponential load, and the class $H$ splits retrieval into two roles, the visible Lagrangian fixing stability and the hidden one the storage scale, two axes that may also guide the design of new Lagrangians.

</details>


### [179] [EGGROLL, Unrolled: Understanding and Improving Low-Rank Evolution Strategies at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10980)
*Ege C. Kaya,Abolfazl Hashemi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Low-rank Gaussian perturbations in ES for LLMs (EGGROLL) are analyzed with finite-rank perturbations; a new leave-one-out estimator (LOO-ROLL) improves estimator MSE and practical performance. Finite-rank corrections are characterized; exactness on quadratics; rank-one perturbations increase variance modestly; empirical gains on GSM8K and transformer benchmarks with diminishing returns for higher ranks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Make evolution strategies practical for very large language models by using low-rank perturbations; understand the mean update field, stability, and estimator variance; improve sample efficiency and performance.

Method: Derive the finite-rank mean update field via a resolvent applied to the smoothed objective; analyze finite-population estimator error; establish nonasymptotic variance bounds under smoothness; prove EGGROLL is exact on quadratics; propose LOO-ROLL to preserve the finite-rank field while reducing evaluations; compare at equal cost and wall time across transformer-scale models; empirical evaluation on GSM8K and transformer blocks.

Result: Exact on quadratic objectives for any rank/radius. For smooth objectives, first finite-rank correction is O(σ^2/r). Under a local affine model, rank-one perturbations increase gradient estimator variance by 2(m+n+1)/(mn+1) relative to dense Gaussian ES (0.098% for 4096×4096). LOO-ROLL halves estimator MSE at equal evaluation cost. Across ten post-training settings and models up to 8B params, LOO-ROLL improves seven outcomes with no significant losses. GSM8K accuracy improves from 38.1% to 63.0% at 0.6B and from 65.9% to 80.0% at 8B. Measurements align with finite-rank variance predictions; rank-eight shows no reproducible reward-based advantage.

Conclusion: Finite-rank perturbations enable practical ES for LLMs; LOO-ROLL offers meaningful estimator and performance gains with minimal downside; optimal benefits observed at low ranks (rank-one to few) with diminishing returns at higher ranks.

Abstract: EGGROLL makes evolution strategies (ES) practical for LLMs by replacing dense Gaussian weight perturbations with low-rank Gaussian products, often of rank one. This choice is computationally attractive but geometrically severe: each rank-one perturbation lies in a zero-volume subset of the ambient matrix space, despite having identity covariance. We characterize the mean EGGROLL update field at finite rank and nonzero perturbation radii, then analyze the error of its finite-population estimator. The population field is obtained by applying an explicit resolvent to the gradient of the objective smoothed by the perturbations. We show that the resolvent can introduce a nonconservative component and can reverse the local stability of an optimum. EGGROLL is nevertheless exact on every quadratic objective at every rank and radius. For smooth objectives, its first local finite-rank correction is $O(σ^2/r)$, and nonasymptotic bounds control the resulting field error under smoothness assumptions. Under a local affine model, rank-one perturbations increase the variance of the gradient estimator by only $\frac{2(m+n+1)}{mn+1}$ relative to dense Gaussian ES, or $0.098\%$ for a $4096\times4096$ matrix. We then introduce LOO-ROLL, a leave-one-out estimator that preserves the finite-rank population field while replacing EGGROLL's two antithetic evaluations per direction by one. At equal evaluation cost, LOO-ROLL halves estimator MSE in transformer blocks. At matched wall time across ten post-training settings and models up to 8B parameters, LOO-ROLL improves seven outcomes in individual paired tests, with no significant loss. On the GSM8K test set, accuracy increases from $38.1\%$ to $63.0\%$ at 0.6B and from $65.9\%$ to $80.0\%$ at 8B. Transformer measurements recover the predicted finite-rank variance, while the rank comparisons show no reproducible reward-based advantage for rank eight.

</details>


### [180] [Thompson Sampling for Non-Monotone Convex Ridge Bandits: Monotonicity Is Not Needed for Polynomial Regret](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10981)
*Xuan Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Monotone links are not necessary for Bayesian regret bounds of Thompson sampling in bandit convex optimization with ridge losses; however, the known d^{5/2} monotone bound may not extend to non-monotone links. The paper proves an upper bound of tilde O(d^{9/2} sqrt n) for exact-posterior TS with arbitrary convex, 1-Lipschitz, [0,1]-valued priors and non-monotone links, and shows the single-removal John-ellipsoid dichotomy fails by a twelve-point counterexample. It replaces this with an O(d^2) bound on “uninformative” configurations via a Boolean rounding argument, constructing d(d+1) uninformative losses to show tightness up to constants in the large-diameter regime. It also provides a self-contained information-ratio-to-regret transfer uniform over fixed measurable selections. Open question remains whether the d^{5/2} dependence can be retained in the non-monotone setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether the monotonicity assumption on the link in bandit convex optimization with ridge losses is essential for achieving the known Bayesian regret bounds for Thompson sampling, and to understand the optimal dependence on dimension under non-monotone links.

Method: Provide a qualitative negative answer: derive a d^{9/2}-type Bayesian regret bound for exact-posterior TS with non-monotone links; identify failure of the single-removal John-ellipsoid dichotomy; replace with an O(d^2) cardinality bound for uninformative configurations via Boolean rounding (a 0-1 matrix within 1/(4r) of a rank-r matrix has rank ≤ 2r−1); construct d(d+1) uninformative losses to show tightness; give a self-contained information-ratio-to-regret transfer uniform over fixed measurable selections.

Result: Bayesian regret for exact-posterior TS is O(((d+1)^4) sqrt(d n) log(e+nd diam K)) = tilde O(d^{9/2} sqrt n) for non-monotone convex ridge losses with [0,1]-valued priors; monotone d^{5/2} bound remains open; the John-ellipsoid dichotomy is replaced by an O(d^2) combinatorial bound; tightness in large-diameter regime demonstrated via d(d+1) uninformative losses; a uniform information-ratio-to-regret transfer is established.

Conclusion: Monotonicity of the link is not required for a Bayesian regret bound in this setting, but the d^{5/2} rate from the monotone case may not extend; the new combinatorial approach yields a universal upper bound of tilde O(d^{9/2} sqrt n) for non-monotone links, with tightness demonstrated in certain regimes. The open question is whether the better d^{5/2} dependence can be recovered under non-monotonicity.

Abstract: Bakhtiari, Lattimore and Szepesvári (COLT 2025) proved that Thompson sampling (TS) has Bayesian regret $\tilde O(d^{5/2}\sqrt n)$ for bandit convex optimisation with convex \emph{monotone} ridge losses $f(x)=\ell(\ip{x}θ)$, and asked whether monotonicity of the link is necessary. We give a qualitative negative answer. For every prior on $[0,1]$-valued, $1$-Lipschitz convex ridge losses with an arbitrary convex, possibly non-monotone, link, and for any fixed measurable selection of minimisers, exact-posterior TS has Bayesian regret $O\big((d+1)^4\sqrt{dn}\,\log(e+nd\max\{1,\diam K\})\big)=\tilde O(d^{9/2}\sqrt n)$. The monotone proof relies on a single-removal John-ellipsoid dichotomy; we show by an explicit twelve-point configuration that this dichotomy fails for non-monotone links, and replace it by an $O(d^2)$ cardinality bound for ``uninformative'' configurations. The bound uses a Boolean rounding argument: a $0$-$1$ matrix within $1/(4r)$ in max-norm of a rank-$r$ matrix has rank at most $2r-1$. We construct $d(d+1)$ uninformative losses, showing that the cardinality bound is tight up to constants in the large-diameter-to-gap regime, and give a self-contained information-ratio-to-regret transfer that is uniform over fixed measurable selections. Whether the $d^{5/2}$ dependence of the monotone case can be retained remains open.

</details>


### [181] [Importance Weighting for Unlabeled-unlabeled Learning under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10994)
*Atsutoshi Kumagai,Tomoharu Iwata,Hiroshi Takahashi,Taishi Nishiyama,Kazuki Adachi,Yasuhiro Fujiwara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A distribution-shift adaptation framework for UU learning using importance weighting with unlabeled train and test data, unifying PU/noise learning under distribution shift; does not assume shift type; validated on real datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses distribution shifts between training and testing in UU learning, where class-conditional densities differ, by reweighting training data to match the test distribution; leverages UU data to cover general learning scenarios.

Method: Apply importance weighting to UU data; estimate density ratios (test/train) using unlabeled samples; train a classifier by minimizing test risk via weighted training distribution; accommodates PU and noisy label learning within a single UU framework; does not assume covariate shift.

Result: Empirical validation on real-world datasets demonstrating effectiveness, showing the proposed method improves performance under distribution shift.

Conclusion: UU learning is a versatile framework; distribution-shift adaptation via importance weighting extends its applicability to PU and noisy-label settings, without assuming fixed shift types; practical when few test UU samples are available.

Abstract: Unlabeled-unlabeled (UU) learning allows us to learn a binary classifier from two sets of unlabeled data with different class-priors. It is a general framework because it includes a wide variety of supervised learning such as positive-unlabeled (PU) learning, noisy label learning, and similarity-based learning. Existing UU learning assumes that the test and training distributions have the same class-conditional densities. However, this assumption rarely holds in practice due to distribution shifts. This paper proposes a distribution shift adaptation method for UU learning that uses UU data in the training distribution and a few UU data in the test distribution. The proposed method is based on the importance weighting, which minimizes the test risk by using training data with estimated importance weights. Although existing importance weighting methods cannot handle UU data, we show that it can be done in a principled manner. Thanks to the generality of UU learning, our method can handle various learning problems such as PU and noisy label learning under distribution shift within a single framework while existing methods are usually tailored to a specific problem. Moreover, it does not require any assumption of the shift types such as covariate shift. We experimentally demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method with real-world datasets.

</details>


### [182] [Topological Necessities: Mechanism-Invariant Strategic Subgoals for Cross-Embodiment Goal-Conditioned Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11014)
*Hao Shi,Xi Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Topological, route-conditioned gates derived from offline trajectories enable executor-agnostic transfer and strong cross-task performance in long-horizon RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing subgoal signals are implicit artifacts of value functions or latent actions and are tied to the specific executor. A planner object—topological necessities—should be invariant to executor changes and recoverable from offline data to enable transfer across agents and tasks.

Method: Extract route-level, unavoidable stages via homology in dimensions 0 and 1 on a transport-weighted carrier built from successful trajectories. Define an enumerable gate set with shell-level certificates, i.e., topological necessities. Build a recursive topological gate hierarchy and test under a fixed, isomorphic free space. Transfer gates across agents/tasks without retraining (PointMaze to Ant/Humanoid).

Result: Gates survive executor replacement and enable high performance across benchmarks: PointMaze planner saturates (≈100%), Humanoid achieves 96.1 aggregate under unified interface (≈+36.0 over map-privileged baseline on multi-route task; p=1.4e-5). On multitask evaluation, AntMaze improves by ≈22.9, Kitchen by ≈15.8/12.6. Demonstrates transferability and competitive advantage over baselines across environments.

Conclusion: A topological gate framework yields transferable, necessary planning components extracted from offline data, providing robust, executor-agnostic performance gains and a unified interface for long-horizon, route-aware RL planning.

Abstract: Long-horizon goal-conditioned reinforcement learning delegates control to a high-level module that proposes subgoals, but existing subgoals are implicit byproducts of value functions or latent actions, tied to the executor that produced them. We study a different object: a route-conditioned order of unavoidable stages that every successful executor must traverse, recoverable from offline trajectories and belonging to none of them. Its defining properties are topological: an unskippable stage is a separating set that every admissible path must cross, and a loop in free space forces a route choice. We read the two by homology in dimensions 0 and 1 over a transport-weighted carrier built from successful trajectories, yielding an enumerable gate set with shell-level certificates; the certified gates are what we call topological necessities. Certified gates enter the decision loop as a recursive topological gate hierarchy. Under a fixed, isomorphic free space, the object survives executor replacement: gates frozen on PointMaze data transfer without retraining to Ant and Humanoid, attaining the highest Humanoid aggregate under a unified interface (96.1), with +36.0 over a map-privileged reference on the multi-route task (p=1.4e-5); the planner saturates PointMaze (100+/-0) and matches or exceeds the strongest baselines on AntMaze (giant +22.9) and Kitchen (+15.8/+12.6).

</details>


### [183] [T1: Terminal Agent Reinforcement Learning for Long-Horizon Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11042)
*Junyao Yang,Yucheng Shi,Zhongzhi Li,Ruhan Wang,Zongxia Li,Haitao Mi,Leowei Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A 122B Mixture-of-Experts RL agent (T1) operating in a real shell for 300+ tool-turn tasks, using TITO and R3 to stabilize training, achieving state-of-the-art long-horizon performance on Terminal-Bench 2.1 and Long-Horizon Terminal Bench with good generalization and reduced drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for robust, long-horizon autonomous agents that transfer to held-out tasks and resist benchmark overfitting, by stabilizing MoE-RL training and enabling real-world tool use.

Method: Aggressively warm-started actor-critic training with dense process rewards (counting verifiers); TITO: training on exact token identifiers with drift repair at turn boundaries and rollout routing replay; log per-token expert choices at every MoE layer and replay during training; fully out-of-distribution corpus with seeds and synthesized tasks disjoint from Terminal-Bench 2.1.

Result: Training-to-inference log-probability gap reduced from 0.021 to 0.013; exactly aligned zero token drift in the loss region; Terminal-Bench 2.1: base model improved from 43.8% to 64.0% resolved; Long-Horizon Terminal Bench: 27.9%, surpassing GPT-5.4 and GLM-5.1.

Conclusion: The combination of TITO and R3 provides stable optimization and genuine capability transfer for long-horizon tool-use tasks, yielding superior benchmark performance and reducing drift and overfitting risk.

Abstract: Agent usage is shifting toward long-horizon tasks such as coding and scientific discovery, among which terminal tasks are especially important. We introduce T1, a Mixture-of-Experts model of 122B total trained with reinforcement learning, operating a real shell in a cloud sandbox for up to 300+ tool-call turns per task, rewarded by executing each task's own verifier. We provide a comprehensive recipe: First, an aggressively warm-started to stabilize actor-critic training, with a dense process reward scoring trajectories by the absolute number of passing verifiers. Second, stable optimization through TITO construction, training on the exact sampled token identifiers with drift repair at turn boundaries, and rollout routing replay, recording the sampler's per-token expert choices at every MoE layer and replaying them during training. Third, fully out-of-distribution training corpus: isolated seeds and synthesized tasks disjoint from Terminal-Bench 2.1 ensures gains reflect genuine capability transfer over benchmark overfitting. Together, TITO and R3 cut the training-to-inference log-probability difference from 0.021 to 0.013, with exactly aligned zero token drift in the loss region. On Terminal-Bench 2.1, our post-train pipeline raises initial base model from 43.8% to T1 with 64.0% resolved. On Long-Horizon Terminal Bench, T1 reaches 27.9% and surpasses GPT-5.4 and GLM-5.1.

</details>


### [184] [EMMI: Edge Multi-Modal Intelligence for Communication-Efficient MLLM Inference via Fused Representation Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11058)
*Motahare Mounesan,Irfan Khan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Edge Multi-Modal Intelligence (EMMI) enables communication-efficient edge-to-server inference by encoding modality-specific features into a compact latent representation, reducing bandwidth 32x with similar accuracy and 3.4x latency gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resource-constrained edge deployment of multimodal LLMs suffers from high communication, compute, and memory demands; a representation-centric approach can reduce data transmitted while enabling high-capacity server-side reasoning.

Method: At the edge, perform modality-specific encoding, cross-modal representation fusion, and learned compression to produce a fixed-size latent representation; transmit this compact latent to server resources where a large MLLM performs reasoning.

Result: On a representative multimodal benchmark, EMMI reduces the communication payload by 32x while maintaining comparable downstream accuracy, and achieves up to a 3.4x reduction in estimated end-to-end inference latency under bandwidth-constrained edge conditions.

Conclusion: A representation-centric, fixed-size interface design enables efficient, privacy-preserving edge-to-server MLLM inference, balancing communication efficiency with accuracy; suitable for heterogeneous edge devices and server resources; invites further exploration of trade-offs and privacy guarantees.

Abstract: Recent advances in multimodal large language mod- els (MLLMs) have opened new opportunities for edge intelligence by enabling reasoning across heterogeneous sensor modalities, such as vision, text, and telemetry data. However, deploying these capabilities on resource-constrained edge platforms remains challenging due to the substantial computational, memory, and communication demands of modern MLLMs. Rather than transmitting raw sensor observations or partitioning neural networks at intermediate layers, Edge Multi-Modal Intelligence (EMMI) communicates a compact representation between edge devices and server resources, enabling communication-efficient edge MLLM inference. To achieve this, EMMI performs modality-specific encoding, cross-modal representation fusion, and learned compression at the edge, transmitting only a compact latent representation to server-side resources for high-capacity MLLM reasoning. This representation-centric design reduces communication overhead, preserves local data privacy, and provides a fixed-size interface between heterogeneous edge devices and server-side MLLMs. Evaluation on a representative multimodal benchmark demonstrates that EMMI can reduce the communication payload by 32x while maintaining comparable downstream accuracy, resulting in up to a 3.4x reduction in estimated end-to-end inference latency under bandwidth-constrained edge conditions.

</details>


### [185] [The information geometry of large language models is shared, learned, and controllable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11063)
*Dario Picozzi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Fisher-Rao geometry of next-token probabilities reveals a shared, output-preserving structure across model architectures, enabling transferable, minimal-disturbance control of behavior and robust predictions of performance and knowledge acquisition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To uncover a common geometric basis underlying diverse neural language models, linking behavior, activation coordinates, and read-out processes, and to enable controllable interventions with limited side effects.

Method: Empirical analysis across Transformer, state-space, and recurrent architectures. The study compares output geometry (Fisher-Rao of next-token distributions) with activation geometry, examines agreement with human word choices, and assesses calibration effects. It links token probabilities with read-out geometry to spectrum and effective dimension, conducts controlled language assignments, randomized experiments, and donor-prompt transfer tests. It analyzes pretraining corpus statistics for fact acquisition, and evaluates minimum-disturbance local interventions and their cost, plus transferability across prompts and tasks (steering, editing, attribution, dictionary learning, fine-tuning).

Result: Output geometries align more strongly across architectures than activation geometries, and shared geometry supports semantic-category transfer. Agreement with human word choices increases with predictive accuracy, scale, and training, and improves after model-only calibration. Token probabilities and read-out geometry jointly predict the spectrum and its effective dimension. Language-law adherence is demonstrated across architectures. Pretraining statistics predict held-out fact acquisition without recalibration; deeper evidence delays acquisition. Geometry prescribes minimum-disturbance interventions with lower cost, enables reusable control via donor-prompt updates that transfer to unseen prompts while better preserving reference prompts than Euclidean control. The same geometric correction improves steering, editing, attribution, dictionary learning, and fine-tuning."

Conclusion: A unified geometric framework (Fisher-Rao) captures shared structure across diverse LLM architectures and provides principled, transferable, low-disruption methods for controlling and understanding model behavior, with predictive links to performance, knowledge acquisition, and downstream tasks.

Abstract: Large language models learn similar behaviours, yet it remains unclear what structure they share or how to change one behaviour without disturbing others. The Fisher-Rao geometry of next-token probabilities connects these questions: behaviour determines this geometry up to output-preserving symmetries, whereas activation geometry depends on coordinates. Across transformer, state-space and recurrent models, output geometries agree more strongly than activation geometries, and shared geometry supports semantic-category transfer. Agreement with human word choices increases with predictive accuracy, scale and training, and improves further after model-only calibration. Token probabilities and read-out geometry jointly predict the spectrum and its effective dimension. Controlled language assignments show that geometry follows the language law across architectures. Pretraining corpus statistics predict held-out fact acquisition without recalibration, while randomised experiments show that deeper evidence substantially delays acquisition across every tested architecture and evidence construction. Finally, the geometry prescribes minimum-disturbance local interventions, predicts their relative cost, and supports reusable control: updates learned on donor prompts transfer to unseen prompts while better preserving behaviour on reference prompts than Euclidean control. The same geometric correction improves steering, editing, attribution, dictionary learning and fine-tuning.

</details>


### [186] [Beyond Solver Verdicts: Generative Reward Models for Autoformalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11085)
*Vikash Singh,Debargha Ganguly,Aman Goel,Ali Torkamani,Xiaoxue Han,Joseph Lilien,Ferhat Erata,Vipin Chaudhary*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GenV provides a reference-free verifier for verdict-preserving unfaithfulness in neurosymbolic systems by distilling a Z3 equivalence oracle into an LM-based score; achieves 0.961 AUROC, zero-shot generalization across translators/styles, and 11.3-point downstream gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address Verdict-Preserving-Unfaithfulness (VPU) where incorrect encodings yield correct verdicts. Verdict-only heuristics are mathematically bounded to fail to detect such traces; a robust, reference-free verification strategy is needed.

Method: Distill an offline Z3-equivalence oracle into a continuous reference-equivalence score using the language model's vocabulary space. Employ mechanistic analysis via decision-projected logit lenses and sparse autoencoders to extract precise spatial error coordinates without explicit localization training. Develop GenV and the verifier GenV+HN (oracle-mined) for reference-equivalence verification.

Result: GenV+HN achieves 0.961 AUROC on reference-equivalence verification, generalizes zero-shot across unseen translators and divergent formal styles, and yields an 11.3-point improvement in downstream accuracy for agentic test-time compute allocation.

Conclusion: Generative verification (GenV) provides a robust, reference-free means to detect true equivalence between encodings and designated formalizations, surpassing structural heuristics and enabling localization of errors; shows strong generalization and practical gains in resource allocation.

Abstract: Neurosymbolic systems rely on mathematical solvers to guarantee reasoning correctness, yet solvers are fundamentally blind to whether a formal translation maintains strict reference-equivalence to a designated formalization. We formalize this vulnerability as Verdict-Preserving-Unfaithfulness (VPU): a failure mode where an incorrect encoding executes successfully and matches the expected verdict. We theoretically prove that structural, verdict-only verification heuristics are mathematically bounded to chance-level detection on these deceptively valid traces. To resolve this, we introduce Generative Verification (GenV), which distills an offline Z3-equivalence oracle into a reference-free, continuous reference-equivalence score by repurposing the language model's native vocabulary space. Mechanistic analysis via decision-projected logit lenses and sparse autoencoders shows this generative readout natively extracts precise spatial error coordinates without explicit localization training. Empirically, our oracle-mined verifier (GenV+HN) achieves 0.961 AUROC in reference-equivalence verification, generalizes zero-shot across unseen translators and divergent formal styles, and yields an 11.3-point downstream accuracy gain in agentic test-time compute allocation.

</details>


### [187] [HERALD: High-Fidelity Exemplar Retrieval with Adaptive Landmark Distillation for Heterophily-Aware Graph Condensation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11123)
*Sujan Chakraborty,Priyanka Saha,Saptarshi Bej*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HERALD is a gradient-free graph condensation method that adapts to heterophily by learning scores and features via Fisher-discriminability and activation-density, using a heterophily-aware node scoring scheme based on prototype representativeness, decision-boundary proximity, and Local Intrinsic Dimensionality (LID); it builds a compact subgraph under BONSAI’s budget via score-ordered BFS and PPR pruning, achieving higher fidelity on heterophilic graphs while remaining competitive on homophilic graphs across four GNNs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing condensers rely on Weisfeiler–Lehman neighborhood aggregation or gradient-based distribution matching, both of which assume label similarity among neighboring nodes. This assumption breaks under heterophily, leading to poor condensed graphs. A gradient-free, heterophily-adaptive condensation method is needed.

Method: HERALD performs feature selection using a joint Fisher-discriminability and activation-density criterion to down-weight aggregated representations on heterophilic graphs. Node scores are a weighted combination of prototype representativeness, decision-boundary proximity, and Local Intrinsic Dimensionality (LID); weights are driven by a smooth sigmoid of the graph's heterophily ratio. Nodes are assembled into a condensed subgraph via score-ordered BFS expansion, Personalized PageRank pruning, and class rebalancing, all under the same storage budget as BONSAI.

Result: Empirical evaluation on eight benchmark datasets spanning both homophilic and heterophilic settings shows that HERALD matches or outperforms state-of-the-art condensers on heterophilic graphs and remains competitive on homophilic graphs across four GNN architectures.

Conclusion: HERALD offers a gradient-free, heterophily-adaptive graph condensation framework that achieves high-fidelity downstream performance under a fixed storage budget, demonstrating robust generalization across heterophilic and homophilic graphs and multiple GNNs.

Abstract: Graph condensation aims to produce a small surrogate graph that preserves the downstream node-classification performance of a much larger original graph. Existing methods rely on Weisfeiler-Lehman neighbourhood aggregation or gradient-based distribution matching, both of which assume that adjacent nodes share the same label, an assumption that breaks down under heterophily. We propose HERALD (High-fidelity Exemplar Retrieval with Adaptive Landmark Distillation), a gradient-free graph condensation framework that adapts the node scoring and feature selection in the condensation pipeline to the graph's measured heterophily. HERALD selects features via a joint Fisher-discriminability and activation-density criterion that down-weights aggregated representations on heterophilic graphs, and scores nodes by a weighted combination of prototype representativeness, decision-boundary proximity, and Local Intrinsic Dimensionality (LID), where the weights are driven by a smooth sigmoid function of the heterophily ratio. Nodes are then assembled into a condensed subgraph through score-ordered BFS expansion, Personalised PageRank pruning, and class rebalancing, all at an identical storage budget to BONSAI, enabling direct comparison. Experiments on eight benchmark datasets spanning homophilic and heterophilic settings show that HERALD matches or outperforms state-of-the-art condensers on heterophilic graphs and remains competitive on homophilic ones across four GNN architectures.

</details>


### [188] [How Wrong Can a Good Predictor Be? Diverging Updates with Vanishing Predictive KL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11132)
*Qifu Wen,Shuaijun Liu,Zihan Zhou,Xi Zeng,Ningxin Su*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Even with an unbounded internal update gap in a stationary symmetric Gaussian HMM, predictive KL can vanish for any fixed K>=2; exact Bayesian mixing and a deterministic radial filter align on the same belief coordinates, and gaps affect internal coordinates but not ultimate predictive cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether accurate posterior prediction requires accurate Bayesian updates; quantify relationship between update map gaps and predictive loss; study compression and decoder sensitivity.

Method: Constructed proof in Gaussian HMM with K-1 belief coordinates; compare exact Bayesian mixing to a radial filter; derive asymptotics as q->0+, analyze centered logits, L_K(q), and KL divergences; study terminal KL over trajectory; perform Gaussian cover experiments over K in {2,4,8}; analyze confidence cones and noise control; discuss saturating vs nonsaturating recurrences.

Result: Unbounded update gap coexists with vanishing predictive KL for fixed K>=2; separation in centered logits grows at least linearly in L_K(q) while D_KL(exact||radial) -> 0; expected terminal KL -> 0 at horizon H(q) ~ ceil(-log q / c) + 1; typical blocks push both filters into common confidence cone; single Gaussian maximal event drives adaptive noise; experiments across K show opposing trends; binary controls at long horizons compare recurrences.

Conclusion: Internal update gaps do not imply predictive failure; need to identify how separating states contribute to loss and decoder sensitivity; the construction is fixed in K and does not generalize to universal criterion for when compression is harmless or when gaps incur task loss.

Abstract: Accurate posterior prediction need not require accurate approximation of Bayesian updates. We prove that an unbounded gap between the update maps can coexist with vanishing predictive KL for every fixed finite $K\ge2$ in a stationary symmetric Gaussian HMM. Exact Bayesian mixing and an explicit deterministic radial filter act on the same $K-1$ belief coordinates. As $q\to0^+$, their separation in centered logits in the worst case grows at least linearly in the natural confidence scale $L_K(q)$, while their categorical $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathrm{exact}\|\mathrm{radial})$ vanishes at the same explicit witness. Along stationary HMM trajectories, the expected terminal KL between filtered posteriors also converges to zero at $H(q)=\lceil-\log(q)/c\rceil+1$. Typical blocks without switches drive both filters into a common confidence cone, where softmax curvature suppresses their disagreement; a single Gaussian maximal event controls adaptive noise. A sweep with equally spaced Gaussians over $K\in\{2,4,8\}$ illustrates the opposing trends, and binary controls at long horizons compare saturating and nonsaturating recurrences. The result isolates two missing links between internal update gaps and predictive cost: the contribution of separating states to expected loss and decoder sensitivity. Thus even an unbounded internal update gap does not by itself certify predictive failure. The construction is fixed in $K$ and does not provide a universal criterion for when compression is harmless or characterize when internal gaps must incur task loss.

</details>


### [189] [Phase-Decoupled, Model-Calibrated Power Control for Disaggregated LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11133)
*Jae Gon Kim,Donghoon Yoo,Hanyul Ryu,Sungho Ha,Juyeon Lee,Soojung Ryu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A phase-decoupled, model-calibrated controller optimizes energy and latency for disaggregated PD inference lanes in MoE LLM serving, surpassing vendor Max-Q performance and meeting SLOs across models by per-lane, model-aware calibration rather than a one-size-fits-all GPU class setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GPU power is the bottleneck for datacenter LLM serving; disaggregation of prefill/decode along with vendor power profiles (e.g., Max-Q) yields suboptimal throughput and latency behavior that is model-dependent. There is a need for per-lane, model-aware power/clock control that preserves latency guarantees (SLOs) while improving energy efficiency.

Method: Propose a phase-decoupled controller that partitions PD into a prefill lane (latency-guaranteed SM-clock floor) and a decode lane (adaptive power cap just above a throughput/latency cliff). Calibrate per-model/quantization/hardware combination; implement an automatic, latency-gated calibration with runtime SLO guard instead of fixed recipes; validate on 8x B200 with Qwen3-Coder-480B (FP8) and Qwen3-235B-A22B (NVFP4); compare against Max-Q and analyze A/B decode actuator; perform three-day sustained run.

Result: Balanced mode yields +20.4% tokens/J with +3.5% end-to-end latency versus +8.6% tokens/J at +5.2% latency for Max-Q on Qwen3-Coder-480B; on Qwen3-235B-A22B, all modes meet ITL p99 SLO while vendor profiles fail; decode-cap calibration dominates energy savings, with 32.3% energy reduction observed over a lane pair in a three-day run; MoE models show ~5x advantage over dense models in energy efficiency, constraining claims to MoE serving.

Conclusion: Optimal power settings depend on the deployed model, quantization, engine, and hardware; per-lane profiles with latency-aware calibration and runtime SLO guards yield Pareto improvements in energy efficiency and latency for disaggregated PD serving on MoE LLMs, outperforming fixed GPU-class profiles; the approach emphasizes model-aware control and robust SLO adherence.

Abstract: Datacenter GPU power is the binding constraint on LLM serving capacity, and production serving has shifted to prefill/decode (PD) disaggregation. Deploying NVIDIA's Max-Q inference profile on a disaggregated B200 system, we found its realized gain modest (+8.6% tokens/J), model-dependent, and carrying a mean end-to-end latency cost (+5.2%) that throughput-only evaluation does not surface; the profile also applies one setting to prefill and decode GPUs that operate in opposite hardware regimes. We hypothesize that the optimal power setting is a property of the deployed (model, quantization, engine, hardware) combination rather than of the GPU class, that each lane warrants its own profile, and that converting SLO headroom into energy safely requires latency-gated calibration under a runtime SLO guard rather than a fixed recipe. We present a phase-decoupled, model-calibrated controller: the prefill lane runs under an SM-clock window whose floor is a latency guarantee by construction, and the decode lane under a power cap placed by automatic calibration just above a measured throughput/latency cliff. Because a disaggregated decode lane draws flat, memory-bound power, the cap binds continuously, the reactive-overshoot weakness that led POLCA to reject capping is absent, and the GPU's own power manager retains throughput under the cap. On an 8x B200 node serving Qwen3-Coder-480B (FP8) under agentic load, our balanced mode delivers +20.4% tokens/J at +3.5% mean e2e versus +8.6% at +5.2% for Max-Q, a Pareto improvement on both axes. On Qwen3-235B-A22B (NVFP4) every operating mode meets the ITL-p99 SLO in every repetition; both vendor profiles miss it. A decode-actuator A/B shows the calibrated cap beats static clock locks, and a three-day sustained run saves 32.3% of a lane pair's electricity. Both models are MoE; a dense model recovers roughly 5x less, so we scope our claims to MoE serving.

</details>


### [190] [Bidirectional Multimodal Fusion of Sky Images and Time-Series for Solar Forecasting with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11135)
*Ken Chen,Maneesha Perera,Wei Wang,Sachith Seneviratne,Hansani Weeratunge,Saman Halgamuge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SolCloudLLM is a multimodal LLM-based forecasting framework that fuses sky-image patches with time-series data to improve short-term PV power and GHI forecasts. It uses bidirectional multimodal fusion to create unified representations mapped into an LLM’s embedding space, achieving superior MSE performance, especially under cloudy conditions and in few-shot settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecast errors in PV and GHI forecasting at short horizons are dominated by cloud ramps. Ground-based sky imagery provides a physically informative signal that complements numerical time-series data. Forecast performance is highly site-specific and data-scarce, necessitating data-efficient, multimodal methods that can leverage limited data.

Method: The approach aligns patches from sky images with corresponding time-series patches, fuses them via bidirectional multimodal fusion to form a unified representation, and maps this representation into the embedding space of a large language model (LLM) for forecasting. It is evaluated on the SIRTA and SKIPP'D datasets.

Result: SolCloudLLM consistently outperforms strong baselines in MSE across forecasting horizons, with up to 25.4% relative MSE reduction. Benefits are predominantly observed under cloudy conditions. It achieves the best performance in nearly all few-shot settings, while other deep learning baselines degrade and traditional non-learning physical methods remain competitive in some scenarios.

Conclusion: Integrating sky imagery with numerical time-series via a bidirectional multimodal fusion layer and embedding-mapping into an LLM yields robust, data-efficient PV/GHI forecasts, particularly under cloud-driven ramps and limited data. The approach highlights the value of multimodal signals in site-specific, short-horizon solar forecasting.

Abstract: Short-term photovoltaic (PV) power and global horizontal irradiance (GHI) forecasts are essential for effective dispatch, reserve scheduling, and grid operations. At these forecasting horizons, errors are predominantly driven by cloud induced ramps: relying solely on historical numerical data may struggle to anticipate an incoming cloud, making ground-based sky images a crucial complementary physical signal. Furthermore, forecast performance is highly sensitive to location and local observing conditions, creating a strong need for site-specific data that are often scarce. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated competitive performance and high data efficiency in time-series forecasting. Despite their success, existing LLM-based forecasting methods remain predominantly unimodal, relying primarily on historical numerical time-series data. Effectively incorporating sky imagery into an LLM-based forecasting framework remains under-explored and an open challenge. In this paper, we propose SolCloudLLM, an LLM-based multimodal forecasting framework. SolCloudLLM aligns sky-image patches with time-series patches and fuses their corresponding representations through bidirectional multimodal fusion, yielding a unified representation that is subsequently mapped into the embedding space of an LLM. Extensive experiments on the SIRTA and SKIPP'D datasets demonstrate that SolCloudLLM consistently outperforms the best baseline methods in MSE across all forecasting horizons, achieving a maximum relative MSE reduction of 25.4%. Stratified analysis further indicates that the benefits of multimodal fusion are concentrated primarily under cloudy conditions. Notably, SolCloudLLM achieves the best performance in nearly all few-shot settings, whereas other deep learning baselines experience substantial performance degradation and are frequently outperformed by the non-learning physical method.

</details>


### [191] [LILA: Calibration-Free Structured Pruning of Large Language Models via Latent Spectral Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11163)
*Sankar Behera,Dhruv Singh,Anshika Agnihotri,Raj Kumar Choudhary,Satyadev Ahlawat,Yamuna Prasad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LILA uses a closed-form, data-free spectral criterion to rank neuron importance for structured pruning by comparing empirical singular value distributions of full vs neuron-ablated FFN weight matrices via KS distance; enables compression without data or training and achieves strong zero-shot performance, with competitive recovery after LoRA and theoretical NTK grounding; extending to dynamic KS-based sparsity budgets yields state-of-the-art preservation and reveals architectural bottlenecks at higher compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide hardware-efficient, data-free structured pruning for large language models, addressing reliance on calibration data, gradient computation, or auxiliary networks during pruning.

Method: Compute KS distance between the empirical singular value distributions of the FFN weight matrix before pruning and after neuron ablation; use this as a scoring rule to rank neuron importance; apply pruning without training or calibration data; optionally perform one epoch of LoRA recovery fine-tuning; extend to dynamic sparsity budgeting via KS-scores.

Result: LILA outperforms PruneNet at 25% sparsity by 1.57 percentage points in zero-shot LLaMA-2-7B; surpasses WikiText-2-calibrated SliceGPT by up to 6.0 pp across sparsity levels; after one epoch of LoRA recovery, matches the calibrated SliceGPT baseline within 0.48 pp on LLaMA-2-7B and Phi-2 with zero calibration data; Neural Tangent Kernel analysis shows 22× reduction in functional distortion vs random pruning; dynamic KS-budgeting yields state-of-the-art generative preservation at moderate compression and reveals single-layer bottlenecks at higher compression.

Conclusion: A data-free, spectral-pruning criterion can achieve strong, hardware-efficient pruning of LLMs without calibration data or training, with theoretical justification via NTK and practical benefits including competitive LoRA-finetuning performance and adaptive sparsity budgeting that exposes architectural bottlenecks.

Abstract: Structured pruning of large language models (LLMs) offers hardware-efficient compression, yet existing methods require calibration data, gradient computation, or large auxiliary policy networks at pruning time. LILA (\emph{Latent-Informed Layer Analysis}) scores neuron importance via the Kolmogorov--Smirnov (KS) distance between empirical singular value distributions of the full and neuron-ablated feed-forward network (FFN) weight matrix, providing a closed-form spectral rule requiring no training, calibration data, or auxiliary network. Without any fine-tuning, LILA surpasses PruneNet (45M-parameter RL policy) by 1.57~pp in zero-shot accuracy on LLaMA-2-7B at 25\% sparsity, and outperforms WikiText-2-calibrated SliceGPT by up to 6.0~pp across all sparsity levels, while preserving the original architecture. After one epoch of LoRA recovery fine-tuning, LILA achieves highly competitive performance, matching the heavily calibrated SliceGPT baseline to within a 0.48~pp margin across LLaMA-2-7B and Phi-2, despite using zero calibration data. A Neural Tangent Kernel analysis confirms a 22$\times$ reduction in functional distortion versus random pruning, providing theoretical grounding for the spectral importance criterion. Finally, extending LILA to dynamically allocate sparsity budgets via KS-scores yields state-of-the-art generative preservation at moderate compression, while uncovering fundamental single-layer architectural bottlenecks at higher compression regimes.

</details>


### [192] [When does a spectral prior help graph learning? Connectivity-loss estimation under road-network disruptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11166)
*Van-Truong Le*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GNNs with a residual correction to first-order Fiedler sensitivity predict algebraic-connectivity loss under multi-edge failures; tested on synthetic and OSM data; shows modest MAE gains; transfer is domain-sensitive; scalable with sparse spectral setup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fast, scalable assessment of spectral connectivity loss after multiple edge disruptions where exact spectral recomputation is costly; leverage a learned residual to a known analytical baseline (first-order Fiedler sensitivity) to achieve accurate yet efficient screening.

Method: Train and compare GCN, GraphSAGE, and edge-aware MPNN architectures to learn a bounded residual correction to the first-order Fiedler sensitivity. Evaluate across independent, spatially clustered, and edge-betweenness-targeted failures; use graph-disjoint synthetic splits and zero-shot transfer to 13 OpenStreetMap areas in six countries. Benchmark against analytical baselines and include second-order perturbation. Analyze transfer (including leave-one-country-out), sparse scaling to 20k nodes, and separation of spectral setup from amortized screening.

Result: Residual GCN improves spatial-failure MAE by 0.0391 (95% CI 0.0151–0.0662); residual GraphSAGE improves targeted-failure MAE by 0.0257 (0.0095–0.0446). Second-order perturbation yields small gains (0.0028–0.0053). Targeted-transfer shows shrinkage of residual slopes; leave-one-country-out transfer is mixed: residual GCN improves targeted-failure MAE by 0.0622 (0.0169–0.1153) but worsens the spatial point estimate. Scaling to 20k nodes preserves amortized screening over a separate spectral setup. Findings indicate the spectral residual is a domain-sensitive but useful inductive bias for screening structural connectivity.

Conclusion: The spectral residual learned by GNNs provides a practical, scalable inductive bias for fast screening of connectivity loss under multiple edge failures, with modest but consistent gains in key regimes; performance is domain-sensitive and transfer-dependent. Code and reproducibility artifacts are archived for verification.

Abstract: Rapid evaluation of many simultaneous road-link disruptions requires a practical compromise between exact spectral recomputation and local approximation. We estimate relative algebraic-connectivity loss after multi-edge deletion using graph neural networks (GNNs) that learn a bounded correction to a first-order Fiedler sensitivity. The study considers independent, spatially clustered, and edge-betweenness-targeted failures, with graph-disjoint synthetic splits and zero-shot transfer to 13 OpenStreetMap (OSM) areas in six countries. GCN, GraphSAGE, and edge-aware MPNN backbones are compared with analytical baselines. In expanded OSM tests, residual GCN improves spatial-failure MAE by 0.0391 (95% hierarchical interval 0.0151-0.0662), while residual GraphSAGE improves targeted-failure MAE by 0.0257 (0.0095-0.0446). Second-order perturbation improves first-order MAE by only 0.0028-0.0053. Correction slopes decrease under targeted transfer, indicating residual shrinkage around systematic prior error. Leave-one-country-out OSM-to-OSM transfer is mixed: residual GCN improves targeted-failure MAE by 0.0622 (0.0169-0.1153) but worsens the spatial point estimate. Sparse scaling extends to 20,000 nodes and separates one-time spectral setup from amortized screening cost. These results characterize the spectral residual as a useful but domain-sensitive inductive bias for structural connectivity screening. Code, cached networks, and reproducibility artifacts are archived at doi:10.5281/zenodo.22307723.

</details>


### [193] [Semi-Tensor Product-Based Multi-Term Randomized T-SVD and Its Visual Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11168)
*Xingchen Xiao,Feng Zhang,Wenjin Qin,Jianjun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a multi-term semi-tensor product SVD (MSTP-SVD) for third-order tensors under a t-product framework induced by arbitrary invertible transforms, with randomized acceleration (MRSTP-SVD) to improve low-rank approximations for images/videos while reducing computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome rigid dimension matching in standard t-product, and the limited accuracy of single-term STP-based SVD, while keeping reasonable computational costs for large-scale tensor data.

Method: Define a semi-tensor product for third-order tensors within a t-product framework driven by arbitrary invertible linear transforms. Develop MSTP-SVD by combining multiple orthogonal decomposition terms for better low-rank approximation. Integrate randomized projection and power iteration to form MRSTP-SVD to accelerate computations.

Result: Empirical results show that MSTP-SVD achieves significantly better low-rank approximation accuracy than single-term STP-SVD. MRSTP-SVD reduces computational cost while maintaining reconstruction quality, with successful application to image/video compression and completion tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed semi-tensor product framework enables flexible dimension handling without sacrificing the closed-form nature of T-SVD, and the multi-term with randomized acceleration offers a favorable balance between accuracy and efficiency for large-scale tensor processing in visual data tasks.

Abstract: Tensor singular value decomposition (T-SVD), which is built upon the tensor-tensor product (t-product), has emerged as a powerful tool for processing high-dimensional visual data such as color images and videos. However, the standard t-product imposes strict dimensional compatibility constraints. Although extensions based on the semi-tensor product (STP) relax this restriction, their single-term formulations still suffer from limited approximation accuracy. Moreover, these deterministic methods incur high computational costs when processing large-scale tensor data. To address these issues, this paper introduces a novel semi-tensor product for third-order tensors under the t-product framework induced by arbitrary invertible linear transforms. The resulting tensor semi-tensor product breaks the rigid dimension matching requirement of the standard t-product, while retaining the closed-form property of T-SVD. Based on this construction, we develop a multi-term semi-tensor product singular value decomposition (MSTP-SVD), which integrates multiple orthogonal decomposition terms to significantly improve low-rank approximation accuracy compared with single-term schemes. To reduce the computational cost of multi-term modeling, we incorporate randomized projection and power iteration techniques into the MSTP-SVD framework, yielding an accelerated multi-term randomized semi-tensor product SVD (MRSTP-SVD) algorithm that achieves a balance between reconstruction accuracy and computational efficiency. Experiments on image and video compression and completion tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.

</details>


### [194] [Hierarchical Clustering Can Jointly Satisfy Richness, Consistency, and Scale Invariance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11173)
*Daichi Kuroda,Maximilien Dreveton,Matthias Grossglauser,Patrick Thiran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: In hierarchical clustering, Kleinberg's impossibility is avoided: the hierarchical analogs of scale invariance, richness, and consistency can be satisfied simultaneously by many methods. There exist uncountably many admissible hierarchical clustering methods, with explicit constructions (e.g., well-separated cluster methods and a non-binary version of single linkage). A refinement partial order governs their relations: some methods refine others, there is no greatest element, and many incompatible maximal methods exist, indicating substantial diversity. Yet, all admissible methods share a nontrivial common backbone—each contains well-separated clusters, and any finite collection of admissible methods has a common backbone. 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess whether Kleinberg's impossibility for flat clustering extends to hierarchies and to understand the landscape of admissible hierarchical clustering methods.

Method: Define hierarchical analogs of Kleinberg's axioms (scale invariance, richness, consistency). Prove their joint satisfiability by constructing uncountably many admissible hierarchical methods; provide explicit constructions (well-separated-cluster-based methods, non-binary single linkage). Establish a refinement partial order among admissible methods and analyze its properties (no greatest element, uncountably many incompatible maximal elements). Prove structural constraints: every admissible method contains a hierarchy with sufficiently well-separated clusters, and any finite collection shares a nontrivial common backbone.

Result: Demonstrates that the hierarchical setting admits a rich, continuously large class of methods satisfying the axioms, in contrast to the flat clustering impossibility. Establishes a partial order via refinement, reveals substantial but constrained diversity (no maximum, many incompatible maxima), and identifies a unifying backbone property across admissible hierarchies.

Conclusion: The Kleinberg impossibility does not carry over to hierarchies; there exists a large, diverse, yet structurally constrained family of admissible hierarchical clustering methods. The results provide both theoretical insight into the space of hierarchies and practical guidance on how to compare and select among admissible methods via refinement and backbone concepts.

Abstract: Despite its ubiquity, clustering lacks a universally accepted definition of what is a cluster. Kleinberg's Impossibility Theorem formalizes this difficulty by showing that no flat clustering method can simultaneously satisfy three natural axioms: scale invariance, richness, and consistency. In this paper, we ask whether this impossibility persists when the output is a hierarchy rather than a single partition. We show that, in contrast to the flat clustering setting, the hierarchical analog of these axioms are jointly satisfiable. In fact, there exist uncountably many hierarchical clustering methods satisfying these axioms, which we call admissible. We explicitly construct several admissible methods, including methods based on well-separated clusters and a non-binary version of single linkage. For certain pairs of admissible methods, the hierarchy produced by one always refines that produced by the other. This refinement relation defines a partial order on the class of admissible methods. This partially ordered set has no greatest element and contains uncountably many pairwise incompatible maximal elements, revealing substantial diversity among admissible methods. Nevertheless, this diversity is constrained: every admissible method contains a hierarchy of sufficiently well-separated clusters, and every finite collection of admissible methods shares such a nontrivial common backbone.

</details>


### [195] [Convex Optimization with Nested Evolving Feasible Sets (CONES) under Time-Varying Loss Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11207)
*Rahul Vaze*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Extends CONES to time-varying losses; achieves trade-offs between regret and movement with a projected proximal algorithm; strong convexity yields constant regret and polylog movement; sublinear anytime regret implies logarithmic movement lower bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Study online optimization under nested evolving feasible sets S1 ⊇ S2 ⊇ ... ⊇ ST with time-varying loss functions, aiming to minimize regret and total movement while staying feasible. Extends NCBC/CONES to dynamic loss landscapes.

Method: Use a projected proximal algorithm. Analyze two regimes: convex losses and strongly convex losses. Introduce a trade-off parameter β ∈ [0,1) to balance regret and movement; derive upper bounds on simultaneous regret and movement: Convex losses yield O(T^{1-β}) regret and O(T^β) movement; for strongly convex losses, O(1) regret and O(log T) movement. Establish lower bounds: any weakly adaptive algorithm with O(T^β) regret has movement Ω(T^{(1-β)/2}); any online algorithm with sublinear anytime regret has movement Ω(log T).

Result: Convex losses: simultaneous bounds O(T^{1-β}) for regret and O(T^β) for movement with any β ∈ [0,1). Lower bound for weakly adaptive: Ω(T^{(1-β)/2}). Strongly convex losses: simultaneous O(1) regret and O(log T) movement. Anytime sublinear regret implies movement Ω(log T).

Conclusion: The work provides a continuum of Pareto-like trade-offs between regret and movement under nested evolving feasible sets, via a projected proximal algorithm. It generalizes CONES to time-varying losses and aligns with NCBC. Results are tight up to constants in the stated regimes, with lower bounds supporting optimality in several cases; practical aspects (computational cost, uncertainty about S_t, and constants) remain to be explored.

Abstract: Convex Optimization with Nested Evolving Feasible Sets (CONES)} was introduced in \cite{CONESVaze} where the objective function \(f\) remains fixed but the feasible region evolves over time as a nested sequence \(S_1 \supseteq S_2 \supseteq \cdots \supseteq S_T\). The goal of an online algorithm is to simultaneously minimize the regret with respect to hindsight static optimal benchmark and the total movement cost $M_\cA(T)$ while ensuring feasibility at all times. CONES is an optimization-oriented generalization of the well-known \emph{nested convex body chasing} (NCBC). In this paper, we extend CONES to allow for loss functions $f_t'$s to also change over time. When all loss functions are convex, we show that the projected proximal algorithm achieves $O(T^{1-β}), O(T^β)$ simultaneous regret and movement cost, respectively, for any $β\in [0,1)$, over a time horizon of $T$. We also show that any {\it weakly adaptive} online algorithm with $O(T^β)$ regret has a movement cost of $Ω\left(T^{\frac{1-β}{2}}\right)$ for any $β\in [0,1)$. When all loss functions are strongly convex, we show that the projected proximal algorithm simultaneously achieves $O(1)$ regret and a movement cost of $O(\log T)$. To complement this, we show that any online algorithm with sublinear {\it anytime} regret has a movement cost of $Ω\left(\log T\right)$.

</details>


### [196] [REVA: Reusable Evidence View Aggregation for Context-Efficient RAG Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11209)
*Tuan Nguyen,Qiran Hu,Banruo Liu,Khoa D. Doan,Kok-Seng Wong,Fan Lai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: REVA (Reusable Evidence View Aggregation) introduces a data-mining approach to RAG compression by mining historical attention traces to build a document-keyed, budget-agnostic score store. It produces readable, budget-specific views that preserve document order and the standard RAG interface, enabling reuse of past evidence rather than per-query compression. Empirically, it yields 1.0–5.8 point gains and substantially lowers compression overhead (5.3–15.6x) with <40 ms additional latency across four benchmarks and modern LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-retrieval compression in RAG often adds latency and memory overhead, with compressors that may underperform simple truncation and require auxiliary models or rewriting. The work seeks to leverage historical query-document-model interactions to create reusable, budget-aware evidence views that reduce online overhead while improving generation quality.

Method: Construct REVA by extracting the target generator's historical attention traces, organizing them into a document-keyed score store that is budget-agnostic. Map token-level attention to readable word units, aggregate document-accessed importance across repeated accesses, and render budget-specific plain-text views that preserve document order and align with standard RAG interfaces.

Result: Across four benchmarks and modern LLMs, REVA improves generation quality by 1.0–5.8 points compared with existing compression methods, while reducing online compression overhead by 5.3–15.6× and adding less than 40 ms of latency.

Conclusion: A data-mining, reusable evidence-view framework for RAG can outperform conventional per-query compressors by exploiting historical attention patterns, delivering better quality with substantially lower overhead and maintaining compatibility with the conventional RAG workflow.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improves knowledge-intensive large language model (LLM) applications by conditioning generation on retrieved documents, but longer contexts increase latency, key-value (KV) cache memory, and token cost. Post-retrieval compression can reduce this cost, yet existing compressors often operate independently for each query, rely on auxiliary models or rewriting, and introduce online overhead that can offset the benefit of shorter prompts. We revisit RAG compression from a data-mining perspective by aggregating historical query--document--model interactions into reusable evidence views. We first show that modern compressors have unstable gains over simple truncation and can add substantial inference-time latency. We then propose Reusable Evidence View Aggregation (REVA), a framework that mines the target generator's historical attention traces into a document-keyed, budget-agnostic score store. REVA maps token-level attention to readable word units, aggregates importance across repeated document accesses, and renders budget-specific plain-text views that preserve document order and the standard RAG interface. Across four representative benchmarks and modern LLMs, REVA improves generation quality by 1.0--5.8 points over existing advances, while reducing compression overhead by a factor of 5.3 to 15.6, adding less than 40 ms of latency.

</details>
