### [197] [Legible Failures: Detecting and Repairing In-Context Binding Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11216)
*Manas Venkata Sai Ravulapalli,Samrath Singh Chadha,Abhinav M. Hari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Probes can extract binding information from frozen model states and can be used to detect and steer model behavior, indicating information presence and use can be disentangled and that in-context binding is actionable in this setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To distinguish whether incorrect outputs reflect missing information or failure to use known information, and to assess whether linear probes and residual-stream interventions can reveal and influence in-context binding across multiple checkpoints.

Method: Evaluate 16 public checkpoints with 3 seeds each. Train a linear probe on a training fold, select the best layer via a validation fold, test on a disjoint test fold. Compare probe accuracy to a strict present-obligation baseline (1/K). Bootstrap over models to estimate CI. Use a query-entity counterfactual, a disagreement-based score (sign of probe-output disagreement) for failure detection (AUROC), and steer the residual stream toward the probe-decoded binding without gold labels to measure effect on accuracy across eight models.

Result: Probe accuracy on wrong trials exceeds the baseline by about 0.196 (CI [0.101, 0.296]). A counterfactual rule rules out token presence/recency. Disagreement-based score improves AUROC by about 0.079 (CI [0.036, 0.126]); raw probe confidence yields no improvement. Steering residual stream toward the probe-decoded binding increases accuracy by about 0.168 (CI [0.066, 0.280]) on eight models. In-context binding probes appear actionable, contrasting with some studies that report probe-detected errors are resistant to intervention.

Conclusion: The study supports the view that probes can reveal and leverage information presence in LMs, enabling improved failure detection and controllable behavior via in-context binding interventions. It also highlights that probe-based interventions can be effective in certain settings, though care is needed regarding stability, generalization, and potential overfitting to probe signals.

Abstract: A wrong answer does not show whether the model lacked the needed information or held it and failed to use it. On an entity-obligation binding task, a language model can emit an incorrect prompt-supplied binding while a linear probe can recover the correct one from its frozen hidden state. We measure how often this occurs across 16 public checkpoints, each evaluated with three seeds. We fit a probe on a training fold, select its layer on a validation fold, and report results on a disjoint test fold. On the trials each model gets wrong, probe accuracy exceeds the strict present-obligation baseline, 1/K = 0.125, by +0.196 (95% CI [+0.101, +0.296], bootstrapped over models). A query-entity counterfactual rules out token presence and recency. A score built from the sign of probe-output disagreement improves failure detection over the model's own confidence by +0.079 AUROC (95% CI [+0.036, +0.126]). Raw probe confidence gives no measurable improvement over model confidence. Steering the residual stream toward the probe-decoded binding, with no gold label, raises accuracy on all eight models tested by a mean of +0.168 (95% CI [+0.066, +0.280]). Where recent studies report that probe-detected errors are resistant to interventions, we find that in-context binding is a setting in which probes are actionable.

</details>


### [198] [Polyhedral Geometry of Time-to-First-Spike Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11227)
*Manjot Singh,Guido Montúfar,Gitta Kutyniok*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Spiking neural networks in the time-to-first-spike model can implement exponentially many affine regions and partition input space more richly than ReLU networks; causal regions are polyhedral; the paper derives bounds for shallow and multilayer SNNs and provides theory plus experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify and compare the representational power of spiking neural networks to conventional feedforward networks by formalizing firing-time representations and causal regions under time-to-first-spike dynamics.

Method: (1) Prove firing times admit a maxout-like representation with exponentially many constrained affine pieces; (2) formalize causal regions as polyhedral regions with fixed causal sets; (3) derive upper and lower bounds on the number of causal regions for shallow and multilayer SNNs; (4) compare with ReLU networks and validate with experiments.

Result: Established a maxout-like representation for neuron firing times; defined polyhedral causal regions and derived bounds on their number for shallow and multilayer SNNs; theoretical and experimental results indicate SNNs can produce richer input-space partitions than conventional ReLU networks.

Conclusion: Time-to-first-spike spiking networks can achieve greater expressivity than conventional networks by generating richer input partitions; supports the potential of asynchronous, event-driven computation and motivates further study of SNN expressivity.

Abstract: We study the expressivity of spiking neural networks, which provide a natural framework for asynchronous, event-driven computation complementary to conventional feedforward neural networks. We consider the time-to-first-spike model in a setting for which the input-output map is continuous and piecewise linear, with affine pieces governed by causal feasibility constraints that determine which presynaptic spikes occur before a neuron fires. We first show that each neuron's firing time admits a maxout-like representation with exponentially many, highly constrained affine pieces. We then formalize causal regions as polyhedral regions with fixed causal sets and derive upper and lower bounds on the maximal number of causal regions in both shallow and multilayer feedforward spiking networks. Our theoretical and experimental results show that spiking networks can generate richer partitions of the input space than conventional feedforward ReLU networks.

</details>


### [199] [Solving Few-Shot Multiobjective Multitask Optimization via Iterative Sequential Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11228)
*Tingyang Wei,Haofeng Wu,Ananda Phan Iman,Zhao Wei,Jiao Liu,Yew-Soon Ong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IST (Iterative Sequential Transfer) reframes multi-task and multiobjective optimization under limited budgets as a sequence of single-target transfer problems, using likelihood-informed task prioritization to select the most transfer-ready task. It claims improved transfer efficiency and solution quality under tight evaluation budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bottleneck in multitask optimization: aligning elite solution distributions across tasks; few-shot/low-budget regimes hinder accurate transfer. In multiobjective settings, each task seeks a Pareto frontier rather than a single optimum, amplifying data scarcity and transfer risk.

Method: Model MTO as a sequence of sequential transfer optimization problems, focusing evaluations on one target per iteration. Introduce a likelihood-informed task prioritization mechanism to identify the task most likely ready for knowledge integration, guiding which transfer to perform at each step.

Result: Empirical results on benchmark and real-world problems demonstrate IST’s effectiveness under tight budgets, showing improved transfer utility and competitive or superior performance with constrained evaluations.

Conclusion: IST successfully mitigates the transfer bottleneck in MTO by sequentially concentrating evaluation effort and using probabilistic task prioritization, enabling effective knowledge transfer under data-scarce, budget-constrained scenarios across single- and multiobjective tasks.

Abstract: Applying knowledge transfer across multiple optimization tasks, multitask optimization (MTO) emerges as a promising approach to solving synergistic optimization tasks simultaneously. However, the development of effective knowledge transfer mechanisms in MTO fundamentally relies on aligning elite solution distributions across tasks. This dependency creates a critical bottleneck in few-shot optimization regimes, as restricted evaluation budgets impede the identification of elite solution distributions required for beneficial transfer. This challenge is exacerbated in multiobjective multitask problems, where each optimizer must approximate a continuous Pareto manifold rather than a single optimal point. This paper introduces Iterative Sequential Transfer (IST) to circumvent this bottleneck. We model MTO as a sequence of sequential transfer optimization problems, concentrating evaluations on a single target per iteration. We propose a likelihood-informed task prioritization mechanism to maximize transfer utility by identifying the task most likely ready for knowledge integration. Empirical results on benchmark and real-world problems verify the effectiveness of the proposed method under tight budgets.

</details>


### [200] [MUtE: A Dual Framework for Concept Erasure and Counterfactual Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11253)
*Antoine Saillenfest*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a theory-grounded class of concept erasure functions yielding a deterministic dual counterfactual mapping, with a translational bias constraint to balance erasure and counterfactual generation; empirically improves fairness and enables counterfactual text synthesis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Erasing concept-specific information from representations can reduce bias and aid model interpretability. The work bridges theoretical optimal bounds on concept erasure with practical representation learning to enable controlled erasure and counterfactual generation.

Method: Revisit optimal bounds to derive a novel class of erasure functions that induce a deterministic dual counterfactual mapping. Implement a translational bias constraint on counterfactual trajectories, aligning with how concepts geometrically manifest in language models. Provide a framework that navigates between concept erasure and counterfactual generation.

Result: Empirically demonstrates improved downstream algorithmic fairness and the ability to generate counterfactual texts.

Conclusion: The framework unifies concept erasure and counterfactual generation, connecting theoretical optimality to practical representation learning and enabling configurable fairness-aware transformation and counterfactual text synthesis.

Abstract: Erasing concept-specific information from representations has been proven useful for mitigating bias or interpreting model decisions. The joint objective is to transform the original representations such that the target concept becomes unpredictable, while maximally preserving concept-unrelated information. In this work, we revisit the optimal bounds of concept erasure to derive a novel class of erasure functions that naturally induce a deterministic, dual counterfactual mapping. Bridging the gap between theoretical optimality and practical representation learning, we design an implementation that imposes a translational bias on counterfactual trajectories - a constraint that aligns with how many concepts geometrically manifest in modern language models. Our framework enables seamless navigation between concept erasure and counterfactual generation. We empirically demonstrate its efficacy in improving downstream algorithmic fairness and generating counterfactual texts.

</details>


### [201] [A Dynamic Fusion Large Language Model for Traffic Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11314)
*Xue Qiu,Jianli Xiao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dynamic Fusion LLM (DF-LLM) for traffic flow prediction that combines spatiotemporal embedding, graph-based fusion, and a specialized LLM backbone to improve accuracy across four datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address limitations of traditional neural networks (single feature modeling) and LLMs (limited spatial/topological and spatiotemporal correlation capture) in traffic forecasting; enhance prediction accuracy and efficiency.

Method: Three components: (1) spatiotemporal embedding module for multi-scale features; (2) spatiotemporal fusion module using graph convolution to integrate spatial topology and dynamic dependencies; (3) LLM backbone with differentiated parameter adaptation, context aggregation attention, and residual connections to improve training efficiency, global dependency modeling, and gradient flow.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows DF-LLM achieves superior performance compared with baselines on four traffic datasets, across standard metrics.

Conclusion: DF-LLM demonstrates effective integration of spatiotemporal graph-augmented features with a specialized LLM backbone, yielding improved prediction accuracy and training efficiency while mitigating train-time gradient issues.

Abstract: Traffic flow prediction is a core supporting technology for intelligent transportation systems. It uses historical data to infer future traffic dynamics in specific areas, thereby helping to alleviate congestion and improve resource allocation efficiency. Traditional neural networks struggle to break through accuracy limits due to their reliance on singular feature modeling, while large language models (LLMs) suffer from insufficient capture of spatial topological information and mining spatiotemporal correlation. This study proposes a Dynamic Fusion Large Language Model (DF-LLM) for traffic flow prediction. The model incorporates three core components: spatiotemporal embedding module, spatiotemporal fusion module, and LLM backbone. The spatiotemporal embedding module enables synergistic representation of multi-scale spatiotemporal features. The spatiotemporal fusion module integrates spatial topology and dynamic dependencies via graph convolution. The LLM backbone adopts a differentiated parameter adaptation strategy to balance training efficiency and traffic data adaptability. Additionally, it introduces a context aggregation attention module to strengthens global dependencies. More importantly, the LLM backbone takes the residual connections to mitigate the gradient vanishing in deep networks. Experiments show that DF-LLM has achieved better performance by comparing the metrics on all the four datasets.

</details>


### [202] [Estimating Inconsistency Response Surfaces under Uncertainty in Cyber-Physical System Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11331)
*Johannes Mäkelburg,Tim Schwabe,Maribel Acosta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Surrogate-response modeling for uncertainty-aware CPS consistency: predicting inconsistency from uncertainty geometry using Saltelli sampling and multi-fidelity MC; fast evaluation enables sensitivity analysis and gradient-based recourse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CPS are modeled by interdependent components; uncertainty (e.g., sensor noise, abstraction) narrows admissible values and can render models jointly unsatisfiable. Existing methods assess a fixed uncertainty config, but systematic exploration across large uncertainty spaces is needed.

Method: Reframe inconsistency as an intervention-response problem. Generate intervention-response data via Saltelli sampling and multi-fidelity Monte Carlo estimation. Train a surrogate to predict inconsistency directly from propagated uncertainty geometry. Use the learned response surfaces for global sensitivity analysis and to derive a gradient-based consistency recourse that minimizes uncertainty interventions.

Result: The surrogate matches Monte Carlo estimates. Evaluation time drops from milliseconds to microseconds, enabling orders-of-magnitude more response-surface evaluations within fixed budgets. Experiments span 48 scenarios and 10 CPS domains.

Conclusion: Inconsistency under uncertainty can be effectively learned, analyzed, and repaired through response-surface modeling, offering a scalable foundation for uncertainty-aware consistency management in CPS development.

Abstract: Cyber-Physical Systems (CPS) are commonly represented through multiple interconnected models. During development, CPS consistency requires that shared model elements remain compatible across these models. Uncertainty, for example, due to sensor noise or model abstraction, changes the admissible values of model elements and can introduce inconsistencies, i.e., situations in which models can no longer be jointly satisfied. While existing approaches can determine consistency for a given uncertainty configuration, they provide limited support for systematically exploring, analyzing, and explaining inconsistency across large uncertainty spaces. We address this challenge by reformulating inconsistency as an intervention response modeling problem. Using Saltelli sampling and multi-fidelity Monte Carlo estimation, we generate intervention-response datasets and train a surrogate model that directly predicts inconsistency from the propagated uncertainty geometry. Experiments on 48 scenarios and 10 CPS domains show that the surrogate matches Monte Carlo estimates while reducing evaluation time from milliseconds to microseconds, enabling orders-of-magnitude more response-surface evaluations within fixed computational budgets. Building on the learned response surfaces, we perform sensitivity analysis to identify dominant uncertainty drivers and introduce a gradient-based consistency recourse method to determine minimal uncertainty interventions that restore consistency. The results show that inconsistency under uncertainty can be effectively learned, analyzed, and repaired through response-surface modeling, providing a scalable foundation for uncertainty-aware consistency management in CPS development.

</details>


### [203] [Reification as a Transferable Vocabulary: Zero-Shot Link Prediction with Vanilla GNNs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11347)
*Camille Pradel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A representation-based transfer method: reify facts as nodes with six meta-relations enabling standard GNNs to achieve zero-shot inductive link prediction across unseen graphs; matches ULTRA; extends to relational databases; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Move the transfer mechanism from model architecture to data representation to enable zero-shot transfer across graphs without specialized foundation models; test across multiple GNNs and benchmarks; explore applicability to relational databases.

Method: Represent each knowledge graph triple as a node connected to subject, object, and relation type via fixed six meta-relations; relation types implemented as anonymous shared nodes rather than parameters; train five classic GNNs (GAT, GINE with sum, GINE with mean+max, GraphSAGE, R-GCN) on a single graph (4,245 triples) for 30 minutes on NVIDIA A100; evaluate zero-shot inductive link prediction on 40 benchmarks; also test on two unseen relational databases; extend fixed vocabulary to databases with rows as entities and foreign-key columns as relation types; release code and checkpoints.

Result: The five GNNs achieve zero-shot transfer to all 40 benchmarks. The best model (an off-the-shelf GAT) matches ULTRA on ULTRA’s evaluation suite. The fixed-vocabulary representation also yields promising preliminary results on unseen databases, outperforming random initialization and degree-based baselines. Code, checkpoints, and evaluation pipeline released.

Conclusion: Recasting the transfer mechanism as a representation choice enables strong zero-shot transfer using standard GNNs, reducing reliance on architecture-specific foundation models; the approach generalizes to relational databases and has practical reproducibility through released resources.

Abstract: Knowledge graph foundation models such as ULTRA achieve zero-shot link prediction on unseen graphs through dedicated architectures that hard-code a transfer mechanism. In this work we move that mechanism out of the architecture and into the representation, by \emph{reifying} the input graph: every fact becomes a node, connected to its subject, object, and relation type through a fixed vocabulary of six meta-relations, with relation types as anonymous shared nodes rather than model parameters. On this representation, five textbook GNNs (GAT, GINE with sum and with mean+max aggregation, GraphSAGE, R-GCN), each trained on a single knowledge graph of 4,245 triples for 30 minutes on one NVIDIA A100, transfer zero-shot to 40 inductive link-prediction benchmarks. The best of them, an off-the-shelf GAT, matches ULTRA, a dedicated foundation model pretrained on three graphs, across ULTRA's own evaluation suite. The same fixed vocabulary extends to relational databases, a row becoming an entity and a foreign-key column a relation type; a preliminary probe on two unseen databases, with no cell values, schema text or in-context labels, shows a model of this family pretrained on three knowledge graphs ranking foreign-key targets far above random-initialization and degree controls. We release the code, the checkpoints, and the evaluation pipeline for all 40 benchmarks.

</details>


### [204] [Local Robustness Quantification for Naive Bayes Classifiers and Generative Forests: a General Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11366)
*Adrián Detavernier,Jasper De Bock*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper defines and tests a robustness score for predictions of naive Bayes and generative forests by perturbing local PGM components within neighborhoods (epsilon-contamination, TV, chi-squared balls) and shows robustness correlates with trustworthiness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify the reliability of predictions from generative classifiers under model perturbations, providing a quantitative trust metric for individual predictions.

Method: Define robustness as the maximum permitted perturbation of local PGM models that does not change the predicted class. Analyze perturbations within general neighborhoods using epsilon-contamination, total variation distance, and chi-squared divergence balls; apply to naive Bayes classifiers and generative forests; compute and compare robustness scores.

Result: Empirical evaluation on benchmark datasets demonstrates that the predicted robustness value correlates with the prediction’s trustworthiness and compares favorably with other reliability indicators.

Conclusion: Robustness quantification offers a practical, model-agnostic indicator of prediction trustworthiness for generative classifiers (naive Bayes and generative forests) and can be extended to other PGMs and perturbation families.

Abstract: We provide methods for calculating the robustness of the predictions of two types of generative classifiers whose underlying distribution is a Probabilistic Graphical Model (PGM): naive Bayes classifiers and generative forests (a probabilistic extension of random forests). Following the paradigm of robustness quantification, we define the robustness of a prediction as the extent to which the distribution of the classifier can be perturbed without changing this prediction. We consider perturbations obtained by varying the local models of the PGMs within general neighborhoods and focus in particular on epsilon-contamination, total variation distance and chi-squared divergence balls. We test our methods on benchmark datasets, demonstrate that the robustness value of a prediction serves as an indicator for its trustworthiness and compare our approach with other such indicators.

</details>


### [205] [Prevalence Determines Precision:Silent Contamination in Detector-Defined Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11449)
*Jia Huang,Yankai Wan,Yangjun Ou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dataset precision produced by detector-driven labeling depends on true-positive prevalence across pools; phantom contamination behaves as a second signal, invalidating simple transfer of precision and some normalizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify how detector-based labeling and pooling bias precision estimates, due to contamination and non-additive effects, and to compare Bayes-based vs naive estimators.

Method: One detector applied to three pools; an independent official index labels each detected item as real or phantom. Measure phantom rates, decompose detected signal into true-event and phantom components, analyze estimator behavior, and compare Bayes-based predictions to observed values across 335 events; evaluate normalization effects.

Result: Phantom rates observed: 81.7%, 9.0%, 0.0% across the three pools. Transferring precision from high-rate pools to the low-rate pool yields a 0.955 predicted precision versus 0.183 observed (+422% error); Bayes expression predicts all three pools within 3.3%. The detected response is an exact convex combination of true-event and phantom components (phantoms outnumber true events 473:308). Contamination acts as a second signal with detector-inherited shape; estimator behavior depends on whether the denominator is contaminated; normalization to a mean of ratios can produce misleading values (example: 0.40 vs well-defined 0.10).

Conclusion: Dataset precision in detector-based labeling is fundamentally affected by contamination that mimics true signals. Bayesian calibration aligns predicted precisions with ground truth, while common normalizations can obscure true performance. Careful modeling of both true and phantom components is essential for reliable dataset evaluation.

Abstract: Many ML datasets are constructed by running a detector, heuristic, or model over candidate pools; accepted items become labels. Dataset precision is then governed by true-positive prevalence in each pool via Bayes, not solely by detector quality. Using one instrument and period, we hold a detector-defined event dataset plus an independent official index labeling every detected item as real or phantom. One detector, three pools yield phantom rates 81.7%, 9.0%, and 0.0%. Transferring precision from the two high-rate pools to the low-rate pool predicts 0.955 versus measured 0.183, a +422% error; the Bayes expression predicts all three within 3.3%. The detected response curve is an exact convex combination of a true-event and a phantom component (residual 1.1e-16), with phantoms outnumbering true events 473 to 308, so contamination is a second signal with detector-inherited shape, not additive noise. Contamination direction depends on the estimator: on identical windows one statistic is diluted and another inflated because its denominator is also contaminated. A common normalization turns the estimator into a mean of ratios whose expectation need not exist; on the same 335 events it returns 0.40 where the well-defined estimator returns 0.10.

</details>


### [206] [Combining Synthetic and Real Data for Low-Resource Historical OCR: A Manchu Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11495)
*Yan Hon Michael Chung,Hanlin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: For low-resource Manchu OCR, combining synthetic and real training data via joint or sequential strategies boosts accuracy; real data essential; ensemble voting further improves accuracy; a small CRNN can match large models with real data; a historical dictionary guides disagreement resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manchu archival materials are critically endangered; large synthetic datasets help but fail to bridge realism gap; evaluating data regimes and model scales to inform practical OCR for historical scripts.

Method: 60,000 synthetic and 20,306 real word images; evaluate three pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) and a compact CRNN under four regimes: synthetic-only, real-only, joint synthetic-real, sequential synthetic-to-real; standard checkpoint-selection and archival evaluation; complementary error analysis via voting; adjudication with an eighteenth-century Manchu dictionary.

Result: Real training images raise leading configurations to 95.09–96.28% word accuracy; synthetic-only configurations remain below 87.92%; synthetic supplementation helps all VLMs; marginal impact for CRNN depends on training objective; joint and sequential training yield similar archival accuracy; CRNN reaches leading performance range with real data; ensemble voting raises accuracy to 98.27%; dictionary provides principled disagreement resolution.

Conclusion: Combining real and synthetic data is beneficial for low-resource historical OCR; model scale alone does not determine performance; complementary errors enable high-accuracy ensembles; practical archival pipelines can achieve high accuracy; dictionaries can aid adjudication.

Abstract: Manchu, now critically endangered, was one of the principal languages of the Qing empire (1636-1912), and its extensive archival record is increasingly digitized but remains difficult to search and analyze at scale. Previous work showed that vision-language models (VLMs) trained only on synthetic Manchu word images can reach 87.4% word accuracy on real Qing manuscripts and prints, leaving a substantial synthetic-to-real gap. This study examines how synthetic and real historical training data should be combined for low-resource OCR. Using 60,000 synthetic and 20,306 real historical word images, we evaluate three pretrained VLMs and a compact convolutional recurrent neural network (CRNN) under four regimes: synthetic-only, real-only, joint synthetic-real, and sequential synthetic-to-real training, following a common checkpoint-selection and archival evaluation protocol. Introducing real training images raises the leading configurations to between 95.09% and 96.28% word accuracy, while no synthetic-only configuration exceeds 87.92%. Synthetic supplementation substantially improves all three VLMs, whereas its marginal effect for the CRNN is sensitive to the training objective. Joint and sequential training yield broadly similar archival accuracy under the tested practical pipelines. A compact CRNN also reaches the leading performance range once real images are available, showing that model scale alone does not determine recognition accuracy. Finally, complementary errors among strong recognizers allow voting to raise accuracy to 98.27% without additional training, while an eighteenth-century Manchu dictionary provides a principled rule for adjudicating disagreements.

</details>


### [207] [DeFiFlowBench: Benchmarking and Improving Safe Executability in Natural-Language DeFi Workflow Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11504)
*Abhinav Rajeev Kumar,Harshit Arora,Varun Singh,Manikandan Nanjappan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A benchmark and safety framework for NLP-generated DeFi workflows finds that naive prompting yields unsafe trades; Koan-Safe reduces unsafe outcomes via a hybrid prompt-parsing, generator, and repair approach, with execution-based evaluation clarifying safety guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap between declared safety predicates in natural-language DeFi workflow synthesis and actual on-chain risk; enable evaluation of prompts against real trade configurations and price impact.

Method: Develop DeFiFlowBench with 207 prompts; measure graph coverage, configuration completeness, declared safety; test on local EVM; analyze different prompting regimes; derive slippage bound; propose Koan-Safe architecture; evaluate on 75 prompts; ablations; policy cap; diagnostic grid.

Result: Direct, constrained, and few-shot prompting yield 14–19 unsafe held-out executions per configuration under a fixed 5% price-impact cap; a slippage bound derived from a quote does not prevent the price impact of the order; Koan-Safe static safety proxy scores 0.67 on 75 held-out prompts vs 0.33 for the best baseline; Koan-Safe records no unsafe executions on saved benchmark outputs; a matched-candidate ablation yields 14–17 unsafe executions when enforcement is disabled; permissive thresholds can still authorize unsafe trades; a policy-cap addresses this on a 36-case diagnostic grid.

Conclusion: Explicit trade protections and execution-based evaluation are necessary when synthesizing DeFi workflows from natural language; Koan-Safe offers a viable, multi-layer safety pipeline that improves static safety metrics and eliminates unsafe outcomes on saved outputs, but declared safety is not a universal guarantee and depends on enforcement and risk controls.

Abstract: A structurally valid DeFi workflow can still authorize a costly trade. We introduce DeFiFlowBench, a benchmark of 207 team-authored prompts for natural-language DeFi workflow synthesis. It measures graph coverage, configuration completeness, and declared safety predicates, then tests supported trade configurations on a local EVM. Direct, constrained, and few-shot prompting produce 14-19 unsafe held-out executions per configuration under a fixed 5% price-impact cap. A slippage bound derived from a quote does not prevent the price impact of the order itself. We propose Koan-Safe, which combines a prompt-only intent parser, a replaceable generator, and structural repair with default safety parameters. On 75 held-out workflow prompts, its hybrid variant scores 0.67 on the static safety proxy, compared with 0.33 for the best baseline. Koan-Safe records no unsafe executions on the saved benchmark outputs. A matched-candidate ablation produces 14-17 unsafe executions when enforcement is disabled. Additional tests expose the limits of default injection: permissive existing thresholds can still authorize unsafe trades. A separately evaluated policy cap addresses this failure on a 36-case diagnostic grid. These results support explicit trade protections and execution-based evaluation, while distinguishing declared safety from a general guarantee.

</details>


### [208] [Generalized Score Matching for Parameter Estimation on Convex Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11521)
*Nishanth Shetty,Saisuchith Mahajan,Chandra Sekhar Seelamantula*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Generalized score matching derived from MPF for unnormalized models on convex domains; unifies classical score matching and domain-adapted variants; proves a second-order local scoring rule with convexity (exponential family) and consistency; demonstrated on constrained domains with experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unnormalized probabilistic models require partition functions for ML estimation, which are often intractable. Score matching offers a partition-function-free alternative. The work extends score matching to convex subsets of R^d and connects it to MPF, providing theoretical guarantees and practical applicability to constrained domains and exponential-family models.

Method: Construct a generalized score matching objective starting from Minimum Probability Flow (MPF) learning, defined on convex subsets of R^d. Show that classical score matching and domain-adapted variants arise naturally within this framework. Prove that the objective is a proper second-order local scoring rule guaranteeing recovery of the true density at the minimum. For exponential-family models, establish convexity of the objective and consistency of the finite-sample estimator. Derive implications for constrained-domain densities and provide experiments on parameter estimation and a generative-modeling use case.

Result: The proposed generalized score matching framework yields a proper second-order local scoring rule. It is convex for exponential-family models, with consistency guarantees. Empirical results demonstrate effective parameter estimation for densities on convex subsets and show applicability to a generative modeling task.

Conclusion: The work broadens the applicability of score matching to constrained domains, unifies classical and domain-adapted variants within a single MPF-based framework, and provides theoretical guarantees (convexity, consistency) alongside practical demonstrations in constrained-domain and generative settings.

Abstract: Maximum likelihood (ML) estimation is a principled and statistically efficient approach for learning probabilistic models. However, for unnormalized models, ML estimation requires evaluating the partition function and differentiating through it, which may not always be tractable. Score matching provides a practically viable alternative that circumvents this obstacle by fitting the score in a way that eliminates dependence on the normalizing constant. We derive the generalized score matching objective on a convex subset of $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ constructively starting from Minimum Probability Flow (MPF) learning, and show how classical score matching as well as domain-adapted variants for non-negative data arise naturally within the proposed framework. We show that the resulting objective is a {\it proper local scoring rule} of second-order, which provides the theoretical guarantee that the true density is recovered when the objective is minimized. Furthermore, for a model belonging to the exponential family, we establish convexity of the objective together with consistency of the finite-sample estimator under standard regularity conditions. Our derivation sheds new light on the scope and applicability of generalized score matching in various problem settings. We compare generalized score matching-based estimators on constrained domains, where the partition function is analytically intractable. We provide experimental results on parameter estimation for model densities belonging to the exponential family defined over convex subsets of $\mathbb{R}^{d}$, and a generative modeling use-case to demonstrate broader applicability of the proposed generalized score matching framework.

</details>


### [209] [Particle GFlowNets: Rethinking Generative Marginalization Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11538)
*Tiago da Silva,Diego Mesquita,Salem Lahlou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MaMs are equivalent to Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets). The paper extends MaMs to non-autoregressive sampling, introduces a Gelman-Rubin-based full-state rejuvenation criterion, and presents Particle GFlowNets, which greatly accelerate training in large combinatorial spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unify efficient neural sampling for discrete distributions by showing MaMs and GFlowNets are equivalent, and improve convergence and scalability through non-autoregressive extension and automatic rejuvenation.

Method: 1) Prove theoretical equivalence between MaMs and GFlowNets. 2) Extend MaMs sampling to non-autoregressive processes. 3) Derive a Gelman-Rubin statistic-based criterion for automatic full-state rejuvenation of the Gibbs sampler. 4) Propose Particle GFlowNets to implement these ideas and accelerate training.

Result: Empirical results show marked acceleration of training in large combinatorial spaces using Particle GFlowNets.

Conclusion: MaMs and GFlowNets are fundamentally equivalent frameworks for discrete stochastic inference; the Particle GFlowNets approach with Gelman-Rubin rejuvenation enhances convergence speed and scalability in complex discrete spaces.

Abstract: Generative Marginalization Models (MaMs) have been recently introduced as efficient neural sampling models for any-order autoregressive modelling of discrete distributions. By learning both the marginal and conditional probabilities of a persistent-block Gibbs sampler, MaMs enable fast posterior evaluation with a single neural network forward pass. While prior work has considered MaMs to be distinct from Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets), a well-established paradigm for inference in discrete stochastic models, we show that they are equivalent. Then, we also extend MaMs' sampling strategy to non-autoregressive generative processes. In particular, we describe an automatic criterion for full-state rejuvenation of the Gibbs sampler, derived from the Gelman-Rubin statistic, which plays a key role in speeding up learning convergence. Our experiments show that our method, called Particle GFlowNets, markedly accelerates training in large combinatorial spaces.

</details>


### [210] [A Dataset and Model for Imputing Water Surface Elevation on a Large and Extremely Sparse Spatiotemporal Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11580)
*Ruben Cartuyvels,Karim Douch,Gabriele Bertoli,Mounia El Baz,Artemis Vrettou,Sébastien Lefèvre,Diego Fernandez Prieto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AmazonSWE introduces a large-scale, real-world dataset for spatiotemporal graph imputation of water surface elevation across ~19k Amazon river sections over 2016–2026, addressing extreme data sparsity and DAG topology; it also proposes a bidirectional selective state-space model that outperforms prior methods and densifies WSE predictions across all sections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scarcity of in situ gauges globally impedes reliable water surface elevation modeling. Satellite altimetry offers imagery, but sparse temporal coverage hinders use. A large, realistic benchmark with realistic topologies is needed to advance spatiotemporal graph imputation methods.

Method: A bidirectional selective state-space model that (1) samples connected subgraphs to manage extreme sparsity, and (2) flattens space and time into a single token sequence with topology-aware positional encodings, enabling effective imputation on a directed acyclic river topology.

Result: The proposed model outperforms prior spatiotemporal graph imputation methods on AmazonSWE, achieving 18–39% RMSE reduction against in situ gauges compared to the state-of-the-art SWOT-based WSE densification, and provides predictions for every river section rather than only those with nearby satellite coverage.

Conclusion: AmazonSWE provides a challenging, real-world benchmark for large-scale, sparse spatiotemporal graph imputation in river networks. The bidirectional selective state-space model demonstrates strong performance gains and broader prediction coverage, supporting improved flood forecasting and water resource management.

Abstract: Continuous monitoring of water surface elevation across river networks is critical for flood forecasting, water resource management, and understanding the global water cycle. Yet, the scarcity of in situ gauges across much of the globe constrains the development of reliable modeling frameworks. Satellite altimetry has the potential to alleviate this problem but its use is currently hindered by sparse temporal coverage. To this end, we introduce AmazonSWE, a dataset for training and evaluating large-scale spatiotemporal graph imputation methods that integrates processed satellite altimetry measurements from a range of sources, including the recent wide-swath SWOT sensor. The dataset covers over 19K river sections and 10 years (2016-2026) in the Amazon river basin, with in situ gauges held out for evaluation. Besides contributing a novel real-world use case with the potential for societal impact, AmazonSWE introduces significant technical challenges: with fewer than 1% of sections observed per day, the dataset is far sparser than existing imputation benchmarks, and its directed acyclic river topology is both structurally different from and larger than graphs in existing datasets. We show that prior spatiotemporal graph imputation methods are not adapted to this topology, scale and sparsity, and propose a simple bidirectional selective state space model that outperforms them by sampling connected subgraphs and flattening space and time into a single token sequence with topology-aware positional encodings. Compared to the state-of-the-art published method for SWOT-based WSE densification, which integrates statistics with physical modeling, our model reduces RMSE against in situ gauges by 18-39%, while producing predictions for every river section rather than only those with sufficient nearby satellite coverage.

</details>


### [211] [LoaDiff: Conditional Generation of Electricity Consumption Time Series for Energy Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11639)
*Mariia Baranova,Adrien Petralia,Etienne Le Naour,Nathan Etourneau,Guillaume Hofmann,Themis Palpanas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LoaDiff is a diffusion-based generative model for year-long, sub-hourly smart-meter load curves that can be conditioned on static household features and dynamic variables (calendar, outdoor temperature). It achieves realistic, diverse synthetic data with low memorization risk and preserves utility for downstream tasks like load forecasting and appliance detection, while offering controllable responses to temperature changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Access to granular smart-meter data is restricted due to data protection regulations; synthetic data are needed to support tasks such as load forecasting, appliance detection, and demand-side flexibility analysis.

Method: LoaDiff is a diffusion-based generative model that produces year-long, sub-hourly load curves. It supports flexible conditioning on static household attributes (e.g., appliance ownership) and dynamic contextual variables (calendar information and outdoor temperature). Evaluation compares against multiple baselines on three residential datasets, focusing on fidelity/diversity, memorization risk, downstream utility, and controllability.

Result: LoaDiff generates realistic and diverse load profiles, balances generation quality with limited memorization, preserves information useful for downstream energy applications, and responds coherently to changes in conditioning variables like temperature.

Conclusion: LoaDiff is a viable synthetic data generator for residential electricity consumption, enabling privacy-preserving data sharing and enabling energy analytics tasks that rely on granular load data.

Abstract: The energy transition is reshaping residential electricity consumption through the increasing adoption of distributed generation, electrified appliances, and demand-response programs. Understanding these evolving behaviors requires access to granular smart-meter data for applications such as load forecasting, appliance detection, and demand-side flexibility analysis. However, such data are subject to strict access restrictions and data-protection regulations. Thus, realistic synthetic alternatives are necessary. In this paper, we introduce LoaDiff, a diffusion-based generative model for year-long, sub-hourly smart-meter load curves. LoaDiff supports flexible conditioning on static household attributes, such as appliance ownership, and dynamic contextual variables, including calendar information and outdoor temperature. We evaluate the model against multiple generative baselines on three residential electricity-consumption datasets. Our experiments assess four complementary dimensions: fidelity and diversity, training-record memorization risk, downstream utility for load forecasting and appliance detection, and conditional controllability under alternative temperature conditions. The results show that LoaDiff generates realistic and diverse load profiles, achieves a favorable trade-off between generation quality and limited evidence of memorization, preserves information useful for downstream energy applications, and responds coherently to changes in conditioning variables.

</details>


### [212] [RDDMPI: Residual Denoising Diffusion Model for Probabilistic Multivariate Time Series Imputation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11648)
*Ramiro Valdes Jara,David Chapman,Adam Meyers*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes RDDMPI, a conditional residual diffusion model for multivariate time series imputation that operates in residual space using a baseline-residual decomposition, with baseline signal and its latent representation guiding the reverse diffusion and a reliability-aware mechanism to modulate their influence; demonstrates improved reconstruction accuracy and uncertainty quantification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional diffusion-based probabilistic imputation in data space is complex because the model must capture global structure, dynamics, and stochasticity simultaneously. Leveraging strong deterministic imputers to provide a baseline and focusing diffusion on residuals can simplify learning and improve uncertainty modeling.

Method: RDDMPI uses a conditional residual diffusion framework that operates in residual space. A pretrained model provides a baseline-completed signal capturing the dominant component; a diffusion process models the residual uncertainty. The reverse denoising process is conditioned on the baseline signal and its latent representation, with a reliability-aware conditioning mechanism that adaptively adjusts the influence of baseline information during residual generation.

Result: Empirical evaluations on multiple benchmark datasets show that RDDMPI consistently improves both reconstruction accuracy and uncertainty quantification compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Modeling in residual space with baseline-guided diffusion simplifies the learning objective, concentrates the diffusion on structured corrections, and yields improved probabilistic imputation performance by combining deterministic guidance with stochastic residual modeling.

Abstract: Multivariate time series imputation (MTSI) aims to recover missing values in temporal data composed of multiple interdependent variables. This problem is central to real-world applications such as healthcare monitoring, traffic networks, and energy systems. Recent diffusion-based approaches have shown strong potential for probabilistic imputation by learning to generate missing values through iterative denoising. However, most existing approaches perform diffusion directly in the original data space, requiring the denoising network to simultaneously capture global structure, temporal dynamics, and stochastic variability. This makes the generative task unnecessarily complex, especially when modern deterministic imputers can already provide accurate initial reconstructions. To address this limitation, we propose RDDMPI, a conditional residual diffusion framework that operates directly in residual space. Instead of modeling the full missing signal directly, we reformulate probabilistic imputation as a baseline-residual decomposition, where a pretrained model captures the dominant signal and a diffusion process models the residual uncertainty. To better exploit deterministic guidance, \model{} conditions the reverse denoising process on both the baseline-completed signal and its latent representation, while a reliability-aware conditioning mechanism adaptively controls the influence of baseline information during residual generation. This formulation simplifies the diffusion learning objective, enabling it to focus on structured correction terms rather than reconstructing the full signal. Experiments on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate that RDDMPI consistently improves both reconstruction accuracy and uncertainty quantification.

</details>


### [213] [Musec: MomentUm SpEctral Clipping for Stable Muon-type Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11655)
*Zhuanghua Liu,Menglian Wang,Luo Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Musec introduces spectral clipping for Muon to stabilize training by clipping dominant momentum singular values rather than flattening them to one, with Soft Musec using Newton-Schulz iterations for efficient, smooth saturation; it offers convergence guarantees for nonconvex nonsmooth stochastic optimization and shows improved stability across diverse settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Muon improves LLM training but suffers from spectral flattening, causing instability (loss spikes, unbounded weights). Existing fixes (weight/attention clipping) are architecture-specific and don't address instability across all components. A device-agnostic optimizer-level solution is needed.

Method: Replace Muon's spectral flattening with spectral clipping that caps singular values exceeding a threshold while preserving the momentum's spectral structure. Develop Soft Musec, which uses a smooth spectral saturation function approximated via coupled Newton-Schulz iterations. Prove convergence guarantees for Musec in nonconvex nonsmooth stochastic optimization.

Result: Empirical studies show Soft Musec improves training stability across a wide range of learning rates and model sizes, remains stable where Muon variants diverge, and matches performance under well-tuned configurations.

Conclusion: Musec provides an architecture-agnostic optimizer-level stabilization for Muon-based training, backed by convergence guarantees in a challenging nonconvex nonsmooth setting and superior stability in practice.

Abstract: Muon has emerged as a highly effective optimizer for large language model training, often achieving superior convergence and performance compared with the widely adopted Adam and AdamW optimizers. Nevertheless, Muon is prone to training instability due to its spectral flattening, manifested by loss spikes and unbounded growth of model weights. Existing approaches primarily rely on weight or attention-logit clipping, which require architecture-specific modifications and do not directly address instability across all model components. We propose MomentUm SpEctral Clipping (Musec), which replaces Muon's spectral flattening with spectral clipping: rather than setting all singular values of the momentum matrix to approximately one, Musec clips singular values that exceed a threshold while preserving the underlying spectral structure of the momentum. Our strategy provides an optimizer-level, architecture-agnostic mechanism for stabilizing Muon training. We further develop Soft Musec, an efficient implementation that uses a smooth spectral saturation function approximated by coupled Newton-Schulz iterations. Theoretically, we establish convergence guarantees for Musec in nonconvex nonsmooth stochastic optimization. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first convergence guarantee for Muon-type methods in the nonconvex nonsmooth setting. We provide empirical studies to show that Soft Musec consistently improves training stability over existing Muon variants across a wide range of learning rates and model sizes. Notably, Soft Musec remains stable in settings where existing Muon variants diverge, while matching their performance under well-tuned configurations.

</details>


### [214] [Learnware and AI Model Management System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11656)
*Zhi-Hua Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transition from AI model storage to AI model management via learnware and Learnware Dock System (LDS), enabling identification, reuse, and assembly of independently developed models without sharing training data; provides a collaboration protocol.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Developers' training data remain private; models are not fully understood even by their creators; a mechanism is needed to compare, discover, and compose models across tasks and data domains without accessing raw data.

Method: Introduce learnware = model + specification; generate specification through a machine learning process that does not disclose training data and has data-preservation properties; specifications are reference-based and comparable across models; implement an LDS that uses specifications as a platform for discovery, reuse, and assembly; establish specification-based collaboration protocol for models and agents.

Result: Proposes a path toward powerful AI model management; specifications enable cross-model discovery, reuse, and composition; data-preservation and collaboration protocol properties are established.

Conclusion: Learnware and the LDS lay the groundwork for scalable AI model management without access to developers’ data; they enable future collaborative AI systems via a common specification protocol; practical realization and evaluation are future directions.

Abstract: The transition from file storage to database management systems transformed stored data into managed resources. AI now faces an analogous transition from AI model storage to AI model management. Existing model pools essentially serve as \textit{AI model storage systems}. What is needed instead are \textit{AI model management systems} that enable models trained by different developers, for different tasks, with different data, and under different objectives to be identified, reused, and even assembled to address future user tasks. Because AI model developers are generally unwilling to share their training data, such systems should operate without accessing the training data of model developers and, ideally, without accessing raw data of future users. This requirement poses a fundamental challenge: the functionality of a modern AI model may not be fully understood even by the developer who trained it. How, then, can a system identify which models are useful for a given user task, let alone assemble models developed independently for different purposes? At first glance, this objective may appear unattainable. It becomes possible, however, by upgrading the basic unit of management from a machine learning model to a \textit{learnware}. \textit{Learnware = Model + Specification}. The specification, whose assignment transforms a trained model into a learnware, is generated with the help of a machine learning process without disclosing the training data of the developer and has a theoretically established data-preservation property. The \textit{Learnware Dock System (LDS)} provides a path toward powerful AI model management systems. Because specifications are generated according to a published reference and are comparable across models, they can also serve as an AI model \textit{collaboration protocol} through which independently developed models, including intelligent agents, can collaborate.

</details>


### [215] [Why Does Post-Training Quantization Work?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11716)
*Yuxiang Chen,Michael Beyer,Jun Zhu,Jianfei Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Post-training quantization preserves LLM performance by two robust mechanisms: (i) per-layer quantization error tends to cancel with the input error via a counteracting residual interaction that develops during pretraining, slowing hidden-state error growth; (ii) the LM-head geometry biases outputs to preserve high-scored tokens, stabilizing predictions. Together, these factors explain why quantization noise has limited impact across depth and models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding why post-training quantization (PTQ) works for pretrained LLMs despite added noise is important for designing effective, low-cost model compression without retraining. The observed robustness suggests underlying dynamics of error propagation and output geometry that can inform quantization strategies.

Method: Compare full-precision and quantized forward passes to analyze error propagation. Identify two mechanisms: (1) counteracting residual interaction where a layer’s newly introduced error opposes the inherited input error, reducing discrepancy growth; (2) LM-head geometry that preserves scores/probabilities of top tokens. Validate the findings by experiments across multiple models and quantization settings.

Result: The analysis shows that the counteracting residual interaction develops during pretraining and is a major factor slowing hidden-error growth; LM-head geometry preferentially preserves high-ranked token predictions, stabilizing outputs. These mechanisms collectively explain why quantization errors can propagate through many layers with minimal impact, and the results hold across different models and quantization configurations.

Conclusion: Pretrained models exhibit intrinsic robustness to PTQ due to (i) error cancellation across layers and (ii) output-geometry bias toward top predictions. This explains successful post-training quantization and provides a basis for designing more effective, noise-tolerant quantization schemes.

Abstract: Post-training quantization compresses large language models (LLMs) by storing their weights at reduced precision, and each quantized weight introduces an error into the hidden states. Naively, these errors should accumulate with depth and corrupt next-token prediction; randomly initialized models accumulate these discrepancies rapidly, whereas quantized pretrained models accumulate much less hidden-state error and largely maintain downstream task performance, even though they were never trained with quantization noise. This raises the question we address: why does post-training quantization work? Comparing full-precision and quantized forward passes, we identify two mechanisms that characterize pretrained quantization robustness. First, the error a layer newly introduces tends to oppose the error it inherits from the layer's input. The two cancel partially such that the discrepancy between full-precision and quantized passes grows slowly. This counteracting residual interaction develops during pretraining. Our quantitative analysis identifies it as a major factor slowing hidden-error growth. Second, LM-head geometry preferentially preserves the scores and probabilities of high-ranked tokens, which typically represent the model's most confident predictions. Together, these mechanisms explain why quantization error that passes through numerous layers can still produce only small output changes, and we verify the findings across models and quantization settings.

</details>


### [216] [Predicting Privacy Leakage from Weight Spectral Density](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11780)
*Richard J. Preen,Jim Smith*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Spectral metrics from WeightWatcher correlate with MIA vulnerability, enabling scalable privacy auditing without shadow models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Shadow-model-based MIAs are expensive; cheap proxies are needed to assess privacy risk at scale. This work investigates whether spectral metrics from heavy-tailed self-regularisation (WeightWatcher) can proxy MIA leakage.

Method: Compute WeightWatcher spectral metrics (e.g., stable rank, Log alpha-Norm) on image and tabular classifiers and evaluate their correlation with MIA leakage, comparing against traditional generalisation measures across datasets.

Result: Stable rank shows a strong positive correlation with overall MIA success; Log alpha-Norm shows a consistent negative correlation with MIA vulnerability at low false-positive rates. These correlations are stronger than those obtained with the generalisation gap.

Conclusion: Neural network spectra encode information about privacy leakage not fully captured by overfitting metrics; spectral analysis is a promising approach for scalable privacy auditing.

Abstract: Membership inference attacks (MIAs) are widely used to audit the privacy disclosure risk of machine learning models, however current state-of-the-art attacks require training computationally expensive shadow models, making large-scale privacy evaluation impractical. In this work, we investigate whether inexpensive spectral metrics derived from the heavy-tailed self-regularisation framework can serve as proxies for MIA vulnerability. We evaluate several WeightWatcher spectral metrics on image and tabular classification tasks and compare their relationship with MIA privacy leakage against conventional measures of generalisation. Across datasets, stable rank exhibits a strong positive correlation with overall MIA success, while Log alpha-Norm shows a consistent negative correlation with MIA vulnerability at the low false-positive regime. These associations are observed to be stronger than those obtained using the generalisation gap. The results indicate that neural network spectra may contain information about privacy leakage that is not fully captured by conventional measures of overfitting, motivating spectral analysis as a promising direction for scalable privacy auditing.

</details>


### [217] [Dynamic language model representations for multi-objective reaction optimisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11790)
*Joshua W. Sin,David Ming Segura,Bojana Ranković,Siu Lun Chau,Marius D. R. Lutz,Andrea Anelli,Ryan P. Burwood,Kurt Püntener,Maximilian J. Notheis,Raphael Bigler,Philippe Schwaller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Learning reaction representations from textual descriptions via a fine-tuned language model integrated with Gaussian process surrogates enables efficient multi-objective Bayesian optimization, achieving rapid convergence and high yields in diverse cross-coupling and hydrogenation reactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Representations for chemically diverse reaction components are hard to generalize; conventional featurizations (one-hot, descriptors) are either uninformative or non-transferable; a shared, adaptable representation is needed to drive model-based optimization across reaction systems.

Method: Joint fine-tuning of a language model on textual reaction-condition descriptions, embedded within a multi-objective Bayesian optimization loop with Gaussian process surrogates; evaluated on nickel- and palladium-catalyzed cross-couplings under sequential and parallel experimentation; prospective validations include palladium-catalyzed cyanation and asymmetric hydrogenation across ligand/denticity and additives.

Result: Faster optimization convergence than descriptor libraries or one-hot encoding; two rounds of high-throughput experimentation (192 reactions, <3% of design space) yielded gram-scale conditions with 94% and 84% isolated yields, the latter at 99.6% enantiomeric excess.

Conclusion: Text-informed, task-adaptive representations enable efficient, generalizable multi-objective reaction optimization across diverse chemistries, reducing experimental burden while delivering high-quality, scalable outcomes.

Abstract: Optimising chemical reactions across multiple objectives, such as yield, selectivity, and safety, is central to chemical synthesis, and model-driven approaches depend critically on how reaction components are represented. Established featurisations are either chemically uninformative, as with one-hot encodings, or, as with molecular descriptors, do not readily extend across chemically distinct components. For structurally and functionally diverse components, it is therefore unclear what a shared representation should contain. Constructing such a representation is itself a challenging research undertaking that must be revisited for each new reaction system. Here we bypass this step by learning the reaction representation dynamically from text. Textual descriptions of reaction conditions are encoded by a fine-tuned language model trained jointly with Gaussian process surrogates, yielding task-adaptive representations within a multi-objective Bayesian optimisation loop. Across nickel- and palladium-catalysed cross-couplings in both sequential and parallel experimentation regimes, this approach reaches optimisation convergence in fewer experiments than descriptor libraries or one-hot encoding. Applied prospectively to a palladium-catalysed cyanation spanning mixed ligand denticity and heterogeneous additives, and to a three-objective asymmetric hydrogenation across chiral iridium and ruthenium catalyst families, two rounds of high-throughput experimentation (192 reactions, under 3% of each design space) delivered conditions translating directly to gram scale in 94% and 84% isolated yield, the latter at 99.6% enantiomeric excess.

</details>


### [218] [Thinking with Looped Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11801)
*Ayhan Suleymanzade,Chanhyuk Lee,Floor Eijkelboom,Nicholas M. Boffi,İsmail İlkan Ceylan,Jinwoo Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Looped flows train recurrence with local denoising objectives across a noise schedule, enabling inference-time iterative computation and multiple valid predictions; they outperform previous looped models on ARC-AGI benchmarks (58.8% and 12.2%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Backpropagation through long recurrent unrolls is often truncated to only a few updates, making it hard to train early recurrence steps to support future ones; there is a need for a training signal that promotes transferable recurrent computation and allows flexible, computation-heavy inference.

Method: Train the recurrence with local denoising objectives and enforce temporal association via progressively decreasing noise levels and shared noise. At inference, solve by integrating the velocity of a probability flow parameterized by the learned denoiser, coupled with recurrent states, enabling more computation through a finer temporal grid and producing multiple valid predictions from different initial noise samples.

Result: Looped flows outperform prior state-of-the-art looped models across six reasoning benchmarks, achieving 58.8% test accuracy on ARC-AGI-1 and 12.2% on ARC-AGI-2.

Conclusion: Looped flows address training-truncation issues in recurrent models, enabling transferable, computation-aware inference and supporting multiple valid outputs, with strong performance gains on reasoning benchmarks.

Abstract: Humans and machines often solve harder problems by spending more time on computation. In deep learning, looped models implement this idea during inference by recurrently updating a hidden state. In practice, however, their training backpropagates through only one or a few updates, making it hard to train early updates to support future ones. We propose looped flows, an approach that sidesteps this issue by training the recurrence with local denoising objectives. By imposing temporal association across denoising objectives through progressively decreasing noise levels and shared noise, the model is incentivized to learn recurrent states that transfer useful computation over time, even when gradients cover only a few updates. We then formulate inference as integrating the velocity of a probability flow parameterized by the learned denoiser, coupled with recurrent states. This allows solving harder problems by spending more computation through a finer temporal grid and enables multiple valid predictions from different initial noise samples. Across six reasoning benchmarks including two multi-solution benchmarks, looped flows outperform prior state-of-the-art looped models overall, achieving 58.8% test accuracy on ARC-AGI-1 and 12.2% on ARC-AGI-2.

</details>


### [219] [Model-Aware Schedules Improve Generation via Fiberwise Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11842)
*Luyi Jia,Boyan Zhang,Yilun Liu,Steffen Rulands*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces fiberwise optimal-transport–based model-aware schedules for diffusion models, achieving superior performance and apparent universality across models; notably a 38.6% relative FID improvement on CIFAR-10 with 16 function evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the limitations of model-agnostic kinetic baselines that ignore prediction error by incorporating model-aware risk into the time allocation along affine probability paths.

Method: Define affine fibers of signal/noise decompositions at fixed time/state on the probability path. Introduce a fiberwise prediction risk by averaging optimal-transport costs between the true and predictor-induced decompositions within these fibers. On a fixed coefficient curve, combine this risk with the coefficient-path kinetic action to obtain a closed-form optimal time allocation. Extend to general linear prediction targets. Estimate the risk from early baseline checkpoints. Evaluate DDPMs and flow matching across targets, configurations, datasets, and architectures. Demonstrate cross-model alignment of risk profiles in kinetic-reference coordinates and observability of universality; provide a frozen analytic allocation template.

Result: Model-aware schedules consistently outperform strong baselines, including a 38.6% relative FID reduction for flow matching on CIFAR-10 at 16 function evaluations. Fiberwise risk profiles, once normalized to unit area by kinetic-reference coordinates, align across independently trained models, and schedule deformations derived from these risks also align, indicating empirical universality. Pretrained-checkpoint diagnostics extend this agreement to larger conditional latent diffusion and 2-RF models; a frozen analytic allocation template preserves most improvements without further risk estimation.

Conclusion: Model-aware, fiberwise-OT scheduling yields robust, transferable improvements across diverse diffusion-model configurations. Risk-informed time allocation can be estimated from early checkpoints, suggesting practical universality and potential for deployment via a simple analytic template without heavy per-model risk fitting.

Abstract: Diffusion and flow-matching schedules control the signal and noise coefficients that mix data and noise along affine probability paths. Minimizing a kinetic action defined on coefficient paths, motivated by optimal transport, helps explain strong baselines but remains model-agnostic and ignores prediction error. Here we introduce a model-aware schedule construction based on fiberwise optimal transport. At a fixed time and state on the probability path, compatible signal/noise decompositions form an affine fiber. We define a fiberwise prediction risk by averaging optimal-transport costs between the true and predictor-induced decompositions within these fibers. On a fixed coefficient curve, combining this risk with coefficient-path kinetic action yields a closed-form optimal time allocation. This construction extends to general linear prediction targets, and the risk profile can be estimated from an early baseline checkpoint. We evaluate DDPMs and flow matching across prediction targets, training configurations, risk-estimation checkpoints, datasets, and architectures. Our model-aware schedules consistently outperform strong baselines, including a 38.6% relative FID reduction for flow matching on CIFAR-10 at 16 function evaluations. Each model-agnostic kinetic baseline determines its own kinetic reference coordinate. In these coordinates, fiberwise-risk profiles from independently trained models in different settings align closely after normalization to unit area. The resulting schedule deformations used in training also align, suggesting empirical universality across the evaluated models and settings. Pretrained-checkpoint diagnostics extend this normalized-risk agreement to larger conditional latent diffusion and 2-RF models. A frozen analytic allocation template retains most of the model-aware improvement without further risk estimation or model-specific fitting.

</details>


### [220] [AdamX: Cosine similarity meets gradient descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11867)
*Francisco Caldas,Ruben Belo,Cláudia Soares*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AdamX introduces a first-order optimizer that uses cosine similarity as an adaptive mechanism to modulate update magnitudes, with a variance rectification scheme for smoother early training; it is scalable, model-agnostic, and easily integrable, with empirical evidence of competitive convergence across benchmarks; code and experiments are provided.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for adaptable step-size control in first-order optimizers to improve convergence speed and stability, particularly in early training where variance can hamper optimization.

Method: Extend a standard first-order optimizer (similar to Adam) by using cosine similarity to adapt update magnitudes dynamically. Add a variance rectification component to promote smoother optimization in early stages. The approach is model-agnostic and designed to be easily plugged into existing training pipelines.

Result: Empirical results show competitive convergence rates across a range of datasets and architectures, measured by the number of epochs to reach predefined performance thresholds under a fixed hyperparameter budget. A public codebase and experimental suite are provided at the linked repository.

Conclusion: AdamX is scalable, model-agnostic, and easy to integrate, providing smoother early optimization and competitive convergence without requiring extensive hyperparameter tuning.

Abstract: We introduce AdamX, a first-order optimizer that incorporates cosine similarity as an adaptive mechanism for controlling update magnitudes. The proposed method is scalable, model-agnostic, and straightforward to integrate into existing training pipelines. We further introduce a variance rectification scheme that promotes smoother optimization during the early stages of training. Overall, we provide empirical evidence that AdamX achieves competitive convergence rates across a range of benchmark datasets and architectures. Performance is evaluated in terms of the number of epochs required to reach predefined performance thresholds under a fixed hyperparameter budget. Code and Experiments available at: https://github.com/FranciscoCaldas/adamX.

</details>


### [221] [The Last AI Built by Humans: Toward Genuine Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11873)
*Yi Duan,Ying Liu,Zirui Tang,Haodong Chen,Jun Zhou,Yumou Liu,Bangrui Xu,Yukai Wu,Sidi Chen,Yuhan Zhou,Haoyu Wang,Xiaoyou Yu,Shaokun Han,Xuzhou Zhu,Le Zhou,Bolin Lu,Wei Zhou,Jiachen Liu,Nuozhou Fang,Jiaxin Tian,Ruoyu Chen,Yuxuan Li,Kai Zuo,Kaiyan Zhang,Jiantao Qiu,Conghui He,Guoliang Li,Bowen Zhou,Zhiyuan Liu,Zhoufutu Wen,Jihua Kang,Xuanhe Zhou,Fan Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes recursive self-improvement (RSI) for AI, using Headroom-Closed Index (HCI) to diagnose LLM limits, and outlines a staged autonomy roadmap culminating in recursive meta-improvement. Analyzes RSI across domains, links to industry practice with preliminary evidence, and highlights key challenges for realising genuine RSI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivated by persistent limits of current LLM performance and growth, the authors seek a principled framework (RSI) and a roadmap to enable systems to autonomously improve their capabilities and improvement process, guided by HCI-driven diagnostics and cross-domain scenarios.

Method: Introduce HCI as a diagnostic tool for LLM shortcomings; define RSI and a four-stage autonomy roadmap (improvement-execution, improvement-strategy, experience-acquisition, environment-adaptation) plus recursive meta-improvement; analyze RSI across applications (scientific discovery, embodied AI, software engineering); draw on industry practices and preliminary empirical evidence to connect RSI to real systems and enumerate challenges.

Result: Conceptual framework with a staged RSI roadmap supported by preliminary evidence; cross-domain applicability; practical system considerations and identified challenges, but no full empirical validation reported within the abstract.

Conclusion: RSI is a promising direction that structurally organises autonomous improvement into progressively deeper autonomy and meta-improvement; realising genuine RSI will require resolving methodological, safety, and integration challenges, and aligning the framework with industry practices and empirical validation.

Abstract: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) enables AI systems to turn experience and feedback into persistent changes that improve both their capabilities and the process of future improvement.
