We first use the Headroom-Closed Index (HCI) to reveal the problems of existing LLMs, then introduce the RSI concept and its development roadmap: from improvement-execution autonomy, improvement-strategy autonomy, experience-acquisition autonomy, and environment-adaptation autonomy, to recursive meta-improvement. Next we examine RSI across scenarios (e.g., scientific discovery, embodied intelligence, software engineering), highlighting their distinct requirements and development speeds. Drawing on diverse industry practices and preliminary empirical evidence, we connect RSI research with practical systems and identify key challenges to achieving genuine RSI.

</details>


### [222] [CoRA-NAS: Coarse Ranking and Anchor-Residual Refinement for Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11884)
*Yifan Yang,Zhaoyan Wang,Zheng Gao,Xiaoyu Li,Jiaojiao Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Zero-cost proxies rank architectures cheaply, but their reliability varies across search spaces. We introduce CoRA-NAS (COarse Ranking + Anchor-residual), a two-stage framework combining a static ranking prior with low-cost learning-curve refinement. CoRA-Rank aggregates capacity and structure-at-initialization proxies through an equal-weight log-rank consensus and a target-free consensus gate. CoRA-Refine samples anchors across this prior, extrapolates their early validation curves, and propagates a learned residual correction with an ExtraTrees model. The refinement uses approximately 1% of the cost of fully training the candidate set. Fully trained architecture-accuracy labels are not used to fit the ranker. One configuration is used across spaces, with space-specific architecture encodings. Across NAS-Bench-201, NAS-Bench-101, TransNAS-Bench-101, and NATS-SSS, CoRA-Refine achieves mean Spearman correlations of 0.946, 0.715, 0.786, and 0.894, respectively. Its worst-space correlation of 0.715 is the highest among the compared methods. On NAS-Bench-201/CIFAR-100, its selected architecture reaches 73.32% accuracy, near the reported ground-truth best of 73.37%. On the pure size space, refinement recovers the static prior's shortfall relative to parameter count, while remaining tied with the strongest capacity proxies within noise. The resulting framework combines cross-space ranking robustness with low-cost architecture selection.

</details>


### [223] [CausalArena: Benchmarking Causal Discovery in the Foundation Model Era](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11897)
*Zi-Rong Li,Si-Yang Liu,Tian-Zuo Wang,Han-Jia Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CausalArena provides a unified, evolvable benchmark for causal discovery across synthetic, semantic, and formula-grounded SCMs plus real-world data, revealing that benchmark regime and pretraining overlap drastically affect evaluation, and that performance does not reliably transfer across SCM families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address fragmentation in causal-discovery benchmarks and the confounding influence of cross-domain pretraining, by creating a common, extensible evaluation framework that probes generalization across diverse SCM families and protocols.

Method: Introduce CausalArena with three SCM types—Synthetic SCMs (controlled structural/mechanistic variety), semantic operational SCMs (human-auditable, grounded environments beyond standard generators), and formula-grounded SCMs (explicit scientific mechanisms). Include public real-world datasets for external validity. Evaluate classical, neural, and pretrained methods under a unified protocol across SCM families.

Result: Experiments show substantial ranking shifts across SCM families and protocols. Strong performance on one benchmark regime does not reliably transfer to others. Benchmark diversity and pretraining–evaluation overlap emerge as central challenges for evaluating causal discovery, especially in the foundation-model era.

Conclusion: Diverse benchmarks and attention to pretraining–evaluation overlap are essential for robust assessment of causal discovery. CausalArena offers a unified, evolvable platform to study generalization across SCM families and evaluation strategies, addressing current benchmark-induced biases.

Abstract: Causal discovery aims to uncover causal structures from data and is fundamental to scientific reasoning and intervention-based decision making. Its evaluation relies heavily on structural causal models (SCMs), which specify a causal graph together with the mechanisms that generate data, yet existing studies differ substantially in graph families, mechanisms, and evaluation protocols. The emergence of causal discovery foundation models (CDFMs) further complicates evaluation: performance may reflect not only causal discovery ability, but also overlap between pretraining environments and test SCMs, making results on fixed synthetic benchmarks difficult to interpret. We introduce CausalArena, a unified and evolvable benchmark for causal discovery under a common protocol. Synthetic SCMs supply controlled breadth over structures and mechanisms; semantic operational SCMs provide human-auditable, semantically grounded environments beyond standard synthetic generators; and formula-grounded SCMs test discovery under explicit scientific mechanisms. Public real-world datasets provide an additional external-validity check. Experiments across classical, neural, and pretrained methods reveal substantial ranking shifts across SCM families and protocols, showing that strong performance in one benchmark regime does not reliably transfer to others. These results highlight benchmark diversity and pretraining--evaluation overlap as central challenges for evaluating causal discovery in the foundation model era.

</details>


### [224] [TART: A Modular Tool for Technique-Aware Audio-to-Tablature Guitar Transcription](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11904)
*Akshaj Gupta,Hwi Joo Park,Andrea Guzman,Shamak Gowda,Samhita Konduri,Jiachen Lian,Robin Netzorg,Gopala Anumanchipalli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TART is a four-stage audio-to-tablature pipeline that jointly transcribes guitar audio into MIDI, classifies expressive techniques, assigns string–fret via an audio-conditioned T5 model, and generates tablature, achieving strong zero-shot results on multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations in automatic music transcription for guitar—namely missing expressive techniques (slides, bends, percussive hits), incorrect string–fret assignments, and poor generalization to noisy real-world audio.

Method: A four-stage pipeline: (1) audio-to-MIDI transcription; (2) expressive technique classifier; (3) audio-conditioned T5 encoder–decoder for string–fret assignment; (4) automated tablature generator.

Result: Zero-shot evaluation on GuitarSet, EGDB, Noisy GuitarSet, and Noisy EGDB shows: audio-to-MIDI F50 = 81.35% (+6.67 over best baseline), string-fret Tab F1 = 71.8% (+8.5), end-to-end Tab F1 = 54.08%. TART is claimed to be the first framework producing guitar tablature with fingering and expressive technique annotations directly from audio.

Conclusion: TART demonstrates strong performance and generalization, introducing a novel end-to-end approach that jointly captures fingering and expressive techniques in guitar tablature from audio.

Abstract: Automatic Music Transcription (AMT) for guitar remains limited by three challenges: existing systems often fail to capture expressive techniques such as slides, bends, and percussive hits; they often assign notes to incorrect string-fret combinations; and they are typically trained on clean recordings, limiting their generalization to noisy real-world audio. To address these challenges, we propose TART, a modular four-stage audio-to-tablature pipeline consisting of (1) an audio-to-MIDI transcription model, (2) an expressive technique classifier, (3) an audio-conditioned T5 encoder-decoder for string-fret assignment, and (4) an automated tablature generator. We evaluate TART in a zero-shot setting on GuitarSet, EGDB, and two augmented benchmarks, Noisy GuitarSet and Noisy EGDB. Averaged across these four benchmarks, TART achieves 81.35% audio-to-MIDI F50 (+6.67 points over the best prior baseline), 71.8% string-fret Tab F1 (+8.5 points over the best prior baseline), and 54.08% end-to-end Tab F1. To our knowledge, TART is the first framework to generate guitar tablature with both fingering and expressive technique annotations directly from guitar audio.

</details>


### [225] [From Protocols to Evidence: Bounded Claims for AI in Service of the Common Good](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11910)
*Nitesh V. Chawla,Paulo Benanti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The abstract argues that AI governance must address institutional failures and proposes a framework (rupture test and RISE AI) to anchor AI deployment in bounded, evidence-based evaluations of Responsibility, Inclusivity, Safety, and Empowerment, linking system assessment to institutional baselines and emphasizing moral-political judgment and institutional repair.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move from principle-based commitments to practical, context-sensitive protocols that account for institutional power dynamics and the potential of AI to repair or exacerbate social ruptures.

Method: Proposes a conceptual framework: (a) rupture test linking institutional baselines to system evaluation; (b) distinction between evidence-bounded deployment and measurement-bounded governance; (c) an architecture (RISE AI) for bounded, evidence-based claims across Responsibility, Inclusivity, Safety, Empowerment; situates within existing governance standards (EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF, ISO 42001).

Result: A unified framework for responsible AI assessment that binds technical evaluation to institutional context, clarifies what protocols can legitimately claim, and provides structured criteria (RISE AI) for evaluating and reporting on Responsibility, Inclusivity, Safety, and Empowerment.

Conclusion: Responsible AI requires improved engineering, institutional repair, and ongoing moral-political judgment; measurement should be bounded by evaluated evidence, and governance should account for power dynamics and the common good.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence does more than create a governance problem. It can also reveal where institutions have already failed to provide responsiveness, belonging, care, and accountability. Once deployed, AI becomes an intervention in those conditions. It can repair, compound, substitute for, or conceal the failures it encounters. Responsible AI must therefore evaluate both the system and the institutional rupture into which it is introduced. The move from principles to protocols is already underway. The EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF, ISO/IEC 42001, and assurance practices translate commitments into roles, requirements, records, oversight, and assessment. The harder questions are what these protocols actually establish, whose power they leave untouched, and where measurement must stop. Pope Leo XIV's Magnifica Humanitas provides a broader moral frame centered on dignity, technological power, and the common good. Drawing on that frame, we develop a rupture test that links institutional baselines to system evaluation. We distinguish evidence-bounded deployment, which limits claims to what has actually been evaluated, from measurement-bounded governance, which records constraints that favorable evidence cannot override. Within those limits, RISE AI provides an architecture for making bounded, evidence-based claims about Responsibility, Inclusivity, Safety, and Empowerment. Responsible AI requires better engineering, institutional repair, and continued moral and political judgment.

</details>


### [226] [Data Scarcity and Model Sparsity: Mixtures-of-Experts Overfit More to Repeated Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11917)
*Atindra Jha,Margaret Li,Jure Leskovec,Percy Liang,Luke Zettlemoyer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MoE models degrade under data repetition more quickly than dense models, with sparsity driving this vulnerability. Some regularization (notably strong masking-based methods) can mitigate or even beat dense models under heavy repetition, but no method fully matches all-unique data. Mechanisms linked to overfitting include routing stabilization and expert specialization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: to understand how data repetition interacts with sparsity in Mixture-of-Experts architectures, across scales and configurations, and how regularization can mitigate memorization-driven degradation.

Method: empirical study varying repetition rates for data mixtures (single- and multi-domain) across MoE configurations (expert count and granularity) with 80M–1B active parameters (8.5B total). Compared against dense baselines. evaluated regularization methods (including dropout and masking-based schemes). Analyzed internal mechanisms: routing behavior and expert specialization in relation to overfitting under repetition.

Result: MoEs degrade more rapidly than dense models as data is repeated; degradation accelerates with sparsity, governed by total (not just active) parameters. 80M dense models tolerate up to ~8x repetition with minimal loss; MoEs begin to suffer at ~4x and deteriorate quickly, underperforming dense models under all-unique data beyond ~32x. Regularization helps; some masking-based methods enable MoEs to outperform dense models even when repetition exceeds 64x, but no method attains all-unique performance. Internal analysis shows early routing stabilization in MoEs and a correlation between expert specialization and overfitting to repeated data.

Conclusion: The study illuminates core mechanisms of overfitting in sparse architectures under data repetition and suggests directions to mitigate over-specialization by disrupting memorization patterns; future work should refine regularization or architecture strategies to reduce memorization and improve robustness to data repetition.

Abstract: As the supply of human-written text is exhausted, it has become standard practice to repeat language model training data. Prior work has studied data repetition for densely activated Transformers, but the effects of data repetition remains largely unexplored for recently dominant sparse architectures such as Mixture-of-Experts (MoE), despite their increased compute efficiency. We vary data repetition rates across single- and multi-domain data mixes, and across MoE settings, including expert count and granularity. We consistently find, for models ranging from 80M to 1B active (8.5B total) parameters, that MoEs degrade more rapidly under data repetition. This effect increases with sparsity, dictated by total rather than active parameters. While 80M dense models can repeat data over 8x with minimal degradation, MoEs instead begin to suffer at 4x, and deteriorate rapidly, ceding their performance benefits in all-unique data settings to underperform dense models after 32x. We experiment with existing regularization methods as a potential remedy. We find that some methods, such as dropout, can mitigate overfitting. In particular, with strong masking-based regularization, MoEs are able to outperform dense models even when data is repeated more than 64 times. However, no method fully matches the performance of all-unique training data. Finally, we analyze internal mechanisms correlated with MoE overfitting in high repetition regimes, and find that MoE routing universally stabilizes early in training, and that expert specialization correlates with overfitting to repeated data. In sum, our work addresses the adverse interactions between sparsity and data repetition: we present evidence for the core mechanisms of overfitting and its potential remediation, and suggest promising avenues for future methods to reduce over-specialization in model parameters by disrupting memorization patterns.

</details>


### [227] [General Quantification of Covariate and Concept Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11918)
*Hongbo Chen,Li Charlie Xia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes γ*-concept shifts using entropic OT to unify covariate and concept shifts, with estimators and a DataShifts tool to bound and quantify generalization error under distribution shift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between theory and practice in learning under distribution shift; existing bounds are narrow, non-estimable, and do not cover broad settings.

Method: Introduce γ*-concept shifts via entropic optimal transport; derive a general, broad-purpose error bound unifying covariate and γ*-concept shifts; develop estimators with concentration guarantees; implement the DataShifts algorithm to quantify shifts and estimate the bound.

Result: A general error bound applicable to broad loss functions and label spaces; estimators with concentration guarantees; DataShifts enables practical quantification of distribution shifts and bound estimation across applications.

Conclusion: Provides a rigorous, general framework for analyzing learning error under distribution shift and a practical toolkit (DataShifts) for estimating both shifts and the accompanying error bound.

Abstract: Generalization under distribution shift remains a core challenge in modern machine learning, yet existing learning bound theory is limited to narrow, idealized settings and is non-estimable from samples. In this paper, we bridge the gap between theory and practical applications. We first show that existing definition of concept shift breaks when the source and target supports mismatch. Leveraging entropic optimal transport, we propose a key notion: $γ^{*}\!$-concept shifts, and derive a general error bound unifying covariate and $γ^{*}\!$-concept shifts, which applies to broad loss functions, label spaces, and stochastic labeling. We further develop estimators for these shifts with concentration guarantees, and the DataShifts algorithm, which can quantify distribution shifts and estimate the error bound in most applications - a rigorous and general tool for analyzing learning error under distribution shift.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [228] [HuRo: Robotizing Human Videos for Scalable VLA Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10706)
*Jinho Jeong,Se June Joo,Jaehyun Kang,Dongyun Kim,Yena Kim,Hanjung Kim,Seon Joo Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Robotized human videos can effectively pretrain vision-language-action (VLA) policies; HuRo scales supervision from human videos, yielding substantial gains in task completion and OOD robustness, with end-to-end retargeted-action pretraining outperforming visual-only transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the human-to-robot embodiment gap and leverage abundant video data to scale supervision for robot manipulation policies by aligning observations and actions across modalities.

Method: Develop a robotization pipeline converting heterogeneous human videos into robot-aligned observations and action trajectories, inferring missing signals; construct HuRo with ~630k robotized episodes and 142M frames from five human-video sources; evaluate on four real-world manipulation tasks; perform ablations on visual robotization and retargeted actions.

Result: Pretraining scale improves completion from 51.5% to 80.3% and OOD completion from 34.9% to 72.2%. Visual robotization enhances OOD robustness; end-to-end pretraining with retargeted actions outperforms visual-only transfer; code and data released.

Conclusion: Robotized human videos are a viable, scalable supervision source for VLA policies, enabling strong in-domain and OOD performance; end-to-end, retargeted-action pretraining is advantageous.

Abstract: Human video datasets have emerged as a compelling alternative to expensive real-robot data, offering rich diversity at scale. To bridge the human-to-robot embodiment gap, existing approaches either robotize videos in task-matched settings or address observation and action alignment separately at scale. In this work, we systematically examine whether robotized human videos can provide effective and scalable supervision for pretraining vision-language-action (VLA) policies. To this end, we develop a robotization pipeline that converts heterogeneous human videos into robot-aligned observations and action trajectories while inferring missing intermediate signals across annotation levels. Using this pipeline, we construct the HuRo dataset, comprising about 630K robotized episodes and 142M processed frames from five human-video sources. Across four real-world manipulation tasks, increasing robotized pretraining scale improves overall completion from 51.5% to 80.3% and OOD completion under spatial and visual shifts from 34.9% to 72.2%. Ablations further show that visual robotization improves OOD robustness and that end-to-end pretraining with retargeted actions outperforms visual-only transfer. Code and data are released on our website: https://3587jjh.github.io/HuRo.

</details>


### [229] [When Information is Worth the Risk: Behavioral Valuation for Hazardous Robotic Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10726)
*Alkesh K. Srivastava,Aamodh Suresh,Carlos Nieto-Granda,Philip Dames*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces risk-augmented valuation for hazardous exploration using Prelec probability weighting to trade off information gain and risk; demonstrates transformed Pareto-frontier and improved risk-aware behavior in grid-world experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable robots to reason whether information gain justifies the risk in hazardous exploration where gathering data can trigger failures and truncation of future observations.

Method: Keep belief update, sensor model, physical risk model, and finite-horizon planner fixed; introduce a scalar objective—risk-augmented Behavioral Information via Prelec weighting; analyze switching boundaries and transformed Pareto frontier; validate with large-scale failure-truncated grid-world experiments comparing to Shannon-information planning and risk-aware baselines.

Result: Valuation reshapes the information-risk frontier; risk-aware objectives can reduce hazard exposure and losses by avoiding failure-truncations; the approach is Pareto-competitive with standard baselines and yields interpretable conservative and intermediate regimes.

Conclusion: The framework enables robots to assess not only how much uncertainty an action reduces but whether the reduction is worth the risk, yielding interpretable policies and competitive performance against risk-aware baselines.

Abstract: Hazardous robotic exploration requires robots to map spatial risks, such as unsafe terrain, radiation, fire, mines, or structural damage, while operating where collecting information can itself cause failure. A highly informative path may expose the robot to hazards, terminate execution, and prevent future observations. Hazardous exploration therefore requires deciding not only where uncertainty is largest, but when reducing it is worth the risk. This paper introduces a valuation-layer view of this problem. We keep the belief update, sensor model, physical risk model, and finite-horizon informative planner fixed, and change only the scalar objective used to rank feasible paths. Within this framework, we introduce a risk-augmented Behavioral Information objective based on Prelec probability weighting, yielding an interpretable family of conservative-to-aggressive information-risk valuations. Theoretically, we show that valuation parameters create switching boundaries between high-information/high-risk and lower-information/lower-risk paths, and induce a transformed Pareto-frontier structure over feasible exploration policies. Large-scale failure-truncated grid-world experiments show that valuation alone reshapes the information-risk frontier. Shannon information planning remains a strong raw-information baseline, while risk-aware objectives can reduce hazard exposure and robot losses by avoiding failures that truncate future sensing. Risk-augmented Behavioral valuation is Pareto-competitive with standard risk-aware baselines and provides interpretable conservative and intermediate regimes. These results support a framework in which robots reason not only about how much uncertainty an action reduces, but whether that reduction is worth the risk required to obtain it.

</details>


### [230] [Lie-Algebraic Bell Recurrences for Arbitrary-Order Twist Jets and Parallel-Mechanism Closure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10748)
*Daniel Condurache*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A high-order, purely kinematic framework unifying serial propagation, parallel-closure, and rigid-platform velocity fields within a dual-screw model. It uses Bell polynomials to organize derivatives, preserves noncommuting joint factors, and yields a platform twist jet mapped to affine invariants of velocity, acceleration, jerk, and snap. Validated on representative mechanisms up to fourth order with tight accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an arbitrary-order, purely kinematic method that simultaneously handles serial and parallel robotic architectures within a single mathematical framework, enabling explicit higher-order motion fields (up to jerk/snap) and affine invariants.

Method: Adopts a cylindrical joint as a native block; treats revolute/prismatic joints as special cases. For each fixed axis, uses ordinary Bell polynomials to organize derivatives of the exponential factor; maintains physical order of noncommuting factors; derives initial-frame prefix and terminal-resolved covariant representations of the serial twist jet. For parallel mechanisms, applies repeated Leibniz differentiation with Bell-polynomial-organized joint derivatives to obtain a triangular active-passive closure recurrence; solves the same passive Jacobian at each derivative order at regular configurations; RHS consists of prescribed active data and lower-order jets. Maps the resulting platform twist jet to affine-invariant velocity/acceleration/jerk/snap fields.

Result: Independent differentiation of rigid motion, affine-field evaluation, and differentiated closures agree through fourth order with residuals < 1e-12 (SI units). The approach provides equivalent representations of the serial twist jet and a consistent active-passive closure for parallel closures, at configurations where the partition is regular.

Conclusion: A purely kinematic, arbitrary-order framework for serial-parallel mechanisms in a dual-screw setting that yields platform affine fields and higher-order motion descriptors, valid at regular configurations and verified by multiple mechanism tests.

Abstract: This paper develops an arbitrary-order kinematic construction that links serial propagation, parallel-mechanism closure, and rigid-platform point fields within one dual screw framework. A cylindrical joint is retained as one native physical block, with revolute and prismatic joints obtained as special cases. For each fixed joint axis, ordinary Bell polynomials organize the derivatives of the exponential factor; across a chain, the noncommuting factors remain in their physical order. Initial-frame prefix and terminal-resolved covariant formulas then produce equivalent representations of the serial twist jet. For a parallel mechanism, repeated Leibniz differentiation, with joint-level derivatives organized by Bell polynomials, yields an arbitrary-order triangular active-passive closure recurrence: the same passive Jacobian is solved at every derivative order at a regular configuration, while the right-hand side contains only prescribed active data and lower-order jets. The resulting platform twist jet is mapped exactly to the point-independent affine invariants of the velocity, acceleration, jerk, and snap fields. The validation is deliberately complementary: a generic 3C chain with noncoplanar axes and nonzero rotational and translational cylindrical coordinates tests ordered serial propagation, an RR+RRR spherical wrist tests active-passive closure, and a Hunt-type 6-RUS mechanism with six active revolute joints tests an independently reconstructed platform jet and its affine fields. Independent differentiation of the rigid motion, evaluation of the affine fields, and the differentiated branch closures all agree through fourth order with residuals below $10^{-12}$ in the corresponding SI units. The formulation is purely kinematic and applies at configurations where the selected active-passive partition is regular.

</details>


### [231] [Expressive Robotic Pianist: Mastering Complex Piano Repertoire with Graph-Mimic and Musical Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10844)
*Yanhong Liang,Xianwei Liu,Chaojie Fu,Shaowen Cheng,Yanyan Yuan,Chengwei Zhuo,Xi Chen,Yongbin Jin,Wei Yang,Hongtao Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A dexterous robotic hand, trained with reinforcement learning and graph-based fingering optimization, achieves human-like expressivity in piano performance by coupling gesture planning with a physics-inspired audio model; it outperforms baselines in morphology and dynamics and is perceptually comparable to humans for non-professional listeners.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge mechanical dexterity and artistic expressivity in robotic pianism by capturing fluid finger transitions and dynamic control and enabling expressive sound production.

Method: An RL-based control framework augmented with a graph-based optimization module to plan natural pre-press and key-press fingering; a physics-inspired acoustic model links keypress velocity to sound dynamics; evaluated with objective metrics (finger morphology, dynamic velocity) and perceptual tests across musical styles.

Result: The expressive control model markedly outperforms baselines in morphology similarity and velocity accuracy; perceptual tests show significant preference over baselines and indistinguishability from human performances for non-professional listeners; robust note-level accuracy across styles.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a viable path for robotic musicianship to reach expressive performance levels comparable to human pianists, moving beyond mere mechanical precision.

Abstract: Enabling robots to perform musical instruments with human-level expressivity represents a frontier in bridging the gap between mechanical precision and artistic interpretation. Despite advances in robotic dexterity, replicating the fluid finger transitions and nuanced dynamic control characteristic of human pianists remains a significant challenge. Through a reinforcement learning-based control framework, we demonstrate that a dexterous robotic hand can achieve high-fidelity performance across a diverse piano repertoire. Central to our approach is a graph-based optimization strategy that guides the robot to generate natural pre-press and key-press fingering strategies that closely resemble human movement patterns. To achieve expressive sound production, the control system is coupled with a physics-inspired acoustic model that modulates keypress velocity to accurately reproduce the dynamic variations specified in musical scores. Quantitative evaluations demonstrate that our expressive control model significantly outperforms baseline methods in both finger morphology similarity and dynamic velocity accuracy. In a perceptual test involving participants from diverse listener groups, performances generated by our system are significantly preferred over baseline robotic performances and are indistinguishable from human performances for non-professional audiences. Furthermore, extensive experiments across multiple musical styles confirm that our method maintains high note-level accuracy while achieving expressive performance. Our approach provides a robust pathway for robotic systems to move beyond mere mechanical accuracy, elevating robotic musicianship to a level of expressive performance comparable to human pianists.

</details>


### [232] [ReactHuman: A Physics-Grounded Benchmark for Human-Like Reactive Decision-Making in Embodied Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10895)
*Yizhan Li,Jianxin You,Mengyang Xiong,Yinhuan Chen,Zicheng Zhao,Dekun Wu,Dongqing Zhang,Bang Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A physics-grounded benchmark, ReactHuman, tests MLLMs as the decision core of a simulated humanoid facing sudden hazards, measuring real-time reactive safety across 17 event families and 1,000 scenes derived from 240 Hz physics. It uses a five-metric safety/grounding rubric and executes plans to produce observable outcomes, evaluating seven MLLMs and revealing persistent safety gaps that do not improve with scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess real-time, physically grounded, safety-critical decision making in embodied agents, addressing the gap where existing benchmarks focus on passive intuitive physics or long-horizon tasks and do not test immediate action under sudden hazards.

Method: Construct a physics-grounded, reproducible benchmark (ReactHuman) with a simulated humanoid and adversarial objects in 240 Hz rigid-body dynamics, 17 event families and 1,000 scenes. Provide annotation-free ground truth and a five-metric scoring suite (reasonable, safe, physically grounded) to evaluate reactive decisions. Plans are physically executed to observe consequences, and seven representative MLLMs are tested.

Result: Reactive safety remains largely unsolved: about one in three hazards are mishandled, agents rely on appearance rather than motion, act from fixed dispositions, fail to intervene at meter-scale interception points, and scaling model size does not mitigate these failures.

Conclusion: ReactHuman offers a fine-grained diagnostic platform and a scalable learning signal toward physically grounded, safety-aware embodied agents; it confirms the need for improved reactive safety and provides a replicable benchmark (hosted on HuggingFace).

Abstract: Reacting to sudden physical hazards (catching a slipping plate, dodging a falling knife) is both a meaningful test of embodied intelligence and a hard requirement for deploying multimodal large language models (MLLMs) as the decision coreof household robots. Existing evaluations, however, probe intuitive physics passively through question answering over videos, or target deliberate, long-horizon tasks such as navigation and rearrangement; none measure whether a model can turn physical understanding into immediate, safety-critical action. We introduce ReactHuman, the first physics-grounded benchmark for human-like reactive decision-making, in which the evaluated MLLM acts as the brain of a simulated humanoid facing sudden household hazards; it spans 17 event families and over 1,000 bit-for-bit reproducible scenes with exact, annotation-free ground truth derived from 240 Hz rigid-body simulation, including adversarial objects whose appearance contradicts their physics (a foam anvil, a steel apple). We further design a five-metric suite that scores each reaction along three axes: reasonable, safe, and physically grounded. We physically execute every committed plan so that decisions have observable consequences. With this harness we evaluate seven representative MLLMs. Results show that reactive safety is far from solved: models mishandle roughly one hazard in three, act from fixed dispositions rather than the observed scene, trust appearance over motion, and miss interception points at meter scale even when the chosen action is correct; none of these failures shrink with model scale. ReactHuman thus offers both a fine-grained diagnosis and a scalable training signal toward physically grounded, safety-aware embodied agents. The benchmark can be found here: https://huggingface.co/datasets/Alan123/reacthuman-benchmark-scaled

</details>


### [233] [Planning along Differentiable Charts of Constraint Manifolds with General-Purpose IK Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10905)
*Thomas Cohn,Seiji Shaw,Harel Biggie,Travis Manderson,Nicholas Roy,Russ Tedrake*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Gradient-enabled analytic IK parameterization via the inverse function theorem to enable differentiable trajectory optimization under kinematic equality constraints; introduces a least-squares domain extension and a gradient-friendly reachability description; validated via numerical experiments and a hardware RB-Y1 demo.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable gradient-based planning and optimization when IK constraints reduce feasible configurations to a measure-zero manifold and many IK solvers are non-differentiable; provide differentiable IK parameterizations that leverage existing forward kinematics.

Method: Use the inverse function theorem to recover IK gradients from the ordinary forward kinematic Jacobian; introduce a least-squares domain extension to maintain gradient signals outside the reachable workspace; present an optimization-friendly formulation of reachability constraints; validate with numerical experiments and a hardware demonstration.

Result: Demonstrates that IK gradients can be recovered from the forward Jacobian via the inverse function theorem; preserves gradient information outside the reachable workspace; numerical experiments support efficacy; hardware demonstration with an RB-Y1 robot picking and placing a box.

Conclusion: IFT-based IK parameterization yields differentiable, optimization-friendly representations of reachability under strict kinematic constraints, enabling gradient-based planning with modifiable analytic IK and with non-differentiable automated IK solvers. It facilitates integration into trajectory optimization and planning loops; future work may extend to more robots and richer constraints.

Abstract: Planning trajectories for robot manipulators under kinematic equality constraints restricts feasible motions to a measure-zero submanifold of the configuration space, requiring special algorithmic treatment. A promising strategy is parametrizing the set of feasible configurations using analytic inverse kinematics (IK). Bespoke analytic IK functions can be written to be differentiable, a necessary property for gradient-based trajectory optimization. But the vast majority of IK functions are computed by automated meta-solvers like IKFast, and are difficult to modify for differentiability. We present a new approach for computing gradients of analytic IK parameterizations: we leverage the inverse function theorem to recover the desired gradients from the ordinary forward kinematic Jacobian. Furthermore, we present a least-squares domain extension and an optimization-amenable description of the reachability constraint, which preserves gradient signal outside the reachable workspace. We demonstrate the efficacy of our approach through numerical experiments and downstream tasks, including a hardware demonstration of an RB-Y1 picking up a box and placing it on a table. Project website: https://cohnt.github.io/inverse-function-theorem-parameterization/

</details>


### [234] [IMLE-VLA: Fast Single-Step Action Generation for Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10915)
*Kian Hosseinkhani,Qinhe Peng,George Shramko,Mehran Aghabozorgi,Jianing Qian,Tristan Engst,Alireza Moazeni,Dinesh Jayaraman,Ke Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: IMLE-VLA replaces iterative action heads with a single-step conditional generator trained via cIMLE, boosting speed and maintaining robustness in vision-language-action tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA policies using diffusion/flow-based action heads incur multi-step sampling, causing stop-and-go motion and limited throughput. A fast, multimodal, one-shot head could improve real-time control while preserving generalization.

Method: Replace the iterative action head in VLA with a single-step conditional generator trained by conditional Implicit Maximum Likelihood Estimation (cIMLE). This promotes multimodal action coverage to avoid mode collapse and eliminates multi-step sampling. Evaluate on π0.5, LIBERO and LIBERO-plus benchmarks, plus real-world Franka Panda tasks.

Result: Inference frequency increases by 3.67x (55 Hz vs. 15 Hz); action throughput up to 11x higher. On LIBERO (40 tasks), average success rate reaches 98.0%, with robust performance under perturbations. Real-world Panda experiments show 2.2x–3.0x lower jerk and faster task completion; VLA inference time per episode reduces by 3.9x–6.6x. Code and videos available.

Conclusion: The cIMLE-based head preserves π0.5-style robustness and generalization while delivering substantial speedups and smoother motion, demonstrating practical advantages for real-time VLA control.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies leverage pretrained vision-language backbones to achieve strong cross-task generalization. A leading design couples this backbone with a dedicated continuous action head trained via diffusion or flow matching. However, such heads rely on iterative multi-step sampling, for example 10 Euler steps in $π_{0.5}$. This creates an inference bottleneck that produces stop-and-go movement in the robot and slower task completion. We introduce IMLE-VLA, which replaces the iterative action head with a single-step conditional generator trained via conditional Implicit Maximum Likelihood Estimation (cIMLE). The cIMLE objective promotes multimodal action coverage, avoiding the mode collapse of naive regression heads while eliminating multi-step sampling entirely. When IMLE-VLA is applied to $π_{0.5}$, it increases inference frequency 3.67x (55 Hz vs. 15 Hz), enabling up to 11x higher action throughput. On the 40-task LIBERO benchmark, IMLE-VLA achieves the highest average success rate (98.0%) among all baselines while leading in inference frequency. Under the test-time perturbations of LIBERO-plus, IMLE-VLA retains $π_{0.5}$'s robustness while other baselines degrade sharply, confirming that the cIMLE head preserves generalization. Real-world experiments on a Franka Emika Panda across four tasks demonstrate smoother motion (2.2x to 3.0x lower jerk) and faster task completion, with IMLE-VLA outperforming $π_{0.5}$ on every task and reducing average VLA inference time per episode by 3.9x to 6.6x. Videos and code are available at https://kianhk6.github.io/IMLE-VLA/

</details>


### [235] [ObstaDiff: Generalizable Diffusion Policy Learning via Obstacle-aware Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10918)
*Jiawen Wang,Kevin Yao,Khalid Jawed*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ObstaDiff: a decomposed diffusion-policy with an obstacle-aware visual encoder for cluttered manipulation, achieving higher success and fewer collisions in real greenhouse tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation-learning policies struggle to generalize to cluttered, unstructured environments with obstacles; there is a need for explicit obstacle-aware motion generation to operate in real-world scenes.

Method: Introduce ObstaDiff, a decomposed diffusion-policy framework with a lightweight obstacle-aware visual encoder that produces a target–obstacle–background representation. The downstream alignment policy then generates end-effector trajectories toward a target-centered bottleneck pose while reasoning about surrounding obstacles.

Result: Evaluated on 61 real-robot greenhouse trials per method (366 executions in total). Achieved 75.41% average task success and 8.20% average obstacle collision rate, outperforming representative imitation-learning baselines and improving generalization in cluttered agricultural scenes.

Conclusion: ObstaDiff demonstrates effective obstacle-aware diffusion-based policy for real-world clutter, improving success rates while reducing collisions in agricultural manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Imitation learning has achieved impressive results in robotic manipulation, yet most existing approaches assume clean backgrounds and lack explicit mechanisms for obstacle-aware motion generation. Extending such policies to cluttered, real-world scenes with unstructured obstacles remains a key generalization challenge. We present ObstaDiff, a decomposed diffusion-policy framework with a lightweight obstacle-aware visual encoder. ObstaDiff extracts a structured target-obstacle-background representation, enabling the downstream alignment policy to generate end-effector trajectories toward a target-centered bottleneck pose while reasoning about surrounding obstacles. We evaluate ObstaDiff on 61 real-robot greenhouse trials per method (366 executions in total). ObstaDiff achieves 75.41% average task success and 8.20% average obstacle collision rate, outperforming representative imitation-learning baselines and improving generalization in cluttered agricultural scenes.

</details>


### [236] [Testing Between the Test Cases: Proving End-to-End Steering in Conditions You Never Drove](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10951)
*Menuka Ghalan,Charles Rodgers,Zachary D. Asher*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Formal verification via bound propagation can detect failure conditions in end-to-end steering models for automated driving that are not found by simulation, making verification a viable complement to simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridging the gap between test conditions and real-world performance: models that pass tests may still fail in reality; need rigorous safety guarantees for AVs.

Method: Train two small end-to-end steering networks in CARLA: one on clear conditions; another on clear plus fog, night, and low sun. Evaluate against a 2.19 ft lane-departure budget. Use bound propagation to read trained weights and compute maximum steering drift as a function of disturbances between two captured images. A single verification run covers enormous scenario space (133 poses with 10 intensities each -> 10^133 combinations).

Result: Formal verification identified conditions that broke the clear-trained policy without additional simulation testing; also provided preliminary evidence of potential failures between test cases, demonstrating the method’s ability to explore large, unseen portions of the input space.

Conclusion: Formal verification is a viable complement to simulation and could be adopted as part of verification and validation workflows for automated driving.

Abstract: AI-based automated vehicle testing is challenging because a model that passes every test condition can still fail in the real world. Formal verification offers a way to directly address this gap. On a simulated highway and an arterial road we trained two small end-to-end steering networks each in CARLA, one on clear conditions alone and one on clear, fog, night and low sun. All four models were driven against a 2.19 ft lane-departure budget. Without driving again, we used bound propagation, a formal method that reads the trained weights, to compute how far steering can drift at every disturbance strength between two captured images. One calculation covers more than a campaign could drive: on the arterial it spans 133 poses, where ten intensities each would be 10^133 combinations, in minutes on one GPU. Not only did formal verification find conditions that broke the clear-trained policy without simulation testing, it provided some preliminary evidence for potential failures between the test cases. Our overall conclusion is that formal verification is a viable complement to simulation, and could be adopted as a part of verification and validation for automated driving.

</details>


### [237] [LTLDiff: Finite Linear Temporal Logic-Guided Data Generation and Diffusion Policies for Multi-agent Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11043)
*Chuhan Meng,Haiyan Yin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LTLDiff learns LTLf specifications from natural language to steer diffusion-based policies for coordinated multi-agent manipulation, addressing timing/ordering issues and improving task success versus baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coordination in multi-agent manipulation requires task-level temporal, logical, and safety constraints. Diffusion policies alone suffer desynchronization and incorrect action ordering in simultaneous/sequential interactions. A mechanism to encode and enforce complex specifications is needed.

Method: LTLDiff combines LTLf specification learning from NL instructions via a large language model, embeds LTLf using an abstract syntax tree into a fixed-dimensional vector to condition data collection and diffusion policy training, and uses logic-guided demonstrations to align trajectories with desired ordering and coordination.

Result: Experiments on multi-agent LTLDiff manipulation tasks show improved task success rates compared to baseline methods.

Conclusion: LTLDiff demonstrates that integrating learned LTLf specifications with diffusion-based policies enables reliable coordination and ordering in multi-agent manipulation, highlighting promise for instruction-driven, scalable coordination.

Abstract: Multi-agent robotic manipulation tasks require coordination among agents to satisfy task-level temporal, logical, and safety constraints. Recently, diffusion policies have been used to perform the task. However, they still suffer from desynchronization, incorrect action ordering, and coordination failures in tasks that require simultaneous or sequential multi-agent interaction. Therefore, LTLDiff is proposed as a framework that combines Finite Linear Temporal Logic (LTLf) specification learning for both the generation of demonstrations and learning via diffusion policies. Each task has a specific LTLf formula that is learned from a set of natural language instructions using a large-scale language model. To enable a fixed-dimensional vector embedding of the learned specification from the language model, LTLf uses an abstract syntax tree representation scheme. This embedding of logic serves as a condition for (i) logic-guided data collection and (ii) diffusion-based policy training, encouraging trajectories that are consistent with the desired ordering and coordination requirements. Experiments on multi-agent LTLDiff manipulation tasks demonstrate improved task success rates compared to the baseline. Together, these contributions demonstrate the effectiveness of LTLDiff for coordinated multi-agent manipulation.

</details>


### [238] [Gait-Dependent Effects on Quadruped Locomotion for Load-Carrying using Passive Mechanism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11059)
*Giovanni B. Dessy,Claudio Semini,Victor Barasuol*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Underdamped passive-arm impedance in a quadruped payload system increases passive-joint oscillations and can shrink ZMP margin during crawl gaits; damped vs underdamped configurations are compared in simulation, with trot used as excitation; results presented as gait-payload-stiffness-damping maps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce actuation by using passive interfaces while maintaining stability in payload-carrying quadruped locomotion; understand how stiffness and damping of a passive arm influence dynamic stability across gaits and payloads.

Method: Simulation study of flat-ground locomotion; compare damped and underdamped passive-arm impedance configurations; analyze crawl gaits with well-defined support polygon; evaluate ZMP-margin with respect to the support polygon; use trot as a passive-arm excitation case but not for direct ZMP-margin comparison; synthesize results into gait-payload-stiffness-damping maps.

Result: Underdamped impedance leads to larger passive-joint oscillations and can reduce ZMP margin during crawl gaits; damped impedance exhibits smaller oscillations and typically better ZMP margins; trot is kept as an excitation scenario but not used for direct stability comparison; results captured in maps showing stability-related metrics across gait, payload, stiffness, and damping.

Conclusion: Passive-arm impedance choice materially affects stability margins and joint dynamics in payload-carrying quadrupeds; optimized design requires balancing lower oscillations against potential ZMP margin reductions, with guidance provided via gait-payload-stiffness-damping maps.

Abstract: Passive mechanical interfaces offer a lightweight alternative to actuated manipulators for quadruped payload carrying, but their impedance directly couples the payload dynamics with the locomotion pattern. This paper analyzes how passive-arm stiffness-damping selection affects payload-carrying locomotion under different gait and payload conditions. We compare damped and underdamped passive-arm impedance configurations in simulation during flat-ground locomotion. For crawl gaits, where the support polygon remains well defined, the results show that underdamped impedance increases passive-joint oscillations and can reduce the ZMP margin with respect to the support polygon. Trot is retained as a dynamic excitation case for the passive arm, but it is not used for direct ZMP-margin stability comparison. The results are summarized in gait-payload-stiffness-damping maps, where ZMP-margin reduction is evaluated for crawl gaits and trot is retained only as a passive-arm excitation case.

</details>


### [239] [Freehand Sketching for End-User Programming of Robot Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11078)
*Riwa Karam,Ian Kuo,Magnus Egerstedt*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Freehand sketching as an intuitive end-user programming interface for robot swarms, enabling non-experts to specify swarm formations and control with a robust, distributed method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable accessible human-swarm collaboration by lowering the programming barrier; allow non-experts to convey spatial intent visually for swarm formation tasks.

Method: Users sketch target formations; the swarm computes formation nodes and a rigid formation graph, assigns robots to nodes, and executes distributed formation control with guarantees against unintended reflected formations; a sketch-to-swarm pipeline is implemented and evaluated.

Result: Human study with 20 participants generating 42 geometric shapes; System Usability Scale score of 84.25 (high usability). The interface supports freehand formation specification without robotics/programming expertise.

Conclusion: Freehand sketching is a viable and intuitive interaction abstraction for human-swarm collaboration, enabling non-experts to specify and control swarm geometries effectively.

Abstract: Robot swarms are increasingly used in applications where accessible interaction with non-expert users is desirable. This paper investigates freehand sketching as an end-user programming interface for specifying robot swarm geometries. Users communicate spatial intent through a drawing, while the swarm autonomously extracts target formation points, constructs a rigid formation graph, assigns robots to formation nodes, and executes distributed formation control with a guarantee against unintended reflected formations. The resulting sketch-to-swarm framework is evaluated through a human study examining the usability of freehand formation specification. Twenty participants generated $42$ geometric shapes, and the interface achieved a mean System Usability Scale score of $84.25$, which conventionally indicates high perceived usability. The results support freehand sketching as an intuitive interaction abstraction for human-swarm collaboration without requiring robotics or programming expertise.

</details>


### [240] [RIDE: Relocalization-Informed Depth Estimation with 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11079)
*Jiarong Lian,Zhe Xiao,Zhaoyang Zhang,Wei Li,Ruizhi Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RIDE fuses PnP-derived sparse depth with a pretrained video-depth prior under a metrically scaled 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) prior to produce dense metric depth from RGB streams, with temporal memory to bridge observation gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exploit localization geometry to support dense, metric-depth perception in robotics and achieve depth consistency during intermittent observations, without explicit per-sequence depth calibration.

Method: Integrate sparse metric depth from PnP-RANSAC inliers with a pretrained video-depth model under a 3DGS prior; employ global and local depth correction and temporal memory to maintain depth estimates through short observation gaps after metric scale initialization; model trained on public RGB-D videos and evaluated on robot sequences without fine-tuning.

Result: Compared to scale-only calibration, RIDE improves depth accuracy and temporal consistency; demonstrates that localization geometry can support both pose recovery and dense perception in robotics.

Conclusion: Localization geometry, when combined with a geometric prior and temporal memory, enables dense depth estimation from RGB streams in robotics, extending relocalization techniques to dense robot perception.

Abstract: Render--match--PnP relocalization establishes correspondences between query image pixels and 3D map points for camera pose recovery, but their potential to support dense depth estimation is often overlooked. To exploit this geometric information, we present RIDE, which estimates dense metric depth from a robot's RGB stream. Given a metrically scaled 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) model, RIDE combines sparse metric depth observations derived from PnP-RANSAC inlier correspondences with the geometric prior of a pretrained video-depth model. To handle uneven and intermittent observations, it integrates global and local depth correction with temporal memory, supporting depth estimation through short observation gaps after metric scale initialization. Trained on public RGB-D videos, RIDE is evaluated on robot sequences without fine tuning. Experiments show improved depth accuracy and temporal consistency over scale-only calibration, demonstrating how localization geometry can support both pose recovery and dense robot perception.

</details>


### [241] [Harness Robotic OS: A Unified Embodied-Agent Runtime for Closed-Loop Quadruped Inspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11225)
*Yaoyuan Yan,Zhiyou Heng,Haoxiang Jie,Gang Liu,Hongjie Yan,Wei Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A unified embodied-agent runtime (HROS) enables integrated sensing, autonomy, language interaction, and memory for deployable inspection robots; Argos demonstrates residential-community inspection with high navigation accuracy and rapid hazard detection, validating a scalable software foundation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current quadruped inspection systems rely on task-specific interfaces, which hinders contextual coordination, knowledge reuse, and safe adaptation. A unified, traceable runtime is needed to connect heterogeneous sensing, autonomy skills, cognition, interaction, and enterprise response in a deployable loop.

Method: Proposes Harness Robotic OS (HROS) as a unified embodiment-runtime architecture with four planes: robot runtime, embodied autonomy skills, cognitive agent runtime, and interaction/operations. Uses a shared context, streaming ASR/TTS, memory hierarchies (working, episodic, semantic), and a safety-gated self-evolution loop for versioned updates. Argos implementation integrates Vbot quadruped, Fast-LIO2 for SLAM, Hobot-Stereo for depth, PCT-Planner global planning, EGO-Planner local planning, and OpenClaw with Qwen3-VL for inspection analysis; evaluated in a residential property.

Result: In experiments, Achieved 100% waypoint reachability, outdoor localization error <10 cm, local obstacle-response latency <200 ms, hazard-detection rates of 85–95%, and 99% success in alarm delivery and structured-report generation.

Conclusion: The results validate the deployed navigation/inspection closed loop and position HROS as an extensible software foundation for memory-augmented, voice-aware, and continuously improvable embodied inspection agents.

Abstract: Autonomous property inspection requires more than robust robot navigation: a deployable system must connect heterogeneous sensing, reusable autonomy capabilities, multimodal scene understanding, human interaction, and enterprise response within a traceable operational loop. Existing quadruped inspection systems commonly integrate these functions through task-specific interfaces, making contextual coordination, knowledge reuse, and controlled adaptation difficult. This paper presents \textit{Harness Robotic OS} (HROS), a unified embodied-agent runtime, and Argos, its realization for residential-community inspection. HROS organizes the system into robot runtime, embodied autonomy skills, cognitive agent runtime, and interaction and operations planes. A shared context connects physical state with agent reasoning; streaming ASR/TTS supports voice-based mission interaction; hierarchical working, episodic, and semantic memory preserves operational knowledge; and a safety-gated self-evolution loop converts execution traces into versioned candidate updates without permitting unconstrained online modification. The Argos prototype integrates a Vbot quadruped, Fast-LIO2 localization and mapping, Hobot-Stereo depth perception, PCT-Planner global planning, EGO-Planner local motion generation, and OpenClaw-orchestrated Qwen3-VL inspection analysis. Experiments in a residential property environment achieved 100\% waypoint reachability, outdoor localization error below 10~cm, local obstacle-response latency below 200~ms, representative hazard-detection rates of 85--95\%, and 99\% success in alarm delivery and structured-report generation. These results validate the deployed navigation and inspection closed loop, while HROS provides an extensible software foundation for memory-augmented, voice-aware, and continuously improvable embodied inspection agents.

</details>


### [242] [Beyond Noise Steering: Dual-Latent Space Reinforcement Learning for Generative Robot Policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11270)
*Pengfei Zhang,Teng Sun,Xianchao Xiu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A dual-latent-space reinforcement learning approach (DLSRL) enhances pretrained generative robot policies by jointly steering initial noise and modulating intermediate representations inside a frozen generator, enabling faster online adaptation without updating the base policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning methods that only steer the noisy latent space fail to influence intermediate action representations during generation, causing inefficiency and degraded performance. There is a need for representation-level control within pretrained generative policies.

Method: The actor outputs two latent variables: (1) an initial-noise latent that guides behavior generation, and (2) an action-representation latent that modulates intermediate features. The representation latent is mapped to adapter features and injected into the hidden states of intermediate action tokens via residual connections, enabling dual control without modifying the base policy.

Result: Experiments across various generative policy architectures and robotic manipulation tasks show accelerated online policy adaptation and competitive performance; code is released at the provided GitHub link.

Conclusion: Dual latent control over generation and intermediate representations improves adaptability of pretrained generative policies, is generalizable across architectures, and facilitates faster, efficient policy adaptation without updating the base policy.

Abstract: Pretrained generative robot policies learn expressive action priors from demonstrations. However, existing reinforcement learning methods only steer the noisy space but fail to modulate intermediate action representations during the generation process, resulting in performance degradation and inefficiency. To address this limitation, we propose a novel Dual-Latent Space Reinforcement Learning (DLSRL) framework, which complements initial-noise steering with representation-level control inside the frozen generator. Specifically, our actor network predicts two distinct latent variables: an initial-noise latent variable that steers behavior generation, and an action-representation latent variable for intermediate feature modulation. Moreover, this representation latent variable is mapped to adapter features and ingeniously injected into the hidden states of intermediate action tokens via residual connections. Our dual-control design enables direct adjustment of action representations without updating the base policy. Experiments across generative policy architectures and robotic manipulation tasks show that DLSRL effectively accelerates online robot policy adaptation and achieves competitive performance. Our code is available at \href{https://github.com/xianchaoxiu/DLSRL}{https://github.com/xianchaoxiu/DLSRL}.

</details>


### [243] [2AM: Grounding Agent-Side Memory as Guidance for Steerable Action Models in Long-Horizon Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11308)
*Yutong Hu,Fengjiao Chen,Xuezhi Cao,Renaud Detry*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 2AM demonstrates that keeping task memory inside the Agent, paired with a simple RGB-based Action Model and structured hints, enables strong long-horizon manipulation without depth/geometry/planners—achieving 76.3% average completion on LIBERO-Mem and major gains over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify whether task memory and steerability reside in the Agent versus external tools, the study constrains tool breadth to isolate the memory interface and assess its contribution to long-horizon manipulation.

Method: A multimodal Agent holds task memory and generates subtasks in language plus optional 2D grasp/place/move hints; a single RGB-based, episodically stateless Action Model executes motion. The agent compiles interaction history into hints and language. Demonstrations are augmented with structured hint labels and trained with condition dropout, spatial noise, and temporal jitter to tolerate imperfect outputs. Evaluation on LIBERO-Mem omits depth, online geometry, and planner-based object motion.

Result: 2AM achieves 76.3% average completion, a 61.5-point improvement over the strongest baseline (14.8%). It also reports 63.0% relaxed and 11.8% strict success.

Conclusion: Task memory can reside on the Agent side; the effectiveness of the Action Model depends on how precisely the Agent steers it. Structured hints and robust training enable steerable, memory-backed, long-horizon manipulation without heavy perceptual/tooling requirements.

Abstract: Long-horizon robot manipulation requires memory, but not necessarily inside the action policy. To address such tasks, current agentic systems often combine VLAs with planners and geometric tools, sometimes using additional depth or calibrated geometry. These systems confound attribution: gains may come from richer observations or alternative motor tools, while failures may stem from either the policy or an under-specified language interface. We isolate this question through a deliberately constrained design: less tool breadth, but greater interface bandwidth. 2AM makes a multimodal Agent the sole holder of task memory and a single RGB-based, episodically stateless Action Model the sole executor of task-relevant motion. The Agent compiles interaction history into subtask language and optional 2D grasp, place, and move hints that bind its physical intention at different time scales. To teach this steerability to the VLA, we augment demonstrations with structured hint labels and train under condition dropout, spatial noise, and temporal jitter to tolerate imperfect Agent outputs. On LIBERO-Mem, without depth, online geometry, or planner-based object motion, 2AM reaches 76.3% average completion, a 61.5-point improvement over the strongest reported baseline of 14.8%, together with 63.0% relaxed and 11.8% strict success. These results show that task memory can remain Agent-side. They further show that Action Model capability depends not only on what the policy has learned, but on how precisely the Agent can steer it.

</details>


### [244] [Modular Kinematic Reduction of Closed-Chain Mechanisms Using Path Assembly and Defect Homotopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11338)
*Mohammad Dastranj,Jouni Mattila*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces PACDM for modular closure resolution in closed-chain kinematics; uses SE(3) log-map mismatch and Jacobians; constructs multi-path modules, uses defect homotopy and predictor-corrector continuation; validated on a 7-DoF manipulator with ultra-high accuracy and speed advantage over defect-homotopy per-sample.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Closed kinematic chains couple active and passive coordinates; modular modeling is hard; need robust closure resolution, kinematic reduction, and efficient motion propagation.

Method: Define closure elements comparing two transformation paths; mismatch via SE(3) logarithm; assemble Jacobian; build multi-path modules from pairwise closures; apply rank-revealing analysis to select independent scalar constraints; use defect homotopy to recover passive coords from approximate estimates along a continuation path; compute local active-to-passive differential mapping via implicit differentiation at regular configurations; use predictor-corrector continuation to drive prescribed motion.

Result: Applied to seven-DoF heavy-duty manipulator with two-path and three-path modules; RMSE trajectory error < 8.5e-10 rad vs Simscape Multibody; predictor-corrector acceleration ~45.8x faster than defect homotopy at every sample.

Conclusion: PACDM enables accurate modular closure resolution and fast motion propagation in closed-chain mechanisms; demonstrates high accuracy and significant computational speedup; promising for scalable modular dynamic modeling.

Abstract: Closed kinematic chains complicate modular modeling by coupling active and passive coordinates through nonlinear closure constraints. This paper presents a Path-Assembled Closure Differential Mapping (PACDM) framework for modular closure resolution and kinematic reduction. Each closure element compares two ordered transformation paths with common endpoints, with their mismatch expressed through the logarithm on SE(3) and the corresponding Jacobian assembled from local transformation derivatives. Multi-path modules are constructed from a minimal set of pairwise closure elements, while rank-revealing analysis selects locally independent scalar constraints. A defect homotopy recovers closure-consistent passive coordinates from approximate estimates along a feasible and regular continuation path. At regular configurations, implicit differentiation yields the local active-to-passive differential mapping, which is subsequently used in a predictor-corrector continuation procedure for prescribed motion. The framework is evaluated on a seven-degree-of-freedom heavy-duty manipulator containing two-path and three-path closed-chain modules. Comparison with Simscape Multibody yields trajectory root-mean-square errors below 8.5 x 10^-10 rad, while predictor-corrector continuation is approximately 45.8 times faster than applying defect homotopy at every trajectory sample.

</details>


### [245] [Morphology-Aware Human Motion Retargeting for Wheeled-Humanoid Loco-Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11357)
*Chenbo Xia,Chao Ye*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A reproducible pipeline converts multi-dataset SMPLX human motion into executable loco-manipulation for a wheeled humanoid (Galaxea R1 Pro), integrating morphology-aware retargeting, torso substitution, and a learned 21D BaseDecode policy to produce trackable wheel-based locomotion with arm manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap in human-to-humanoid retargeting for wheeled-humanoid systems, where lower-body motion must be redistributed across base motion and torso while preserving manipulation-relevant arm geometry, enabling a complete bridge from human motion to executable loco-manipulation.

Method: 1) Canonical body-shape preprocessing and planar-base normalization. 2) Morphology-aware differential inverse kinematics. 3) Shoulder-rooted hierarchical arm retargeting. 4) Continuous torso substitution for bending/squatting. 5) Reference-twist-driven planning to convert planar base motion into three-wheel steering and rolling commands with hysteresis, kinematic continuity, acceleration, and actuator-rate limits. 6) 21-dimensional BaseDecode policy trained in Isaac Lab with directional joint-limit scaling, focused upper-body tracking, and staged wheel-contact reward.

Result: A complete, reproducible pipeline translating multi-dataset SMPLX motion into executable loco-manipulation behavior for the Galaxea R1 Pro wheeled humanoid, demonstrated in simulation (Isaac Lab) with a plan to compare policies in a future revision.

Conclusion: The framework provides a full bridge from human motion data to physically trackable loco-manipulation on a wheeled-humanoid, integrating retargeting, planning, and learning; quantitative policy comparisons are slated for a future revision.

Abstract: Human-to-humanoid retargeting has largely been studied on legged platforms, while comparatively few wheeled-humanoid systems support coupled locomotion and manipulation from general human motion. Building on GMR's configurable general-motion retargeting and BeyondMimic's physically simulated R1 Pro learning framework, we present a reproducible pipeline that converts multi-dataset SMPLX motion into executable loco-manipulation behavior for the Galaxea R1 Pro wheeled humanoid. The robot has a planar three-wheel base, a serial torso, and two arms but no leg joints, so human lower-body motion must be redistributed across base motion and torso posture without sacrificing manipulation-relevant arm geometry. Our pipeline combines canonical body-shape preprocessing, planar-base normalization, morphology-aware differential inverse kinematics, shoulder-rooted hierarchical arm retargeting, and continuous torso substitution for bending and squatting. A reference-twist-driven planning layer then decodes planar base motion into continuous three-wheel steering and rolling commands subject to hysteresis, kinematic continuity, acceleration, and actuator-rate limits. Finally, a 21-dimensional BaseDecode policy is trained in Isaac Lab with directional joint-limit scaling, focused upper-body tracking, and a staged wheel-contact reward. The resulting system provides a complete bridge from human motion data to physically trackable wheeled-humanoid loco-manipulation rather than a visualization-only retargeter; quantitative policy comparisons remain scheduled for a later revision.

</details>


### [246] [GeoTrussRover: Morphological Computation with Contact-Semantic Control Primitives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11361)
*Muyuan Ma,Yi Zhang,Yang Yang,Xuanyan Zheng,Ruiqi Hu,Boxuan Ke,Zhenyu Chen,Yicong Lin,Xin Hao Yang,Daliang Xiao,Zhinan Hou,Wanhao Niu,Yuan Sun,Yan Yang,Yue Xie*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A multi-component reconfigurable robot integrates a variable-geometry truss with a wheeled base, introducing contact-semantic planning with four primitives, physics-constrained projection, and partial re-planning to enable efficient traversal across varied step heights. Demonstrates substantial efficiency gains and a hardware demonstration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable agile locomotion over complex step obstacles using a reconfigurable morphology that distributes deformation through a load-bearing truss while maintaining a mobile base, addressing the high-dimensional coordination problem.

Method: Extract one source traversal and derive four contact-semantic primitives describing coordination among 21 members; apply physics-constrained projection to adapt to unseen step heights while preserving the contact topology; when all phases feasible, reuse adaptation without full recomputation; if a phase violates new constraints, recompute only that phase; use a full-space QP to track the adapted motion and correct member and wheel errors; evaluate efficiency by comparing partial recomputation against full recomputation, and analyze transfer across step heights; validate with a hardware electric prototype traversing multiple wheel radii.

Result: Achieves a 63.7% reduction in objective-function evaluations during transfer (0.10 m to 0.075 m) compared with full recomputation; covers step heights from 0.10 to 0.46 m (1.08 to 4.97 wheel radii), with the upper bound near feasibility limits; hardware prototype traverses 2.11 wheel radii.

Conclusion: The approach stores task coordination in a compact, hyper-redundant morphology and reuses it across locomotion, enabling efficient, physics-consistent adaptation to varied step heights while maintaining robust tracking via a full-space QP.

Abstract: Reconfigurable robots can change their contact geometry when a fixed body cannot negotiate an obstacle. A variable-geometry truss (VGT) distributes this shape change through a load-bearing structure, but coupling it to a mobile base creates a high-dimensional coordination problem. GeoTrussRover combines an electrically actuated VGT, a wheeled base, and contact-semantic morphology planning and control. We solve one source traversal and extract four contact-semantic primitives that describe coordination among 21 members. Physics-constrained projection adapts them to unseen step heights with the same contact topology. When every phase remains feasible, adaptation does not recompute the complete motion. If one phase violates the new physical constraints, only that phase is recomputed. A full-space QP then tracks the adapted motion and corrects member and wheel errors. For transfer from 0.10m to 0.075m, the method reduces objective-function evaluations by 63.7% relative to full recomputation. Contact-phase feasibility analysis covers step heights from 0.10 to 0.46m, or 1.08 to 4.97 wheel radii, with the upper value near the theoretical feasible boundary. The electric prototype traverses 2.11 wheel radii. The resulting low-dimensional representation stores task coordination in a hyper-redundant, load-bearing morphology and reuses it during locomotion.

</details>


### [247] [SwarmNxt: Open-source Software-Hardware Platform for Fast and Agile Aerial Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11382)
*Charbel Toumieh,Niel Mistry,Benjamin Jarvis,Simon Jeger,Peize Liu,Shaojie Shen,Dario Floreano*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Open-source ROS 2-based multi-robot swarm platform (SwarmNxt) on OmniNxt hardware that provides end-to-end tooling for swarm robotics, with onboard perception, planning, and depth estimation, validated in indoor multi-drone experiments (6 drones for decentralized planning with high-speed collision avoidance; 4 drones for collective flight with onboard depth estimation).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust autonomous swarm navigation is hampered by closed-source drone platforms, limited onboard compute for agile vision-based coordination, and high software engineering costs for multi-robot systems.
