An open, end-to-end research infrastructure is needed to enable rapid development and experimentation of aerial swarm systems.

Method: SwarmNxt integrates state-of-the-art control, planning, and depth estimation into a ROS 2-based multi-agent system on the open OmniNxt hardware. It includes hardware assembly instructions, automated deployment and swarm-wide updates, and a unified software stack for perception, planning, and control. The authors validate the platform with two indoor experiments: (i) a six-drone swarm performing decentralized planning with high-speed inter-drone collision avoidance, and (ii) a four-drone swarm executing collective flight with onboard depth estimation in obstacle-rich environments, with global pose provided by external motion capture and all perception, planning, and control running onboard.

Result: The platform demonstrates (a) decentralized planning with high-speed inter-drone collision avoidance among six drones, and (b) collective flight with onboard depth estimation among four drones in obstacle-filled environments. It offers a complete end-to-end solution, from hardware assembly and deployment tooling to a ROS 2 multi-agent framework, enabling onboard perception, planning, and control.

Conclusion: SwarmNxt appears to be a comprehensive, open-source platform that lowers barriers to swarm robotics research by providing hardware-aligned tooling, onboard autonomous capabilities, and a ROS 2-based infrastructure for physical swarm experimentation. The indoor validations demonstrate practical viability, though outdoor or GPS-denied deployment and larger-scale tests remain as future steps, along with evaluating scalability and real-world robustness.

Abstract: Aerial robot swarms have the potential to transform time-critical safety, security, and search-and-rescue operations. By coordinating multiple robots, they can rapidly survey disaster sites, map collapsed or GPS-denied environments, and search cluttered areas faster than a single robot, reducing response times and minimizing risks to first responders. Realizing this potential, however, requires robust autonomous swarm navigation, which remains an active research challenge. Progress is further constrained by existing platforms, as commercial drones are often closed-source or lack the onboard computational resources needed for agile, vision-based collective flight. Moreover, developing, deploying, and maintaining software across multiple aerial robots requires significant engineering effort. To address these challenges, we present SwarmNxt, an open-source software platform built on the open-source OmniNxt drone hardware. SwarmNxt provides an end-to-end toolkit, including detailed hardware assembly instructions with a video tutorial, automation tools for parallel software deployment and swarm-wide updates, and a ROS 2-based framework for autonomous navigation. The platform integrates state-of-the-art control, planning, and depth estimation into a single ROS 2 multi-agent system, providing an open research infrastructure for physical swarm experimentation. We validate SwarmNxt through two real-world experiments: a six-drone swarm performing decentralized planning with high-speed inter-drone collision avoidance, and a four-drone swarm executing collective flight with onboard depth estimation in an obstacle-filled environment. Both experiments were run indoors with global position from external motion capture; perception, planning, and control run onboard.

</details>


### [248] [Safety-aware Skill Adaptation for Reinforcement Learning in Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11433)
*A K M Nadimul Haque,Sheila Sutjipto,Marc G. Carmichael,Teresa Vidal-Calleja*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Dist-GPRL is a GP-based, distance-aware RL framework for safe, local, structured skill adaptation in cluttered/dynamic environments, enabling stable learning and sim-to-real transfer by updating local trajectory windows with safety priors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning for robot skills often assumes fixed observations or controlled exploration, leading to unsafe or unstable learning in cluttered/dynamic settings; need methods that enable safe, local trajectory adaptation.

Method: Use Gaussian Process-based parameterisation to update overlapping local windows of sparse trajectory via-points; correlate policy outputs via GP covariance for temporal coherence; incorporate safety via a Hausdorff-based safe subspace prior (HAP) and distance-field rewards; include trajectory-kinematics similarity regulariser; train in simulation and transfer to real robot.

Result: Approach yields higher task success, lower collision frequency, and more stable learning than baselines; preserves demonstrated kinematic characteristics; demonstrates sim-to-real transfer.

Conclusion: Dist-GPRL provides a principled, distance-aware, safety-guided framework for structured skill adaptation, addressing exploration instability and credit assignment by local, coherent trajectory updates with safety priors, validated on dynamic tasks.

Abstract: Skill adaptation frameworks based on reinforcement learning often require restrictive assumptions to maintain stability, such as fixed observations or tightly controlled exploration schedules. In cluttered and dynamic environments, however, unrestricted exploration can lead to unsafe behaviour and unstable learning, particularly when task-relevant observations lie near obstacles or involve moving objects. In this work, we present Dist-GPRL, a distance-aware and safety-guided reinforcement learning framework for structured robot skill adaptation. Building upon Gaussian Process (GP)-based skill parameterisation, our framework sequentially adapts overlapping local windows of sparse trajectory via-points rather than modifying the complete skill at every policy step. Raw policy outputs are correlated through the GP covariance structure, producing temporally coherent trajectory updates while reducing the action-space and credit-assignment difficulties associated with global trajectory adaptation. Safety is incorporated through two complementary forms of guidance. A safe-subspace prior derived from the Hausdorff Approximation Planner (HAP) biases policy exploration toward feasible regions, while dynamically updated distance field clearance and gradient rewards provide local obstacle awareness. A trajectory-kinematics similarity regulariser further preserves the demonstrated velocity and acceleration characteristics during adaptation. We evaluate the framework on two dynamic object-manipulation tasks in simulation and transfer the learned policy to real-world robot execution. Experimental results demonstrate higher task success, lower collision frequency, and more stable learning than the baselines, while preserving the kinematic characteristics of the demonstrated skill.

</details>


### [249] [FARM: Reading Failure Signals from the Internal Predictive States of a Frozen Robotic World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11445)
*Haoran Pei,Mingrui Luo,Senbao Wang,Haoran Lv,Jie Guo,Sheng Zhong,Ruixi Ci*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A small supervised readout on frozen predictive states enables reliable, low-overhead failure monitoring for robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether internal predictive states of a frozen pretrained robotic world model contain directly decodable failure information to enable online monitoring without updating the backbone.

Method: Train a 33,985-parameter supervised readout over frozen VLA-JEPA predictive states (FARM). Evaluate with five-fold out-of-fold across seven source tasks; compare against 15 baselines on a 10-task benchmark; test transfer on four real-robot populations (PIPER X, SO-101, Franka); report latency.

Result: Pooled AUROC/AUPRC: 85.68 / 88.59 across tasks. FARM yields the best Seen performance among 15 baselines on the 10-task benchmark. Demonstrates fixed-readout transfer and readout-only adaptation in deployment without updating the backbone. Adds 0.2256 ms mean CUDA latency once the frozen state is available.

Conclusion: Frozen predictive world-model states can serve as reusable features for causal, transferable, and low-overhead execution monitoring.

Abstract: Reliable robot deployment requires online failure monitoring, yet existing monitors mainly derive risk from proxy signals or train dedicated monitoring components. We ask whether the internal predictive states of a frozen pretrained robotic world model already contain directly decodable failure information. Failure-Aware Readout from World Models (FARM) trains only a 33,985-parameter supervised readout over frozen VLA-JEPA predictive states, producing step-wise failure scores and causal trajectory risk. Five-fold out-of-fold evaluation across seven source tasks reaches 85.68/88.59 pooled AUROC/AUPRC, and FARM gives the best Seen performance among 15 matched baselines on the 10-task benchmark. Across four real-robot populations on PIPER X, SO-101, and Franka, fixed-readout transfer and readout-only adaptation test deployment shifts without updating the predictive backbone. FARM also discriminates failures from partial causal histories and adds 0.2256 ms mean CUDA latency once the frozen state is available. These results support frozen predictive world-model states as reusable features for causal, transferable, and low-overhead execution monitoring.

</details>


### [250] [3D Euler-Angle Orientation Control for Two-Ray Fading Mitigation in Maritime Air-to-Sea Communications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11476)
*Mohammed Bajja,Abdoul Karim A. H. Saliah,Hajar El Hammouti,Daniel Bonilla Licea,Giuseppe Silano*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Attitude-controlled two-ray interference mitigation for maritime A2S links using a tilting multirotor; affine constraint in Euler angles; soft-min reference for NMPC; throughput gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sea-surface reflections create deep fades in maritime air-to-sea links. Attitude has been neglected as a control variable; exploiting full 3D attitude to modulate phase-centre and two-ray interference offers a physical-layer control channel to improve link performance.

Method: Model the full 3D attitude of a tilting multirotor; derive an affine constraint on Euler angles under small-angle and far-field assumptions to ensure constructive interference; obtain closed-form minimum-norm attitude candidates; use a differentiable soft-minimum rule to produce a smooth reference; track this reference with a constrained nonlinear model predictive controller (NMPC) on a fully-actuated tilting multirotor; enforce actuator limits.

Result: Throughput increases of 11.4% over a pitch-only baseline and 22.2% over a zero-orientation baseline; trajectory tracking is preserved; actuator limits are respected.

Conclusion: Treating attitude as a physical-layer control variable for maritime A2S links is viable and can be integrated with NMPC; the framework yields a tractable design via affine constraints and closed-form attitude candidates, enabling practical performance gains.

Abstract: Maritime Air-to-Sea links are dominated by a line-of-sight ray and a sea-surface reflected ray whose destructive combination produces deep fades. Existing mitigation strategies optimize Unmanned Aerial Vehicle position or trajectory but leave attitude unexploited. This paper treats the full three dimensional attitude as a physical-layer control variable that shapes the two-ray interference through antenna phase-center displacement. Under small-angle and far-field assumptions, the constructive-interference condition reduces to an affine constraint in the Euler angles and admits a closed-form family of minimum-norm attitude candidates. A differentiable soft-minimum rule yields a smooth reference tracked by a constrained Nonlinear Model Predictive Control controller on a fully-actuated tilting multirotor. The proposed scheme increases cumulative throughput by 11.4% over a pitch-only benchmark and 22.2% over a zero-orientation baseline, while preserving trajectory tracking and respecting actuator limits.

</details>


### [251] [CARLAverse: A Highly Modular, Distributed, and Multimodal Framework for Human-in-the-Loop Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11478)
*Patrick Rebling,Philipp Nenninger,Reiner Kriesten*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CARLAverse is a distributed, multimodal HITL simulation platform that decouples ego dynamics from NPC physics to deliver low-latency, high-fidelity haptic feedback in mixed-traffic scenarios with VRUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To capture authentic human social negotiations with VRUs in autonomous-driving testing; existing multi-agent platforms struggle with latency and synchronization for high-frequency haptic feedback.

Method: A distributed physics architecture: latency-critical ego dynamics and high-frequency force feedback run on client nodes, while a central CARLA server handles NPC physics and global traffic. It extends modular hardware abstraction to integrate driving (DrivoCARLA), cycling (CycloCARLA), and pedestrian (WalkoCARLA) simulators in a shared environment.

Result: Enables scalable, cross-institutional HITL experiments without sacrificing physical immersion by alleviating network bottlenecks and preserving high-frequency feedback.

Conclusion: CARLAverse advances HITL testing for autonomous driving in VRU-rich environments, offering an open-source solution with code and docs at the provided repository.

Abstract: The development of autonomous driving demands comprehensive testing in mixed-traffic scenarios involving vulnerable road users (VRUs), where purely artificial agents often fail to capture authentic human social negotiations. While human-in-the-loop (HITL) simulators enable safe investigation of these interactions, existing multi-agent platforms struggle with the network latency and synchronization constraints required for high-fidelity haptic feedback. To resolve this, we present CARLAverse, an open-source, multimodal simulation ecosystem. Extending modular hardware abstraction, CARLAverse integrates driving (DrivoCARLA), cycling (CycloCARLA), and pedestrian (WalkoCARLA) simulators into a shared virtual environment. Its core methodological contribution is a distributed physics architecture: latency-critical ego dynamics and high-frequency force feedback are computed locally on client nodes, while a central CARLA server orchestrates non-player character (NPC) physics and global traffic. By decoupling haptic control loops from network bottlenecks, CARLAverse enables scalable, cross-institutional HITL experiments without compromising physical immersion. Code and documentation: https://git.ieem-ka.de/simulator-environments/carlaverse

</details>


### [252] [Using Automated Vehicles Operational Data to Confirm Safety and Anticipate Threats](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11549)
*Riccardo Donà,Espedito Rusciano,Germana Trentadue,Anastasios Tsakalidis,Maria Cristina Galassi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EU regulation for Automated Driving Systems introduces in-service monitoring and reporting (ISMR) to collect real-world data, build a scenario catalogue, and enable data sharing among OEMs and safety authorities, aiming to supplement traditional homologation with ongoing safety assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the safety assurance gap for ADS by leveraging real-world data and UNECE best practices to validate safety targets and enable continual improvement across stakeholders.

Method: Adopt an ISMR framework inspired by UNECE, collect operational data from ADS on public roads, catalog safety-relevant driving scenarios, share insights among OEMs and regulators, and use data to confirm homologation safety targets and inform regulatory oversight.

Result: Describes a regulatory approach and expected benefits rather than empirical results; anticipates improved evidence base for safety certification, enhanced understanding of scenario-related risks, and iterative learning across industry and authorities.

Conclusion: ISMR is presented as a necessary tool to address ADS safety assessment challenges, complementing traditional homologation by providing ongoing data-driven insights rather than replacing initial approvals.

Abstract: European Union (EU) policymakers adopted revolutionary data collection provisions for Automated Driving Systems (ADS) in the recently approved regulation that allows driverless vehicles to be operated on public roads. The framework is inspired by best practices developed at the United Nations Economic Commission for Europe(UNECE) level: the In-Service Monitoring and Reporting (ISMR); and by similar operational data collection regulatory approaches in nuclear energy production and transportation fields. The collection of real-world data will enable the competent safety authorities to gather the information needed to confirm the homologation safety target. Safety-relevant driving scenarios discovered during the real-world operation of a given ADS can also be stored in a scenario catalogue to investigate how other ADS types might have addressed such a traffic conflict. Moreover, lessons learnt deriving from the data collected can be shared among original equipment manufacturers (OEMs) and safety authorities. Ultimately, the ISMR is recognised as a necessary tool to properly tackle the challenges associated with ADS safety assessment given the number of unknowns that might remain undisclosed by leveraging the traditional homologation validation scheme only.

</details>


### [253] [CAP: Continuously Adaptive Perception-Blind Humanoid Locomotion via Learned Denoising](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11553)
*Hongjin Chen,Zijun Xu,Shihao Ma,Yi Zhao,Xilai Liu,Ke Ma,Wei Zhang,Chunyang Xie,Pengfei Li,Jieru Zhao,Wenchao Ding*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CAP is a single-stage humanoid locomotion policy that denoises depth through a world-model encoder and fuses depth-free body-state via a proprioceptive variational encoder, trained with a depth-noise curriculum and feature dropout to achieve graceful degradation under degraded perception, demonstrated in simulation and on the Unitree G1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing perceptive policies often assume clean depth observations and rely on switching between sub-policies, leaving information recoverable in partially corrupted depth untapped; there is a need for a unified approach that robustly leverages belated or corrupted perceptual input.

Method: A perceptive world-model encoder acts as a learned denoiser to reconstruct clean depth from corrupted input, paired with a co-active proprioceptive variational encoder that provides depth-free state information. Training uses a depth-noise curriculum on world-model input and world-model feature dropout on the policy-facing latent, forming a coupled training recipe that exposes the policy to failures across the perception-quality spectrum. The result is a single-stage policy without separate perceptual/sub-policy switching.

Result: In simulation, CAP matches or improves perceptive baselines when depth is informative and degrades more smoothly than a binary-switching baseline as perception worsens. On the Unitree G1, controlled trials and indoor-outdoor deployments demonstrate perception-robust locomotion under intermittent occlusion, real-sensor corruption, and outdoor depth artifacts.

Conclusion: CAP provides a unified, perception-robust locomotion policy that gracefully handles degraded depth inputs, outperforming switching baselines in robustness and functioning effectively in real hardware.

Abstract: Humanoid locomotion across complex terrain demands forward-looking exteroception to anticipate obstacles, yet this signal is unreliable in real-world deployment, failing partially and intermittently. Existing perceptive policies often assume that depth observations remain clean and in-distribution, while recent attempts to unify perceptive and blind control typically route or switch between separate sub-policies, leaving recoverable information in partially corrupted depth unexploited. We instead propose CAP, a single-stage humanoid locomotion policy that recovers this signal with a perceptive world-model encoder trained as a learned denoiser to reconstruct clean depth from a corrupted input, together with a co-active proprioceptive variational encoder that supplies depth-free body-state information. A coupled training recipe pairs a depth-noise curriculum on the world-model input with world-model feature dropout on the policy-facing latent, exposing the policy to failures across the entire perception-quality spectrum. In simulation, CAP matches or improves upon perceptive baselines when depth remains informative, and degrades more smoothly than a binary-switching baseline as perception worsens. On the Unitree G1, controlled trials and indoor-outdoor deployments demonstrate perception-robust locomotion under intermittent occlusion, real-sensor corruption, and outdoor depth artifacts.

</details>


### [254] [Memory as Plans: World-Action Modeling with Memory-Grounded Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11561)
*Sizhe Zhao,Haozhe Xie,Weiyu Zhao,Chenchu Zhang,Huan Wang,Chenyang Wang,Qinglin Liu,Shengping Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MaP-WAM proposes a Memory-as-Plans framework that separates memory-grounded planning from plan-conditioned execution, using long-horizon episodic context converted into compact plans, enabling fixed executor latent and near-SOTA results with constant latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world manipulation tasks are non-Markovian and require long-term memory. Existing memory mechanisms either compress history via language or enlarge visual windows, causing loss of detail or inefficiency. A memory-as-plans approach seeks efficient, structured use of episodic memory for planning.

Method: Memory is stored as completed segment records with language instructions and sparse visual context. Episodic memory is transformed into compact plans comprising a next-segment language plan and visual guidance. A World-Action-Progress (WAP) model executes plans over unknown durations by predicting action chunks and execution progress, with plan-observation alignment for adaptive segment transitions. The executor maintains a fixed context length, using structured attention and key-value caching in planning and execution.

Result: MaP-WAM achieves state-of-the-art performance on RMBench with 83.3% success and 78.0% on real-robot tasks, while maintaining approximately constant executor inference latency as task history grows.

Conclusion: By decoupling memory and execution and encoding episodic memory as plans, MaP-WAM enables long-horizon action reasoning with efficient, online adaptation, improving performance without increasing runtime cost as history grows.

Abstract: Mainstream robotic policies often adopt a Markovian formulation, but many complex real-world manipulation tasks are inherently non-Markovian, requiring long-horizon memory beyond the current observation. Existing memory mechanisms often rely on language summaries, growing visual windows, or their combinations, and may therefore lose fine-grained visual evidence or face a trade-off between history coverage and execution efficiency. We introduce MaP-WAM, a Memory-as-Plans framework that decomposes memory-dependent world-action modeling into memory-grounded planning and plan-conditioned execution, and uses long-term multimodal episodic context as planning-time evidence rather than repeatedly conditioning the executor on the full history. MaP-WAM represents memory as completed segment records containing language instructions and sparse visual context, and converts this episodic memory into compact plans comprising the next segment-level language plan and corresponding visual guidance. A World-Action-Progress (WAP) model executes each plan over an unknown duration by jointly predicting action chunks and corresponding execution progress at inference time, calibrating predicted progress through plan-observation alignment for adaptive segment transitions and closed-loop context updates. MaP-WAM keeps the executor context length fixed, while structured attention further enables key-value caching in both planning and execution. MaP-WAM achieves state-of-the-art performance on RMBench with an 83.3% success rate and attains 78.0% success on real-robot tasks, while maintaining approximately constant executor inference latency as task history grows.

</details>


### [255] [Quasi-static analysis of passive stability in a novel underactuated multi-finger hand](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11579)
*Léonie Plancoulaine,Sylvain Guégan,Franck Plestan,Damien Chablat*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A quasi-static model for passive stability in underactuated multi-finger hands is developed, featuring a novel three-finger hand with a differential spring-loaded slider, and it analyzes how such a mechanism and object size affect stable equilibrium for cylindrical and spherical grasps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underactuated hands reduce actuation complexity but predicting stable grasp poses remains difficult; a formal analytical tool is needed to quantify passive stability across object shapes and sizes.

Method: Develop a quasi-static analytical framework for passive stability and introduce a three-finger hand architecture incorporating a differential spring-loaded slider mechanism; study how the differential mechanism shapes grasp behavior and analyze the influence of object size on equilibrium configurations for cylindrical and spherical objects.

Result: The framework yields analytical insights into how the differential mechanism affects grasp stability and characterizes size-dependent stable equilibrium configurations for cylindrical and spherical grasps, providing design-and-control guidance.

Conclusion: The quasi-static approach coupled with the differential mechanism offers a principled basis to predict and design for passive stability in underactuated three-finger hands across varying object sizes and canonical shapes.

Abstract: Underactuated robotic hands achieve adaptive and robust grasping with a reduced number of actuators, but predicting the stable equilibrium pose of the grasped object remains a significant challenge. This paper introduces a quasi-static analytical approach to assess passive stability in underactuated multi-finger hands. A novel three-finger hand architecture integrating a differential spring-loaded slider mechanism is introduced, enabling versatile and adaptive grasping. The study focuses on how the differential mechanism influences the overall grasp behavior and analyzes the effect of object size on the stable equilibrium configurations for two canonical grasp types: cylindrical and spherical.

</details>


### [256] [Contact-Aware Incremental Model Predictive Control for an Underactuated Aerial Manipulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11661)
*Darwin Liu,Tamas Keviczky,Sihao Sun*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Robust contact-aware control for aerial writing on an underactuated quadrotor with a simple arm, fusing NMPC for end-effector pose and normal-force tracking with whole-body INDI for disturbance rejection; validated in simulation and real experiments, achieving five-DoF pose and contact-force tracking without a fully actuated platform or complex arm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable precise, robust aerial manipulation during contact tasks (aerial writing) on underactuated platforms. Address frictional and aerodynamic disturbances and ensure consistent performance across controller tunings without reliance on fully actuated hardware or specialized sensing.

Method: A two-tier control framework: (1) nonlinear model predictive control (NMPC) for accurate end-effector position and normal-force tracking at small penetration depths with consistent tuning; (2) whole-body incremental nonlinear dynamic inversion (INDI) for robustness to frictional and aerodynamic disturbances during contact. Implemented on a quadrotor-based aerial manipulator with a rigid, single-link, 1-DoF arm; validated in simulation and real-world experiments that include vertical and inclined surfaces, multiple reference forces, varying friction, and wind disturbances.

Result: Robust simultaneous five-DoF end-effector pose and contact-force tracking achieved on a standard underactuated quadrotor with a simple arm. Validation across surface orientations, friction conditions, and wind, without requiring a fully actuated platform, a complex arm, or dedicated force/torque sensing.

Conclusion: The proposed framework demonstrates robust, contact-aware aerial manipulation on underactuated platforms using a simple arm, delivering consistent performance across tunings and practical viability for aerial writing without specialized actuation or sensing.

Abstract: We present a robust contact-aware control framework for aerial writing on an underactuated platform. The framework combines nonlinear model predictive control (NMPC) for accurate end-effector position and normal-force tracking at small reference penetration depths, with consistent performance across controller tunings, with whole-body incremental nonlinear dynamic inversion (INDI) for robustness to frictional and aerodynamic disturbances during contact. The proposed controllers are validated on a quadrotor-based aerial manipulator with a rigid, single-link, one-degree-of-freedom (DoF) arm in simulation and real-world experiments. The aerial writing experiments span vertical and inclined surfaces, multiple reference forces, different friction conditions, and wind disturbances. The results demonstrate that robust simultaneous five-DoF end-effector pose and contact-force tracking is achievable on a standard underactuated quadrotor with a simple, rigid, single-link arm, without requiring a fully actuated platform, a complex arm, or dedicated force/torque sensing.

</details>


### [257] [ActSafeGuard: Differentiable and Training-Aligned Constraint Enforcement for Flow-Matching Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11697)
*Jianming Ma,Rongjun Jin,Xiaxi Si,Yang Zhang,Yiheng Li,Yue Gao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ActSafeGuard is a differentiable safety layer for flow-matching policies that enforces hard action feasibility during training, achieving 100% per-step safety while maintaining or enhancing task success across foundation backbones (π_0.5, Fast-WAM).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To close the gap between training-time safety and execution-time safety in Vision-Language-Action and World-Action models, avoiding unsafe or infeasible actions that existing methods either miss guarantees or rely on inference-time corrections.

Method: Introduce an analytical ray-scaling operator forming a differentiable safeguard layer integrated into the policy's learning, enforcing boundary-aware gradients that align policy optimization with constraint satisfaction; applicable to flow-matching-based policies.

Result: Empirical evaluation on multiple standard backbones and tasks shows 100% step safety rate with preserved or improved task success rates.

Conclusion: ActSafeGuard offers a scalable, minimally invasive, training-aligned safety solution for safe embodied AI deployment, potentially reducing risk of unsafe actions in real-world robotic manipulation.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) and World-Action Models (WAMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in general-purpose robotic manipulation, yet their generated actions may violate hard physical constraints and therefore be unsafe or infeasible for deployment. Existing safety approaches either optimize statistical safety objectives without deterministic per-step guarantees or correct unsafe actions only during inference, creating a mismatch between policy training and execution. We introduce ActSafeGuard, a differentiable and training-aligned safeguard layer for flow-matching based policies. ActSafeGuard integrates hard action feasibility into policy learning, not merely treating safety as an inference-time external component. Through an analytical ray-scaling operator design, ActSafeGuard enables boundary-aware gradients to guide the model to naturally learn constrained manifolds. Extensive experiments on multiple standard foundation backbones ($π_{0.5}$ and Fast-WAM) across various tasks demonstrate that ActSafeGuard consistently achieves a $100\%$ step safety rate while fully preserving or even boosting task success rates, providing a scalable and minimally invasive solution for safe embodied AI deployment.

</details>


### [258] [Aerodynamic Prior-Free Coordinated Trajectory Generation and Tracking Control for a Tail-Sitter UAV](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11698)
*Erchao Rong,Zihao Liu,Junning Liang,Jianguo Wang,Xiao Jie,Haoran Fu,Ziliang Chen,Ximin Lyu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A two-model coordinated trajectory generation and tracking framework for tail-sitter UAVs that avoids airframe-specific aerodynamic priors, using a phi-theory-based differential flatness mapping for planning and a local simplified model for MPC-based tracking; validated in simulation and real flights with mild winds; claims first real-world full-envelope trajectory tracking without aerodynamic identification; open-source code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve accurate, robust trajectory generation and tracking for tail-sitter UAVs across the full flight envelope without requiring airframe-specific aerodynamic identification, addressing highly nonlinear aerodynamics and model uncertainty.

Method: Phase-specific aerodynamic modeling: employ the phi-theory model under coordinated flight to derive an analytic differential flatness mapping for planning; use a simplified, locally accurate model for predictive control to enable real-time aerodynamic parameter estimation; integrate these into a coordinated trajectory generation and tracking framework.

Result: Extensive simulation and challenging real-world flight tests under mild wind conditions show high-precision tracking and adaptability across tested aerodynamic conditions.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that phase-specific aerodynamic modeling enables accurate trajectory tracking across the full envelope without aerodynamic identification campaigns; provides open-source code for replication and further development.

Abstract: This paper presents a coordinated trajectory generation and tracking control framework for a tail-sitter unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV), which does not require aerodynamic priors identified for a specific airframe while addressing the challenge of flight control under highly nonlinear aerodynamics across the full flight envelope. The core innovation lies in employing phase-specific aerodynamic modeling strategies for planning and tracking, tailored to their distinct functional characteristics, without requiring airframe-specific aerodynamic priors. Specifically, the phi-theory model under coordinated flight is employed to derive an analytic differential flatness mapping, and a simplified but locally accurate model is established for predictive control to enable real-time aerodynamic parameter estimation. The proposed framework is evaluated extensively through both simulation and challenging real-world flight tests under mild wind conditions, showing high-precision tracking and adaptability across the tested aerodynamic conditions. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first real-world demonstration of accurate trajectory tracking over tested flight regimes spanning the full envelope of a tail-sitter UAV without relying on aerodynamic identification campaigns. The source code of our framework is available at: https://github.com/SYSU-HILAB/AP-PnC.

</details>


### [259] [Reflex-Informed Neuromuscular Reinforcement Learning for Muscle-Driven Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11733)
*Jian Zhou,Xingyu Zhang,Rui Ma,Yu Cao,Shane Xie,Zhi-qiang Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A reflex-informed neuromuscular reinforcement learning framework combines a fixed phase-dependent reflex controller with an RL policy that outputs residual parameters to modulate hip, knee, and ankle reflex gains/thresholds, yielding physiologically plausible, robust muscle-driven locomotion with improved kinematic accuracy, symmetry, and stride consistency under disturbances without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The main challenge is achieving locomotion that is both physiologically plausible and adaptable to musculoskeletal changes and external disturbances; traditional fixed-reflex controllers lack adaptive modulation, limiting robustness and generalization.

Method: A fixed phase-dependent reflex controller provides the neuromuscular backbone. A reinforcement learning policy learns four residual parameters that adjust reflex gains and thresholds related to hip swing, knee support, and ankle propulsion based on the current state, enabling adaptive coordination while preserving physiological structure.

Result: The framework produces locomotion that is physiologically plausible with better kinematic accuracy and dynamic consistency, and improved bilateral symmetry and stride-to-stride consistency under nominal walking. The learned policy demonstrates robustness to muscle weakness and external perturbations without retraining.

Conclusion: Integrating a fixed reflex controller with a learnable residual modulation via reinforcement learning yields physiologically grounded, adaptable muscle-driven locomotion that remains robust to capacity changes and disturbances without retraining, highlighting the value of neurophysiologically informed RL in neuromuscular control.

Abstract: Muscle-driven locomotion provides a physically grounded approach to generating realistic human movement. However, achieving both physiological plausibility and adaptability to changes in musculoskeletal capacity and external disturbances remains a fundamental challenge. To address this limitation, we propose a Reflex-Informed Neuromuscular Reinforcement Learning framework for muscle-driven locomotion. Within this framework, a fixed phase-dependent reflex controller serves as the underlying neuromuscular control mechanism, while the reinforcement learning policy produces four biomechanically meaningful residual parameters to modulate key reflex gains and thresholds associated with hip swing, knee support, and ankle propulsion according to the current state. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed framework generates physiologically plausible locomotion with improved kinematic accuracy and dynamic consistency, as well as better bilateral symmetry and stride-to-stride consistency under nominal walking conditions. The learned policy remains robust under muscle weakness and external perturbations without retraining.

</details>


### [260] [SEED-UMI: Sharing the Exoskeleton between human and robot for onE-to-one Dexterous demonstration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11753)
*Tengbo Yu,Jiahao Wu,Daohan Li,Bingxu Chen,Hao Liu,Xiaojian Ma,Hangxin Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SEED-UMI enables joint exoskeleton-worn demonstrations for dexterous imitation learning by making encoders and wrist cameras shared measurements, enabling paired cross-embodiment supervision and training directly on exoskeleton-centric wrist images; achieves higher data efficiency and solid success across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dexterous hand imitation learning is bottlenecked by collecting contact-rich demonstrations and faithful transfer across embodiment; prior exoskeleton retargeting is open-loop and degrades under interaction.

Method: Both human and robot wear the same exoskeleton so joint encoders are physically shared; wrist cameras mounted on the exoskeleton observe the outer mechanism during data collection and policy execution; reduces retargeting to paired cross-embodiment supervision and enables training on raw exoskeleton-centric wrist images without segmentation or inpainting.

Result: Threefold improvement in data collection efficiency (3.0x) over exoskeleton-based teleoperation and a 70% average rollout success rate across five contact-rich tasks.

Conclusion: Joint embodiment sharing enables direct, robust learning from contact-rich demonstrations by aligning sensing and perception across human and robot, eliminating mismatch due to retargeting and simplifying perception for policy training.

Abstract: Imitation learning for dexterous hands is bottlenecked by the difficulty of collecting contact-rich demonstrations that transfer faithfully to the robot. Prior wearable-exoskeleton systems record only on the human side and retarget via open-loop mappings calibrated in free space, which degrade under contact. We present SEED-UMI, a framework in which both the human and the robot wear the same exoskeleton: joint encoders become a physically shared measurement, and wrist cameras mounted to the exoskeleton observe the same outer mechanism during both human data collection and robot policy rollouts. This turns retargeting into paired cross-embodiment supervision and lets policies train directly on raw exoskeleton-centric wrist images, without segmentation or inpainting. On five contact-rich tasks, SEED-UMI achieves 3.0x greater data collection efficiency than exoskeleton-based teleoperation and a 70.0% average rollout success rate.

</details>


### [261] [Visual-SLAM for the detection of hidden tomatoes in greenhouses by Hierarchical Localization and GLOMAPfor robotized harvesting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11766)
*Fernando Cañadas-Aránega,José C. Moreno,José L. Blanco-Claraco,Francisco Rodríguez*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Monocular Visual-SLAM for greenhouse tomato mapping using GLOMAP/FM with hierarchical localization; low-cost, accurate 3D crop model validated against ground truth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce equipment cost for crop monitoring by replacing expensive LiDAR/stereo sensors with a monocular camera SLAM approach, enabling 3D crop mapping and growth analysis in greenhouses.

Method: Monocular Visual-SLAM using the GLOMAP mapper (Structure-from-Motion) integrated with the Hierarchical Localization toolbox, implemented as a ROS 2 Humble node to capture images on a robot; offline processing to build a 3D map. A coarse-to-fine localization strategy combines global retrieval with local feature fusion in candidate regions.

Result: Correctly identified tomato clusters, including occluded/inaccessible parts; produced a 3D crop model; validated against manual ground-truth measurements of fruit size, centroid position, and orientation, confirming geometric accuracy of the low-cost monocular pipeline.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a cost-effective monocular SLAM pipeline for greenhouse crop mapping, validated against ground truth and serving as a foundation for future growth-pattern analysis and optimized agricultural management.

Abstract: Advanced crop monitoring inside greenhouses is becoming one of the primary objectives of research centers. High-performance sensors, such as LiDAR or stereo cameras, have traditionally been employed for this purpose, though these often have a high cost. This work proposes a Visual-SLAM system using a monocular camera, which is significantly more cost-effective and specifically tailored for agricultural applications, such as mapping tomato crops in a greenhouse. Tests were carried out on a real tomato bunch, located in the Agroconnect experimental greenhouse. A ROS 2 Humble node was developed to run on the robot in order to capture images of these crops, which were then stored for offline processing. To generate a 3D mapped model for the crop in the greenhouse, the GLOMAP mapper, based on Structure-From-Motion, was integrated with the Hierarchical Localization toolbox. This initial mapping is a foundation for future, more advanced algorithms to analyze growth patterns, and optimize agricultural management. The system leverages a hierarchical localization paradigm based on a coarse-to-fine strategy: it first performs global retrieval to generate location hypotheses, then combines local features within the identified candidate regions. The results show a correct identification of the tomato cluster, correctly characterising the tomato that is occluded and inaccessible by classical vision technologies. The reconstructed 3D model was further validated against manual ground-truth measurements of fruit size, centroid position, and orientation, confirming the geometric accuracy of the proposed low-cost monocular pipeline.

</details>


### [262] [Rapid Learning of Dexterous In-Hand Pen Writing through Real-Time Jacobian Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11775)
*Kai Stewart,Yasunori Toshimitsu,Robert K. Katzschmann*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A real-time, Jacobian-based embodied controller enables dexterous in-hand manipulation of a grasped pen with anthropomorphic hands without analytic models, simulation training, or precollected demos, achieving sub-millimeter precision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome the data-intensive, model-heavy nature of RL/IL and simulator-based approaches in dexterous in-hand manipulation; enable online adaptation on physical hardware without extensive pretraining or hand-object models.

Method: Real-time estimation of the task Jacobian for the combined hand–object system and an online controller run entirely on CPU on a laptop. The approach does not rely on an analytic hand–object kinematic/contact model, simulation-trained policies, or precollected demonstrations. Demonstrated on three hand systems (one physical, two simulated) for in-hand articulation of a grasped pen, after ~18 s initialization.

Result: Sub-millimeter in-plane precision (mean ~0.6 mm) across letters and shapes written in air and on paper on a physical robot. The same estimator/controller formulation transfers across hardware and simulation, enabling arbitrary single-stroke trajectories with a grasped pen purely in-hand.

Conclusion: The work presents a data- and compute-efficient alternative to RL/IL for dexterous in-hand manipulation, with embodiment-independent formulation and online adaptation, achieving high-precision pen writing with a grasped object.

Abstract: Dexterous in-hand manipulation of a grasped object with an anthropomorphic hand is an unsolved frontier for robot dexterity. The contact-richness and highly dynamic nature of object-hand interactions tend to require extensive modeling or data-collection efforts for learning-based approaches. Modern simulators used for reinforcement learning (RL) cannot fully replicate the required contact complexity, while collecting dexterous demonstrations for imitation learning (IL) remains an open problem. In this research, we present an embodied control approach based on real-time task Jacobian estimation of the combined hand and object system on the physical robot. Using only the CPU on a laptop, the proposed controller begins in-hand pen writing after approximately 18 s of initialization and continues to adapt online, without an analytic hand--object kinematic/contact model, simulation training, or precollected task demonstrations. We demonstrate that the same estimator/controller formulation works on three anthropomorphic robotic hand systems (one physical, two simulated) to show human-like, in-hand articulation of a grasped pen by an embodiment-independent formulation. Sub-millimeter in-plane precision (mean 0.6 mm across runs) is achieved across letters and shapes written in the air and on paper on a physical robot. To our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of an anthropomorphic hand writing arbitrary single-stroke trajectories with a grasped pen through purely in-hand motion, and it showcases an alternative to compute- and data-heavy approaches such as RL and IL for achieving dexterous manipulation through computationally simple and data-efficient algorithms.

</details>


### [263] [Learning Agent-based Model Predictive Control for Holistic Vehicle Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11871)
*Jiaming Zhong,Reza Valiollahi Mehrizi,Mohammad Pirani,Chao Yu,Alireza Kasaiezadeh,Yash Vardhan Pant,Amir Khajepour*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Hybrid LAMPC integrates AMPC with online Gaussian Process learning to predict unknown contributions, using multi-step horizon predictions and soft chance constraints to ensure safety, achieving improved tracking and constraint satisfaction in multi-agent systems with real-time capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the reliance on perfect prediction and full knowledge of all agent contributions in agent-based MPC, by introducing a data-driven learning core to model unknown effects and uncertainties.

Method: Gaussian Process Regression (GPR) with online data management learns unknown contributions; predicted mean augments the MPC model; a multi-step prediction horizon leverages learning across time; safety/feasibility guaranteed via soft chance constraints based on GPR variance; applicable to various agent topologies.

Result: Simulations and experiments show LAMPC surpasses traditional AMPC: higher tracking performance in well-learned scenarios and guaranteed constraint satisfaction even in less-learned scenarios; real-time executable with flexible topology.

Conclusion: LAMPC is a promising hybrid control framework for multi-agent systems, effectively combining model-based MPC with data-driven learning to handle unknown contributions while maintaining safety guarantees; future work may address scalability, robustness, and deeper theoretical guarantees.

Abstract: Agent-based model predictive control (AMPC) has recently been proposed as a distributed scheme that collaborates with all agents to achieve optimal holistic performance. However, its optimality highly depends on the prediction accuracy that requires all agents or their contributions to be known, which is too idealistic for actual implementation. This research proposes a novel practical hybrid control scheme - learning agent-based MPC (LAMPC), combining the model-based AMPC approach and data-based learning methods to improve the holistic vehicle performance for multi-agent systems. The Gaussian process regression (GPR) enhanced by an online data management strategy serves as the learning core to predict unknown contributions. A novel multi-step prediction mechanism leverages the GPR learning potential along the horizon. The predicted mean, representing the learned unknown contributions, completes the system model in the MPC for more accurate control. Meanwhile, a stochastic framework is formulated to guarantee control safety and feasibility using soft chance constraints based on the prediction variance. Both simulations and experiments show that, with the learning capability, LAMPC outperforms the traditional AMPC. LAMPC can achieve higher tracking performance in well-learned scenarios and always guarantee constraint satisfaction even in less-learned scenarios. Moreover, the proposed hybrid control scheme is efficient for real-time implementation and is flexible to any control agent topology.

</details>


### [264] [UniMPA: A Unified Memory-Prediction-Action Model via Action-Grounded Transition Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11875)
*Wei Li,Rui Shao,Jie He,Lingsen Zhang,Ziwei Liu,Liqiang Nie*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: UniMPA: a unified memory-prediction-action framework to bridge observation-to-action gaps in Vision-Language-Action robotic manipulation by leveraging persistent prediction streams and memory banks to ensure executable, context-aware transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Observation-to-action learning in VLA for robotic manipulation is hindered by a transition realizability gap, manifested as transition ambiguity, prediction-execution mismatch, and experience-realization mismatch. There is a need to ground future predictions in executable history and adapt past experience to the current scene.

Method: Introduce a shared action-grounded transition interface with (1) Persistent-Selective Future Prediction: a persistent latent stream for task progress and a transition-critical pixel stream with memory-grounded prediction; (2) Temporal Visual-Action Memory Bank to verify executability by retrieving past realized visual-action experiences; (3) Action-Visual Memory Bank with Prototype-Biased Flow to adapt past actions to the current scene and guide flow toward historically supported action manifolds.

Result: Conceptual framework proposed with integrated modules; the abstract does not report empirical results or datasets.

Conclusion: A unified memory-grounded approach to resolve the three coupled observation-to-action problems, enabling executable, context-aware learning for Vision-Language-Action robotic manipulation.

Abstract: Recent advances in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have improved robotic manipulation, yet observation-to-action learning remains limited by a fundamental transition realizability gap, manifested in three tightly coupled problems: (i) Transition ambiguity. Visually similar current observations may correspond to different manipulation phases and imply different subsequent transitions. (ii) Prediction--execution mismatch. A visually plausible predicted future observation does not necessarily correspond to a physically realizable transition. (iii) Experience--realization mismatch. A historically executable action pattern may not necessarily realize the intended transition in the current scene and therefore requires context-aware adaptation. Accordingly, we propose UniMPA, a Unified Memory-Prediction-Action model that addresses these problems through a shared action-grounded transition interface. (i) UniMPA introduces Persistent-Selective Future Prediction to resolve transition ambiguity by modeling the intended future state evolution. A persistent latent stream continuously tracks task-level progress, while a transition-critical pixel stream selectively resolves fine-grained interaction changes through memory-grounded prediction. (ii) To assess the physical executability of the anticipated transition, the predicted transition queries a temporal Visual-Action Memory Bank. The bank retrieves historically realized visual-action experience, grounding future prediction in executable evidence. (iii) To adapt executable experience to the current scene, an Action-Visual Memory Bank retrieves visually grounded action prototypes from historical action evolution. Prototype-Biased Flow then shifts the flow source toward a historically supported action manifold for context-aware refinement.

</details>


### [265] [EVPeriscope: Extended Perception across Aerial and Ground Vehicles with Event-based Propeller Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11920)
*Dexter Ong,Vijay Kumar,Pratik Chaudhari*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Event-based perception system enabling reliable relative localization of a quadrotor with a ground robot by detecting the quadrotor's propeller signatures using an upward-facing event camera; achieves high-frequency onboard control and robust operation under occlusion and varied lighting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address limitations of frame-based cameras (motion blur, lighting sensitivity, payload constraints) for relative localization and tight coordination between aerial and ground robots, enabling operation in challenging environments with occlusion and degradation of onboard sensors.

Method: Use an upward-facing event camera on the ground robot to detect high-frequency propeller events; estimate relative pose and control the quadrotor with onboard sensing and computation at 200 Hz; operate as an extended perception system when ground sensors are degraded or occluded; validate through field experiments under wind up to 15 mph, daylight, and night, including dense foliage.

Result: Demonstrated reliable localization and closed-loop navigation in challenging conditions; quadrotor functions as an extended perception system for the ground robot; event-based approach maintains performance where traditional cameras falter; project page provides further details and videos.

Conclusion: Event-based perception with propeller-signature detection enables robust heterogeneous collaboration and tight coordination between aerial and ground robots, offering high-frequency onboard perception and resilience to occlusions and lighting variations.

Abstract: Reliable relative localization between aerial and ground robots is a key requirement for tightly coordinated heterogeneous teams. This can be difficult to do using conventional frame-based cameras and fiducial markers because they are sensitive to motion blur, lighting variations, and payload constraints. This paper presents EVPeriscope, an event-based perception system that enables detection, localization and control of a quadrotor using an upward-facing event camera on a ground robot by detecting the high-frequency visual signature of its propellers. This system allows the quadrotor to function as an extended perception system for the ground robot when onboard sensors exhibit degradation or occlusion. We demonstrate the capabilities of this marsupial ground-aerial system via experiments in challenging field conditions with wind speeds of up to 15 mph, in both daylight and at night. We show that the system supports localization and closed-loop navigation through dense foliage where the ground robot's sensors are occluded. Our control system for the quadrotor operates at 200 Hz entirely with onboard sensing and computation. More details and experiment videos can be found on the project page: https://ongdexter.github.io/evperiscope.

</details>
